<SYSTEM>This is the abridged developer documentation for restactd</SYSTEM>

# restactd

> Upload a small sandboxed program, call it at a URL.

restactd is the reference server for REST:ACT. Clients deploy verbs, short programs in JavaScript, Python or any language that compiles to WebAssembly, and call them with `GET` and `POST`. Each call runs in a sandbox under compute, time and memory limits, with only the access it was granted and only the authority of its caller. Build restactd and the JavaScript runner first: see [Install](/docs/start/install/). 
```sh
cargo run -p restactd        # terminal 1: the server on :8080
# terminal 2:
echo 'function invoke(input) { return `Hello, ${input || "world"}!`; }' > hello.js
curl -X POST --data-binary @hello.js \
  'localhost:8080/v1/modules?verb=greeter:hello&kind=function&lang=text/javascript'
curl 'localhost:8080/v1/greeter:hello?input=Ada'        # Hello, Ada!
```
 ## Where to start Get started Install restactd, deploy your first verb, then build a small multi-user app. [Install](/docs/start/install/), then the [quickstart](/docs/start/quickstart/). Concepts Objects, verbs, capabilities, budgets, identity and discovery, explained once. [The model in one page](/docs/concepts/overview/). Guides Write verbs in JavaScript, Python or Rust, store data, compose verbs and add authentication. [Write verbs in JavaScript](/docs/guides/javascript/). Operate Run restactd in Docker, choose a store, watch it, and check it against a production checklist. [Production checklist](/docs/operate/production/). Reference Every route, environment variable, feature flag, WIT interface and error code. [HTTP API](/docs/reference/http-api/). Architecture How the host, the sandbox executor and the capability layer fit together, drawn as C4 diagrams generated from the source. [Overview](/docs/architecture/overview/).

# Host components

> This diagram opens the host container. Each component is one module of the host crate, and an arrow means that one module imports and uses the other (use crate::…), or refers to it by path often…

This diagram opens the host container. Each component is one module of the host crate, and an arrow means that one module imports and uses the other (`use crate::…`), or refers to it by path often enough to count. Every arrow is labelled `uses`. The number of references between the pair, counted from the source, decides which arrows the overview shows, so an arrow is evidence of a real dependency in the source and nothing else. The host container carries the accent outline; modules are neutral. Modules are grouped by the architectural concern they serve. The groups come from the repository’s subsystem notes, and a module that no note lists falls into support. [](/docs/diagrams/2ba4a0bbb82b1fdc.svg) [Open full size (opens in a new tab)](/docs/diagrams/2ba4a0bbb82b1fdc.svg)[Download SVG](/docs/diagrams/2ba4a0bbb82b1fdc.svg) The overview shows the most-connected modules, those joined to another by an arrow of at least 35 references, and the group views below list every module. It hides the arrows under 35 references, the minor ones. Each group below has its own view with the group’s modules and the neighbours joined to a member by an arrow of at least 35 references. A group with more than 12 modules is split into several views. ## HTTP surface The router and the handlers behind it: invocation, the management plane for deploying and configuring verbs, the object data plane, listing, schedules and subscriptions, and mediated outbound calls. This is where a request enters and where errors become problem documents. [](/docs/diagrams/c6a9a9639d92f38e.svg) [Open full size (opens in a new tab)](/docs/diagrams/c6a9a9639d92f38e.svg)[Download SVG](/docs/diagrams/c6a9a9639d92f38e.svg) ## Execution engine The core of the host. It resolves a route to a verb, instantiates the component with its limits, runs it, and mediates every capability call. It also owns the verb registry, composition, and native verbs. [](/docs/diagrams/dcb8e9e2476a21c0.svg) [Open full size (opens in a new tab)](/docs/diagrams/dcb8e9e2476a21c0.svg)[Download SVG](/docs/diagrams/dcb8e9e2476a21c0.svg) ## Capability confinement The code that decides what a verb may do: the policy matchers, the identity chain, and the shared read and write rules for object data. A verb’s authority is bounded here, and the data plane and the verb capabilities share this code. [](/docs/diagrams/7bf5fc9ee50ca561.svg) [Open full size (opens in a new tab)](/docs/diagrams/7bf5fc9ee50ca561.svg)[Download SVG](/docs/diagrams/7bf5fc9ee50ca561.svg) ## Executor backends The seam between the HTTP layer and the code that runs verbs, with the in-process executor and the process-isolation executor behind it, plus the stores: memory, filesystem and PostgreSQL, and the coordination of writes. [](/docs/diagrams/889526cf7cf2f219.svg) [Open full size (opens in a new tab)](/docs/diagrams/889526cf7cf2f219.svg)[Download SVG](/docs/diagrams/889526cf7cf2f219.svg) ## Discovery The discovery document and the generated OpenAPI description. Both are built from the live state of the server, so optional features appear exactly when they are active. [](/docs/diagrams/3cafef98b5918623.svg) [Open full size (opens in a new tab)](/docs/diagrams/3cafef98b5918623.svg)[Download SVG](/docs/diagrams/3cafef98b5918623.svg) ## Support Modules that serve several concerns: configuration of quotas and limits, telemetry, secrets, events, the compile cache and shared helpers. **HostSupport1** [](/docs/diagrams/9727530d96767508.svg) [Open full size (opens in a new tab)](/docs/diagrams/9727530d96767508.svg)[Download SVG](/docs/diagrams/9727530d96767508.svg) **HostSupport2** [](/docs/diagrams/3e9d1558aa809859.svg) [Open full size (opens in a new tab)](/docs/diagrams/3e9d1558aa809859.svg)[Download SVG](/docs/diagrams/3e9d1558aa809859.svg) **HostSupport3** [](/docs/diagrams/8869b59555d2c19e.svg) [Open full size (opens in a new tab)](/docs/diagrams/8869b59555d2c19e.svg)[Download SVG](/docs/diagrams/8869b59555d2c19e.svg) ## restact mcp components The MCP bridge is the `restact mcp` subcommand, with its own small set of modules: tool listing and calling, the built-in admin tools and the transports. Discovery reading, route building and the HTTP calls live in the client core below. The bridge turns what the host advertises into tools an AI agent can call. [](/docs/diagrams/ec322ea601b5e093.svg) [Open full size (opens in a new tab)](/docs/diagrams/ec322ea601b5e093.svg)[Download SVG](/docs/diagrams/ec322ea601b5e093.svg) ## Client core components The `restact` client core is the library the MCP bridge builds on for every call to a host. A library is not a deployable unit, so the diagram shows the bridge depending on it and no arrow from it to restactd. It holds the discovery model, the route builder that refuses unsafe paths, problem parsing and the HTTP client. [](/docs/diagrams/8c405d058a1f0625.svg) [Open full size (opens in a new tab)](/docs/diagrams/8c405d058a1f0625.svg)[Download SVG](/docs/diagrams/8c405d058a1f0625.svg)

# Containers

> This diagram looks inside restactd. Its containers are the boxes typed [Container: …]. The others, including restact mcp and the conformance suite, are separate systems. A container here is a…

This diagram looks inside restactd. Its containers are the boxes typed `[Container: …]`. The others, including `restact mcp` and the conformance suite, are separate systems. A container here is a separately deployable or separately trusted unit, not necessarily a Docker container. ## What you are looking at * Host. The Rust server: HTTP routing, authentication, policy, the executor and the discovery document. Almost all logic is here. It embeds Wasmtime to run verbs. * Verbs. The uploaded components. They are untrusted code and run inside the host’s sandbox, one instance per invocation. * JavaScript and Python runners. Two trusted components, built once and loaded by the host. Script verbs are stored as source and interpreted by the matching runner inside the same sandbox. * Object and blob store. Where deployments, object data and binary values live. It can be in memory, on the filesystem, or in PostgreSQL. * `restact mcp`. The MCP bridge, a subcommand of the `restact` client that talks to the host over HTTP. It holds no state of its own and has no access to the host’s internals. * Conformance suite. A black-box test client that checks any compliant server against its discovery document. The line between trusted and untrusted is the most important thing to read from this view. The host and the two runners are trusted. The verbs are not. [](/docs/diagrams/18c73677015c64c6.svg) [Open full size (opens in a new tab)](/docs/diagrams/18c73677015c64c6.svg)[Download SVG](/docs/diagrams/18c73677015c64c6.svg) ## Relationships worth noting The host is the only container that touches the store. Verbs reach data only through the host’s capability interfaces, and script verbs reach it through a runner that goes through the same interfaces. `restact mcp` and the conformance suite see only what any HTTP client sees. For the guarantees this arrangement provides, read the [Security model](/docs/architecture/security-model/).

# System context

> This diagram shows restactd as one box and the people and systems around it. It is the outermost view of the C4 model: who uses the server, and what it depends on.

This diagram shows restactd as one box and the people and systems around it. It is the outermost view of the C4 model: who uses the server, and what it depends on. The diagram is generated from the repository (see [How the diagrams are made](/docs/architecture/c4-model/)). Elements appear only when the repository has the code for them, so an integration that is not built into this checkout does not show up. ## What you are looking at * People and clients. API clients and applications call the server over HTTP. Verb authors upload verbs to it. AI agents reach it through the `restact mcp` bridge, which is a client of the same HTTP API. * restactd. The server itself: it stores objects and verbs and runs verbs in a sandbox. Its internals are the next level down, in [Containers](/docs/architecture/c4-containers/). * Conformance suite. A black-box test client that checks a host against its discovery document. It appears as an external system that calls the server over HTTP. * External systems. The server can use an OpenID Connect identity provider to verify bearer tokens, PostgreSQL as a shared store, a secrets backend for credentials, an OpenTelemetry collector for traces and metrics, and external HTTP origins that it calls on a verb’s behalf under an operator allowlist. Each is optional. Arrows point from the caller to the thing called, and each carries a short label for the protocol or purpose. [](/docs/diagrams/babb034fb7abe234.svg) [Open full size (opens in a new tab)](/docs/diagrams/babb034fb7abe234.svg)[Download SVG](/docs/diagrams/babb034fb7abe234.svg) ## Reading it The server never calls out on its own initiative to an arbitrary address. Every outbound arrow is configured by the operator: the identity provider URL, the database URL, the secrets mount, the collector endpoint and the egress allowlist. See the [Security model](/docs/architecture/security-model/) for how outbound calls are bounded.

# Deployment

> This diagram shows how the reference container image and the compose files lay the system out at run time.

This diagram shows how the reference container image and the compose files lay the system out at run time. ## What you are looking at * A multi-stage `Dockerfile` builds the host, the example verbs and both runners into a slim image that runs as a non-root user. It binds `0.0.0.0:8080` inside the container, with `localhost` as the allowed host name, and keeps durable state in a `/data` volume. * The PostgreSQL compose file: two host nodes share one PostgreSQL database. This is the active-active layout: the nodes are stateless, and a load balancer in front of them can send any request to either. * The OpenBao compose file: a host reads credentials from a secrets backend through a mounted token. This is the layout egress mediation requires. The services are taken from those files, so the diagram follows them when they change. The generated model is `docs/architecture/c4/workspace.dsl`, and [How the diagrams are made](/docs/architecture/c4-model/) lists the rule for each node. [](/docs/diagrams/607a44560e48d5e6.svg) [Open full size (opens in a new tab)](/docs/diagrams/607a44560e48d5e6.svg)[Download SVG](/docs/diagrams/607a44560e48d5e6.svg) ## Reading it A single node needs only the image and a volume. The other two files add a database or a secrets store. For the options that wire them up, see [Configuration](/docs/reference/configuration/), and for running the image, [Run with Docker](/docs/operate/docker/). The [Production checklist](/docs/operate/production/) lists what to review before you expose a node.

# How the diagrams are made

> The architecture diagrams are derived from the repository, not drawn by hand, so a diagram cannot show a module that no longer exists or miss one that does. The derived model is a Structurizr DSL…

The architecture diagrams are derived from the repository, not drawn by hand, so a diagram cannot show a module that no longer exists or miss one that does. The derived model is a Structurizr DSL file, `docs/architecture/c4/workspace.dsl`, which is committed. A check fails when the file no longer matches the source, so a change to the code that moves an arrow shows up as a diff in review. ## What each part comes from | Part of the model | Derived from | Rule | | ------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | System description | `README.md`, or `docs-site/c4-descriptions.json` | The `system` entry of the override file whenever it is present. Otherwise the whole sentences of the first prose paragraph that fit in 160 characters, with the title and badges skipped and markdown removed. | | People | Fixed, plus `cli/src/mcp` | A client and a verb author always; an AI agent when the `restact mcp` bridge module exists. | | Containers | Directories | The host, the verbs and the object store always; the JavaScript runner when `examples/js-runner` exists and the Python runner when `examples/py-runner` exists. The host’s technology line carries the Wasmtime version from `host/Cargo.toml`. | | External systems | Files | An external system appears only when its source does: the identity provider needs `host/src/oidc.rs`, PostgreSQL needs `host/src/pg`, outbound origins need `host/src/egress.rs`, telemetry needs `host/src/telemetry`, the secrets backend needs `docker-compose.openbao.yml`, and the conformance suite needs `conformance/Cargo.toml`. | | Host components | `host/src` | One component per `.rs` file or subdirectory. Test files, modules the crate root declares under `#[cfg(test)]`, and the `tests` and `bin` directories are left out. | | Component description | The module’s leading `//!` comment | The first sentence of the first paragraph, with markdown and references to internal documents removed, cut at 120 characters with a trailing `…`. A module whose comment cannot be made to read well on its own gets an entry in `docs-site/c4-descriptions.json`. | | Groups | `docs/architecture/subsystems/*.md` | Each subsystem note lists the files of one concern under `sources:` in its frontmatter. A module belongs to the first group, in a fixed priority order, that lists its file or a path inside its directory. Modules in none go to Support. Every listed path must exist, or the generator fails and names the note and the path. | | Arrows between components | The crate source | A lexical scan counts the references from each module to each other module: the names a `use` brings in, qualified paths such as `store::Store::new`, and later uses of an imported name. Comments, strings and `#[cfg(test)]` items are not counted, and method calls are not seen. An arrow exists when a non-test `use` links two modules, or when the references between them reach the minimum weight. The crate root `lib.rs` is the component `lib`. Every arrow is labelled `uses`. | | Which arrows show | `--min-weight` (default 35) | A host arrow whose ordered pair of modules has fewer references than that is minor. The `HostComponents` overview hides minor arrows. A group view shows its members and the neighbours joined to a member by a non-minor arrow. Arrows in the client core and the bridge are never minor. | | Split views | Member count | A group of more than 12 members becomes numbered views (`HostSupport1`, `HostSupport2`, …) over the module names in sorted order. | | restact mcp components | `cli/src/mcp` | The same module rule as the host, without groups. One scan of `cli/src` serves the bridge and the client core. | | Client core components | `cli/src` | The same module rule as the host, without groups, when `cli/Cargo.toml` exists. The crate root `lib` and the `testing` support module are left out. The bridge builds on the core (a library) and reaches its `host` module; the core has no edge to the host, because the bridge process makes the HTTP calls. | | Deployment | `Dockerfile`, `docker-compose.postgres.yml`, `docker-compose.openbao.yml` | One node per service in the compose files and one for the image. | ## Colours The diagram colours are not set in the generator. It reads the `--c4-*` colour tokens from the site’s stylesheet, `docs-site/src/styles/theme.css`, and exits with an error naming any token that is missing. Only the element in scope carries the accent outline: the restactd system in the context view, and the host container in the container, deployment and host component views. The `restact mcp` and client core component views have none. The site build removes the accent from the enclosing restactd boundary so that each view has one lit element. Other elements use the neutral element, person and external colours. Relationship lines use the edge colour; the plate, soft ink and edge-label tokens are carried by the page styles and the Mermaid theme, which the DSL cannot set. ## From DSL to page 1. `make c4-gen` scans the source and writes `docs/architecture/c4/workspace.dsl`. Commit the result. 2. `make check-c4` regenerates the model in memory and fails if it differs from the committed file. The message names the first line that differs. It reads only the repository, so it needs no Docker and no prebuilt data. 3. Structurizr validates the file and exports each view to Mermaid with `export -format mermaid`, run from a Docker image pinned by digest. The exports land under `docs-site/build/c4/` and are not committed. 4. The site build replaces each `<!-- c4:ViewKey -->` line in the architecture pages with the matching diagram. A marker ending in `*`, such as `<!-- c4:HostSupport* -->`, embeds every numbered view of that name. In the container view it also removes the restactd boundary box, because Mermaid cannot lay the external systems out cleanly around it. 5. The Mermaid renders to SVG at build time. If a required source is missing, the generator exits with an error that names the path. If a subsystem note lists a file that does not exist, it names the note and the path. Output is sorted and has no timestamps, so two runs on the same tree are byte-identical, and a fresh checkout reproduces the committed file. ## Build the site locally You need the tools in `mise.toml` (`mise install` gives Node 24 and pnpm) and Docker for real diagrams. `make docs-site-install` installs the site’s dependencies and also downloads the headless Chromium that draws the diagrams; it draws them even when `C4_EXPORT=skip` replaces the C4 views with placeholders. 
```sh
make docs-site-install                 # dependencies and the diagram browser
make c4-gen                            # rewrites docs/architecture/c4/workspace.dsl from the source
make check-c4                          # fails if the committed file is stale
make c4-local                          # serves the Structurizr views for browsing
make docs-site-dev C4_EXPORT=skip      # site dev server with placeholders, no Docker
make docs-site-dev                     # dev server with real diagrams (needs Docker)
make docs-site-check                   # the full gate
```
 The dev server listens on `http://127.0.0.1:9191/restactd/`. It serves the copy of the pages under `docs-site/src/content/docs/`, so an edit under `docs/` shows up only after you run `make docs-site-sync` again. `C4_EXPORT=skip` lets you edit pages without Docker. The generator and the site checks have their own tests in `docs-site/scripts/`. ## Changing what the diagrams show To change a group, edit the subsystem note that lists the files. When code moves, update the `sources:` of the note that names it, because a missing path fails the generator. To change an arrow, change the code: the diagram reports the references that exist, and a pair drawn as minor becomes visible in the overview once it reaches 35. To change a description, edit the module’s doc comment, or add an entry to `docs-site/c4-descriptions.json`. To change a colour, edit the `--c4-*` tokens. Then run `make c4-gen` and commit the DSL with the change. Do not edit the generated file by hand.

# Architecture

> This page maps the repository layout, the host's modules, and the execution and capability model. Request lifecycle follows a request.

This page maps the repository layout, the host’s modules, and the execution and capability model. [Request lifecycle](/docs/architecture/request-lifecycle/) follows a request. ## Repository layout (components) REST:ACT is a protocol *and* a Rust reference implementation. The repo is organized so the protocol artifacts (spec, contract, schema, conformance) are independent of the particular server that implements them. | Path | Component | Role | | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------ | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `spec/` | **Decisions** | Maintainer design notes. The protocol specification (the draft, concepts, registries) lives in [stackumi/restact](https://github.com/stackumi/restact). | | [`wit/`](https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/tree/main/wit) | **Contract** | The `rest-act:plugin@0.1.0` WIT world — the host/guest interface every verb implements. | | [`openapi/`](https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/tree/main/openapi) | **Description format** | The `x-restact` OpenAPI 3.1 extension for describing verbs (kind, binding, capabilities, call graph), plus a strict JSON Schema and a validator. | | [`host/`](https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/tree/main/host) | **Reference server** | The Rust implementation: axum HTTP layer + Wasmtime executor. The management and invocation planes. | | [`sdk/`](https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/tree/main/sdk) | **Guest SDK** | `restact-sdk` (`sdk/rust/`), the Rust crate for *writing* verbs, plus guidance for other languages. | | [`examples/`](https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/tree/main/examples) | **Sample verbs** | `word-count` (function), `summarize` (action + composition), and the test verbs the black-box suites use. | | [`conformance/`](https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/tree/main/conformance) | **Conformance suite** | A black-box runner that drives *any* compliant server and is the executable definition of “compliant”. | | [`site/`](https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/tree/main/site) | **Website + playground** | Static site and an interactive verb playground, served same-origin by the host — opt-in (`RESTACT_BUILTIN_SITE=1`, off by default). | | [`scripts/`](https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/tree/main/scripts) | **Dev/test scripts** | Bootstrap, builds, and the black-box test suites. See [Development](/docs/project/development/). | ## The host (`host/src/`) The reference server has two planes — a **management plane** (deploy and configure verbs) and an **invocation plane** (run them) — layered over a small set of pluggable subsystems. 
```plaintext
                       ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   HTTP request  ───▶  │  lib.rs   (axum: routing, dispatch, errors)  │
                       │   • /healthz, site + in-repo docs (opt-in)   │
                       │   • /.well-known/restact  (discovery)        │
                       │   • /v1/modules           (upload/deploy)    │
                       │   • /v1/<obj>:<verb>      (invoke)           │
                       │   • /v1/<obj>:<verb>/<prop> (code/policy/…)  │
                       │   • /operations/{id}      (async)            │
                       └───────┬───────────────┬───────────┬─────────┘
                               │               │           │
                    ┌──────────▼──┐   ┌─────────▼────┐  ┌───▼─────────┐
                    │ registry.rs │   │  policy.rs   │  │  quota.rs   │
                    │ verb lookup │   │ auth.rs      │  │ per-tenant  │
                    │ + binding   │   │ authorization│  │ caps        │
                    └──────┬──────┘   └──────────────┘  └─────────────┘
                           │
                ┌──────────▼───────────┐        ┌──────────────────────┐
                │  executors.rs (seam) │───────▶│  store.rs (Store)     │
                │   ├ wasm_exec.rs     │        │   ├ MemStore (ephemeral)│
                │   │  in-process      │        │   └ FsStore (durable,   │
                │   └ process_exec.rs  │        │      content-addressed) │
                │      worker subproc  │        └──────────────────────┘
                └──────────────────────┘
```
 ### Core modules The table covers the core modules and the security-relevant ones. It is not an exhaustive list of `host/src/`. | Module | Responsibility | | --------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | **`lib.rs`** | The **library crate root** + axum HTTP layer: the `run`/`run_default` entry points (the embedding boundary — [Embed restactd in a Rust app](/docs/guides/embedding/)), `run_worker`, routing/dispatch (`handle_v1`), discovery, the `:verb/{code,policy,meta,props}` management plane, async operations, the static site + curated in-repo doc serving, verb bootstrap, the `HostBuilder` + `register_route` adapter, and the per-concern handler modules (`discovery.rs`, `management.rs`, `invoke.rs`, `data_plane.rs`, `object_list.rs`, `registrations.rs`). RFC 9457 problem mapping lives in `http_util.rs`, and the site and bootstrap code in `site.rs` and `bootstrap.rs`. **`main.rs`** is a thin binary that calls `restactd::cli::main()`, which runs the `--help`, `--version`, `healthcheck`, `checkpoint`, `restore`, `reown`, `audit ancestry` and `token mint` commands to completion and otherwise calls `restactd::run_default()`. See [HTTP surface](https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/blob/main/docs/architecture/subsystems/http-surface.md). | | **`wasm_exec.rs` + `wasm_exec/`** | `WasmtimeExecutor`. **One execution path** — every verb is a wasip2 **component** (`bindgen!` on the superset `plugin-object-compose-event-llm` WIT world). Owns fuel/epoch/memory limits, composition (shared budget, runs-as-caller), the gated `object`/`event`/`identity` capabilities, and data-property enforcement. **Native (host-implemented) verbs** are a `DeployedVerb` kind `invoke_core` dispatches to a Rust fn via `wasm_exec/native.rs`’s `NativeCtx` — the trusted analogue of the sandbox host state, reusing the same capability cores. The **JavaScript/Python tiers** register a shared QuickJS / monty **runner** component and *interpret* the stored source under the same limits/capabilities (no per-upload compilation). | | **`registry.rs`** | Verb resolution, keyed on `(object-path-pattern, verb)` with an ancestor walk. `Binding` = Entity (`{id}`) / Collection / Unbound. A `DeployedVerb` carries the component `hash` (or, for a script verb, the shared runner + a `source_sha256`; or a `native` handle), `imports`, `allowed_calls`, and the `PropertyDef` declarations. Native verbs are defaults registered by the embedder. | | **`store.rs`** | The `Store` trait: `MemStore` (ephemeral) and the durable `FsStore` (content-addressed `blobs/`, `deployments.json`, `registrations.json`, `grants.json`, a `format.json` layout stamp, per-object attribute `state/` + an `objects.json` path index; `@shared/<def-path>` keys hold `shared`-scoped properties). The ordered `query(prefix/order_by/predicate/cursor)` primitive backs `GET /v1` + the native `query` capability — a default scan on Mem/Fs, an indexed SQL query on PostgreSQL (`pg/store.rs`, feature `postgres`). Binary **object values** reuse the blob store via a `$blob` attribute reference. | | **`policy.rs`** | Authorization via canonical subject matchers (`public`/`owner`/`sub:`/`role:`/`group:`/`scope:`/`claim:`/`prop:`, plus the verb-policy-only `readers:<path>`). Verb policy actions (`read`/`execute`/`write`) are OR-lists of matchers, as are per-property ACLs and object `$policy`. A verb deployed through `POST /v1/modules` is born owner-only for every action; an object defaults to public read and owner write. See [Capability confinement](https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/blob/main/docs/architecture/subsystems/capability-confinement.md). | | **`auth.rs`** | The ordered identity chain → `Principal`: trusted forwarded-identity header + HS256 JWT. Absent secret → anonymous-only. | | **`oidc.rs`** | Generic OIDC/JWKS verification of a forwarded RS256/ES256 bearer JWT: alg pinned from the JWK, audience mandatory, `iss`/`aud`/`exp`/`nbf`/`iat` fail-closed; JWKS fetched over HTTPS behind an injectable seam (TTL-cached, throttled, run off-reactor). Consulted between the trusted header and HS256. | | **`quota.rs`** | Per-tenant fuel, call, storage, object-count and egress (`egress_calls`, `egress_bytes`, `egress_wall_ms`) quotas, fail-closed — lifetime cumulative or windowed (`RESTACT_QUOTA_WINDOW_SECS`); names the tripped dimension + window rollover for the 429. | | **`executors.rs`** | The `Executor` trait — the seam that lets the same verb run in-process or via process isolation. | | **`process_exec.rs`** | The process-isolation backend: a worker subprocess carrying the full principal (`RESTACT_ISOLATION=process`). | | **`sync.rs`** | Small synchronization helpers used across the planes. | | **`egress.rs`** | The mediated outbound call (`RouteContext::mediated_call`, feature `mediated-egress`): one bounded request through a connector object, with the owner’s secrets spliced in host-side. Reachable only from embedder routes. See [HTTP surface](https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/blob/main/docs/architecture/subsystems/http-surface.md). | | **`origin_guard.rs`** | The `Host` allowlist, the cross-origin request check and the non-loopback bind interlock. | | **`grants.rs`** | The delegation grants API (`/v1/tokens/grants`), the token exchange, and client-actor code redemption. Answers 404 while `delegated-tokens` is not advertised. | | **`delegation.rs`** | The delegation record that rides beside a delegated principal, and the two refusals every edge draws from it. | | **`sub_tokens.rs`** | Path-scoped sub-tokens: minting (`POST /v1/tokens/sub`) and the deny-only gate consulted at every `/v1` entry point. | | **`secrets.rs`** | The external secrets backend seam. No verb or response ever resolves a value; the mediated-egress splice is the only resolver. | | **`llm.rs`** | The `llm` typed connector’s dispatch, sent through the same `egress.rs` machinery as the mediated call. | | **`events.rs`** | The in-memory event bus: a bounded ring per topic for replay, plus a broadcast channel for live SSE fan-out. | | **`checkpoint.rs`** | Durable checkpoints (`POST /admin/checkpoint`, off unless `RESTACT_ADMIN_CHECKPOINT` is set) and the `@host/checkpoint` events. | | **`mcp_facet.rs`** | The optional remote MCP facet at `/mcp` (`RESTACT_MCP=1`): a stateless Streamable HTTP server on the host’s own router. | ## Execution & capability model Every verb runs as a sandboxed WebAssembly guest. There is **no ambient authority**: a verb can only reach capabilities its WIT world *imports*. * **Substrate.** [Wasmtime](https://wasmtime.dev) (Cranelift), running WASI 0.2 Component Model components. Components are validated and content-addressed at upload; a cached `InstancePre` is reused per invocation under a pooling allocator. * **The worlds (capability manifest).** A verb targets `plugin` (host `log` only), `plugin-compose` (adds gated `compose.invoke`), `plugin-object` (adds gated `object` data access), or `plugin-object-compose` (both). The `identity` import comes with the object worlds; `event` and `llm` have their own worlds. The host binds the superset world. The world *is* the declaration: the host links a gated interface only for a verb that imports it, and Wasmtime wires only declared imports — so deny-by-default falls out for free. See [`wit/`](https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/tree/main/wit) and [Authoring Verbs](/docs/guides/authoring-verbs/#the-worlds). * **Native verbs & embedding.** Beside uploaded components, a `DeployedVerb` may be backed by a **host Rust function** — a TCB verb the embedding application registers through `HostBuilder`, resolved/composed/run-as-caller exactly like any verb (it consumes no fuel; it’s trusted). This is how a platform exposes its own privileged primitives (an indexed cross-user query, a media transform) as composable verbs that untrusted uploads may call but not bypass. See [Embed restactd in a Rust app](/docs/guides/embedding/). * **Object-data capability.** The `object` interface (`get`/`set` on the bound object’s declared data properties) is ACL- and scope-aware and attenuated like `compose`; the decision logic is the pure `prop_read`/`prop_write_check`. The bare-object **data plane** (`GET`/`PUT /v1/<obj>`) is the same data viewed as the object’s representation. * **Resource limits (the triad).** Epoch interruption (wall-clock → `408`), fuel (deterministic CPU → `429`), and a `ResourceLimiter` (memory → `507`). These are normative regardless of which executor backend runs the verb. * **Import allow-list.** At upload the host inspects the component’s imports and refuses anything outside the world (e.g. a verb importing `wasi:sockets` is rejected `403 disallowed-import`). * **Script tiers (interpreted, not compiled).** A `text/javascript` upload is stored as the verb’s *source* and **interpreted** at invoke time by one trusted, shared QuickJS **runner** component the host ships: the host frames the stored script + request input into the runner’s `invoke(payload)`, and the runner bridges the host capabilities (`log`, `compose`, `obj.get`/`set`/`getAt`) to JS globals — so a JS verb has the same behaviors, gating, and limits as a wasm verb. There is no per-upload compilation (no compiler attack surface), and no second execution path: the runner is itself a component. A `text/x-python` upload is interpreted the same way by a shared monty runner component, with `log`, `compose`, `publish`, `obj`, `caller` and `llm` bridged to the matching imports. ### Composition A `plugin-compose` verb may invoke another verb via `compose.invoke` (by route id, resolving up the tree). The host mediates every edge, deny-by-default: 1. **Declared call graph** — every callable edge is declared at deploy time (`?calls=a,b`); undeclared edges are refused before execution and the graph is statically auditable. 2. **Authority attenuates, never escalates** — the callee runs with a *subset* of the caller’s authority (consistent with runs-as-caller). 3. **One shared budget** — the entire call tree debits a single resource pool (the primary denial-of-wallet defense). 4. **Bounds** — max depth, max fan-out, and host-side cycle detection (a cycle → `508`). 5. **Side-effect rule** — functions chain freely; actions chain only when declared. 6. **Cross-server composition** is just REST:ACT calling REST:ACT over HTTP — the protocol is recursive. ### Identity: runs-as-caller A verb runs with the **caller’s** principal. The invariant: > A verb’s effective authority = caller’s permissions ∩ the verb’s declared capabilities ∩ the target’s policy. Cost is charged to the caller. A verb may opt into running with its **owner’s** subject-only authority instead (setuid-style, `?runs_as=owner`) — the execute check still runs against the real caller, billing stays the caller’s, and it’s the deliberate exception for a privileged primitive a platform exposes. ## Storage The `Store` trait abstracts persistence. `RESTACT_DATA_DIR` selects the durable `FsStore`; without it the host uses the ephemeral `MemStore`. * **`blobs/`** — content-addressed component/module bytes and original source (dedup by hash). * **`deployments.json`** — the verb registry (route id → binding, kind, code hash, policy, call graph). * **`registrations.json`** — schedules and topic subscriptions. * **`grants.json`** — delegation grants. * **`format.json`** — the layout stamp the host checks before it loads the directory. * **`page-token.key`** — the 32-byte key (mode 0600) that seals listing page tokens and verb query cursors. The server creates it at first boot; checkpoints do not copy it. On PostgreSQL it is the `host_keys` row `page-token`. * **`state/`** — per-object attribute state written by verbs through the data-property ABI; `@shared/<def-path>` keys hold `shared`-scoped properties. An `objects.json` path index backs the ordered listing. Two data-plane enablers sit on this store: * **The query primitive (collections).** `Store::query` — ordered (by a property value), prefix-scoped, predicate-filtered, keyset-paginated — backs `GET /v1` object listing and the native `query` capability (ACL-filtered above the store, so listing ⊆ readable). A default scan on Mem/Fs; a real indexed SQL query with a `(order, path)` keyset on PostgreSQL. * **Binary object values.** A property whose value is binary (an avatar, post media) stores the bytes in the content-addressed blob store and keeps a small `$blob` reference in the attribute map — so the per-object size cap and whole-map rewrite apply only to the reference, not the media. Served back with the stored `Content-Type` + a content-hash `ETag`; read by verbs via `get-bytes`. The active-active **PostgreSQL backend** (feature `postgres`, `RESTACT_DATABASE_URL`) is a third `Store` letting N stateless nodes share one database. Durability survives restart: an uploaded verb and its compiled module are re-loaded from the store (verified by `scripts/persistence-test.sh`). ## Executor abstraction & portability The host does not hard-wire in-process Wasmtime. The `Executor` seam (`executors.rs`) lets the *same* component run in-process (`wasm_exec.rs`) or under per-request process isolation (`process_exec.rs`, `RESTACT_ISOLATION=process`), with identical observable behavior — proven by running the full conformance suite against both backends. A verb’s declared limits are normative regardless of backend. > **Worker stdout is sacred.** On the `RESTACT_WORKER` subprocess path, only the response goes to stdout; all diagnostics use stderr. See [Development → Conventions](/docs/project/development/#conventions--constraints). ## Conformance & optionality The protocol is a **small mandatory core + independently-advertised optional features**. A host advertises what it supports in `service.features` of the discovery document; a client never assumes an unadvertised feature, and a verb may require a feature (the host returns `501` if it can’t satisfy it). The [conformance suite](https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/tree/main/conformance) gates each check on the discovery document — so the same suite validates a thin stateless gateway and a full transactional world server.

# Request lifecycle

> This page follows two requests through the host: a GET that runs a function and a POST that runs an action. Module names refer to files under host/src/. The two verbs in the example are bootstrapped…

This page follows two requests through the host: a `GET` that runs a function and a `POST` that runs an action. Module names refer to files under `host/src/`. The two verbs in the example are bootstrapped JavaScript examples that a host with the JS runner deploys at startup when it runs with `RESTACT_EXAMPLES=1` (as `make run` does). 
```sh
curl 'localhost:8080/v1/hello:greet?input=Ada'          # a function, GET: Hello, Ada!
curl -X POST localhost:8080/v1/counters/a:increment     # an action, POST: {"count":1}
```
 ## The path in one picture [](/docs/diagrams/17d305f485d95ac2.svg) [Open full size (opens in a new tab)](/docs/diagrams/17d305f485d95ac2.svg)[Download SVG](/docs/diagrams/17d305f485d95ac2.svg) ## A function call, step by step 1. Router. `build_router` in `lib.rs` maps `/v1/{*path}` to `handle_v1`. Two middlewares in `origin_guard.rs` run first: the `Host` allowlist and the cross-origin check. A request timeout and in-flight cap apply to the whole request. 2. Path split. `handle_v1_inner` splits the path at the last colon. With no colon, the request is on the data plane (`data_plane.rs`). With one, the left side is the object and the right side is the verb, optionally followed by `/<property>`. 3. Authentication. `authenticate_request` in `lib.rs` walks the identity chain (trusted header, then OIDC through `oidc.rs`, then an HS256 token through `auth.rs`) and produces a principal. A `restact.auth` tracing span wraps the whole dispatch. 4. Admission rules. The router rejects a mutation by an anonymous caller when authentication is configured. For a bare verb, `GET` means the function kind and `POST` the action kind. 5. Invocation. `invoke_op` in `invoke.rs` hands the request to the executor on a blocking thread, behind a limiter that caps concurrently executing verbs. The request body or the `?input=` query string becomes the verb’s input. 6. Resolution. In `wasm_exec.rs`, `WasmtimeExecutor::invoke` asks `registry.rs` to resolve the route: the object path is matched against deployed patterns with an ancestor walk, and the deepest match wins. Among patterns at one level, the one with a literal at the first differing segment from the left wins. A kind mismatch is `405`. 7. Quota. `quota.rs` checks the tenant’s fuel and call counters. Over quota returns `429`. 8. Policy. `invoke_core` checks the verb’s execute policy (`policy.rs`) for the principal. Anonymous callers who fail get `401`, named callers `403`. A verb deployed to run as its owner switches principal after this check. 9. Instantiation. The verb’s component, compiled once and cached by `compile_cache.rs`, is instantiated with a fresh store carrying fuel, an epoch deadline and a memory limiter. Script verbs instantiate the shared runner and pass it the stored source with the input. 10. Capabilities. While the verb runs, each host call goes through the linked interfaces. `object.get` consults `store.rs` through the same read rules as the data plane. `compose.invoke` re-enters `invoke_core` for the callee with the shared budget. A function’s attempt to write returns `403`. 11. Response. Output bytes return to `invoke_op`. For a function it computes an `ETag`, honours `If-None-Match` with `304`, and adds `Cache-Control: private, no-cache` and `Vary: Authorization`. Errors are mapped to RFC 9457 problem documents by `http_util.rs`. ## An action call, step by step The first five steps match. The differences: [](/docs/diagrams/1852a201eb61fd33.svg) [Open full size (opens in a new tab)](/docs/diagrams/1852a201eb61fd33.svg)[Download SVG](/docs/diagrams/1852a201eb61fd33.svg) * `invoke_action` first handles an `Idempotency-Key`. A repeated key from the same authenticated caller replays the original result instead of running again. A claim on a key is released if the request is dropped. * The executor runs the action under the same checks. Writes to the verb’s own object are buffered and merged into the store only if the invocation succeeds. Writes to other objects commit at the call, unless the server runs at the `atomic-invocation` consistency level, which commits the whole write set together. * After the response is decided, a data change the verb made can trigger lifecycle hooks and event delivery. These run as separate invocations under their registrant’s authority and do not alter the response. * An action `POST` that sends `Prefer: respond-async` returns `202` with `Location: /operations/{id}`, which the client polls. ## With process isolation With `RESTACT_ISOLATION=process`, step 5 changes: `process_exec.rs` starts a worker subprocess, passes it the principal and the input, and reads back the output. The worker opens the same durable store and applies the same resolution, policy and limits. The `Executor` trait in `executors.rs` is the seam that lets both backends return the same results.

# Security model

> restactd runs code that strangers upload. The design assumes from the first request that an upload may be malicious, and each layer below limits what such a verb can do. This page describes what the…

restactd runs code that strangers upload. The design assumes from the first request that an upload may be malicious, and each layer below limits what such a verb can do. This page describes what the server enforces and where. ## Threat model The assets are the host process, other tenants’ data and compute, and the secrets the host holds. The attacker is a caller who can upload a verb (where policy allows it) and invoke verbs, and who controls the verb’s code, input and the data it writes. The attacker has no access to the host’s operating system, the network between operator and server, or the operator’s configuration. The trust boundary is the Wasmtime sandbox. Everything inside it is untrusted: uploaded components, and the scripts that run in the shared runners. Everything outside it is the trusted computing base. ## Layers ### Sandbox Every verb is a WebAssembly component. A component has no ambient authority: it can call only the host interfaces it imports. No world offers sockets, and the filesystem interface is linked with no directories. At upload the host reads the component’s imports and rejects any outside the offered set with `403`. See [Capabilities and budgets](/docs/concepts/capabilities-and-budgets/). ### Capability confinement Two invariants hold for every capability, in plain terms: * A verb never exceeds its caller. The `object` capability applies the same read and write rules as the HTTP data plane, evaluated for the caller, against the object’s owner. A verb bound to another user’s object can read and write only what the caller could through `GET` and `PUT`. The write check is shared code, so the data plane and the capability cannot drift. * A function is pure. Functions cannot write, publish or call actions, and the host refuses each at runtime. A `GET` therefore cannot change state, which is what makes the result safely cacheable. ### Metering Fuel, a wall-clock deadline and a memory cap bound every invocation, on both executor backends. Operators set ceilings that uploader-requested limits are clamped to, and per-tenant quotas on fuel, calls and storage. A composition tree draws on one shared budget, with bounded depth and fan-out and cycle detection. See [Capabilities and budgets](/docs/concepts/capabilities-and-budgets/#metering). ### Run as the caller A verb executes with the caller’s authority, never more. Verbs that call verbs pass down at most the same authority. A verb can opt into running as its owner, but the execute check still runs against the real caller first, and cost still goes to the caller. Lifecycle hooks run as the principal who deployed them, so the user who triggers a hook does not lend it their authority. See [Identity and authority](/docs/concepts/identity-and-authority/). ### Anonymous mutation Once any authentication is configured, anonymous callers can read and invoke public actions but cannot mutate. Deploys, management writes and data-plane writes return `401`. With no authentication configured, the server is open by design and meant for loopback only. ### Request admission Every request’s `Host` header must be a loopback name or listed in `RESTACT_ALLOWED_HOSTS`, which stops DNS-rebinding pages from reaching the server. `/healthz` and `/readyz` are exempt, so probes that address the container by IP work. A non-loopback bind without that list refuses to start. Browser-origin writes and every `/mcp` request from another origin are refused unless `RESTACT_CORS_ALLOW_ORIGIN` admits the origin. Global limits cap in-flight requests and concurrently executing verbs, and requests have a timeout that also covers the body transfer. ### Authentication Identity comes from a verified OIDC token (RS256 or ES256, with the algorithm pinned from the signing key and a mandatory audience), an HS256 token, or a trusted header from a proxy. OIDC verification fails closed on any fetch or claim failure. All three OIDC variables (issuer, JWKS URL and audience) are required: setting only some of them, or any to an empty value, refuses boot and names what is missing. The JWKS URL must be `https://` (plain `http://` only to a loopback address), and the boot line `auth: OIDC/JWKS verification enabled …` confirms it. A set-but-unusable JWT secret or trusted-header setting refuses boot too, so a host never starts with auth silently off. The trusted header is unsigned, so it is safe only when the server is reachable solely through the proxy that sets it. ### Process isolation With `RESTACT_ISOLATION=process`, each request runs in a separate worker subprocess, so tenants do not share a process address space. The worker carries the full principal and applies the same limits, and the full conformance suite passes on both backends. The compile cache that workers share is sealed with an HMAC and checked before each load. ### Egress mediation A verb has no network access. When the host makes an outbound call on a verb’s behalf, such as for the `llm` interface, the host does the work: * The destination must match an operator allowlist on the declared name. The host resolves it once, checks every resolved address against blocked ranges, and pins one, so DNS cannot rebind between check and use. Redirects are refused, and TLS verification is on. * The credential is stored as a secret, spliced in by the host at send time, and never visible to the verb. Secret values are zeroed on drop and never printed. * Request and response sizes, time and call counts are bounded and metered per principal. * A server configured with an egress allowlist but no secrets mount, or without a connector prefix, refuses to start. * Connector records must be governed so that only their owner can change them, both at call time and when they are created. ### Secrets handling in logs Caller-, guest- and deployer-chosen values are escaped before they reach a log line, so they cannot forge entries. Credentials embedded in operator-configured URLs are stripped before logging. ### Storage PostgreSQL connections require TLS by default. Weaker modes are refused unless you set the explicit plaintext opt-out, which is intended for loopback development. A durable data directory that cannot be opened stops the server rather than silently falling back to memory. ## Trusted computing base These components must be correct for the guarantees above to hold: * The host (`restactd`), including its HTTP layer, policy engine and executor. * Wasmtime, the WebAssembly runtime. * The two runner components, QuickJS and monty, which interpret all script verbs. Every script verb shares a runner, so a runner defect would affect all of them. They are built once and the host loads them from files you choose. * Native verbs registered by an embedding program, which run as trusted host code. ## Operator responsibilities The server cannot enforce these for you: * Configure authentication before exposing the server beyond loopback. * Terminate TLS in front of the server and set `RESTACT_ALLOWED_HOSTS` to the names clients use. * Keep the trusted-header mode behind a proxy that sets the header and strips client copies, or use OIDC. * Decide who may deploy verbs, and seed collection roots before users arrive. Object ownership is first-writer-owns. * Set quotas and request ceilings that fit your load, and use a durable store. * Protect the secrets mount, the JWT secret and the compile cache key. * Run the production checklist in [Production checklist](/docs/operate/production/). ## Reporting a vulnerability Report a suspected vulnerability privately, using GitHub’s private vulnerability reporting on the restactd repository. Please do not open a public issue for a security problem.

# Capabilities and budgets

> A verb can reach only what it imports, and it can spend only what its budget allows. This page explains both limits and what a caller sees when one is hit.

A verb can reach only what it imports, and it can spend only what its budget allows. This page explains both limits and what a caller sees when one is hit. ## Capabilities Every verb is a WebAssembly component built for a WIT world. The world lists the host interfaces the component imports, and the host links nothing else. No world offers sockets, and the WASI filesystem interface is linked with no directories, so a verb has no way to read a file or open a connection. At upload the host inspects the component’s imports and refuses any it does not offer: a component importing `wasi:sockets` is rejected with `403 disallowed-import`. The interfaces live in [`wit/`](https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/tree/main/wit). Some are ambient, granted to every verb that imports them. Others are gated, meaning the host links them only for verbs that import them and then checks each call. | Interface | Kind | What it lets a verb do | | -------------- | ------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `host` (`log`) | ambient | Write a structured log record. Rate limited per tenant. | | `identity` | ambient | Read the caller: `subject`, `is-anonymous`, `roles`, `scopes`, `groups`, `claim` and `actor`. `invoker` is also in the interface but is not ambient: it returns a value only for a verb deployed with `?invoker=1`. It grants no authority the verb does not already have. | | `object` | gated | Read and write the data of the object the verb is bound to, read and write other objects by path, list an object’s shape, read binary values, run a paged `query`, and use `set-at-if` for compare-and-swap writes. | | `compose` | gated | Call another verb by route. | | `event` | gated | Publish an event to a topic. | | `llm` | gated | Request one bounded text completion through a connector object, a stored record that names the endpoint and a secret. The model credential never reaches the verb. | Outbound HTTP follows the same rule. A verb has no network access of its own. The only outbound paths are host-mediated calls, such as the `llm` interface, where the host holds the credential, checks the destination against an operator allowlist and sends the request itself. See [Outbound calls and secrets](/docs/guides/egress/). A JavaScript or Python verb lists only the interfaces it holds, in discovery and in the deploy response. It is granted `llm`, and so reports `networkAccess: "outbound"`, only when it is deployed with `POST /v1/modules` while the server offers `llm-connector`. To grant it after enabling the connector, deploy the verb again that way; code and policy writes keep the stored grant. Which worlds exist, and which Cargo feature or language global exposes each interface, is in [Authoring reference](/docs/guides/authoring-verbs/#the-worlds). Whether a given server offers an interface is advertised in discovery; see [Discovery and optional features](/docs/concepts/discovery/). ### Gated does not mean broader than the caller Gating decides whether a verb can call an interface at all. It does not widen what a call can do. The `object` capability applies the same read and write rules as `GET` and `PUT /v1/<obj>`, evaluated for the caller. A verb bound to someone else’s object reads and writes only what the caller could through the HTTP API. The write check for other objects is the same code the data plane uses. Function purity works the same way: a function that calls `object.set`, `event.publish` or an action is refused at runtime, and a function that declares a call to a deployed action is rejected at deploy with `400 invalid-call-graph`. Script verbs are in the same position, with one difference. The JavaScript and Python runners are single shared components, so their capability set is not a per-verb import list. The host enforces the same call graph, object rules and policy at runtime. See [Language tiers](/docs/concepts/language-tiers/). ## Metering Each invocation runs under three limits, enforced by Wasmtime: | Resource | Mechanism | When it runs out | | ---------- | ---------------------------------------------- | ---------------- | | CPU | Fuel, a deterministic instruction count | `429` | | Wall clock | Epoch interruption, a deadline in milliseconds | `408` | | Memory | A cap on guest linear memory | `507` | A verb that requests nothing gets 200,000,000 fuel units, 2 seconds of wall clock and 32 MiB of memory. It may request its own limits at deploy time with `?fuel=`, `?wall_ms=` and `?mem_mb=`. The host clamps each request to an operator ceiling (`RESTACT_MAX_VERB_FUEL`, `RESTACT_MAX_VERB_WALL_MS`, `RESTACT_MAX_VERB_MEMORY_MB`; defaults 100000000000, 30000 and 512), so an upload cannot grant itself unbounded compute. The wall-clock limit is the real bound on CPU time. The same limits apply on both executor backends. A JavaScript or Python verb is parsed again on every call, and the parse uses part of the run fuel before the verb’s own code starts. A large source can use up the default 200,000,000 units on loading alone and answer `429`. Roughly, depending on the source, Python hits this at about 73 KB and JavaScript at about 185 KB; both are accepted at upload up to 256 KiB. Deploy a large source with a higher `?fuel=`, up to the operator’s ceiling. Beyond the per-invocation limits, operators can set per-tenant quotas on cumulative fuel, call count and stored bytes, optionally over a rolling window. A tenant over quota gets `429` and, for a windowed quota, an accurate `Retry-After`. See [Configuration](/docs/reference/configuration/). ## One budget under composition A verb in a composing world can call other verbs with `compose.invoke`. Each call must be listed in the calling verb’s declared call graph (`?calls=a,b` at deploy), and the host refuses an undeclared edge before anything runs. An action may also declare a pattern like `*:audit`, which names a verb of that name on any object. A function that declares a pattern is rejected at deploy with `400 invalid-call-graph`. The whole call tree draws on a single pool. The host debits the pool at each call boundary, so a tree of depth eight does not get eight budgets. The host also bounds depth (8) and fan-out (16 calls per frame) and detects cycles. A callee gets at most the caller’s authority, unless it is deployed to run as its owner. Functions chain freely, and a function may call only functions. ## What a caller sees Budget failures are ordinary HTTP errors with an RFC 9457 problem body. | Situation | Status | `restact_code` | | ---------------------------------------------- | ------ | ----------------------- | | Wrong method for the verb kind | `405` | `method-not-allowed` | | Out of fuel, or shared budget exhausted | `429` | `cpu-budget` | | Tenant quota exceeded | `429` | `quota` | | Wall-clock deadline reached | `408` | `timeout` | | Memory limit reached | `507` | `memory-limit` | | Composition cycle, depth or fan-out limit | `508` | `composition-cycle` | | Call to a verb outside the declared call graph | `403` | `not-declared` | | A function attempting a side effect | `403` | `forbidden-side-effect` | 
```json
{
  "detail": "CPU budget exhausted",
  "restact_code": "cpu-budget",
  "restact_verb": ":spin",
  "status": 429,
  "title": "Too Many Requests",
  "type": "about:blank"
}
```
 The full list of codes is in [Errors and status codes](/docs/reference/errors/). Writes to the verb’s own object are merged only if the invocation succeeds, so a verb that runs out of budget leaves them unwritten. A write to another object with `set-at` commits at the call. With `RESTACT_CONSISTENCY=atomic-invocation`, the whole write set of an invocation, including those, commits together or not at all.

# Discovery and optional features

> A client asks the server what it supports instead of assuming. The answer is a JSON document at /.well-known/restact, and a generated OpenAPI description at /openapi.json.

A client asks the server what it supports instead of assuming. The answer is a JSON document at `/.well-known/restact`, and a generated OpenAPI description at `/openapi.json`. 
```sh
curl localhost:8080/.well-known/restact
```
 The response has two top-level members: `service`, the server’s configuration, and `verbs`, the deployed verbs visible to the caller (those it may read, run or own) with their route and descriptor. 
```json
{
  "service": {
    "version": "…",
    "consistency": "conditional",
    "objectBinding": "implicit",
    "accessorDispatch": true,
    "lifecycleHooks": true,
    "openapi": "/openapi.json",
    "features": ["data-plane", "composition", "…"],
    "acceptedSourceLanguages": ["application/wasm", "…"],
    "defaultCapabilities": { "world": "plugin", "networkAccess": "none", "…": "…" }
  },
  "verbs": [ { "route": "…", "verb": { "…": "…" } } ]
}
```
 This is trimmed; the real document carries more members. Some, such as `delegation` and `subTokenTtl`, appear only when their feature is on. See the [HTTP API](/docs/reference/http-api/#discovery) for the full shape. ## What `service` tells you | Field | Meaning | | ------------------------------------ | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `features` | Tokens naming each optional capability the server implements. | | `consistency` | The guarantee for object writes: `conditional` (per-object, with `ETag` and `If-Match`) or `atomic-invocation` (a verb’s whole write set commits or none of it does). | | `objectBinding` | How deploying a verb relates to its object: `implicit`, `auto-create` or `strict`. | | `accessorDispatch`, `lifecycleHooks` | Whether accessor verbs and lifecycle hooks are active. | | `acceptedSourceLanguages` | The source media types an upload may name, in `?lang=` on `POST /v1/modules`: always `application/wasm`, plus `text/javascript` and `text/x-python` when their runner is loaded. | | `defaultCapabilities` | The baseline world and limits for a verb. | Each entry in `verbs` has the `route`, and a `verb` descriptor with its `name`, `kind` (function or action), `binding`, `sourceMediaType`, `callGraph` and `capabilities`. The `capabilities` object holds the world, the imported interfaces, the declared limits (`maxComputeUnits`, `maxDurationSeconds`, `maxMemoryBytes`) and `networkAccess`. `produces` and `runsAs` appear when the verb sets them. Owner-sensitive fields are left out of the descriptor. ## Optional features The protocol has a small mandatory core. Everything else is optional and advertised as a token in `features`. Examples are `object-listing` for `GET /v1`, `verb-object-query` for the paged `query` call, `object-self-path` for a verb reading its own object’s path, `caller-groups`, `compose-patterns`, `runs-as-owner`, `lifecycle-hooks`, `mediated-egress` and `llm-connector`. Some tokens depend on configuration: `mcp-facet` appears only when `/mcp` is mounted, and `mediated-egress` and `llm-connector` only when an operator has configured the outbound allowlist. The reference of tokens is [Feature flags in discovery](/docs/reference/features/). Two rules follow: * A client checks for a token before using the feature, and falls back or stops when it is absent. * A verb that needs a feature the server does not offer is refused. A component that imports an interface the server lacks cannot be deployed, and a request for an unsupported optional behaviour returns `501`. The conformance suite relies on the same mechanism. It reads the discovery document and runs only the checks whose features are advertised, so one suite validates a minimal server and a full one. See [Conformance suite](/docs/reference/conformance/). ## OpenAPI `GET /openapi.json` returns an OpenAPI 3.1 document of the live surface: every verb route the caller may read or invoke as a path, with the REST:ACT descriptor attached under `x-restact`. It is built from the same source and filtered the same way as discovery, so it reveals nothing the discovery document does not show that caller. Its strong `ETag` is a hash of the filtered body, so a tag from one caller’s view never matches another’s. It honours `If-None-Match`. The server always serves it, and `service.openapi` points to it. An operator can set `RESTACT_OPENAPI_SPEC` to a hand-written OpenAPI 3.1 file. The server then merges that document with the live parts on each request: verb descriptors always stay current, and the operator’s own operation objects and data-plane documentation pass through. See [Configuration](/docs/reference/configuration/).

# Identity and authority

> Every request is made by a principal, and a verb acts with that principal's authority. This page covers who the principals are, how access rules are written, and what changes once you turn…

Every request is made by a principal, and a verb acts with that principal’s authority. This page covers who the principals are, how access rules are written, and what changes once you turn authentication on. ## Principals The host resolves the caller from the request through an ordered chain: a trusted identity header from a proxy (if configured), a verified OIDC bearer token (if configured), an HS256 bearer token, and finally anonymous. The first source that yields a named principal wins. A principal has a subject, and optionally roles, scopes and groups. Setup is in [Add authentication](/docs/guides/authentication/). With no authentication configured, every request is the `anonymous` principal. That is meant for local development on loopback. The subject name `anonymous` is reserved, and a credential that claims it is refused. ## Run as the caller A verb executes as the principal that called it. Its effective authority is the intersection of: * what the caller is allowed to do, * what the verb declared it needs (its imports and call graph), * what the target’s policy allows. A verb therefore cannot do anything the caller could not do directly, unless it is deployed to run as its owner. When verbs call verbs, each callee runs with at most the caller’s authority. Cost is charged to the caller. A verb can read who is calling through the `identity` capability: `subject`, `is-anonymous`, `roles`, `scopes`, `groups`, individual token claims, and `actor` (the delegation actor, when one bounds the call). That lets it key data per user or record an audit line. It cannot change them. ### Running as the owner A verb deployed with `?runs_as=owner` is the exception. It runs with its owner’s subject-only authority, like a setuid program. Inside it `subject` is the owner; `invoker`, available only to a verb deployed with `?invoker=1`, returns the subject that started the call tree. The execute check still runs against the real caller, and the bill still goes to the caller. Use it for a privileged operation the platform deliberately exposes, such as a write that only the owner could otherwise perform. Lifecycle hooks work the same way: they run as the principal who deployed the hook, not as the user who triggered it. ## Policies and matchers Verbs, individual object properties and whole objects each carry an access policy. A policy maps actions to lists of matchers. A verb policy takes `owner`, `read`, `execute` and `write`; managing a verb is always owner-only. An object `$policy` takes `read`, `write` and `delete`. A request is allowed if any matcher in the list admits the principal. | Matcher | Admits | | ---------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `public` | everyone, including anonymous callers | | `owner` | the object’s or verb’s owner | | `sub:<id>` | one subject | | `role:<r>` | principals holding a role | | `group:<g>` | principals in a group | | `scope:<s>` | principals whose token carries an OAuth scope | | `claim:<k>=<v>` | principals whose token has that claim value | | `prop:<name>` | the subject stored in the named property of the object (object policies and property ACLs; admits no one on an object with no data) | | `readers:<path>` | whoever may read the object at `<path>`, checked live (verb policies only) | A new verb defaults to `owner` for `execute`, `read` and `write`, so on a host with authentication only its deployer can run it until the policy is widened (for example `{"execute":"public"}` on `…:verb/policy`). The first writer of an object becomes its owner, and a verb’s deployer owns the verb. Object-level `$policy` sets a baseline `read`, `write` and `delete` rule for an object, and property ACLs can narrow it. A property can also be declared write-once. For per-instance rules, `prop:<name>` admits the subject named by that object’s own `<name>` value. The routes for editing policies are in the [HTTP API](/docs/reference/http-api/). ## Who owns a new object The first principal to write an object at a path where nothing exists owns it. Ownership attaches to the path being created, not to its neighbours: a principal that creates `ws/notes` beneath someone else’s `ws` owns `ws/notes`, as long as the policy that applies at that path lets it write there. The default policy on a path with no governing policy admits any authenticated principal. A parent does not hand out a separate right to create beneath it, and the owner of `ws` has no say over who creates `ws/notes` unless `ws` is an anchor. On a host shared by several tenants this means a child path under another principal’s object can be taken by whoever writes first. To keep that in your hands: * Create the top-level containers yourself, as the operator, before users arrive, and create each container before the objects that belong under it. * Turn on `RESTACT_POLICY_INHERITANCE` and mark the containers as anchors: an object whose `$policy` carries `"inherit": true` governs every path below it that has no nearer anchor. A first write under an anchor is admitted only when the anchor’s `write` policy admits the writer, so an anchor with `"write": ["owner"]` lets only its owner create beneath it. * Turn on `RESTACT_GOVERNED_CREATION` as well. A creating write outside every anchor is then refused `403` unless it carries a `$policy` of its own, so no one can quietly take an ungoverned path. Verbs can never write `$policy`, so a verb that creates objects outside an anchor is refused too. * Use ids that cannot be guessed where a path would otherwise be predictable. A dedicated permission to create beneath an object, granted by the object’s owner, is planned. Until it exists, the steps above are the way to control who may create where. See [Configuration](/docs/reference/configuration/) for both variables. ## Anonymous callers Once any authentication is configured (a JWT secret, OIDC, or a trusted identity header), anonymous callers can read and invoke public actions but cannot mutate. Deploys, verb management writes, and data-plane `PUT`, `PATCH` and `DELETE` answer `401` for them. A verb’s execute policy still decides whether an anonymous caller may invoke it. With no authentication configured, the deployment is open on purpose: every caller is anonymous and therefore the owner of anything anonymous created. Do not expose such a host beyond loopback. ## Who may define a verb Replacing the code of an existing verb is governed by that verb’s write policy. Defining a verb at a new route depends on the binding mode (see [Verbs, objects and routes](/docs/concepts/verbs-and-objects/#binding-modes)): under the coupled modes you must own the object you deploy onto. On a host with auth configured, every verb at the service root or on a leading-wildcard route is limited to the operator named by `RESTACT_ADMIN_RESET`. The full rules are in [Configuration](/docs/reference/configuration/#who-may-define-a-verb-where).

# Language tiers

> Every verb runs as a WebAssembly component in one sandbox. What differs is how it gets there. You can bring a compiled component, or upload JavaScript or Python source that the host interprets…

Every verb runs as a WebAssembly component in one sandbox. What differs is how it gets there. You can bring a compiled component, or upload JavaScript or Python source that the host interprets inside a shared runner component. | Tier | You upload | Needs a toolchain | Who runs it | | ---------- | ----------------------------------- | ----------------- | ------------------------- | | Component | a `.wasm` built for `wasm32-wasip2` | yes | the component itself | | JavaScript | a `.js` source file | no | the shared QuickJS runner | | Python | a `.py` source file | no | the shared monty runner | The upload names its language with the `?lang=` query parameter on `POST /v1/modules` (`lang=text/javascript` or `lang=text/x-python`), or with the `Content-Type` header on a `PUT` to `…:verb/code`. A server accepts only the languages in `service.acceptedSourceLanguages` in [discovery](/docs/concepts/discovery/). A language the server does not offer returns `415 unsupported-language`. On `POST /v1/modules`, a body that is not WebAssembly and has no `?lang=` is read as a component and fails with `400 invalid-component`, whatever its `Content-Type`. ## Components Any language that compiles to a WASI 0.2 component can write a verb. Rust is the reference path, with an SDK crate (`sdk/rust/`) that hides the binding boilerplate. The component targets a WIT world, and that world is its capability list. The host rejects imports outside the offered interfaces at upload. Components get the full capability surface with per-verb enforcement, and they handle binary input and output. See [Write verbs in Rust](/docs/guides/rust/). ## JavaScript A JavaScript verb defines `function invoke(input)`. The host stores the source and, on each request, passes it with the request input to one trusted QuickJS runner component. Nothing is compiled per upload, so the host has no compiler to attack. The runner exposes the host capabilities as globals: `log`, `compose`, `publish`, `obj` (`get`, `getBytes`, `getBytesAt`, `set`, `getAt`, `setAt`, `setAtIf`, `remove`, `removeAt`, `query`, `path`, `shape`, `shapeAt`), `llm` (`complete`) and `caller` (`subject`, `isAnonymous`, `roles`, `scopes`, `groups`, `claim`, `actor`, `invoker`). The runner caps its heap at 24 MiB and its stack at 512 KiB, below the host limits. A script that recurses without end exhausts memory and the request fails with `507` and `restact_code: memory-limit`; the host is unaffected. A source that does not parse is rejected at upload with `422`. See [Write verbs in JavaScript](/docs/guides/javascript/). ## Python A Python verb defines `def invoke(input)`. It is interpreted by monty, a Python interpreter written in Rust, embedded in a runner component. monty implements a subset of the language, and the runner narrows it further: * `log`, `compose` and `publish` are functions. `obj`, `llm` and `caller` are host-provided objects in snake_case: `obj.get`, `get_bytes`, `get_bytes_at`, `set`, `get_at`, `set_at`, `remove`, `remove_at`, `shape`, `shape_at`, `set_at_if`, `path`, `query`, `llm.complete` and `caller.subject`, `is_anonymous`, `roles`, `scopes`, `groups`, `claim`, `actor`, `invoker`. `obj.query` takes keyword arguments and returns a page object, with the same fields as the JavaScript page in snake_case. * Input is a `str`. Return a string, or a dict or list that is serialised as JSON; `None` gives an empty body. What runs, checked against the pinned runner: * Works: functions, lambdas, default arguments, `*args` and `**kwargs` parameters, decorators, `global` and `nonlocal`, comprehensions (list, dict, set) and generator expressions, f-strings with format specs and `!r`, `try`/`except`/`else`/`finally`, `raise ... from`, `assert`, the walrus operator, type annotations, slicing, big integers, and `async def` with `await`, `asyncio.run` and `asyncio.gather`. * Also works: `class` definitions, `%` formatting and `str.format`. * Rejected at upload with `422 invalid-source`: generator functions (`yield`) and other unsupported statements such as `del`, class inheritance and `async for`/`async with`. `match` statements and dataclass `field` work. * Imports: `json`, `re`, `math`, `sys`, `typing`, `asyncio`, `collections`, `itertools`, `functools`, `datetime` values and `random` after an explicit `random.seed(n)`, `hashlib` (digests, not `file_digest` or `scrypt`), `csv`, `bisect`, `tomllib`, `decimal` and the string functions of `os.path` work. The clock and sleep (`time.time`, `time.sleep`, `datetime.datetime.now`), unseeded `random`, `open`, directory listing, `os.path` existence and stat checks, path resolution (`os.path.realpath`) and environment reads fail with `script-error: unsupported operation (os/filesystem)`. Other modules, for example `socket` and `subprocess`, fail with a catchable `ModuleNotFoundError`. There are no third-party packages. * `eval()` and `exec()` run inside the same sandbox, with the script’s own names and limits.
