Never pass caller input to them: the code would run with the verb’s authority, including `runs_as=owner`. * Exceptions: you can catch builtin exceptions, including `RecursionError`. An uncaught one returns `422 script-error` with the traceback. * Limits: recursion is capped at 1000 frames and the heap at 24 MiB. Exceeding the heap raises `MemoryError`. A source that does not parse is rejected at upload with `422`. See [Write verbs in Python](/docs/guides/python/). ## Shared properties of the script tiers * Input arrives as text. A string output passes through, an object or list is serialised as JSON, and a JavaScript `Uint8Array` or `ArrayBuffer` or a Python `bytes` is sent as raw bytes. A script can read a stored binary value up to 6 MiB with `obj.getBytes()` (JavaScript) or `obj.get_bytes()` (Python). For anything larger, or for heavy transforms of the bytes, use a compiled verb. * An uncaught error becomes a `script-error` whose message is returned to the caller. Do not put secrets in error messages. * The script is parsed on each invocation. Keep scripts small. * Both runners are part of the trusted computing base. Every script verb shares one runner, so capability checks happen in the host at run time, not through a per-verb import list. See the [Security model](/docs/architecture/security-model/). * A runner is optional. A server started without one omits that language from discovery. ## Choosing a tier Use JavaScript or Python for glue logic, data shaping and quick iteration: you can upload with `curl` and edit in place. Use a component when you need binary data, a language with a mature ecosystem, or the tightest control over which interfaces the verb imports. A script and a component can call each other with `compose`; the caller cannot tell which tier a callee uses.

# The model in one page

> restactd serves objects over HTTP. Each object has data and verbs: small programs you upload that run in a sandbox on the server. You read and write the data with ordinary GET and PUT, and you run a…

restactd serves objects over HTTP. Each object has data and verbs: small programs you upload that run in a sandbox on the server. You read and write the data with ordinary `GET` and `PUT`, and you run a verb by naming it after the object with a colon. ## An example This verb counts how many times an object has been viewed. It is JavaScript, so there is nothing to compile. 
```js
function invoke(input) {
  const n = Number(obj.get("views") ?? "0") + 1;   // read this object's "views" property
  obj.set("views", String(n));                      // write it back
  return `views: ${n}`;
}
```
 Upload it as an action bound to any object under `profiles/`, then call it: 
```sh
curl -gX POST -H 'Content-Type: text/javascript' --data-binary @view.js \
  'localhost:8080/v1/modules?verb=profiles/{id}:view&kind=action&lang=text/javascript'


curl -X POST localhost:8080/v1/profiles/ada:view      # views: 1
curl -X POST localhost:8080/v1/profiles/ada:view      # views: 2
curl localhost:8080/v1/profiles/ada                   # the object's data, now holding "views"
```
 The verb is deployed once, at the pattern `profiles/{id}:view`. It runs against whichever object you name, here `profiles/ada`, and `obj` is that object’s data. A different `profiles/<name>` gets its own counter. The JavaScript tier needs the shared runner component; see [Language tiers](/docs/concepts/language-tiers/). ## The pieces An object is addressed by a path. A path has three forms: 
```text
/v1/<obj>                     the object's data
/v1/<obj>:<verb>              run a verb on the object
/v1/<obj>:<verb>/<property>   a property of the verb: code, policy, meta, props, history, rollback
```
 A verb is either a function or an action. A function has no side effects and runs on `GET`, so responses can be cached. An action may change state and runs on `POST`. The host enforces the method: `POST` to a function or `GET` to an action returns `405`. See [Verbs, objects and routes](/docs/concepts/verbs-and-objects/). A verb runs inside a WebAssembly sandbox with no authority except what it declares. It cannot open files or sockets. It can write a log line, and, if it imports them, read and write object data or call other verbs. See [Capabilities and budgets](/docs/concepts/capabilities-and-budgets/). By default a verb runs as the caller. Whatever the caller could not do through the HTTP API directly, the verb cannot do either; a verb deployed with `runs_as=owner` runs with its owner’s authority instead. The caller also pays: every invocation draws on a fuel, time and memory budget, and a tree of verbs calling verbs shares one. See [Identity and authority](/docs/concepts/identity-and-authority/). A client learns what a server supports from the discovery document at `/.well-known/restact` instead of assuming. Optional features are advertised there, and a request for an optional behaviour the server lacks returns `501`. A component that imports an interface the server does not offer is refused at upload with `403 disallowed-import`. See [Discovery and optional features](/docs/concepts/discovery/). ## Where to go next * To run a verb of your own: [Quickstart](/docs/start/quickstart/). * To see how a request is handled: [Request lifecycle](/docs/architecture/request-lifecycle/). * To judge the security properties: [Security model](/docs/architecture/security-model/).

# Verbs, objects and routes

> Everything restactd serves is an object at a path. An object holds data, and verbs attach to objects or to patterns of objects. This page covers how paths resolve to verbs and how object data behaves.

Everything restactd serves is an object at a path. An object holds data, and verbs attach to objects or to patterns of objects. This page covers how paths resolve to verbs and how object data behaves. ## Paths 
```text
GET  /v1/animals/dogs/fido            the data of the object animals/dogs/fido
GET  /v1/animals/dogs/fido:noise      run the function "noise" on it
POST /v1/animals/dogs/fido:feed       run the action "feed" on it
```
 The colon separates the object from the verb. Verb names are camelCase and avoid the standard method names. A verb is itself addressable: `/v1/animals/dogs/fido:noise/code` is its source or component, `/policy` holds its owner and access policy, and `/meta` its descriptor: the kind, limits and declared capabilities. `/props` holds its declared data properties, `/history` its earlier versions, and a `POST` to `/rollback` reactivates one. ## Functions and actions | | Function | Action | | ------------ | ------------------------------ | ------------------------------ | | Side effects | none | allowed | | HTTP method | `GET` | `POST` | | Cacheable | yes (`ETag`, `304` on a match) | no | | Composition | may call only functions | may call functions and actions | You choose the kind at deploy time with `kind=function` or `kind=action`. If you leave `kind` out, the verb is an action. A function cannot write object data, publish events or call an action. The host refuses each of those at runtime with `403 forbidden-side-effect`. Function results carry `Cache-Control: private, no-cache` and `Vary: Authorization` because the output can depend on the caller. An action can take an `Idempotency-Key` header, and when the caller is authenticated a retried request with the same key replays the first result instead of repeating the effect. Anonymous callers get no replay. Long-running actions may return `202` with a `Location` header to poll. See [HTTP API](/docs/reference/http-api/). ## Bound verbs and route patterns A verb is deployed at a route, and the route decides which objects it answers for. | Binding | Route | Matches | | ---------- | --------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------ | | Entity | `docs/{id}:wordCount` | `docs/42:wordCount`, any one segment in place of `{id}` | | Collection | `docs:bulkImport` | `docs:bulkImport`, and by the lookup below any object under `docs` | | Unbound | `:ping` | the service root, `/v1/:ping` | When you invoke `docs/42:wordCount`, the verb runs bound to the concrete object `docs/42`. Resolution also walks up the path: `:ping` invoked on `docs/42` looks at `docs/42`, then `docs`, then the root, and runs the first match, still bound to `docs/42`. If two routes both match at one level, they are compared segment by segment from the left, and at the first segment where one has a literal and the other a `{id}`, the literal one wins, regardless of deploy order (`ws/1/{id}` beats `ws/{id}/pages`). A verb can read the concrete path of the object it is bound to, so it can address itself without being told its own name. See the `path` function in [`wit/object.wit`](https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/blob/main/wit/object.wit). ## Object data as a plain noun A path without a verb is the object’s data. It is a JSON object of typed properties (any JSON value; a top-level `null` removes one). 
```sh
curl -X PUT -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
     --data-binary '{"title":"Hello"}' localhost:8080/v1/docs/page
curl localhost:8080/v1/docs/page          # {"title":"Hello"}
```
 `PUT` merges the fields you send. `GET` returns the properties you are allowed to read. A missing object is `404`. The same data is what a verb sees through its `object` capability, so a verb and a client are two ways to reach one store. A non-JSON `PUT` stores binary content, served back with its content type. The first writer of an object becomes its owner. Properties can be declared with read and write rules and a write-once flag. See [Store and query object data](/docs/guides/object-data/). ## Collections `GET /v1` lists the objects you can read, ordered by path and paged with `pageSize` and `pageToken`. `filter=kind=note` keeps objects whose readable `kind` property equals `note`, and `read_mask=title,kind` adds those properties to each entry. A verb can read a page of objects under a prefix with the `query` function of the `object` capability. Both honour the same read rules as a direct `GET`, so a listing contains only what you could fetch one by one. ## Binding modes The binding mode decides whether an object and its verbs are coupled. The operator sets it with `RESTACT_OBJECT_BINDING`, and discovery reports it as `service.objectBinding`. | Mode | Deploying a verb on an object that does not exist | | ------------- | ----------------------------------------------------- | | `implicit` | allowed; object and verbs are independent | | `auto-create` | allowed; the object is created, owned by the deployer | | `strict` | refused with `409`; create the object first | In the coupled modes (`auto-create` and `strict`) you also need to own an existing object to deploy a verb onto it, and `DELETE /v1/<obj>` removes every verb anchored at the object (those bound to it and the `{id}` patterns beneath it) along with its data. If any of them isn’t yours to remove, the whole delete is refused with `403`. A server with authentication configured defaults to `strict`; one without defaults to `implicit`. See [Configuration](/docs/reference/configuration/). ## Accessor verbs A few verb names are reserved to mediate the data plane: `get`, `list`, `create`, `update`, `set` and `delete`. If you deploy a verb with one of these names on an object, a matching request for that object’s data (a `GET`, a `PUT` and so on) runs your verb instead of reading or writing the store directly. This lets an object present a computed representation, or validate and transform writes, while clients keep using plain `GET` and `PUT`. The accessor runs with the caller’s authority like any verb. The lifecycle names `onCreate`, `onChange` and `onDelete` are also reserved: they run after a write or delete commits. See [Lifecycle hooks](/docs/guides/lifecycle-hooks/). ## Native verbs A verb can also be a function in the host’s own Rust code, registered by a program that embeds restactd. A native verb is addressed, composed and authorised exactly like an uploaded one, and it is trusted code, so it consumes no fuel. An uploaded verb at the same route replaces the native default, and removing the upload brings the native one back. See [Embed restactd in a Rust app](/docs/guides/embedding/).

# Use restactd from AI agents

> An AI agent can call the verbs on a restactd server as MCP tools, deploy new verbs when it needs one that does not exist, and follow skills that teach it the HTTP API. This guide connects an agent…

An AI agent can call the verbs on a restactd server as MCP tools, deploy new verbs when it needs one that does not exist, and follow skills that teach it the HTTP API. This guide connects an agent three ways: the MCP endpoint built into the server, the `restact mcp` bridge, and the agent plugins. ## Prerequisites * A running restactd ([Quickstart](/docs/start/quickstart/)). * An MCP client such as Claude Code, or `curl` to follow the protocol by hand. * For authentication, a token as described in [Add authentication](/docs/guides/authentication/). ## Choose a connection | Option | What the agent gets | Runs as | | --------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | ---------------------------------------------------------------- | | Built-in MCP endpoint (`RESTACT_MCP=1`) | Four fixed tools: list and invoke verbs, list and read objects. No writes, no deploy. | Part of the restactd process | | `restact mcp` bridge | One tool per verb on the server, plus tools to deploy, inspect and manage verbs and to read and write object data. | A separate process that is an ordinary HTTP client of the server | | Agent plugins | Skills that teach the agent the HTTP API, and the `restact mcp` bridge registered for you in Claude Code. | A skill set plus the bridge | In every case the agent acts with the caller’s token, so it can do exactly what that user can do and nothing more. ## 1. Use the built-in MCP endpoint Start the server with the endpoint switched on: 
```sh
RESTACT_MCP=1 cargo run -p restactd
```
 The log confirms it: 
```text
mcp facet: on at /mcp
```
 The endpoint speaks MCP over HTTP at `/mcp`. It is stateless, so `GET /mcp` answers `405`, and discovery lists the `mcp-facet` feature. List the tools: 
```sh
curl -s -X POST http://localhost:8080/mcp \
  -H 'Authorization: Bearer '"$TOKEN" \
  -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
  -H 'Accept: application/json, text/event-stream' \
  -d '{"jsonrpc":"2.0","id":1,"method":"tools/list"}'
```
 The server offers four tools: | Tool | What it does | | -------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `list_verbs` | Returns the route, kind and descriptor of each verb you may read or invoke, the same listing `GET /.well-known/restact` gives you. | | `invoke_verb` | Invokes a verb by its concrete route, for example `me:whoami`. A function runs as `GET` and an action as `POST`. Takes an optional `input` object. | | `list_objects` | Lists readable objects, like `GET /v1`. Takes a `prefix`; end it with `/`. A page can come back short or empty while it still carries a `nextPageToken`, so keep paging until there is none. | | `get_object` | Reads one object by `path`. | Call a verb the way an agent would. This example invokes the `me:whoami` verb from [Add authentication](/docs/guides/authentication/#2-show-who-is-calling), with a token from `restactd token mint --sub alice`: 
```sh
curl -s -X POST http://localhost:8080/mcp \
  -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" \
  -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
  -H 'Accept: application/json, text/event-stream' \
  -d '{"jsonrpc":"2.0","id":2,"method":"tools/call","params":{"name":"invoke_verb","arguments":{"route":"me:whoami"}}}'
```
 
```json
{"jsonrpc":"2.0","id":2,"result":{"content":[{"type":"text","text":"{\"anon\":false,\"groups\":[],\"roles\":[],\"scopes\":[],\"subject\":\"alice\"}"}],"structuredContent":{"anon":false,…,"subject":"alice"},"isError":false}}
```
 Each call goes through the same authentication, policy and quota path as the equivalent HTTP request. A missing or bad token comes back as a tool result with `isError: true` and the usual problem body, for example `401 invalid bearer token`. An anonymous request follows whatever the HTTP side allows, so on a host with no authentication configured the endpoint is open too. For a client that supports remote MCP servers over HTTP, point it at `http://localhost:8080/mcp` and give it the bearer token in its header configuration. ## 2. Use the restact mcp bridge `restact mcp` is a subcommand of the `restact` client that translates between MCP and a restactd server. It works against any REST:ACT server, because it builds its tools from the discovery document. It gives the agent more than the built-in endpoint: | MCP surface | Comes from | | ----------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | One tool per verb | `/.well-known/restact`, with input and output schemas taken from the server’s `/openapi.json` when it publishes one | | `invoke_verb` | Any verb by route, including ones deployed after the agent connected | | `deploy_verb`, `delete_verb` | `POST /v1/modules` and `PUT`/`DELETE …/code`. The agent can write a JavaScript or Python verb and use it straight away. | | `get_verb`, `verb_history`, `rollback_verb` | Verb metadata, source and history | | `list_objects`, `get_object`, `put_object`, `delete_object` | The object data plane | | Resources | Objects as MCP resources | The bridge sends `tools/list_changed` after each deploy, so a verb the agent just wrote appears in its tool list. This is the forge loop: the agent finds that no tool answers a question, writes a small function verb, deploys it and calls it once instead of making many calls and carrying the intermediate results. Build it from source. It is part of the `restact` binary in the same Cargo workspace: 
```sh
cargo build -p restact              # produces target/debug/restact
```
 By default it serves MCP over standard input and output and talks to `http://127.0.0.1:8080`. Configure it with environment variables: 
```sh
RESTACT_MCP_HOST_URL=http://127.0.0.1:8080 \
RESTACT_MCP_BEARER="$TOKEN" \
./target/debug/restact mcp
```
 To register it with Claude Code, add it to a project `.mcp.json`: 
```json
{
  "mcpServers": {
    "restact": {
      "command": "/path/to/restact",
      "args": ["mcp"],
      "env": {
        "RESTACT_MCP_HOST_URL": "http://127.0.0.1:8080",
        "RESTACT_MCP_BEARER": "<token>"
      }
    }
  }
}
```
 The common settings: | Variable | Effect | | ----------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `RESTACT_MCP_HOST_URL` | The server to bridge. Without any chosen server (this variable, `RESTACT_MCP_HOSTS`, `RESTACT_URL`, `--url` or a context) the bridge uses `http://127.0.0.1:8080` and sends it no token. | | `RESTACT_MCP_HOSTS` | Several named servers, `alpha=http://a:8080,beta=http://b:8080`. Tool names then carry the server name as a prefix. | | `RESTACT_MCP_BEARER` | The token presented to the server. Without it the agent is anonymous. It is not sent to the unchosen default `http://127.0.0.1:8080`, so set a server too (for example `RESTACT_MCP_HOST_URL`). `RESTACT_MCP_LISTEN` refuses to start with no chosen server. | | `RESTACT_MCP_ALLOW_PLAINTEXT` | The bridge refuses to start when it would send a token to a non-loopback `http://` server named by `RESTACT_MCP_HOST_URL` or `RESTACT_MCP_HOSTS`. `1` accepts plain http for those. A context follows its own `allow-plaintext` setting, and `RESTACT_URL` and `--url` are never covered. Whatever the token, a server URL must be http or https with a host and no credentials, query or fragment, or the bridge exits 2. | | `RESTACT_MCP_ALLOW_DEPLOY` | Set a false value (`0`, `false`, `off` or `no`) to hide the deploy, rollback and delete tools and give the agent a read and invoke bridge only. | | `RESTACT_MCP_ALLOW_DATA_WRITES` | Set a false value to hide `put_object` and `delete_object`. | | `RESTACT_MCP_ALLOW_VERB_ADMIN`, `RESTACT_MCP_ALLOW_REGISTRATIONS` | Off by default. A true value (`1`, `true`, `on` or `yes`) adds tools to set verb policy and to create schedules and subscriptions. | | `RESTACT_MCP_LISTEN` | Serve MCP over HTTP at `http://<host:port>/mcp` instead of stdio. | Over the HTTP transport, an `Authorization: Bearer` header on each MCP request replaces the configured token for that request. Several people can then share one bridge and each act as themselves. A non-loopback `RESTACT_MCP_LISTEN` address makes the configured token the identity of anyone who can reach the port, so the bridge refuses to start there unless you also set `RESTACT_MCP_ALLOW_NONLOOPBACK` to `1` or `true`. Any other value exits 2. The bridge has no authority of its own. Whatever the agent deploys runs in the server’s sandbox with its capability confinement, metering and quotas. ## 3. Install the agent plugins The repository ships two sets of [Agent Skills](https://agentskills.io). A skill is a `SKILL.md` that tells an agent how to do a task, here checked against the server’s discovery document so it works with any compliant server. | Plugin | For an agent that is | | ----------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `restact-client` | Using a live server. Skills cover HTTP basics, auth tokens, publishing verbs, the data plane, and schedules and events. It also registers the `restact mcp` bridge as an MCP server in Claude Code. | | `restact-builder` | Building a system on restactd. Skills cover embedding the host, building applications from verbs, the Rust SDK, conformance, and deployment. | In Claude Code, add the marketplace from a checkout of the repository and install a plugin: 
```text
/plugin marketplace add /path/to/restactd
/plugin install restact-client@restact
```
 For Codex CLI and Cursor, which both read `.agents/skills/`, copy the skills from a checkout: 
```sh
./plugins/install-skills.sh --plugin restact-client --project /path/to/your/project
./plugins/install-skills.sh --global        # every project, into ~/.agents/skills
```
 `--check` reports which installed skills are out of date, and `--uninstall` removes them. On Claude Code, `restact-client` starts the bridge through a script that locates a `restact` binary, so it picks up the same `RESTACT_MCP_HOST_URL` and `RESTACT_MCP_BEARER` variables from the environment Claude Code is launched in. The first start can take several minutes if the bridge has to be built from source. If the connection times out, raise `MCP_TIMEOUT` and reconnect. The binary is cached for later sessions. ## Models in verbs A verb can also call a language model. The `llm` capability asks the host for one text completion through a connector object that names the provider and holds the credential. The verb never sees the key. From JavaScript the call looks like this: 
```js
const reply = llm.complete({
  connector: "ws/1/connectors/claude",
  system: "Answer in one sentence.",
  messages: [{ role: "user", content: input }],
  maxOutputTokens: 200,
});
// reply: { text, stopReason, model, usage: { inputTokens, outputTokens, … } }
```
 The verb must be an action, because a completion has a cost and varies between calls. A verb deployed with `llm=deny` loses the capability. The connector uses the same destination allowlist, secrets and response scrubbing as [mediated egress](/docs/guides/egress/), and the discovery document lists the `llm-connector` feature. ## Next steps * [Write verbs in JavaScript](/docs/guides/javascript/) covers the verbs an agent is most likely to forge. * [Add authentication](/docs/guides/authentication/) shows how to give each agent its own identity and limit what it can do. * [Outbound calls and secrets](/docs/guides/egress/) sets up the connectors an `llm` call uses.

# Add authentication

> By default restactd has no authentication: every caller is anonymous and can do anything. This guide turns on authentication with signed tokens, shows two users getting different results from the…

By default restactd has no authentication: every caller is anonymous and can do anything. This guide turns on authentication with signed tokens, shows two users getting different results from the same verb, and then covers verifying tokens from a real identity provider and running behind an authenticating proxy. ## Prerequisites * restactd built from source with the JavaScript tier ([Install](/docs/start/install/)). * `curl` and `openssl`. Node is needed only for the OIDC section. ## Pick a mode restactd resolves the caller in a fixed order and uses the first source that identifies someone: 1. A trusted identity header set by your proxy, if `RESTACT_TRUSTED_IDENTITY_HEADER` is configured. 2. An OIDC bearer token verified against your identity provider’s keys, if `RESTACT_OIDC_*` is configured. 3. An HS256 bearer token signed with a shared secret, if `RESTACT_JWT_SECRET` is configured. 4. Anonymous. A bearer token or identity header that fails verification is refused before any handler runs, on `/v1` and on custom routes alike: `401`, or `403` for a delegated token on a custom route. It is never served as anonymous. | Mode | Use it for | | -------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | HS256 (`RESTACT_JWT_SECRET`) | Local development, tests and service-to-service tokens you mint yourself. | | OIDC/JWKS (`RESTACT_OIDC_*`) | Production with an identity provider that publishes signing keys. | | Trusted header (`RESTACT_TRUSTED_IDENTITY_HEADER`) | Production behind a proxy that already authenticated the user. | ## 1. Start restactd with HS256 
```sh
export RESTACT_JWT_SECRET=replace-with-at-least-32-random-bytes
cargo run -p restactd
```
 The value above is a placeholder. Generate a real secret with `openssl rand -base64 32`; the host refuses to start with an HS256 secret shorter than 32 bytes. The log confirms the mode: 
```text
auth: HS256 bearer tokens
…
object binding: strict
…
```
 Once any authentication is configured two things change. Anonymous callers can still read, but any write (deploy, data `PUT`, `PATCH` or `DELETE`) answers `401`. And `RESTACT_OBJECT_BINDING` now defaults to `strict`: deploying a verb onto an object that does not exist answers `409`, so create the object first, and deploying onto an existing object requires owning it (`403` otherwise). [Who may define a verb where](/docs/reference/configuration/#who-may-define-a-verb-where) has the full rules. 
```sh
curl -i -X PUT -H 'Content-Type: application/json' -d '{}' http://localhost:8080/v1/x
```
 
```text
HTTP/1.1 401 Unauthorized
{"detail":"authentication required to mutate on this server",…}
```
 ## Mint a test token An HS256 token is a signed JWT. `restactd token mint` signs one for a subject you name. It reads the secret from `RESTACT_JWT_SECRET` in its own environment, never from an argument, so run it where the secret is set (your shell, or `docker exec` into the container): 
```sh
export RESTACT_JWT_SECRET=...   # the secret the server runs with
ALICE=$(cargo run -q -p restactd -- token mint --sub alice --role editor --scope notes:read --scope notes:write --group eng --claim dept=platform)
BOB=$(cargo run -q -p restactd -- token mint --sub bob --role viewer)
```
 Inside the container image, `restactd` is on the path: `docker exec <container> restactd token mint --sub alice`. The container needs `RESTACT_JWT_SECRET` in its environment, which it has when you passed it to the host. `--role`, `--group`, `--scope` and `--claim K=V` repeat. The token lasts one hour unless you pass `--ttl` (`90s`, `5m`, `12h`, `7d`; the longest is `30d`). `--json` prints `{"token": ..., "expiresAt": ...}`, with `expiresAt` in Unix seconds. The command refuses a blank subject, one with leading or trailing whitespace, one with control or format characters (zero-width and bidirectional marks included), `anonymous`, a subject starting `client:`, and claim names the token already uses (`sub`, `exp`, `aud` and the like). The claims restactd reads: | Claim | Becomes | | ---------------- | ---------------------------------------- | | `sub` | the caller’s subject | | `roles` | an array of roles | | `scope` | a space-separated list of scopes | | `groups` | an array of groups | | any other scalar | available by name through `caller.claim` | A token whose subject is exactly `anonymous` is refused, because that name is reserved for unauthenticated callers. ## 2. Show who is calling A verb can read the caller through the `caller` global. Save this as `whoami.js`: 
```js
function invoke(input) {
  return JSON.stringify({
    subject: caller.subject(),
    anon: caller.isAnonymous(),
    roles: caller.roles(),
    scopes: caller.scopes(),
    groups: caller.groups(),
    dept: caller.claim("dept"),
  });
}
```
 Create the object it lives on, then deploy the verb as Alice: 
```sh
curl -X PUT -H "Authorization: Bearer $ALICE" -H 'Content-Type: application/json' -d '{}' \
  http://localhost:8080/v1/me


curl -gX POST -H "Authorization: Bearer $ALICE" -H 'Content-Type: text/javascript' \
  --data-binary @whoami.js \
  'http://localhost:8080/v1/modules?verb=me:whoami&kind=function&lang=text/javascript'
```
 
```sh
curl -H "Authorization: Bearer $ALICE" 'http://localhost:8080/v1/me:whoami'
```
 
```json
{"subject":"alice","anon":false,"roles":["editor"],"scopes":["notes:read","notes:write"],"groups":["eng"],"dept":"platform"}
```
 ## 3. See two users get different answers A new verb is private to its owner. Bob, Carol and anonymous callers are refused: 
```sh
curl -i -H "Authorization: Bearer $BOB" 'http://localhost:8080/v1/me:whoami'
```
 
```json
{"detail":"'bob' may not execute 'me:whoami'","restact_code":"forbidden","status":403,…}
```
 Anonymous callers get `401 authentication required` instead, since there is no identity to refuse. Share the verb with anyone who has the `viewer` role. Policies are lists of matchers; the common ones are `public`, `owner`, `sub:<id>`, `role:<r>`, `group:<g>`, `scope:<s>` and `claim:<k>=<v>`: 
```sh
curl -X PUT -H "Authorization: Bearer $ALICE" -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
  -d '{"execute":["owner","role:viewer"]}' \
  'http://localhost:8080/v1/me:whoami/policy'
```
 Bob has the `viewer` role in his token, so his request now succeeds and reports his own identity: 
```sh
curl -H "Authorization: Bearer $BOB" 'http://localhost:8080/v1/me:whoami'
```
 
```json
{"subject":"bob","anon":false,"roles":["viewer"],"scopes":[],"groups":[]}
```
 Carol has a token with no roles (mint one with `CAROL=$(cargo run -q -p restactd -- token mint --sub carol)`), so she still gets `403`. To open a verb to everyone, deploy it with `exec=public` or use `{"execute":"public"}`. Objects carry the same kind of policy. Alice creates a note only she can read: 
```sh
curl -X PUT -H "Authorization: Bearer $ALICE" -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
  -d '{"title":"private","$policy":{"read":["owner"],"write":["owner"],"delete":["owner"]}}' \
  http://localhost:8080/v1/notes/a1
```
 When Bob reads `/v1/notes/a1` he gets `404`, the same answer as for an object that does not exist. ## What just happened Every verb runs as the caller who invoked it. The host checks the verb’s `execute` policy against the caller, and the verb can then read or write only what that caller could. `caller` is read-only and grants nothing the caller does not already hold. [Identity and authority](/docs/concepts/identity-and-authority/) explains the model. ## Verify tokens from an identity provider For production, have restactd verify RS256 or ES256 tokens against your provider’s published keys. Set all three of the following. All three variables are required: setting only some of them (or any to an empty value) refuses boot and names what is missing. The JWKS URL must be `https://` (plain `http://` only to a loopback address). Confirm the boot line `auth: OIDC/JWKS verification enabled …`. | Variable | Meaning | | ----------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `RESTACT_OIDC_ISSUER` | The expected `iss` claim. | | `RESTACT_OIDC_JWKS_URL` | The provider’s JWKS URL. It must be `https://`, except for a loopback address in development. Redirects are not followed. | | `RESTACT_OIDC_AUDIENCE` | The expected `aud` claim. Audience checking cannot be turned off. | Optional settings: `RESTACT_OIDC_JWT_HEADER` (the header carrying the token, `Authorization` by default), `RESTACT_OIDC_SUBJECT_CLAIM` (default `sub`) and `RESTACT_OIDC_JWKS_TTL_SECS` (key cache lifetime, default 3600). The signing algorithm is taken from the key (RSA means RS256, EC P-256 means ES256), never from the token header, so `alg: none` and algorithm-swap tokens are rejected. `exp`, `nbf`, `iat`, `iss` and `aud` are all checked. The host fetches the key set over HTTPS, so allow outbound access to your provider on port 443. ### Try it without a provider This Node script plays the identity provider: it serves a key set on `127.0.0.1:9400` and prints a signed token for each name you give it. Save it as `fake-idp.js`: 
```js
const http = require('http');
const crypto = require('crypto');


const ISSUER = 'https://idp.example.test';
const AUDIENCE = 'restactd';
const { publicKey, privateKey } = crypto.generateKeyPairSync('rsa', { modulusLength: 2048 });
const jwk = { ...publicKey.export({ format: 'jwk' }), kid: 'dev-key-1', alg: 'RS256', use: 'sig' };


const b64 = (b) => Buffer.from(b).toString('base64url');
function token(sub, extra = {}) {
  const now = Math.floor(Date.now() / 1000);
  const header = b64(JSON.stringify({ alg: 'RS256', typ: 'JWT', kid: jwk.kid }));
  const claims = b64(JSON.stringify({ sub, iss: ISSUER, aud: AUDIENCE, iat: now, nbf: now, exp: now + 3600, ...extra }));
  const sig = crypto.sign('RSA-SHA256', Buffer.from(`${header}.${claims}`), privateKey);
  return `${header}.${claims}.${b64(sig)}`;
}


for (const name of process.argv.slice(2)) console.log(`${name}=${token(name, { roles: ['editor'] })}`);


http.createServer((req, res) => {
  if (req.url === '/jwks') {
    res.writeHead(200, { 'content-type': 'application/json' });
    res.end(JSON.stringify({ keys: [jwk] }));
  } else { res.writeHead(404); res.end(); }
}).listen(9400, '127.0.0.1');
```
 
```sh
node fake-idp.js alice bob        # prints alice=<token> and bob=<token>, then keeps serving


RESTACT_OIDC_ISSUER=https://idp.example.test \
RESTACT_OIDC_JWKS_URL=http://127.0.0.1:9400/jwks \
RESTACT_OIDC_AUDIENCE=restactd \
cargo run -p restactd
```
 
```text
auth: OIDC/JWKS verification enabled (issuer 'https://idp.example.test', aud 'restactd', header 'authorization', jwks 'http://127.0.0.1:9400/jwks')
```
 Tokens pass through the same checks as HS256 ones. A token with a damaged signature answers `401` with `OIDC token verification failed`. When OIDC and HS256 are both configured, an OIDC token is tried first, and a token that is not an OIDC token falls through to HS256 so service tokens keep working. Keep the two sets of subject names apart, because both produce plain subjects and the same name is the same person. ## Hand out a path-scoped sub-token A sub-token lets you give someone, or a browser page, read access to one part of the object tree without handing over your own credential. It is signed with the HS256 secret, so it exists only on a host that has `RESTACT_JWT_SECRET` set; an OIDC-only or trusted-header host has none. Discovery lists the feature as `path-scoped-tokens`, and `RESTACT_SUB_TOKENS=off` removes the route. Any authenticated caller can mint one, with their own token or one from `restactd token mint`. An anonymous caller gets `401`. A sub-token cannot mint another, and neither can a principal acting through a delegated grant or the actor of a live grant it has exchanged, even with its own bearer token (`403`). On a host without `RESTACT_JWT_SECRET`, the route is mounted but an authenticated OIDC or trusted-header caller gets `503`. The prefix has to be an object that exists, so create it first if it does not: 
```sh
curl -s -X PUT http://localhost:8080/v1/notes/shared \
  -H "Authorization: Bearer $ALICE" \
  -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
  -d '{"title": "shared notes"}'


curl -s -X POST http://localhost:8080/v1/tokens/sub \
  -H "Authorization: Bearer $ALICE" \
  -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
  -d '{"prefix": "notes/shared", "ttl_seconds": 600}'
```
 
```json
{"token": "eyJ…", "expires_at": 1793000000, "prefix": "notes/shared", "read_only": true}
```
 | Field | Meaning | | ------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `prefix` | Required, a non-empty object path. The token covers that object and everything below it, matched on whole path segments: `notes/shared` covers `notes/shared/a` but not `notes/shared-old`. | | `ttl_seconds` | Optional. Defaults to 300 and is clamped to between 1 and 900. | You can mint a token only for a prefix that exists and that you can read yourself. A prefix that is absent and one you cannot read both answer `403`. A caller may make 30 mint requests a minute, counted in a fixed 60-second window per host process. A request counts once it passes body and prefix validation, so the read-check `403` and the no-secret `503` count, while a `401`, `400` or delegation `403` does not; past that the host answers `429` with `Retry-After: 60`. The token does one thing: a `GET` or `HEAD` of the stored value of an object under the prefix. It cannot invoke a verb, read `?meta=` or a verb’s management pages, subscribe to events, list collections, write, deploy, or reach any route an embedding application adds. Outside the prefix the answer is `403`. 
```sh
SUB=eyJ…   # the "token" value from the mint response
curl -s -H "Authorization: Bearer $SUB" http://localhost:8080/v1/notes/shared/todo   # 200 if that object exists
curl -s -H "Authorization: Bearer $SUB" http://localhost:8080/v1/notes/private/diary # 403
```
 A page running in a sandboxed frame sends `Origin: null`. When `RESTACT_CORS_ALLOW_ORIGIN` is set, the host accepts that origin for a valid sub-token on a plain `GET` or `HEAD`, so such a page can read the object; with CORS off, browsers block the read. The route is listed in the [HTTP API reference](/docs/reference/http-api/#schedules-subscriptions-and-tokens). ## Run behind an authenticating proxy If a proxy such as oauth2-proxy, Authelia or a cloud access gateway has already authenticated the user, restactd can take the identity from a header: 
```sh
RESTACT_TRUSTED_IDENTITY_HEADER=X-Forwarded-User \
RESTACT_TRUSTED_ROLES_HEADER=X-Forwarded-Roles \
RESTACT_TRUSTED_GROUPS_HEADER=X-Forwarded-Groups \
cargo run -p restactd
```
 `RESTACT_TRUSTED_SCOPES_HEADER` works the same way for scopes. The headers carry the subject and lists: roles and groups may be separated by commas or spaces, scopes by spaces only: 
```sh
curl -H 'X-Forwarded-User: alice' -H 'X-Forwarded-Roles: viewer' \
     -H 'X-Forwarded-Groups: eng,oncall' 'http://localhost:8080/v1/me:whoami'
```
 
```json
{"subject":"alice","anon":false,"roles":["viewer"],"scopes":[],"groups":["eng","oncall"]}
```
 Caution restactd does not check a signature on these headers. Any client that can reach restactd directly can claim any identity by sending the header. Use this mode only when the proxy is the sole route to restactd, and the proxy removes any copy of the header the client sends. Keep restactd bound to a private address or network so nothing bypasses the proxy. Prefer OIDC when your provider supports it. At startup the host logs which header it trusts, so the mode is never silent. ## Keep a subject namespace for your own services If you mint HS256 tokens for your own services under a naming scheme such as `agent:<workspace>:<name>`, reserve the prefix so an identity provider or proxy can never produce it: 
```sh
RESTACT_RESERVED_SUBJECT_PREFIXES='agent:' restactd
```
 A subject from OIDC or the trusted identity header that starts with a reserved prefix gets `401`; your HS256 tokens are unaffected. The same two sources also refuse subjects containing `:`, whitespace or an invisible format character. If your provider issues colon subjects on purpose (GitHub Actions, Kubernetes service accounts, SPIFFE), set `RESTACT_EXTERNAL_SUBJECT_ALLOW=colon`; the reserved prefixes still hold. Control characters in a subject are refused on every path. ## Check your setup * A request without a token can read but not write. Verify with the `PUT` shown in step 1. * `RESTACT_BIND` defaults to loopback. Set `0.0.0.0:8080` only when something in front of restactd enforces authentication, or when `RESTACT_JWT_SECRET` or OIDC is set. A non-loopback bind also needs `RESTACT_ALLOWED_HOSTS` (the host names clients use), or the host refuses to start. * Store `RESTACT_JWT_SECRET` in your secret manager and rotate it like any signing key. Whoever holds it can mint a token for any subject. ## Next steps * [Identity and authority](/docs/concepts/identity-and-authority/) explains runs-as-caller and ownership. * [Production checklist](/docs/operate/production/) lists the settings to review before you expose a host. * [Configuration](/docs/reference/configuration/#authentication--authorization) has the full variable table.

# Authoring verbs

> A verb is a sandboxed unit of functionality you upload and invoke over HTTP. Physically it is a wasm32-wasip2 WebAssembly component implementing the rest-act:plugin@0.1.0 contract. You write it…

A verb is a sandboxed unit of functionality you upload and invoke over HTTP. Physically it is a `wasm32-wasip2` WebAssembly component implementing the [`rest-act:plugin@0.1.0`](https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/blob/main/wit/world.wit) contract. You write it directly in Rust, or as a JavaScript or Python file that the host interprets through a shared runner component, with no toolchain ([JavaScript](#javascript-server-side-no-toolchain), [Python](#python-server-side-no-toolchain)). This page is the reference for all three. For a task-shaped walkthrough, start with [Write verbs in JavaScript](/docs/guides/javascript/). ## Concepts you need first * A *function* is pure and side-effect-free. You invoke it with `GET`, and its responses are safe to cache. An *action* has side effects and you invoke it with `POST`. You declare the kind at deploy time (`?kind=function`; leaving it out makes the verb an action), and the host enforces the HTTP method rule. * A verb targets exactly one WIT world, and that world is the complete list of host capabilities it can reach. Everything else is denied. * A verb runs with the caller’s authority, intersected with what it declared and what the target’s policy allows. It can never do something the caller could not. See [The model in one page](/docs/concepts/overview/) for the full model. ## The worlds The world a verb is built against is its capability manifest. | World | Imports | Use it for | | --------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `plugin` | `host` (structured `log`) | The minimal verb. Most business logic. | | `plugin-compose` | `host` + gated `compose` | A verb that invokes other verbs. | | `plugin-identity` | `host` + ambient `identity` | A verb that reads its caller. | | `plugin-object` | `host` + gated `object` (+ ambient `identity`) | A verb that reads and writes its object’s data. | | `plugin-object-compose` | `host` + `object` + `compose` (+ ambient `identity`) | Data access and composition. | | `plugin-object-event` | `plugin-object` + gated `event` | A data verb that also publishes events. | | `plugin-object-compose-event` | `plugin-object-compose` + gated `event` | Data access, composition and events. | | `plugin-llm` | `plugin` + gated `llm` | A verb that makes bounded model completions through a configured connector. | | `plugin-object-llm` | `plugin-object` + gated `llm` | A data verb that also makes model completions. | | `plugin-object-compose-event-llm` | `plugin-object-compose-event` + gated `llm` | Every capability. The reference host binds this superset, so a verb built against any narrower world still links. | No world offers `wasi:sockets`, and `wasi:filesystem` is linked with no directories. A verb that does not import a capability cannot reach it. Targeting `plugin-compose` is itself the composition declaration: the host links `compose` only for verbs built against a world that imports it, and refuses to link it to a bare `plugin` verb. The `identity` capability is the exception. It is ambient like `log`: a verb imports it to read its caller, and the host links it with no grant or attenuation, because it conveys no authority the caller does not already hold. Inspect/validate the contract with: 
```sh
wasm-tools component wit wit/
```
 ## Authoring in Rust without the SDK Verbs are built with [`cargo-component`](https://github.com/bytecodealliance/cargo-component), which generates the `wit-bindgen` bindings. The two annotated examples are the canonical reference: * [`examples/word-count`](https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/tree/main/examples/word-count) is a pure function on the minimal `plugin` world (`GET /…:wordCount`, cacheable, only `host.log`). * [`examples/summarize`](https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/tree/main/examples/summarize) is a side-effecting action on `plugin-compose` that composes `word-count` (`POST /…:summarize`). ### 1. Crate setup A verb crate is a `cdylib` that picks its world in `Cargo.toml`: 
```toml
[dependencies]
wit-bindgen-rt = { version = "0.44", features = ["bitflags"] }


[lib]
crate-type = ["cdylib"]


[package.metadata.component.target]
path  = "../../wit"
world = "plugin"          # or "plugin-compose" to compose other verbs
```
 ### 2. Implement the `Guest` trait 
```rust
mod bindings;
use bindings::rest_act::plugin::host::{log, LogLevel};
use bindings::{Guest, InvocationError, Payload};


struct Component;


impl Guest for Component {
    fn invoke(input: Payload) -> Result<Payload, InvocationError> {
        let text = String::from_utf8(input).map_err(|_| InvocationError {
            code: "invalid-input".into(),
            message: "not UTF-8".into(),
        })?;
        log(LogLevel::Debug, "counting");
        Ok(text.split_whitespace().count().to_string().into_bytes())
    }
}


bindings::export!(Component with_types_in bindings);
```
 The contract is `invoke(payload) -> result<payload, invocation-error>`. The payload is opaque bytes (`list<u8>`); encoding is an HTTP-layer concern. An `invocation-error` carries a stable machine `code` and a human-readable `message`, which the host maps onto an HTTP status + RFC 9457 Problem Details response. The code is a lower-kebab-case token of at most 64 bytes; any other code is reported as `invalid-input`. ### 3. Build and deploy 
```sh
cargo component build --release
# component lands in target/wasm32-wasip1/release/<crate>.wasm


curl -X POST --data-binary @target/wasm32-wasip1/release/word_count.wasm \
  'localhost:8080/v1/modules?verb=docs:wordCount&kind=function'        # -> 201
```
 Or upload as a verb property after the route exists: 
```sh
curl -X PUT -H 'Content-Type: application/wasm' \
  --data-binary @path/to/verb.wasm  localhost:8080/v1/docs:wordCount/code   # -> 204
```
 See [HTTP API → Management plane](/docs/reference/http-api/#management-plane) for the full deploy surface (`code`/`policy`/`meta`, replace, delete). ## Acting on object data A verb that only transforms its input is a function. A verb that acts on server-side data, the object it is bound to (`this`), does more. Target the `plugin-object` world and import the gated `object` interface: 
```rust
use bindings::rest_act::plugin::object;


// inside invoke():
let doc = object::get("document");                 // Option<PropertyValue>: Text(s) for a string, Json(t) otherwise
object::set("views", &object::PropertyValue::Json((n + 1).to_string()))  // Result<_, error>: write this.views (a number)
    .map_err(|e| InvocationError { code: e.code, message: e.message })?;
```
 An object path a verb names is never percent-decoded, so `%2e%2e` there is an ordinary name, while a `.` or `..` segment is refused like an `@` segment. * `get(name)` returns the property’s value, or `None` if it is absent or the caller may not read it. A read-denied property is indistinguishable from an absent one. A declared `inherit` property the object does not store returns the nearest ancestor’s value that the caller could read on that ancestor directly, else the declared default. * `set(name, value)` writes it, or returns an error on a write-ACL denial (`forbidden`), when a write-once (`final`) property is already set (`property-final`), or `not-found` when the object exists but the caller may not read it (the answer `set` gets for an absent object). A creation the host refuses fails here, at the call. Writes to the bound object are held until the verb returns and dropped if it fails. `set`, `set-at` and `set-at-if` refuse a name starting with `$` or `@` with `forbidden`, and a path, property name or value containing U+0000 with `invalid-input` before anything is buffered. * `get-at(path, name)` reads a property of another object, such as an ancestor on the inheritance chain, gated by that object’s read ACL. * `set-at(path, name, value)` writes a property on another object. It is gated exactly as a data-plane `PUT /v1/<path>` is, by the runs-as-caller principal, so a verb can never write what its caller could not. Each `set-at` commits when it is called. * `remove(name)` removes a property of the bound object and `remove-at(path, name)` removes one from another object (feature `object-remove`). Each is authorized exactly as the matching `set`, so a verb cannot remove what its caller could not write. Removing a property that is not set succeeds. A `final` property that is set returns `property-final`, and `remove-at` returns `not-found` when no object is stored at `path`. `remove` gives `not-found` when the bound object holds no data. `remove` is held until the verb returns, and `remove-at` commits when it is called (buffered under `atomic-invocation`). * `shape()` and `shape-at(path)` return an object’s shape, `{ properties, children, verbs }`: the readable property names, the readable direct child paths and the verb names the caller’s discovery listing would show. `shape()` reflects committed state, so a `set` earlier in the same invocation is not visible yet. * `get-bytes()` and `get-bytes-at(path)` read an object’s binary value: the `(content-type, bytes)` of its content-addressed blob, or `None`, with the same read gate as `get`. The host advertises them as the `object-binary` feature, and the SDK exposes `object::get_bytes()`. Because a verb’s output is already binary, this suits read-time media transforms: an `avatar:get` accessor reads the stored image, transforms it and returns the new bytes. Compiled verbs and both script tiers can read binary values; a script read is capped at a quarter of the script heap (6 MiB). Access is ACL- and scope-aware (see [data properties](/docs/reference/http-api/)). A property may be `instance`-scoped (per concrete object, the default) or `shared` (one value on the definition ancestor), `final` (write-once), and gated by per-property read and write ACLs. Everything is evaluated against the runs-as-caller principal, so a verb can never read or write what the caller could not. Capability access is gated and attenuated like composition: a verb that does not import `object` cannot reach any object data. The [`render-html`](https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/tree/main/examples/render-html) example is the reference consumer: it reads `this.document` (Markdown) and returns HTML. A verb reads and writes data through the capability. The data is stored through the [data plane](/docs/reference/http-api/#data-plane-the-noun) (`PUT /v1/<obj>`) or by another verb’s `set`. ## Reading the caller (identity) Any verb can read who invoked it through the `identity` interface. It exposes, read-only, the same runs-as-caller principal the host uses for authorization. Target the `plugin-identity` world (the object worlds import it too) and use: 
```rust
use bindings::rest_act::plugin::identity;


// inside invoke():
let who   = identity::subject().unwrap_or_else(|| "anonymous".into());  // Option<String>
let signed_in = !identity::is_anonymous();
let roles = identity::roles();                                          // Vec<String>
let dept  = identity::claim("dept");                                    // Option<String>
```
 Unlike `object` and `compose`, `identity` is ambient: it has no deploy-time grant, attenuation or ACL. It conveys no authority the verb does not already hold, because the verb runs as this caller, and the sandbox has no network to leak it through. Use it to audit, personalize or key per-user data, for example `object::set("lastEditor", &object::PropertyValue::Text(who.clone()))`. Anonymous callers have no subject and empty roles, scopes and claims. ## Composition To call another verb, target `plugin-compose` (or `plugin-object-compose`) and declare every edge at deploy time: 
```rust
use bindings::rest_act::plugin::compose::invoke as call_verb;


// inside your invoke():
let counted = call_verb("docs:wordCount", text.as_bytes())
    .map_err(|e| InvocationError { code: e.code, message: e.message })?;
```
 
```sh
# declare the call graph with ?calls=
curl -X POST --data-binary @summarize.wasm \
  'localhost:8080/v1/modules?verb=docs:summarize&calls=docs:wordCount'
```
 The host enforces the declared graph (undeclared edge → `403`), a shared per-request budget, depth/fan-out limits, cycle detection (`508`), and capability attenuation. Every edge must be declared. A function may declare calls only to other functions; an action may call either kind. See [Architecture → Composition](/docs/architecture/overview/#composition). The verb you call may be native: a privileged primitive the platform exposes (an indexed cross-user query, a media transform) that your uploaded verb composes but cannot bypass. This is how an uploaded feed-ranking or search verb reaches ordered queries: it calls a platform verb like `feed:candidates` (run as you, so it sees only what you could). It is indistinguishable from calling any other verb. If you are building the platform, registering those primitives and embedding the host, see [Embed restactd in a Rust app](/docs/guides/embedding/). ## JavaScript (server-side, no toolchain) You can author a verb in plain JavaScript and upload it as source, with no `cargo-component` and no build step. The host does not compile it. It stores the script and interprets it at invoke time through one trusted, shared QuickJS runner component it ships. The script runs inside the same wasmtime sandbox under the same fuel/epoch/memory limits as any verb, and reaches the same host capabilities, so a JS verb has the same behaviors (path inheritance, object data, composition, ACLs, runs-as-caller) as a wasm one, gated identically. Write a single file defining `function invoke(input)`: 
```js
function invoke(input) {
  const n = Number(obj.get("views") ?? "0") + 1;   // read this.views
  obj.set("views", String(n));                       // write it back
  log(`viewed ${n} times`);                          // host.log
  return `views: ${n}`;                              // string body; an object/array is JSON-stringified
}
```
 The host capabilities are available as globals (each bridges to the matching WIT import; per-verb authority is still gated by `allowed_calls` + the property ACLs + grants + policy): | Global | Maps to | Notes | | ------------------------------------------------------ | ---------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `log(msg)`, `console.log(msg)` | `host.log` | structured logging | | `compose(verb, input?)` | `compose.invoke` | returns the callee’s output string; throws on a compose error | | `obj.get(name)` | `object.get` | reads `this`’s property (or `undefined` if absent / read-denied) | | `obj.set(name, value)` | `object.set` | writes it; throws at the call for a write-ACL denial or a `final` property, and for a refused creation (the anchor’s write policy, the governed-creation gate, a secrets-mount or connector path); on an existing object the caller may not read, it throws what the same `set` throws on an absent object: the creation refusal, else `not-found` | | `obj.getAt(path, name)` | `object.get-at` | reads another object’s property (ACL-gated) | | `obj.setAt(path, name, value)` | `object.set-at` | writes another object’s property (gated as the caller) | | `obj.remove(name)` / `obj.removeAt(path, name)` | `object.remove`/`remove-at` | removes a property of `this` / another object (authorized as the matching write); throws `property-final` for a set `final` property, and `removeAt` throws `not-found` when no object is stored at `path` | | `obj.setAtIf(path, name, value, expected)` | `object.set-at-if` | writes only if the property still equals `expected` (`null` means absent); throws `precondition-failed` otherwise | | `obj.shape()` / `obj.shapeAt(path)` | `object.shape`/`shape-at` | `{properties, children, verbs}` of `this` / another object | | `caller.subject()` | `identity.subject` | the caller’s id, or `undefined` when anonymous | | `caller.isAnonymous()` | `identity.is-anonymous` | `true` when unauthenticated | | `caller.roles()`, `caller.scopes()`, `caller.groups()` | `identity.roles`/`scopes`/`groups` | string arrays (may be empty) | | `caller.claim(name)` | `identity.claim` | a single scalar token claim, or `undefined` | | `caller.invoker()` | `identity.invoker` | the principal on whose behalf the whole invocation tree runs; not ambient: `undefined` unless the verb was deployed with `?invoker=1` | | `publish(topic, name, data?)` | `event.publish` | publishes an event; gated by the event grant, throws when denied | | `obj.query(options)` | `object.query` | a page of objects under a path prefix, ACL-filtered (feature `verb-object-query`); Python: `obj.query(prefix=..., order_by=..., limit=..., cursor=...)` returning a page with `.objects` and `.cursor` | | `obj.path()` | `object.path` | the concrete path of the object the verb is bound to, or `null` when unbound | | `caller.actor()` | `identity.actor` | feature `identity-actor`, ambient: the delegation actor’s id when a delegation bounds this frame, else `undefined` | Deploy the source the same way as a component. On `POST /v1/modules` the `lang` query parameter declares the language (the host reads it only from `?lang=`, not from `Content-Type`). On `PUT …/code` the `Content-Type` selects it: 
```sh
# As a new route (POST /v1/modules), declaring the source language:
curl -gX POST -H 'Content-Type: text/javascript' --data-binary @view.js \
  'localhost:8080/v1/modules?verb=profiles/{id}:view&kind=action&lang=text/javascript'   # -> 201


# Or as a verb's code property after the route exists:
curl -gX PUT -H 'Content-Type: text/javascript' --data-binary @view.js \
  'localhost:8080/v1/profiles/{id}:view/code'                                            # -> 204
```
 `GET …:verb/code` with `Accept: text/javascript` returns the stored source (the source representation of the verb; there is no compiled twin, since the executable is the shared runner). The JavaScript tier is optional: it is present only when the host was started with the runner (`scripts/build-js-runner.sh`; `RESTACT_JS_RUNNER_WASM`). When it’s absent, discovery omits `text/javascript` and an upload with `?lang=text/javascript` returns `415`. Check `acceptedSourceLanguages` in the [discovery document](/docs/reference/http-api/) (`/.well-known/restact`). A source upload is syntax-checked at deploy by a compile-only parse; a script that does not parse is rejected up front with `422` (`restact_code: invalid-source`), carrying the engine’s message, rather than a `201` that only fails at first invoke. Runtime errors (reference errors, thrown values) still surface at invoke as `script-error`. ### Distinguishing host errors (both scripting tiers) A host-capability error, such as a write-ACL denial, a `final` property or a failed compare-and-swap, is thrown as an ordinary catchable exception (`Error` in JS, `RuntimeError` in Python). It has no `.code` property. The structured code travels inside the message, delimited by SOH (`U+0001`), written `\u0001` below: 
```text
\u0001restact-host-error\u0001<code>\u0001<message>
```
 The obvious-looking test never matches: 
```js
catch (e) { if (e.code !== "precondition-failed") throw e; }   // WRONG: e.code is always undefined,
                                                               // so this always rethrows and a retry
                                                               // loop never retries even once.
```
 Parse the message instead: 
```js
function hostCode(e) {                       // -> "precondition-failed" | "forbidden" | … | null
  const m = String((e && e.message) || "");
  return m.startsWith("\u0001restact-host-error\u0001") ? m.split("\u0001")[2] : null;
}
```
 
```python
def host_code(e):                            # -> str | None
    m = str(e)
    return m.split("\u0001")[2] if m.startswith("\u0001restact-host-error\u0001") else None
```
 The runners read the tag from the message of the exception that finally propagates, whatever built it ([js-runner](https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/blob/main/examples/js-runner/src/lib.rs) and [py-runner](https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/blob/main/examples/py-runner/src/lib.rs) mirror each other). A script can therefore return any code, as a Rust verb can: rethrowing the original exception, or an `Error` built from its `message`, keeps the code and the status that goes with it, and a message that carries a tag you wrote yourself is reported under that code, provided the code is a lower-kebab-case token of at most 64 bytes. Any other code, such as `Not_Found` or one longer than 64 bytes, is reported as `invalid-input` with its message kept. The code ends at the first `\u0001` after it, and anything past that is part of the message. An exception without a tag is reported as `script-error`. Three codes are asserted by the host: * `delegation-bound` and `verb-unlinkable` reach the request’s caller only when the host recorded the refusal in the same request. A composing verb can still receive either from a callee, so treat them from a composed call as a refusal like any other code. * `verb-not-deployed` means that no verb is deployed at the route the caller named. A verb that ran cannot produce it: the host answers that verb’s caller `not-found` and keeps the message, at every level of a composition. When a verb composes another only if one is deployed (a veto, an extension point), catch the call and treat `verb-not-deployed`, and only that code, as absence. Any other failure, `not-found` included, can come from a deployed verb that refused, so it is a refusal: 
```js
try {
  compose(String(id) + ":accept", proposal);   // a veto that returns normally accepts
} catch (e) {
  if (hostCode(e) !== "verb-not-deployed") throw e;   // any other failure refuses the change
}
```
 A target built from input should be validated first. A well-formed route is answered truthfully as absent, and a target with a `.`, `..`, `@` or empty path segment is answered `not-found`, which refuses. Rust verbs are unaffected. In Rust the error crossing the WIT boundary is a structured `invocation-error` record, so `e.code` is a real field and the [object-data examples above](#acting-on-object-data) (`InvocationError { code: e.code, … }`) are correct as written. The scripting tiers cannot carry a second field across a thrown exception, which is why the code is folded into the message there. ### Caveats and operational notes The JavaScript tier is interpreted, and a few properties follow from that design: * Text in, text or bytes out. A script’s `invoke(input)` receives the request input decoded as UTF-8 (invalid bytes are replaced lossily, so binary input is not preserved). Its return value becomes the body: a `Uint8Array` or `ArrayBuffer` is sent as raw bytes, a string passes through, an object/array is JSON-stringified, and `null`/`undefined` is an empty body. Compose results are decoded as UTF-8. To accept binary input, author the verb in Rust. * A thrown error’s message reaches the caller. An uncaught exception surfaces as a `script-error` whose message text (including any QuickJS source location) is returned in the RFC 9457 `detail`. Don’t put secrets in error messages, and treat a script’s diagnostics as caller-visible. * The source is parsed on every invoke. There is no persistent bytecode cache; each invocation re-evaluates the stored script. Keep hot scripts small. * The runner is trusted (TCB). Every JS verb shares the one runner component, so a script’s capability surface is enforced by the host’s runtime gates (`allowed_calls`, property ACLs, grants, policy), not by a per-verb WIT world. The runner is built from a network-fetched WASI SDK; its content hash is reproducible only when the build is pinned (the runner’s `Cargo.lock` is committed and `rquickjs` is pinned to an exact version). ## Python (server-side, no toolchain) You can author a verb in plain Python and upload it as source, with no `cargo-component` and no build step. The host does not compile it. It stores the script and interprets it at invoke time through one trusted, shared monty runner component it ships. The script runs inside the same wasmtime sandbox under the same fuel/epoch/memory limits as any verb, and reaches the same host capabilities, so a Python verb has the same behaviors (path inheritance, object data, composition, ACLs, runs-as-caller) as a wasm one, gated identically. Write a single file defining `def invoke(input)`: 
```python
def invoke(input):
    n = int(obj.get("count") or 0) + 1   # read this.count
    obj.set("count", str(n))             # write it back
    return {"count": n}                  # dict body; JSON-serialized
```
 The host capabilities are available (each bridges to the matching WIT import; per-verb authority is still gated by `allowed_calls` + the property ACLs + grants + policy): | Capability | Maps to | Notes | | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `log(msg)` | `host.log` | structured logging | | `compose(verb, input)` | `compose.invoke` | returns the callee’s output string; raises on a compose error | | `obj.get(name)` | `object.get` | reads `this`’s property (or `None` if absent / read-denied) | | `obj.set(name, value)` | `object.set` | writes it; raises at the call for a write-ACL denial or a `final` property, and for a refused creation (the anchor’s write policy, the governed-creation gate, a secrets-mount or connector path); on an existing object the caller may not read, it throws what the same `set` throws on an absent object: the creation refusal, else `not-found` | | `obj.get_at(path, name)` | `object.get-at` | reads another object’s property (ACL-gated); note the snake_case name (vs JS `getAt`) | | `obj.set_at(path, name, value)` | `object.set-at` | writes another object’s property (gated as the caller) | | `obj.remove(name)` / `obj.remove_at(path, name)` | `object.remove`/`remove-at` | removes a property of `this` / another object (authorized as the matching write); raises `property-final` for a set `final` property, and `remove_at` raises `not-found` when no object is stored at `path` | | `obj.set_at_if(path, name, value, expected=None)` | `object.set-at-if` | compare-and-swap write; raises with `precondition-failed` when the current value is not `expected` (`None` means absent) | | `obj.path()` | `object.path` | the concrete path of the bound object, or `None` | | `obj.query(prefix=None, order_by=None, descending=False, predicate=None, limit=None, cursor=None)` | `object.query` | one ACL-filtered page: `.objects` (rows with `.path`/`.properties`/`.order_value`) and `.cursor` (`None`, or pass it back as `cursor=`) | | `obj.shape()` / `obj.shape_at(path)` | `object.shape`/`shape-at` | a dataclass with `.properties`/`.children`/`.verbs` | | `caller.subject()` | `identity.subject` | the caller’s id, or `None` when anonymous | | `caller.is_anonymous()` | `identity.is-anonymous` | `True` when unauthenticated | | `caller.roles()`, `caller.scopes()`, `caller.groups()` | `identity.roles`/`scopes`/`groups` | lists of strings (may be empty) | | `publish(topic, name, data)` | `event.publish` | publishes an event; gated by the event grant, raises when denied | | `caller.claim(name)` | `identity.claim` | a single scalar token claim, or `None` | | `caller.actor()` | `identity.actor` | the delegation actor’s id when a delegation bounds this frame (not necessarily a subject), else `None` | | `caller.invoker()` | `identity.invoker` | the principal on whose behalf the whole invocation tree runs; not ambient: `None` unless the verb was deployed with `?invoker=1` | The `obj` and `caller` methods, `compose`, `publish` and `llm.complete` check their arguments before they call the host. A missing argument, a value that is not a string where one is required (`None` included), an extra positional argument or an unknown keyword raises `invalid-input`. Each argument may also be passed by its WIT name (`name`, `path`, `value`, `verb`, `input`, `topic`, `data`; `req` for `llm.complete`). The last argument of `compose` (`input`) and of `publish` (`data`) may be left out, and then reads as empty. If it is given it must be a string, and `None` is not a string: unlike `set_at_if` and `query`, which read `None` as omitted, `compose("v", None)` raises `invalid-input`. A method belongs to its own object, so `caller.get(...)` or `obj.subject()` raises `AttributeError`. The JavaScript tier also throws on a missing or non-string argument. Deploy the source the same way as a component. On `POST /v1/modules` the `lang` query parameter declares the language (the host reads it only from `?lang=`, not from `Content-Type`). On `PUT …/code` the `Content-Type` selects it: 
```sh
# As a new route (POST /v1/modules), declaring the source language:
curl -gX POST -H 'Content-Type: text/x-python' --data-binary @view.py \
  'localhost:8080/v1/modules?verb=profiles/{id}:view&kind=action&lang=text/x-python'   # -> 201


# Or replace an existing verb's code in place (here the `Content-Type` selects the language):
curl -gX PUT -H 'Content-Type: text/x-python' --data-binary @view.py \
  'localhost:8080/v1/profiles/{id}:view/code'                                          # -> 204
```
 `GET …:verb/code` with `Accept: text/x-python` returns the stored source (the source representation of the verb; there is no compiled twin, since the executable is the shared runner). The Python tier is optional: it is present only when the host was started with the runner (`scripts/build-py-runner.sh`; `RESTACT_PY_RUNNER_WASM`). When it’s absent, discovery omits `text/x-python` and an upload with `?lang=text/x-python` returns `415`. Check `acceptedSourceLanguages` in the [discovery document](/docs/reference/http-api/) (`/.well-known/restact`). A source upload is syntax-checked at deploy by a parse-only validation (like the JS tier, not type-checking); a script that does not parse is rejected up front with `422` (`restact_code: invalid-source`), carrying the engine’s message, rather than a `201` that only fails at first invoke. Runtime errors still surface at invoke as `script-error`. ### Caveats and operational notes The Python tier is interpreted, so the same properties as the JS tier apply: * Text in, text or bytes out. A script’s `invoke(input)` receives the request input as a string, and its return value becomes the body: `bytes` are sent unchanged, a `str` passes through, a `dict`/`list`/number is JSON-serialized, and `None` is an empty body. To accept binary input, author the verb in Rust. * A raised error’s message reaches the caller. An uncaught exception surfaces as a `script-error` whose message text is returned in the RFC 9457 `detail`. Don’t put secrets in error messages, and treat a script’s diagnostics as caller-visible. * The source is parsed on every invoke. There is no persistent cache; each invocation re-evaluates the stored script. Keep hot scripts small. * The runner is trusted (TCB). Every Python verb shares the one monty runner component, so a script’s capability surface is enforced by the host’s runtime gates (`allowed_calls`, property ACLs, grants, policy), not by a per-verb WIT world. ## Language-agnostic uploads WASM is the substrate, but the upload declares the source language (`?lang=` on `POST /v1/modules`, `Content-Type` on a `PUT …/code`) and the host decides how to store and run it: either compiling the source to a component, or storing it and interpreting it through a host-provided runtime component: * `application/wasm` runs directly. * `text/javascript` is stored as source and interpreted by the embedded QuickJS runner ([above](#javascript-server-side-no-toolchain)); no per-upload compilation. * `text/x-python` is stored as source and interpreted by the embedded monty runner ([above](#python-server-side-no-toolchain)); no per-upload compilation. * Other source types (for example `text/x-rust`) would be compiled or transpiled to a component *if* the host has a sandboxed toolchain. The reference host has none, so these return `415`. The host stores the original source (and, for the compile path, the compiled WASM, content-addressed); `GET …:verb/code` honors `Accept` to return the source. Discovery advertises which source languages a host accepts; an unsupported one returns `415`. ## Deployment gotchas * With authentication on, object binding is `strict`: the parent object must exist before you deploy a `{id}` verb under it, or the deploy answers `409`. * A new verb is owner-only. Deploy with `exec=public` (or share it with `PUT …/policy`) for anyone else to invoke it. * Verbs named `list`, `get`, `set`, `create`, `update` and `delete` are accessors: they take over the matching data-plane requests on their object instead of being ordinary verbs. * Delete a `{id}` verb with `DELETE /v1/<pattern-with-{id}>:verb/code`, using `curl -g` so the braces survive. ## Describing a verb (OpenAPI) To document a verb in an OpenAPI 3.1 spec, use the [`x-restact`](https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/tree/main/openapi) extension: declare `kind`, `binding`, `capabilities`, `callGraph`, `consistency`, and async support, aligned field-for-field with the WIT contract. The schema is strict and enforces spec rules as cross-field constraints (a function can’t invoke an action, composition requires `plugin-compose`, accessor names are the reserved data-method names, etc.). Validate with: 
```sh
python3 openapi/scripts/validate.py
```
 ## SDK status * Rust: the [`restact-sdk`](https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/tree/main/sdk/rust) crate gives you a `Verb` trait, `log`/`compose` helpers and an `export_verb!` macro, so a verb builds with plain `cargo build --target wasm32-wasip2`. The examples and the smoke suite exercise it end to end. * JavaScript and Python: host-interpreted tiers that need no toolchain, run by the shared QuickJS and monty runners; see the sections above. * Other languages (TinyGo, C/C++ with the WASI SDK): any toolchain that produces a `rest-act:plugin@0.1.0` component can upload `application/wasm`. See [Write verbs in Rust](/docs/guides/rust/) for the SDK.

# Compose verbs

> One verb can call another and use its result. The host runs the whole call tree under one shared budget, with the original caller's authority at every step, and turns a failure anywhere in the tree…

One verb can call another and use its result. The host runs the whole call tree under one shared budget, with the original caller’s authority at every step, and turns a failure anywhere in the tree into a defined response. This guide builds a small chain, then shows how the budget, the caller’s identity and failures travel through it. ## Prerequisites * A running restactd on `localhost:8080` with the JavaScript tier. The same calls work from Python and Rust verbs. * For the identity step, a host with HS256 authentication configured. [Add authentication](/docs/guides/authentication/) shows how to set it up and mint tokens. ## Call a verb from a verb 1. Deploy the callee, a function that upper-cases its input. 
   ```sh
   curl -gX POST -H 'Content-Type: text/javascript' \
     --data-binary 'function invoke(i){ return i.toUpperCase(); }' \
     'localhost:8080/v1/modules?verb=util:shout&kind=function&lang=text/javascript'
   ```
 2. Deploy the caller. It calls `compose(verb, input)`, and `calls=` declares the edge. 
   ```sh
   curl -gX POST -H 'Content-Type: text/javascript' \
     --data-binary 'function invoke(i){ return compose("util:shout", "hello " + i); }' \
     'localhost:8080/v1/modules?verb=util:greetLoud&kind=function&lang=text/javascript&calls=util:shout'
   ```
 3. Call the outer verb. 
   ```sh
   curl -g 'localhost:8080/v1/util:greetLoud?input=ada'
   ```
 
   ```text
   HELLO ADA
   ```
 What just happened: `compose` sent `"hello ada"` to `util:shout` and returned its output as a string. The caller does not need to know which language the callee is written in. A Python verb can call that same `util:shout`: 
```python
def invoke(input):
    shouted = compose("util:shout", "hello " + input)
    return shouted + "!"
```
 
```sh
curl -gX POST -H 'Content-Type: text/x-python' --data-binary @loud.py \
  'localhost:8080/v1/modules?verb=py:loud&kind=action&lang=text/x-python&calls=util:shout'
curl -gX POST --data-binary ada localhost:8080/v1/py:loud       # HELLO ADA!
```
 In a Rust verb built with the SDK’s `compose` feature, the call is `restact_sdk::compose("util:shout", &input)?`; see [Write verbs in Rust](/docs/guides/rust/#compose-another-verb). ## Declare every call The call graph is part of the deployment.
