A verb that calls something it did not declare is refused when the call happens: 
```sh
curl -gX POST -H 'Content-Type: text/javascript' \
  --data-binary 'function invoke(i){ return compose("util:shout", i); }' \
  'localhost:8080/v1/modules?verb=util:sneaky&kind=function&lang=text/javascript'
curl -g 'localhost:8080/v1/util:sneaky?input=ada'
```
 
```json
{"detail":"verb 'util:sneaky' is not declared to call 'util:shout'","restact_code":"not-declared","restact_verb":":sneaky","status":403,"title":"Forbidden","type":"about:blank"}
```
 Functions and actions follow different rules. A function has no side effects, so a function may only declare calls to other functions. Declaring a call from a function to an action fails at deploy time, once the action exists: 
```sh
curl -gX POST -H 'Content-Type: text/javascript' \
  --data-binary 'function invoke(i){ return "side effect"; }' \
  'localhost:8080/v1/modules?verb=eff:act&kind=action&lang=text/javascript'
curl -gX POST -H 'Content-Type: text/javascript' \
  --data-binary 'function invoke(i){ return compose("eff:act", ""); }' \
  'localhost:8080/v1/modules?verb=eff:fn&kind=function&lang=text/javascript&calls=eff:act'
```
 
```json
{"detail":"a function may not declare a call to the action 'eff:act'","restact_code":"invalid-call-graph","restact_verb":":fn","status":400,"title":"Bad Request","type":"about:blank"}
```
 An action may declare calls to both kinds. This is what keeps `GET` safe: a function’s whole tree is free of side effects. ## One budget for the whole tree Every verb in a call tree draws from the same pool of compute, so a request cannot get more compute by splitting work across verbs. Wall-clock time and memory are different: each verb instance has its own time limit and memory cap, and `RESTACT_REQUEST_TIMEOUT_MS` (60 seconds by default) bounds the request as a whole. The defaults are in the discovery document under `defaultCapabilities`: 200 million compute units, 2 seconds and 32 MiB. This pair makes the point. `bud:burn` loops `input` times, and `bud:tree` calls it four times: 
```sh
curl -gX POST -H 'Content-Type: text/javascript' \
  --data-binary 'function invoke(i){ let s=0; for(let k=0;k<Number(i);k++){ s+=k } return s; }' \
  'localhost:8080/v1/modules?verb=bud:burn&kind=function&lang=text/javascript'
curl -gX POST -H 'Content-Type: text/javascript' \
  --data-binary 'function invoke(i){ let t=0; for(let k=0;k<4;k++){ t += Number(compose("bud:burn", i)); } return t; }' \
  'localhost:8080/v1/modules?verb=bud:tree&kind=function&lang=text/javascript&calls=bud:burn'


curl -g 'localhost:8080/v1/bud:burn?input=20000'      # 199990000
curl -g 'localhost:8080/v1/bud:tree?input=20000'      # 429
```
 
```json
{"detail":"CPU budget exhausted","restact_code":"cpu-budget","restact_verb":":tree","status":429,"title":"Too Many Requests","type":"about:blank"}
```
 One `burn` call at 20000 fits in the budget. Four of them in one tree do not. The exact threshold depends on the script and the runner. Two structural limits apply too: a call chain may be at most 8 verbs deep (the entry verb counts), and one verb may make at most 16 `compose` calls per invocation. Past either limit the host answers `508`. 
```sh
curl -gX POST -H 'Content-Type: text/javascript' --data-binary 'function invoke(i){ return "x"; }' \
  'localhost:8080/v1/modules?verb=fan:leaf&kind=function&lang=text/javascript'
curl -gX POST -H 'Content-Type: text/javascript' \
  --data-binary 'function invoke(i){ for(let k=0;k<Number(i);k++){ compose("fan:leaf",""); } return i; }' \
  'localhost:8080/v1/modules?verb=fan:many&kind=function&lang=text/javascript&calls=fan:leaf'
curl -g 'localhost:8080/v1/fan:many?input=16'      # 16
curl -g 'localhost:8080/v1/fan:many?input=17'
```
 
```json
{"detail":"max fan-out 16 exceeded","restact_code":"composition-cycle","restact_verb":":many","status":508,"title":"Loop Detected","type":"about:blank"}
```
 `fan:many` calls the leaf verb `input` times in a loop. A call that would close a cycle is refused with the same status, `508`, and a detail such as `cycle detected: 'loop:a' is already on the call stack`. ## The caller’s identity follows the chain Each verb in the tree runs as the original caller, not as the verb above it and not as the verb’s owner (unless the verb is deployed with `runs_as=owner`; see [Identity and authority](/docs/concepts/identity-and-authority/)). A callee cannot see or change anything the caller could not. An authenticated host uses strict binding, so create the parents first: 
```sh
curl -X PUT -H "Authorization: Bearer $ALICE" -H 'Content-Type: application/json' --data '{}' localhost:8080/v1/vault
curl -X PUT -H "Authorization: Bearer $ALICE" -H 'Content-Type: application/json' --data '{}' localhost:8080/v1/api
```
 Then store an object only its owner can read: 
```sh
curl -X PUT -H "Authorization: Bearer $ALICE" -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
  --data '{"body":"launch codes","$policy":{"read":["owner"]}}' localhost:8080/v1/vault/1
```
 Deploy a reader and two layers above it. `exec=public` lets any caller run them. Save the three functions below as `vault-read.js`, `via-read.js` and `outer.js`: 
```js
// vault:read
function invoke(i) {
  const v = obj.getAt("vault/1", "body");
  return { subject: caller.subject(), saw: v ?? null };
}
// api:viaRead (calls=vault:read)
function invoke(i) { return JSON.parse(compose("vault:read", "")); }
// api:outer (calls=api:viaRead)
function invoke(i) {
  return { outer: caller.subject(), inner: JSON.parse(compose("api:viaRead", "")) };
}
```
 Deploy them as Alice: 
```sh
deploy() { curl -gs -o /dev/null -w "%{http_code}\n" -X POST -H "Authorization: Bearer $ALICE" \
  -H 'Content-Type: text/javascript' --data-binary @"$1" \
  "localhost:8080/v1/modules?verb=$2&kind=function&lang=text/javascript&exec=public$3"; }
deploy vault-read.js vault:read
deploy via-read.js api:viaRead '&calls=vault:read'
deploy outer.js api:outer '&calls=api:viaRead'
```
 Call `api:outer` as the owner, as another user, and with no credentials: 
```sh
curl -g -H "Authorization: Bearer $ALICE" localhost:8080/v1/api:outer
curl -g -H "Authorization: Bearer $BOB"   localhost:8080/v1/api:outer
curl -g                                    localhost:8080/v1/api:outer
```
 
```json
{"outer":"alice","inner":{"subject":"alice","saw":"launch codes"}}
{"outer":"bob","inner":{"subject":"bob","saw":null}}
{"inner":{"saw":null}}
```
 The innermost verb reports the same subject as the outermost, and it reads the vault only for the person who could read it. The permission check happens at each hop, so composing verbs never widens what a caller can do. ## What happens when a callee fails A failure inside a callee comes back to the caller as an exception (`compose` throws in JavaScript, raises `RuntimeError` in Python, returns `Err` in Rust). You can handle it, or let it propagate. 
```js
// calc:div: [a, b] -> a / b
function invoke(i) {
  const [a, b] = JSON.parse(i);
  if (b === 0) throw new Error("divide by zero");
  return String(a / b);
}
// calc:safe (calls=calc:div): recover with a fallback
function invoke(i) {
  try { return compose("calc:div", i); }
  catch (e) { return "fallback: " + e.message.split("\u0001").pop(); }
}
// calc:strict (calls=calc:div): do not catch
function invoke(i) { return compose("calc:div", i); }
```
 
```sh
curl -g -G --data-urlencode 'input=[1,0]' localhost:8080/v1/calc:safe
curl -gi -G --data-urlencode 'input=[1,0]' localhost:8080/v1/calc:strict
```
 
```text
fallback: Error: divide by zero
    at invoke (eval_script:3:26)
```
 
```http
HTTP/1.1 422 Unprocessable Entity
{"detail":"Error: divide by zero\n    at invoke (eval_script:3:26)\n",...}
```
 When a callee’s error reaches the top uncaught, the caller of the whole chain gets that error’s status and `detail`, such as `403` for a refused write or `422` for a script error. If the callee’s failure is a host capability error, the message carries a code between SOH characters; the [JavaScript guide](/docs/guides/javascript/#handle-errors) shows how to read it. Do not put secrets in error messages: they travel back through every layer. Writes follow the consistency level of the host. With `RESTACT_CONSISTENCY=atomic-invocation`, a failure that reaches the top of the tree discards the writes of every verb in it, and a callee failure that its caller catches discards only that callee’s writes. See [Store and query object data](/docs/guides/object-data/#make-an-invocation-atomic). ## Next steps * [Capabilities and budgets](/docs/concepts/capabilities-and-budgets/): the model behind declared edges and shared budgets. * [Identity and authority](/docs/concepts/identity-and-authority/): `runs_as`, scopes and delegation. * [Lifecycle hooks](/docs/guides/lifecycle-hooks/): verbs that fire on object changes, and the composition bounds that apply to them. * [HTTP API](/docs/reference/http-api/): status codes and error bodies.

# Outbound calls and secrets

> restactd can make an outbound HTTP call on a caller's behalf and add a stored credential to it without the credential ever reaching the caller, a verb, a log line or a stored object. This guide…

restactd can make an outbound HTTP call on a caller’s behalf and add a stored credential to it without the credential ever reaching the caller, a verb, a log line or a stored object. This guide configures that mechanism, calls a local origin through it, and walks through the checks that keep a misconfigured host from starting. ## How outbound calls work A verb runs in a sandbox with no network access, so it cannot open a socket. Two host-mediated paths reach the outside world: * Mediated egress is a function of the Rust host API, `RouteContext::mediated_call`. An application that [embeds restactd](/docs/guides/embedding/) registers its own route and calls it. restactd adds no HTTP route for egress by itself, so there is nothing for an anonymous client to probe. * The `llm` capability lets a verb request one text completion from a model provider through a connector. It uses the same allowlist, secrets and scrubbing as mediated egress. It is a typed call, not general HTTP, and a verb that imports it cannot be deployed as a function. This guide covers the first path. The demo embedder route `POST /demo/egress` exists to exercise it over HTTP and is the worked example below. It is switched on with `RESTACT_DEMO_NATIVE=1` and is not meant for production. ## Prerequisites * A restactd binary built from source (see [Install](/docs/start/install/)). * `curl`, `openssl` and `python3`. * A shell variable `SECRET` holding the signing secret for test tokens. Authentication is required, because a connector is owned by the person who created it. [Add authentication](/docs/guides/authentication/) explains the token you mint here. ## 1. Start a local origin The origin prints the `Authorization` header it receives, which shows the credential arriving. Save this as `origin.py`: 
```python
import http.server, socket, sys


class Handler(http.server.BaseHTTPRequestHandler):
    def do_GET(self):
        auth = self.headers.get("Authorization") or ""
        sys.stderr.write("origin saw Authorization: %s\n" % auth); sys.stderr.flush()
        if self.path.startswith("/v1/echo"):
            body = auth.encode()                # echoes the credential back
        else:
            body = b'{"things":["a","b"]}'
        self.send_response(200)
        self.send_header("Content-Type", "application/json")
        self.send_header("Content-Length", str(len(body)))
        self.end_headers()
        self.wfile.write(body)
    def log_message(self, *a): pass


# Bind the address family that "localhost" resolves to first, since the host
# resolves the same name.
family = socket.getaddrinfo("localhost", 9290, type=socket.SOCK_STREAM)[0]
class Server(http.server.HTTPServer):
    address_family = family[0]


Server((family[4][0], 9290), Handler).serve_forever()
```
 
```sh
python3 origin.py
```
 ## 2. Understand what the host refuses to start with Egress needs three settings to agree. If they do not, restactd exits at startup with an `error:` line, so a half-configured host never serves traffic. | Variable | Meaning | | ------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `RESTACT_EGRESS_ALLOW` | Comma-separated `host[:port]` destinations the host may call. A DNS name, a `*.`-prefixed suffix or an address literal. Unset means the feature does not exist. | | `RESTACT_SECRETS_URL` and `RESTACT_SECRETS_MOUNT` | The secrets backend, and the object-path pattern where secrets are written, for example `ws/{ws}/secrets/{name}`. Both must be set together. | | `RESTACT_EGRESS_CONNECTOR_PREFIX` | Where connector objects are created, for example `ws/{ws}/connectors`. | An allowlist with no way to supply a credential is a mistake, so the check is one-sided: setting the allowlist without the rest is fatal, while setting the secrets mount alone boots normally and advertises `secrets-management` but not `mediated-egress`. Each of these refusals is easy to reproduce. Create an empty secrets file the host can use, then start the host three ways: 
```sh
mkdir -p secrets && chmod 700 secrets
echo '{}' > secrets/secrets.json && chmod 600 secrets/secrets.json


# 1. An allowlist and nothing else.
RESTACT_EGRESS_ALLOW=api.example.com:443 cargo run -p restactd
```
 
```text
error: RESTACT_EGRESS_ALLOW is set but no secrets mount is in service — a destination allowlist with nothing to splice is a misconfiguration. Configure RESTACT_SECRETS_URL + RESTACT_SECRETS_MOUNT, or unset RESTACT_EGRESS_ALLOW.
```
 
```sh
# 2. A secrets mount, but no connector prefix.
RESTACT_EGRESS_ALLOW=api.example.com:443 \
RESTACT_SECRETS_URL=file:$PWD/secrets/secrets.json \
RESTACT_SECRETS_MOUNT='ws/{ws}/secrets/{name}' cargo run -p restactd
```
 
```text
error: RESTACT_EGRESS_ALLOW is set but RESTACT_EGRESS_CONNECTOR_PREFIX is not — the host must know where connectors live before it will call through one. Set it to the positional prefix connectors are created under, e.g. 'ws/{ws}/connectors'.
```
 
```sh
# 3. A loopback destination with no acknowledgement.
RESTACT_EGRESS_ALLOW=127.0.0.1:9290 \
RESTACT_SECRETS_URL=file:$PWD/secrets/secrets.json \
RESTACT_SECRETS_MOUNT='ws/{ws}/secrets/{name}' \
RESTACT_EGRESS_CONNECTOR_PREFIX='ws/{ws}/connectors' cargo run -p restactd
```
 
```text
error: RESTACT_EGRESS_ALLOW names the loopback destination(s) [127.0.0.1] — the only path by which a non-public destination is ever reachable, and never the default. Set RESTACT_EGRESS_ALLOW_LOOPBACK to acknowledge it (deterministic test harnesses only), or remove the entry.
```
 The third case matters in production as well as in tests. Private and loopback addresses are blocked as destinations unless you name them and acknowledge the risk, so a connector cannot be pointed at an internal service by mistake. A real deployment allows public hostnames and never sets `RESTACT_EGRESS_ALLOW_LOOPBACK`. ## 3. Start the host For this guide the origin is on loopback, so the acknowledgement is set: 
```sh
export SECRET=replace-with-at-least-32-random-bytes
RESTACT_JWT_SECRET=$SECRET \
RESTACT_DATA_DIR=./data \
RESTACT_DEMO_NATIVE=1 \
RESTACT_SECRETS_URL=file:$PWD/secrets/secrets.json \
RESTACT_SECRETS_MOUNT='ws/{ws}/secrets/{name}' \
RESTACT_EGRESS_ALLOW=localhost:9290 \
RESTACT_EGRESS_ALLOW_LOOPBACK=1 \
RESTACT_EGRESS_CONNECTOR_PREFIX='ws/{ws}/connectors' \
cargo run -p restactd
```
 The startup log reports the mode and prints a warning for the loopback override: 
```text
secrets: file backend at …/secrets/secrets.json
warning: RESTACT_EGRESS_ALLOW_LOOPBACK is set — the host will reach the loopback destination(s) [localhost] in plain http …
mediated egress: on for 1 destination(s), connectors under 'ws/{ws}/connectors', loopback acknowledged
```
 Discovery now lists `mediated-egress` and `secrets-management` under `service.features`. ## 4. Store a secret Mint a token for `alice` (see [Add authentication](/docs/guides/authentication/#mint-a-test-token)) and write the credential to the secrets mount. The secret lists the connectors allowed to use it, and the connector lists the secrets it uses; a secret travels only where both agree. A second write binds the secret to the connector: 
```sh
export RESTACT_JWT_SECRET=replace-with-at-least-32-random-bytes     # the value the host was started with
TOKEN=$(cargo run -q -p restactd -- token mint --sub alice)
curl -X PUT -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
  -d '{"value":"sk-live-EXAMPLE-0123456789abcdef"}' \
  http://localhost:8080/v1/ws/1/secrets/key


curl -X PUT -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
  -d '{"connectors":"[\"ws/1/connectors/api\"]"}' \
  http://localhost:8080/v1/ws/1/secrets/key
```
 Both answer `204`. Reading the secret back returns metadata only: 
```sh
curl -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" http://localhost:8080/v1/ws/1/secrets/key
```
 
```json
{"$create_time":"2026-10-18T16:42:40Z","$update_time":"2026-10-18T16:42:40Z","connectors":"[\"ws/1/connectors/api\"]","version":"1"}
```
 No request returns the value. Another user asking for the same path gets `404`. ## 5. Create a connector A connector is an object under the connector prefix that names a destination, the secrets it may use, how to place them in the request, and which paths may be called. Save this as `connector.json`: 
```json
{"$policy":{"read":["owner"],"write":["owner"],"delete":["owner"]},
 "destination":"{\"host\":\"localhost\",\"port\":\"9290\",\"tls\":\"required\"}",
 "secretRefs":"[\"ws/1/secrets/key\"]",
 "splice":"{\"headers\":{\"authorization\":\"Bearer ${secret:ws/1/secrets/key}\"}}",
 "pathPrefixes":"[\"/v1\"]"}
```
 
```sh
curl -X PUT -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
  -d @connector.json http://localhost:8080/v1/ws/1/connectors/api
```
 The `${secret:…}` placeholder in `splice` is the only place the host substitutes a value. The connector must carry its own `$policy` that limits `write` and `delete` to the owner. A create under the prefix without one is refused, because holding `read` on a connector lets someone use its owner’s credential: 
```json
{"detail":"\"ws/1/connectors/bad\" is created under the declared connector prefix … so it must be born governed …","restact_code":"connector-birth-ungoverned","status":422,…}
```
 Updating an existing object under the prefix is not affected by that rule. ## 6. Make the call 
```sh
curl -X POST -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" \
  'http://localhost:8080/demo/egress?connector=ws%2F1%2Fconnectors%2Fapi&target=%2Fv1%2Fthings'
```
 
```json
{"body":"{\"things\":[\"a\",\"b\"]}","bytes_in":20,"bytes_out":0,"headers":[["content-type","application/json"],["content-length","20"]],"status":200,"truncated":false}
```
 The origin’s terminal shows what it received, and the response above shows what you received: 
```text
origin saw Authorization: Bearer sk-live-EXAMPLE-0123456789abcdef
```
 The origin got the credential. Your response, the host’s log and the data directory do not contain it. ## What just happened The route called `mediated_call` with the connector path and a target path. The host then did the following, in order: 1. Resolved the caller from the token and checked that the caller can read the connector. 2. Checked the destination against `RESTACT_EGRESS_ALLOW` and the target path against the connector’s `pathPrefixes`. 3. Read the secret from the backend at the moment of sending and substituted it into the `splice` template. 4. Sent the request with bounds on request size, response size and wall time, refusing redirects. 5. Removed any occurrence of the secret from the response before returning it, and dropped response headers such as `Set-Cookie` that are not on the host’s allowlist. 6. Wrote one audit line to the log, without the value: 
```text
egress-audit: connector=ws/1/connectors/api principal=alice method=GET target=localhost:9290/v1/things secrets=ws/1/secrets/key outcome=ok status=200 bytes_in=20 bytes_out=0 wall_ms=0 ts=1792341769
```
 ## Check that secrets stay out of responses The `/v1/echo` path on the origin returns the `Authorization` header it received, which is an attempt to launder the credential back to the caller: 
```sh
curl -X POST -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" \
  'http://localhost:8080/demo/egress?connector=ws%2F1%2Fconnectors%2Fapi&target=%2Fv1%2Fecho'
```
 
```json
{"body":"Bearer [redacted:secret]","bytes_in":39,…,"status":200,"truncated":false}
```
 The host replaces the value with `[redacted:secret]`. The value also does not appear in the host’s own log or in any stored object. ## Refusals you will meet These answers are the host working as intended: | Request | Answer | | --------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | A different user calls the connector | `egress-denied`: no connector readable at that path for this principal | | A target outside `pathPrefixes`, such as `/admin` | `egress-not-allowed` | | A request body containing the literal `${secret:` | `placeholder-in-caller-data`; placeholders are only honoured in the connector’s own template | | The origin answers with a redirect | `egress-redirect-refused`; redirects are never followed | | A connector whose destination is not in `RESTACT_EGRESS_ALLOW` | `egress-not-allowed`: `the destination example.org:9999 is not in RESTACT_EGRESS_ALLOW` | | A connector the secret does not list in its `connectors` member | `secret-not-bound`: a secret travels only where both its owners point | ## Limits and quotas Hard ceilings apply to every call. A connector can only lower them. | Variable | Default | Effect | | ---------------------------------------------------- | ------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `RESTACT_EGRESS_MAX_INFLIGHT` | `8` | Concurrent calls in the process. Beyond it a call is refused with `egress-busy`. | | `RESTACT_EGRESS_MAX_REQUEST_BYTES` | 1 MiB | Request body ceiling. | | `RESTACT_EGRESS_MAX_RESPONSE_BYTES` | 2 MiB | Response body ceiling. | | `RESTACT_EGRESS_MAX_WALL_MS` | `30000` | Wall-clock ceiling for one call. | | `RESTACT_QUOTA_EGRESS_CALLS`, `…_BYTES`, `…_WALL_MS` | unset | Per-principal caps that refuse calls once reached. Unset means metered but not enforced. | See [Configuration](/docs/reference/configuration/#mediated-egress) for the remaining header limits. ## Next steps * [Embed restactd in a Rust app](/docs/guides/embedding/) shows how to register the route that calls `mediated_call` in your own application. * [Add authentication](/docs/guides/authentication/) covers the tokens used above. * [Observability](/docs/operate/observability/) explains where the audit lines and egress metrics go.

# Embed restactd in a Rust app

> Use restactd as a Rust library: register native verbs and custom HTTP routes in your own binary, and let users extend your platform with uploaded verbs.

Use restactd as a Rust library: register native verbs and custom HTTP routes in your own binary, and let users extend your platform with uploaded verbs. This page is for platform builders. [Authoring verbs](/docs/guides/authoring-verbs/) is written for the people who upload verbs to your platform. ## The two-tier model A REST:ACT application has two kinds of code, with a hard trust boundary between them: | | Trusted platform | Untrusted experience layer | | ------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------ | | Who writes it | You (the operator) | Your users / third parties | | What it is | Native Rust verbs + custom HTTP routes, compiled into your binary | Sandboxed verbs uploaded over HTTP (wasm / JavaScript / Python) | | Trust | TCB, full authority of the process | Confined to what its world imports and what the caller could do | | How it runs | A host function | Wasmtime sandbox, fuel/epoch/memory metered | | Examples | `feed:candidates`, a WebFinger route, federation delivery | a user’s custom feed ranking, a profile widget, a redaction filter | The platform exposes primitives; users compose them. A native `feed:candidates` verb does the privileged, expensive work (an indexed query over everyone’s posts, run as the caller); an uploaded `timeline:rank` verb a user wrote calls it and re-orders the result however they like. The user’s code never touches the database directly and is bounded by fuel, but the behavior is theirs. It runs with that user’s authority, so every read is checked against what the user may read. ## The embedding API The host is a Rust library, `restactd`, as well as the stock `restactd` binary. Your application is its own crate that depends on it. 
```toml
# Cargo.toml of your platform crate
[dependencies]
restactd = { path = "../restactd/host" }   # or a git dep; the crate is not on crates.io
anyhow = "1"
```
 
```rust
// src/main.rs of your platform crate: a thin entry, exactly like the stock binary.
use std::sync::Arc;
use restactd::{HostBuilder, NativeCtx, NativeVerbSpec, Policy, VerbKind};


fn main() -> anyhow::Result<()> {
    let builder = HostBuilder::new()
        .register_native_verb(
            "feed:candidates",
            NativeVerbSpec::new(VerbKind::Function, Policy::default_for("anonymous")),
            Arc::new(|ctx: &mut NativeCtx| {
                // ...privileged work, run as the caller (see below)...
                Ok(b"[]".to_vec())
            }),
        );
    restactd::run(builder)
}
```
 `restactd::run(builder)` is the embedding entry point: it opens the configured backends (the same `RESTACT_DATA_DIR` / `RESTACT_DATABASE_URL` / isolation / auth env vars as the stock host), applies your `HostBuilder`, bootstraps, and serves. The stock binary calls `restactd::cli::main()`, which parses the command line: `--help`, `--version` and the `healthcheck`, `checkpoint`, `restore`, `reown`, `audit ancestry` and `token mint` subcommands run to completion without starting a server, and with no subcommand it calls `restactd::run_default()`. `cli::main()` returns `anyhow::Result<ExitCode>`. `run_default()` is `run(configure_host())` with the default extension set, and both it and `run` return `anyhow::Result<()>`. Both print the build line on stdout before the host’s own boot output (a process-isolation worker never prints it). Your binary keeps every operational knob in [Configuration](/docs/reference/configuration/). Embedding only adds native verbs and routes and, opt-in, the reference host’s own built-in static site (`HostBuilder::with_builtin_site()`). A default `HostBuilder` never mounts it, so `GET /` and the other site paths are free for your own routes. ### `HostBuilder` A builder collected before serving. Four chainable methods: 
```rust
HostBuilder::new()
    .register_native_verb(route, spec, handler)   // a host-implemented verb
    .register_route(method, path, handler)        // a custom non-/v1 HTTP route
    .with_builtin_site()                          // opt in to the reference host's own site
    .reserve_subject_prefix("agent:")             // keep a subject namespace for your own HS256 principals
```
 `with_builtin_site()` mounts the reference host’s demo static site and its `repo_doc` fallback, which serves several top-level directories of the process’s working directory read-only. That is a disclosure surface, not something to turn on casually off-box. It’s idempotent, takes no argument, and there’s no disabling counterpart: off is the default, so a plain `HostBuilder::new()` never mounts it and `GET /`/`GET /index.html`/etc. are ordinary custom-route territory for your own `register_route` calls. `HostBuilder` is `Clone` and cheap to clone (handlers are `Arc`s). That matters for process isolation. Under `RESTACT_ISOLATION=process` the per-request worker re-execs your binary and re-runs `main`, which rebuilds the same builder, so your native verbs resolve identically in the parent and the worker. The only rule: build the `HostBuilder` deterministically (the worker must produce the same registrations the parent did). Reading env vars to decide what to register is fine (the worker inherits them); registering based on a random seed or wall-clock is not. ### Native verbs A native verb is the trusted analogue of an uploaded one. It is resolved, composed, accessor-dispatched, run-as-caller, policy-checked, and quota’d exactly like any verb. It’s just backed by a Rust function instead of a wasm component, and is protocol-invisible (a client cannot tell). It consumes no fuel (it’s TCB code) and is bounded by wall-time + the per-tenant quota. 
```rust
use restactd::{NativeCtx, NativeVerbSpec, Policy, VerbKind};


// A native ACTION that reads + bumps a counter property on its bound object.
let mut spec = NativeVerbSpec::new(VerbKind::Action, Policy::default_for("anonymous"));
spec.granted_data = true;                       // grant the `object` capability
let handler = std::sync::Arc::new(|ctx: &mut NativeCtx| {
    let n: u64 = ctx.get("count").and_then(|s| s.parse().ok()).unwrap_or(0);
    ctx.set("count", &(n + 1).to_string())?;    // buffered; commits if the invocation succeeds
    Ok(serde_json::to_vec(&serde_json::json!({ "count": n + 1 })).unwrap_or_default())
});
builder = builder.register_native_verb("ctr/{id}:bump", spec, handler);
```
 [`NativeVerbSpec`](https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/blob/main/host/src/wasm_exec/native.rs) carries everything a deployed verb would: `kind`, `policy`, `produces`, `data_properties`, `runs_as_owner`, `limits`, `imports`, `allowed_calls`, and the capability grants (`granted_data` / `granted_compose` / `granted_event` / `granted_llm`, plus `invoker_grant` for `invoker()`). The grants are deny-by-default: a verb that doesn’t set `granted_data` provably can’t reach object data, same as an uploaded verb whose world doesn’t import `object`. The handler receives a `NativeCtx`, the trusted mirror of the sandbox’s host state, reusing the identical capability cores so native and wasm behavior match: | `NativeCtx` method | What it does | | -------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `input()` | the request payload bytes | | `get` / `set` / `get_at` / `set_at` | object-data capability (ACL- & scope-aware, run-as-caller); a path, property name or value holding U+0000 is `invalid-input` | | `get_value` / `set_value` / `get_value_at` / `set_value_at` | the same, with JSON values instead of strings | | `remove(name)` / `remove_at(path, name)` | remove a property, under the same authority as `set` | | `get_bytes(path)` | read a binary object value `(content_type, bytes)` ([binary values](#binary--media-values)) | | `query(prefix, order_by, descending, predicate, limit, cursor)` | the [ordered collection query](#the-query-primitive-collections), ACL-filtered as the caller | | `compose(verb, input)` | invoke another verb under the shared attenuated budget | | `publish(topic, name, data)` | emit an event (gated by `granted_event`); name 1-128 bytes, no ASCII control chars, and not `onCreate`/`onChange`/`onDelete`/`gap` (`forbidden`); data empty or one JSON object with no top-level `$` member, else `invalid-input`; the host adds a `$publisher` member naming the verb’s route; topic at most 1024 bytes (`invalid-input`); data over 64 KiB stamped, or over 4 MiB published in one invocation, is `413 object-too-large` | | `subject` / `is_anonymous` / `roles` / `scopes` / `groups` / `claim` | the run-as-caller identity | | `principal()` / `invoker()` / `actor()` | the full principal; the frame’s originator, set only when the spec sets `invoker_grant`; the delegation actor id, or none when no delegation applies | | `accept` / `header` / `method` / `set_content_type` | the request context, for [representation negotiation](#representation-negotiation) | | `block_on(future)` | drive an `async` future to completion (e.g. an outbound HTTP call) from the synchronous handler | | `object_path()` | the bound object’s path (for accessor verbs) | Native verbs are defaults registered by the embedder. An uploaded verb at the same `(object, verb)` key takes precedence over the native, and removing the upload brings the native back. Precedence compares the route patterns first, so an upload at a more specific pattern outranks a native at a less specific one, and a native at a more specific pattern outranks an upload at a less specific one. This lets a platform ship a built-in such as `timeline:rank` that each user can replace on their own object. Who may upload follows the usual deploy rules: the object’s owner under a coupled binding mode. At the native’s exact key the native’s own write policy also applies. A native registered with `Policy::default_for("anonymous")`, as the examples are, admits no named user there on an authenticated host. ### Custom routes Native verbs live under `/v1`. To serve URLs the protocol doesn’t define (a WebFinger endpoint, an ActivityPub actor document or inbox, an OAuth callback), register a plain HTTP route: 
```rust
use restactd::{RouteContext, RouteError, RouteResponse};


builder = builder.register_route(
    "GET",
    "/.well-known/webfinger",
    std::sync::Arc::new(|rc: RouteContext| {
        let resource = rc.query.split('&')
            .find_map(|kv| kv.strip_prefix("resource="))
            .unwrap_or("");
        if resource.is_empty() {
            return Err(RouteError::new(400, "missing ?resource="));  // → RFC 9457 problem
        }
        // `rc.exec` is the narrowed RouteExecutor (read, query and invoke, run as a principal).
        let body = serde_json::json!({ "subject": resource });
        Ok(RouteResponse::ok("application/jrd+json", serde_json::to_vec(&body).unwrap_or_default()))
    }),
);
```
 The handler is a synchronous `Fn(RouteContext) -> Result<RouteResponse, RouteError>` run on the blocking pool (so it may `block_on` outbound I/O without starving the reactor); an `Err(RouteError)` maps to an `application/problem+json` response, so `?` works for failures. [`RouteContext`](https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/blob/main/host/src/lib.rs) gives it the method, path, raw query, headers (lowercased; `rc.header("user-agent")`), body bytes, and a `RouteExecutor` (`rc.exec`), a narrowed read/query/invoke handle (`object_get`/`object_put`/`object_get_blob`/`object_put_blob`/`query_objects`/ `invoke`/`resolve_kind`, each taking an explicit `&Principal`). It deliberately does not expose the control-plane methods (`reset`/`remove`/`set_policy`/…), so a custom route can’t reach them by construction. `object_put_blob` refuses a content type that is not exactly one media type with `invalid-input`; an empty one is stored as `application/octet-stream`. The host authenticates the request before your handler runs: a request that presents a bearer or identity header which fails verification gets the same `401` as `/v1` and never reaches the handler, and a request with no credential runs as anonymous. The host owns `Authorization: Bearer` on custom routes, including on a host with no auth configured (any bearer gets `401`), so a route cannot accept its own bearer scheme; other schemes (`Signature`, `Basic`) reach the handler. Federation routes then verify their own HTTP signatures and choose the principal; a public WebFinger uses `Principal::anonymous`. A `register_route` path at or under `/v1` would shadow an object, so the host refuses to start and names the path. [`RouteResponse`](https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/blob/main/host/src/lib.rs) has `ok(content_type, body)`, `json(body)`, `status(code, msg)`, and `.with_header(name, value)`. Custom routes are mounted only in the parent’s router; a `(path, method)` pair that collides with a built-in route panics at startup (an operator error, surfaced loudly). The built-in site paths are free custom-route territory unless `with_builtin_site()` is on, in which case they collide like any other built-in. ## The enablers Four capabilities make a data-shaped application (a feed, a profile, media) buildable. ### The query primitive (collections) Object data is more than key/value lookups: the store has an ordered, prefix-scoped, predicate-filtered, cursor-paginated query. A native verb reaches it via `ctx.query(...)`, and it is the foundation of any collection: a timeline, an outbox, a search result, a leaderboard. Uploaded verbs reach the same primitive through the `object` capability’s `query` (feature `verb-object-query`), which returns an ACL-filtered, cursor-paginated page under a host-clamped per-call cap. The separate `object-query` token gates only the `GET /v1?filter=/read_mask=` listing refinement. 
```rust
// "newest 20 posts in this user's outbox", run as the caller, so ACLs apply.
let (rows, next) = ctx.query(
    "outbox/alice/",      // prefix: scope the scan to one collection
    Some("ts"),           // order_by: a property value (store ISO-8601 → lexicographic = chrono)
    true,                 // descending
    None,                 // optional (key, value) equality predicate
    20,                   // limit
    None,                 // cursor: the sealed token from a previous page's `next`
)?;
// rows: Vec<QueryObject{ path, readable (its readable props), order_value }>;
// next: Option<String>, an opaque sealed token, when more remain. A cursor this host did not
// issue for this query and caller is InvocationError { code: "invalid-input" }.
```
 ACL filtering happens above the store (`Executor::query_objects`): the listing is always ⊆ what the caller could read, with honest keyset pagination (it over-fetches so a page is never silently truncated by ACL drops). On the PostgreSQL backend this is a real indexed SQL query with a `(order, path)` keyset, not a scan. Uploaded verbs can call `object.query` directly, or compose a native query verb you expose (e.g. `feed:candidates`) when the platform should control exactly what is queryable. The same primitive backs `GET /v1` object listing (`order_by`-by-path, `filter=`, `pageToken`). ### Binary / media values A property’s value can be binary (an avatar, a header image, post media), stored as a content-addressed blob with a tiny `$blob` reference in the attribute map, not base64 in JSON (features `binary-values` + `object-binary`): * Data plane: `PUT /v1/users/alice/avatar` with `Content-Type: image/png` stores the bytes; a `GET` serves them back with that content type + a content-hash `ETag`. The object’s representation is the bytes. Internally the dedup’d blob store holds them and the object keeps a tiny `$blob` reference, so the per-object size cap bounds the reference, not the media. * Verbs: a native verb reads bytes with `ctx.get_bytes(path)`; an uploaded wasm verb with the additive `object.get-bytes` / `get-bytes-at` methods (SDK: `object::get_bytes()`). Because a verb’s output is already binary, the canonical pattern is a read-time transform: an `avatar:get` accessor that reads the stored image, transforms it by caller/time/locale, and returns the new bytes, with no second stored copy and no write capability needed. (Verb-persisted binary, `set-bytes`, and streaming are not available.) ### Representation negotiation One object, many representations. A native verb (or accessor) inspects `Accept` and emits the right content type per invocation: 
```rust
let handler = std::sync::Arc::new(|ctx: &mut NativeCtx| {
    if ctx.accept().unwrap_or("").contains("application/activity+json") {
        ctx.set_content_type("application/activity+json");
        // ...return the ActivityStreams actor document...
    } else {
        ctx.set_content_type("text/html");
        // ...return an HTML profile page...
    }
    Ok(/* bytes */ Vec::new())
});
```
 This is the general seam (`text/html`, `application/json`, `application/activity+json`, JSON:API’s `application/vnd.api+json`, …), not an ActivityPub one-off. The mechanism is in the host; each representation’s *document shape* is yours. ### Run as the caller Every capability, native or uploaded (`query`, `get`, `set`, `compose`), is evaluated against the caller’s principal. A native `feed:candidates` invoked by Alice reads only what Alice could read; the same verb invoked by Bob sees Bob’s view. This is *why* you can safely let users upload feed/search/view logic that composes your primitives: their code inherits their authority and can never exceed it. (A platform verb that must act with its owner’s authority instead (for example a moderation hook) opts in with `runs_as_owner`, setuid-style.) ## Worked example: a social platform users can reshape Here is a motivating case: an ActivityPub server that interoperates with Mastodon, whose differentiator is that users shape their own experience: custom feed ranking, custom search, custom views of their own data. The split: the federation engine (HTTP signatures, delivery, inbox processing, key management) is trusted native code in your binary (it makes outbound calls and owns crypto, which untrusted code can’t be handed). The experience layer (how a timeline is ranked, what a search does, how a profile renders) is untrusted, user-uploaded verbs that run as the user and compose your primitives. 
```rust
fn main() -> anyhow::Result<()> {
    let b = HostBuilder::new()
        // ── platform primitives (native, composable, run-as-caller) ──────────────
        // Candidate posts for a timeline: an indexed query over the people the caller
        // follows. Privileged + expensive; users compose it but can't bypass it.
        .register_native_verb("feed:candidates", feed_spec(), Arc::new(feed_candidates))
        // A read-time avatar transform (binary in, binary out).
        .register_native_verb("users/{id}/avatar:get", avatar_spec(), Arc::new(avatar_get))
        // ── federation surfaces (custom routes, the engine owns crypto/egress) ────
        .register_route("GET",  "/.well-known/webfinger", Arc::new(webfinger))
        .register_route("GET",  "/users/{id}",            Arc::new(actor_document)) // Accept-negotiated
        .register_route("POST", "/users/{id}/inbox",      Arc::new(inbox));         // verifies HTTP sigs
    restactd::run(b)
}
```
 Now a user makes the platform theirs without you shipping anything. They upload a verb (JavaScript, no toolchain) that re-ranks the native candidates: 
```javascript
// timeline:rank, deployed by the user at users/alice/timeline:rank.
// It calls the native feed:candidates (run as the user, so it sees only their network),
// then orders by the user's own rule: boost posts with media, demote link-only posts.
function invoke(input) {
  const posts = JSON.parse(compose("feed:candidates", "") || "[]");
  return posts
    .map(p => ({ ...p, score: (p.hasMedia ? 2 : 1) - (p.linkOnly ? 0.5 : 0) }))
    .sort((a, b) => b.score - a.score)
    .slice(0, 40);
}
```
 The same shape gives you user-defined search (`search:run` composing a native `posts:query`), custom views (an accessor verb that reshapes the user’s own object data), and reactions (an action that writes a `shared`-scoped count). Every one of them is fuel-bounded and runs with the user’s authority, so every read is checked against what the user may read. Where each enabler lands: `feed:candidates`/`posts:query` need the [query primitive](#the-query-primitive-collections); avatars/media need [binary values](#binary--media-values); the actor document negotiates `application/activity+json` vs HTML via [negotiation](#representation-negotiation); per-user scoping is [run-as-caller](#run-as-the-caller); the federation engine reaches the network from native handlers via `block_on`. An untrusted verb’s one outbound path is the `llm` capability; the mediated outbound call (see [Configuration → Mediated egress](/docs/reference/configuration/#mediated-egress)) is available only to embedder routes, through `RouteContext::mediated_call`; a native verb makes its own outbound calls with its own client through `block_on`. In this architecture the federation engine’s egress stays trusted native code. ## Operating an embedded host An embedded host is configured and secured exactly like the stock one. It is the stock host plus your registrations. So everything in [Configuration](/docs/reference/configuration/) applies unchanged: durable storage (`RESTACT_DATA_DIR`) or the active-active PostgreSQL backend (`RESTACT_DATABASE_URL`), authentication (`RESTACT_JWT_SECRET` / OIDC / trusted headers), per-request process isolation (`RESTACT_ISOLATION=process`; remember the deterministic-builder rule above), quotas, CORS, and the binary-blob cap (`RESTACT_MAX_BLOB_BYTES`). Run `RESTACT_DEMO_NATIVE=1` against the stock binary to see a live native verb (`sys:objects`), a native query verb (`sys:query`), and a demo custom route (`/.well-known/demo-webfinger`) before writing your own. These are the same registrations [`scripts/native-test.sh`](https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/blob/main/scripts/native-test.sh) exercises on both backends. ## See also * [Authoring Verbs](/docs/guides/authoring-verbs/): the other side, writing the uploaded verbs your users deploy. * [HTTP API](/docs/reference/http-api/): the wire surface your native verbs and routes sit beside. * [Architecture](/docs/architecture/overview/): where native verbs, the embedding seam, the query primitive, and blobs live in the code.

# Write verbs in JavaScript

> Upload a JavaScript file and call it over HTTP. There is no toolchain and no build step: the host stores your source and runs it in a shared QuickJS runner inside the same sandbox, with the same…

Upload a JavaScript file and call it over HTTP. There is no toolchain and no build step: the host stores your source and runs it in a shared QuickJS runner inside the same sandbox, with the same limits and capability checks as a compiled verb. This guide takes you from a one-line function to an action that reads and writes object data, reads its caller, and calls another verb. For the full list of globals and the reasoning behind them, see the [authoring reference](/docs/guides/authoring-verbs/). ## Prerequisites * A running restactd with the JavaScript tier enabled. [Install](/docs/start/install/) shows how to build the runner; a host without it answers `415` to a JavaScript upload. * `curl`, and a shell. Commands use `localhost:8080`; the `-g` flag stops curl from treating `{id}` as a glob. Check that the host accepts JavaScript: 
```sh
curl -s localhost:8080/.well-known/restact | grep -o '"acceptedSourceLanguages":\[[^]]*\]'
```
 
```text
"acceptedSourceLanguages":["application/wasm","text/javascript","text/x-python"]
```
 ## A function you can call with GET A verb is a function or an action. A function has no side effects, is called with `GET`, and its responses can be cached. An action may write data and is called with `POST`. You choose the kind at deploy time. 1. Write the verb. It defines `invoke(input)`, where `input` is the request body or `?input=` value as a string. hello.js 
   ```js
   function invoke(input) {
     const name = input.trim() || "world";
     log("greeting " + name);
     return "Hello, " + name + "!";
   }
   ```
 2. Deploy it. The `lang` parameter names the source language, and `verb` is the route. 
   ```sh
   curl -gX POST -H 'Content-Type: text/javascript' --data-binary @hello.js \
     'localhost:8080/v1/modules?verb=greet:hello&kind=function&lang=text/javascript'
   ```
 The host parses the script before it answers. The `201` body lists the imports the verb ended up with and its content hash. 3. Call it. 
   ```sh
   curl -g 'localhost:8080/v1/greet:hello?input=Ada'
   ```
 
   ```text
   Hello, Ada!
   ```
 A request body works too: `curl -g -X GET --data-binary 'Grace' localhost:8080/v1/greet:hello`. 4. Try the wrong method. The host enforces the function/action split. 
   ```sh
   curl -gX POST localhost:8080/v1/greet:hello
   ```
 
   ```json
   {"detail":"verb is a Function; invoke it with GET","restact_code":"method-not-allowed","restact_verb":":hello","status":405,"title":"Method Not Allowed","type":"about:blank"}
   ```
 What just happened: `greet:hello` is a route with no object in front of it (a collection verb). The script’s return value became the response body. `log` wrote a structured record to the host’s log, where it appears as `[guest greet:hello log info] greeting Ada`. ## Return structured data A string passes through unchanged. An object or array is serialized with `JSON.stringify`. `null` and `undefined` give an empty body. The input is always text. A `Uint8Array` or `ArrayBuffer` return value is sent as raw bytes. To accept binary input, use a [Rust verb](/docs/guides/rust/). Replace the source with `PUT …/code`. The route stays and the code changes: hello.js 
```js
function invoke(input) {
  const name = input.trim() || "world";
  return { greeting: "Hello, " + name + "!", length: name.length };
}
```
 
```sh
curl -gX PUT -H 'Content-Type: text/javascript' --data-binary @hello.js \
  localhost:8080/v1/greet:hello/code          # 204
curl -g 'localhost:8080/v1/greet:hello?input=Ada'
```
 
```json
{"greeting":"Hello, Ada!","length":3}
```
 `GET /v1/greet:hello/code` with `Accept: text/javascript` returns the stored source. A script that does not parse is refused at upload with `422` and `restact_code: invalid-source`, and the stored version stays as it was: 
```sh
curl -gX PUT -H 'Content-Type: text/javascript' --data-binary 'function invoke( {' \
  localhost:8080/v1/greet:hello/code
```
 
```json
{"detail":"Error: invalid property name\n    at verb:1:19\n","restact_code":"invalid-source","restact_verb":":hello","status":422,"title":"Unprocessable Entity","type":"about:blank"}
```
 ## An action that uses object data An entity verb is bound to an object: the route `pages/{id}:view` runs against `pages/home`, `pages/about` and any other `pages/<name>`. Inside the script, `obj` reads and writes that object’s data. view\.js 
```js
function invoke(input) {
  const n = (obj.get("views") ?? 0) + 1;
  obj.set("views", n);
  log("page viewed " + n + " times");
  return { views: n, path: obj.path() };
}
```
 
```sh
curl -gX POST -H 'Content-Type: text/javascript' --data-binary @view.js \
  'localhost:8080/v1/modules?verb=pages/%7Bid%7D:view&kind=action&lang=text/javascript'
curl -gX POST localhost:8080/v1/pages/home:view
curl -gX POST localhost:8080/v1/pages/home:view
curl localhost:8080/v1/pages/home
```
 
```json
{"views":1,"path":"pages/home"}
{"views":2,"path":"pages/home"}
{"views":2}
```
 The deploy URL spells `{id}` as `%7Bid%7D` so the query string stays valid. The `-g` flag stops curl from treating braces as a glob in the other commands. Property values are typed. `obj.get` returns `undefined` when the property is absent or the caller may not read it. If you declare `kind=function`, the host refuses the write: functions may read object data but not change it (`403`, `forbidden-side-effect`). **Typed values.** A property holds a string or a JSON value, and the runner maps them at the boundary: | JavaScript | Stored as | Read back as | | ---------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------- | | a string | `text` | the same string (the string `"5"` stays a string) | | a number, boolean, array or object | `json` (via `JSON.stringify`) | the `JSON.parse`d value | | `undefined` or `null` | refused: `invalid-input` (`422`); use `obj.remove` | n/a | For `obj.setAtIf`, `expected` maps the same way, and `null` or `undefined` means “absent”. Query rows carry the same typed `properties` and `orderValue`. The `obj` object has these methods: | Method | Does | | ------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `obj.get(name)`, `obj.set(name, value)` | Read or write a property of the bound object. | | `obj.getAt(path, name)`, `obj.setAt(path, name, value)` | Read or write a property of another object, checked against the caller’s permissions. | | `obj.remove(name)`, `obj.removeAt(path, name)` | Remove a property of the bound object or of another object, checked against the caller’s permissions like a write. Removing an unset property succeeds. `remove` throws `not-found` when the bound object holds no data, and `removeAt` throws `not-found` when no object is stored at `path`. | | `obj.setAtIf(path, name, value, expected)` | Write only if the current value equals `expected` (`null` means “absent”). Throws `precondition-failed` otherwise. | | `obj.shape()`, `obj.shapeAt(path)` | `{properties, children, verbs}` that the caller can see. | | `obj.path()` | The concrete path the verb was invoked on, for example `pages/home`. | | `obj.getBytes()`, `obj.getBytesAt(path)` | The binary value of the bound or another object as `{contentType, data}` with `data` a `Uint8Array`, or `undefined` when there is none. | | `obj.query(options)` | A page of objects under a path prefix. See [Store and query object data](/docs/guides/object-data/). | A value over 6 MiB (a quarter of the script heap) throws an error whose message carries `memory-limit`; read it from a [Rust verb](/docs/guides/rust/). A verb can return a `Uint8Array` or `ArrayBuffer` and the response body is those bytes: 
```js
function invoke(input) {
  const img = obj.getBytes();
  return img ? img.data : "";
}
```
 ## Read the caller `caller` describes who made the request. It grants no authority; it reports the identity the verb already runs under. 
```js
function invoke(input) {
  return {
    subject: caller.subject(),
    anonymous: caller.isAnonymous(),
    roles: caller.roles(),
    scopes: caller.scopes(),
    groups: caller.groups(),
    dept: caller.claim("dept"),
  };
}
```
 On a host with no authentication configured, every caller is anonymous and the answer is `{"anonymous":true,"roles":[],"scopes":[],"groups":[]}`. Absent values are `undefined`, so `JSON.stringify` leaves them out. With a bearer token for `alice` that carries `roles: ["editor"]` and `dept: "eng"`: 
```sh
curl -g -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" localhost:8080/v1/util:whoami
```
 
```json
{"subject":"alice","anonymous":false,"roles":["editor"],"scopes":[],"groups":[],"dept":"eng"}
```
 [Add authentication](/docs/guides/authentication/) shows how to configure tokens. [Identity and authority](/docs/concepts/identity-and-authority/) explains why the verb always runs as the caller. ## Call another verb `compose(verb, input)` runs another verb and returns its output as a string. Declare every verb you intend to call with `calls=` at deploy time: 
```sh
curl -gX POST -H 'Content-Type: text/javascript' \
  --data-binary 'function invoke(i){ return i.toUpperCase(); }' \
  'localhost:8080/v1/modules?verb=util:shout&kind=function&lang=text/javascript'


curl -gX POST -H 'Content-Type: text/javascript' \
  --data-binary 'function invoke(i){ return compose("util:shout", "hello " + i); }' \
  'localhost:8080/v1/modules?verb=util:greetLoud&kind=function&lang=text/javascript&calls=util:shout'


curl -g 'localhost:8080/v1/util:greetLoud?input=ada'
```
 
```text
HELLO ADA
```
 An undeclared call is refused with `403` and `restact_code: not-declared`. [Compose verbs](/docs/guides/composition/) covers budgets, errors and cycles. ## Handle errors A thrown error becomes a `422` with `restact_code: script-error`, and the error text, including the line in your script, goes into `detail`: 
```js
function invoke(i) { throw new Error("nope: " + i); }
```
 
```json
{"detail":"Error: nope: x\n    at invoke (eval_script:1:49)\n","restact_code":"script-error","restact_verb":":boom","status":422,"title":"Unprocessable Entity","type":"about:blank"}
```
 Callers see that text, so keep secrets out of error messages. A failure inside a host capability (a denied write, a failed `setAtIf`, an undeclared `compose`) is also a catchable exception, but it has no `.code` property. The code travels inside the message, between SOH characters (`\u0001`). Use this helper to read it, and rethrow what you do not handle: 
```js
function hostCode(e) {
  const m = String((e && e.message) || "");
  return m.startsWith("\u0001restact-host-error\u0001") ? m.split("\u0001")[2] : null;
}
```
 If an uncaught host error reaches the top of the script, the caller gets the original status, such as `403` for `forbidden`. Throwing a new `Error` in its place keeps the code only if its message carries the tag, and otherwise gives a generic `script-error`. In a composed call, `verb-not-deployed`, and only that code, means nothing is deployed at the route; any other code, `not-found` included, is a refusal. See [Distinguishing host errors](/docs/guides/authoring-verbs/#distinguishing-host-errors-both-scripting-tiers). As a worked example, here is a counter that retries when another request wins the race. It uses `setAtIf`, which writes only if the stored value is still the one you read: 
```js
function invoke(input) {
  const path = obj.path();
  for (let attempt = 0; attempt < 5; attempt++) {
    const current = obj.getAt(path, "count");
    const next = String(Number(current ?? "0") + 1);
    try {
      obj.setAtIf(path, "count", next, current);
      return { count: Number(next), attempts: attempt + 1 };
    } catch (e) {
      if (hostCode(e) !== "precondition-failed") throw e;
    }
  }
  throw new Error("too much contention");
}
```
 
```json
{"count":1,"attempts":1}
{"count":2,"attempts":1}
{"count":3,"attempts":1}
```
 ## What the runner provides and what it does not | Available | Not available | | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | Modern ECMAScript (arrow functions, destructuring, template strings, optional chaining), `JSON`, `Math`, `Date`, `Map`, `Set`, `RegExp`, `BigInt`, typed arrays, `atob`/`btoa`, `encodeURIComponent` | `fetch`, `XMLHttpRequest`, sockets, a filesystem | | `log`, `console.log`, `compose`, `obj`, `caller`, `publish(topic, name, data)` (events, when the verb has the event grant), `llm.complete(request)` (messages take the role `"user"` or `"assistant"`, and the host refuses any other; only when the host has an llm connector configured, and not in a function) | `setTimeout`, `require`, ES `import`, `TextEncoder`, `structuredClone` | | Synchronous code | Awaiting: an `async` function returns a promise, which is serialized as `{}` | Every invocation runs under per-request limits, which discovery reports under `defaultCapabilities`: 200 million compute units, 2 seconds of wall-clock time and 32 MiB of memory by default. The runner also caps its own heap at 24 MiB. * A script that runs out of compute is stopped with `429` and `restact_code: cpu-budget`. A tight `while (true) {}` hits this in a fraction of a second. * A script that allocates past the heap cap fails with `script-error` and `Error: out of memory`. * Wall-clock overruns are `408`; memory overruns at the sandbox level are `507`. Source is parsed on every invocation, out of the run fuel, so keep hot scripts small and give a large source a higher `?fuel=` (see [Capabilities and budgets](/docs/concepts/capabilities-and-budgets/#metering)). The runner is a single trusted component shared by every JavaScript verb; what a script can reach is decided by the host’s grants, ACLs and policy, not by the script. ## Editor completion and type checks The runner installs `obj`, `caller`, `log`, `compose`, `publish`, `llm` and `console` as globals, so an editor reports them as undefined. [`sdk/js/restact.d.ts`](https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/blob/main/sdk/js/restact.d.ts) declares them. Copy it next to your verb and add `/// <reference path="restact.d.ts" />`, or list it in `tsconfig.json` with `checkJs` on. [`sdk/README.md`](https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/blob/main/sdk/README.md#editor-types-for-javascript-and-python-verbs) has the config. The file declares what the runner exposes through the host. It does not declare `atob`, `btoa`, `performance` or `queueMicrotask`, which the QuickJS engine provides (see the table above). If a verb uses one, add your own declaration. ## Next steps * [Store and query object data](/docs/guides/object-data/): conditional writes, collections, `obj.query`. * [Compose verbs](/docs/guides/composition/): shared budgets and run-as-caller through a call chain. * [Write verbs in Python](/docs/guides/python/): the same model with a different runner. * [Authoring reference](/docs/guides/authoring-verbs/): every global, the world model and the compiled-verb path.

# Lifecycle hooks

> A lifecycle hook is a verb that restactd runs for you when an object is created, changed or deleted. This guide deploys three hooks that write an audit trail, then shows what runs the hook, how…

A lifecycle hook is a verb that restactd runs for you when an object is created, changed or deleted. This guide deploys three hooks that write an audit trail, then shows what runs the hook, how failures are handled and how to switch hooks off. ## Prerequisites * A running restactd with the JavaScript tier (see [Install](/docs/start/install/) and [Quickstart](/docs/start/quickstart/)). The examples use the default `http://localhost:8080` with no authentication configured. * `curl`. ## 1. Write the hook A hook is an ordinary action verb with one of three reserved names: `onCreate`, `onChange` or `onDelete`. The host passes it a JSON event as the input. Save this as `audit.js`: 
```js
function invoke(input) {
  const event = JSON.parse(input);
  obj.setAt("audit", event.event, JSON.stringify(event));
  return "ok";
}
```
 The hook records each event on a separate `audit` object. It does not write back to the object that fired it, which would be circular for `onDelete`. ## 2. Deploy it for every `notes/{id}` Bind the hook to the `notes/{id}` pattern so it applies to every note. Deploy the same source under all three names. 
```sh
for h in onCreate onChange onDelete; do
  curl -gs -o /dev/null -w "$h %{http_code}\n" -X POST \
    -H 'Content-Type: text/javascript' --data-binary @audit.js \
    "http://localhost:8080/v1/modules?verb=notes/{id}:$h&kind=action&lang=text/javascript"
done
```
 
```text
onCreate 201
onChange 201
onDelete 201
```
 A hook must be an action. Deploying one with `kind=function` is refused at deploy time: 
```json
{"detail":"lifecycle hook 'onChange' must be an action (got function)","restact_code":"invalid-input","status":400,…}
```
 ## 3. Trigger the hooks Write, rewrite and delete a note, then read the audit object after each step: 
```sh
curl -X PUT -H 'Content-Type: application/json' -d '{"title":"first"}'  http://localhost:8080/v1/notes/1
curl http://localhost:8080/v1/audit
```
 
```json
{"onCreate":"{\"event\":\"onCreate\",\"object\":\"notes/1\",\"subject\":null}"}
```
 
```sh
curl -X PUT -H 'Content-Type: application/json' -d '{"title":"second"}' http://localhost:8080/v1/notes/1
curl -X DELETE http://localhost:8080/v1/notes/1
curl http://localhost:8080/v1/audit
```
 
```json
{"onChange":"{\"event\":\"onChange\",\"object\":\"notes/1\",\"subject\":null}","onCreate":"…","onDelete":"{\"event\":\"onDelete\",\"object\":\"notes/1\",\"subject\":null}"}
```
 The first `PUT` creates the object, so it fires `onCreate`. The second `PUT` changes an existing object and fires `onChange`. The `DELETE` fires `onDelete`. `subject` is `null` here because the host has no authentication configured; with a token it carries the caller’s subject. ## What just happened After each data-plane write, the host looked up a verb with the reserved name on the object, walking up the ancestry and the `{id}` pattern, and invoked it synchronously. The event payload has three members: | Member | Value | | --------- | --------------------------------------------------- | | `event` | `onCreate`, `onChange` or `onDelete` | | `object` | the concrete path of the object that changed | | `subject` | the caller who made the write, for information only | Hooks also fire when a verb changes object data with `obj.set`, `obj.setAt`, `obj.remove` or `obj.removeAt`, not only for HTTP requests. A hook that writes an object which has its own hooks can start a chain; the host stops a chain at depth 4. The discovery document advertises the behaviour as `service.lifecycleHooks: true`, the `lifecycle-hooks` feature, and `"role": "lifecycle-hook"` on each hook verb. ## Whose authority a hook runs with A hook runs as the user who deployed it, not as the user whose write triggered it. The hook’s owner supplies the identity, the `object` permissions and the quota. This lets a hook write to a place the triggering user cannot. To see it, start a host with authentication ([Add authentication](/docs/guides/authentication/)); it uses strict binding, so create the parents first. As Alice, create `notes`, and create `audit` so that only its owner may write it: 
```sh
curl -X PUT -H "Authorization: Bearer $ALICE" -H 'Content-Type: application/json' --data '{}' http://localhost:8080/v1/notes
curl -X PUT -H "Authorization: Bearer $ALICE" -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
  --data '{"$policy":{"write":["owner"]}}' http://localhost:8080/v1/audit
```
 Deploy the three hooks as Alice (add `-H "Authorization: Bearer $ALICE"` to the deploy command from step 2), then let Bob create `notes/b2`. The hook runs as Alice and writes the audit entry; the entry names Bob as the subject: 
```sh
curl -X PUT -H "Authorization: Bearer $BOB" -H 'Content-Type: application/json' -d '{"title":"b"}' http://localhost:8080/v1/notes/b2
```
 
```sh
curl http://localhost:8080/v1/audit
```
 
```json
{"onCreate":"{\"event\":\"onCreate\",\"object\":\"notes/b2\",\"subject\":\"bob\"}"}
```
 A direct write by Bob to `audit` is refused with `403`, because only Alice may write there. A hook can never do more than its owner could do. ## Objects the hook’s owner cannot read A hook fires only if its owner could read the object it fired for. If the object’s `$policy` leaves the owner out, the hook is skipped: it does not run, no ancestor’s hook runs in its place, and nothing is charged to the owner’s quota. The check uses the owner’s own identity, not the writer’s, and not any role, group or scope the owner’s token happens to carry. For `onDelete` it uses the object’s policy as it stood just before the delete. The server logs one line naming the hook’s route, not the object, with `reason=not-readable`. ## Failure behaviour Hooks are best-effort. If a hook throws, restactd logs the failure and keeps the write that triggered it. Deploy a hook that always fails and write an object it covers: 
```sh
echo 'function invoke(i){ throw new Error("boom"); }' > boom.js
curl -gs -X POST -H 'Content-Type: text/javascript' --data-binary @boom.js \
  'http://localhost:8080/v1/modules?verb=flaky/{id}:onCreate&kind=action&lang=text/javascript' -o /dev/null
curl -i -X PUT -H 'Content-Type: application/json' -d '{"k":"v"}' http://localhost:8080/v1/flaky/1
```
 The `PUT` still answers `204 No Content` and `GET /v1/flaky/1` returns `{"k":"v"}`. The host log has the reason: 
```text
[hook flaky/1:onCreate] not fired: script-error: Error: boom …
```
 Do not use a hook to enforce a rule that must reject a write. A hook cannot veto the write that triggered it. ## Turn hooks off Set `RESTACT_LIFECYCLE_HOOKS=off` (also `0` or `false`) when you start restactd: 
```sh
RESTACT_LIFECYCLE_HOOKS=off cargo run -p restactd
```
 Deployed hooks stay deployed but no write fires them. Discovery reports `service.lifecycleHooks: false` and omits the `lifecycle-hooks` feature. You can still call a hook directly, as with any action: `POST /v1/notes/1:onCreate`. ## Next steps * [Schedules and subscriptions](/docs/guides/schedules/) run a verb on a timer or when an object publishes an event. * [Store and query object data](/docs/guides/object-data/) covers the `object` capability a hook uses. * [Configuration](/docs/reference/configuration/#eventing-lifecycle--object-features) lists the eventing variables.

# Store and query object data

> Every path under /v1 can hold data: a flat set of named properties, each holding a JSON value. You read and write it with plain HTTP, list it with a paginated collection endpoint, and let verbs act…

Every path under `/v1` can hold data: a flat set of named properties, each holding a JSON value. You read and write it with plain HTTP, list it with a paginated collection endpoint, and let verbs act on it through the `object` capability. This guide covers the HTTP side first, then the verb side: reads and writes, queries, the object’s own path, and atomic invocations. ## Prerequisites * A running restactd on `localhost:8080`. Object data needs the `data-plane` feature, which the reference host advertises. * The JavaScript tier for the verb examples. Rust and Python equivalents are noted where they exist. ## Write, read and delete an object 1. Store properties with `PUT`. The body is a JSON object whose members are any JSON value (stored typed), or `null` to remove a property, and the write merges into whatever is already there. 
   ```sh
   curl -X PUT -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
     --data '{"title":"Hello","kind":"note"}' localhost:8080/v1/notes/n1
   ```
 The response is `204`. Creating the object needs no earlier step. 2. Read it. 
   ```sh
   curl -i localhost:8080/v1/notes/n1
   ```
 
   ```http
   HTTP/1.1 200 OK
   content-type: application/json
   etag: "a6622863de43132f54a337e9c2c59be257ea3cf8d68fe252e66cd481f3cd478e"


   {"kind":"note","title":"Hello"}
   ```
 3. Add a property. Because `PUT` merges, a second write that names only `extra` keeps `title` and `kind`. 
   ```sh
   curl -X PUT -H 'Content-Type: application/json' --data '{"extra":"1"}' localhost:8080/v1/notes/n1
   curl localhost:8080/v1/notes/n1
   ```
 
   ```json
   {"extra":"1","kind":"note","title":"Hello"}
   ```
 4. Remove a property. A `null` member removes that property and leaves the others alone. 
   ```sh
   curl -X PUT -H 'Content-Type: application/json' --data '{"extra":null}' localhost:8080/v1/notes/n1
   curl localhost:8080/v1/notes/n1
   ```
 
   ```json
   {"kind":"note","title":"Hello"}
   ```
 A `null` for a property that is not set changes nothing. Removing needs the same authority as setting: the object’s write policy and the property’s write ACL decide, and a `final` property that is set answers `409` with code `property-final`. A `null` for `$policy` removes the object’s own policy, so the object falls back to its governing anchor’s policy or the default. It needs the authority to rewrite the policy, and it is refused on a secrets-mount slot (`422`), under the connector prefix (`422`) and, with governed creation on, at a path no anchor governs (`403`). A `null` for `$owner`, any other `$` attribute or an `@` name is `400`, and so is any other value that is not a string. A `PUT` with several members is one write, so a refused member refuses all of it. A property that has been removed reads as one that was never set, so an `inherit` property falls back to its ancestor’s value or its default. Writing an empty string is not a removal, because the property stays present. Removing the last property leaves the object in place (`GET` answers `{}`) and fires `onChange`; `DELETE` deletes it. 5. Delete the object. 
   ```sh
   curl -X DELETE localhost:8080/v1/notes/n1       # 204
   curl -i localhost:8080/v1/notes/n1              # 404
   ```
 `GET` returns only the properties the caller may read. `POST` on an object path answers `405` unless a `create` accessor verb is deployed there, and `PATCH` answers `405` unless a `set` or `update` accessor is. Without accessors the data plane takes `GET`, `PUT` and `DELETE`; to invoke a verb, `POST` to a path with a `:verb` suffix. An object holds at most 1024 properties and 1 MiB of data; going past either is `413`. A string, property name or path holding U+0000 is `400 invalid-input` on every store. [HTTP API](/docs/reference/http-api/#data-plane-the-noun) lists the rest of the rules. ## Conditional requests Reads carry an `ETag`. Use it for caching and for safe updates. 
```sh
curl -X PUT -H 'Content-Type: application/json' --data '{"title":"One"}' localhost:8080/v1/notes/n2
ETAG=$(curl -si localhost:8080/v1/notes/n2 | grep -i '^etag' | cut -d' ' -f2 | tr -d '\r')


curl -si -H "If-None-Match: $ETAG" localhost:8080/v1/notes/n2 | head -1        # HTTP/1.1 304 Not Modified
curl -si -X PUT -H 'Content-Type: application/json' -H 'If-Match: "stale"' \
  --data '{"title":"X"}' localhost:8080/v1/notes/n2 | head -1                   # HTTP/1.1 412 Precondition Failed
curl -si -X PUT -H 'Content-Type: application/json' -H "If-Match: $ETAG" \
  --data '{"title":"Updated"}' localhost:8080/v1/notes/n2 | head -1             # HTTP/1.1 204 No Content
curl -si -X PUT -H 'Content-Type: application/json' -H "If-Match: $ETAG" \
  --data '{"title":"Again"}' localhost:8080/v1/notes/n2 | head -1               # HTTP/1.1 412 Precondition Failed
```
 The last request fails because the successful write changed the tag. `DELETE` honours `If-Match` as well. A client that loses a race gets `412`, rereads and retries. ## Binary values A `PUT` with a `Content-Type` that is not JSON stores the body as a binary value. A `GET` serves the same bytes back with the stored type. 
```sh
curl -X PUT -H 'Content-Type: image/png' --data-binary @avatar.png localhost:8080/v1/users/alice/avatar
curl -i localhost:8080/v1/users/alice/avatar
```
 
```http
HTTP/1.1 200 OK
content-type: image/png
cache-control: private, max-age=3600
vary: authorization
x-content-type-options: nosniff
content-security-policy: default-src 'none'; style-src 'unsafe-inline'; img-src http: https:; base-uri 'none'; form-action 'none'
etag: "10410a90…"
```
 The body is byte-identical to what you stored. The `ETag` is a hash of the content, so `If-None-Match` gives `304`. Script-capable types (`text/html`, `image/svg+xml`, any `+xml`) are served as downloads instead of rendering inline. A value over `RESTACT_MAX_BLOB_BYTES` (8 MiB by default) is `413`; see [Configuration](/docs/reference/configuration/). An object holds either a binary value or JSON properties; a JSON content type (`application/json`, `application/*+json`) is the property form. A compiled Rust verb can read the bytes with `restact_sdk::object::get_bytes()`, which suits read-time transforms such as resizing an image. A JavaScript or Python verb reads one with `obj.getBytes()` or `obj.get_bytes()`, up to 6 MiB (a quarter of the script heap); a larger value raises `memory-limit`, so read it from a compiled verb. ## Inherited values A property declared with `inherit` takes its value from the nearest ancestor object that stores it when the object itself does not. A caller receives the inherited value only if they could read that property on the ancestor directly: the ancestor’s `$policy.read` and the property’s read ACL there both admit them, and the declaration in effect on the child admits them too. The child’s declaration is evaluated against the ancestor that holds the value: that ancestor’s owner, and the members the caller can read there, not the child’s owner. An ancestor that refuses is skipped and the walk continues upward. If no ancestor admits the caller, the declared `default` applies, checked against the child’s own declaration. The inherited value is never copied into the child; it exists only in what the caller reads. ## List objects and page through them `GET /v1` lists the objects with stored data that the caller may read, ordered by path in byte order. Seed five notes, then list the first two that are notes: 
```sh
for i in 1 2 3 4 5; do
  curl -s -X PUT -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
    --data "{\"title\":\"T$i\",\"kind\":\"$([ $i -le 3 ] && echo note || echo todo)\"}" \
    localhost:8080/v1/lib/n$i
done
curl 'localhost:8080/v1?pageSize=2&filter=kind=note&read_mask=title,kind'
```
 
```json
{"nextPageToken":"…","objects":[{"path":"lib/n1","properties":2,"values":{"kind":"note","title":"T1"}},{"path":"lib/n2","properties":2,"values":{"kind":"note","title":"T2"}}]}
```
 * `pageSize` sets the page length. * `pageToken` takes the `nextPageToken` from the previous response and returns the following page. The token is opaque and sealed by the server: pass it back unchanged, on the same `filter` and as the same caller. A token the server did not issue for that listing answers `400 invalid-input`; an empty `pageToken` is the first page. When a response has no `nextPageToken`, you have the last page. * `filter=name=value` keeps objects whose readable property `name` equals `value`. * `read_mask=a,b` adds a `values` map with just those properties to each entry. Without it, an entry carries only its `path` and a `properties` count. * `prefix=lib/` keeps only objects whose path begins with `lib/`. It must be empty or end in `/`, and it never lists `lib` itself. A prefix that does not end in `/`, or that no path can start with (a leading `/`, `a//`, a segment starting with `@`, a NUL), answers `400 invalid-input`. Encode a literal `+` as `%2B`. Pass the same `prefix` with every `pageToken`: the token is bound to it. Send `prefix` only when discovery lists `object-listing-prefix`; an older host may ignore it. 
```sh
curl 'localhost:8080/v1?pageSize=2&filter=kind=note&pageToken=<nextPageToken>'
```
 
```json
{"objects":[{"path":"lib/n3","properties":2}]}
```
 To list one subtree over HTTP, add `prefix=` (feature `object-listing-prefix`). From inside a verb, use `obj.query`, below. ## Let a verb act on its object An entity verb such as `pages/{id}:view` is bound to the object in its URL, so `pages/home:view` acts on `pages/home`. The `object` capability is how it reads and writes that data.
