Everything is checked against the caller’s permissions: a verb never reads or writes what its caller could not. | Operation | JavaScript | Python | Rust (`restact_sdk::object`) | | ------------------------------------ | -------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------ | | Read a property of this object | `obj.get(n)` | `obj.get(n)` | `get(n)` | | Write a property of this object | `obj.set(n, v)` | `obj.set(n, v)` | `set(n, v)` | | Read a property of another object | `obj.getAt(p, n)` | `obj.get_at(p, n)` | `get_at(p, n)` | | Write a property of another object | `obj.setAt(p, n, v)` | `obj.set_at(p, n, v)` | `set_at(p, n, v)` | | Remove a property of this object | `obj.remove(n)` | `obj.remove(n)` | `remove(n)` | | Remove a property of another object | `obj.removeAt(p, n)` | `obj.remove_at(p, n)` | `remove_at(p, n)` | | Write only if the value is unchanged | `obj.setAtIf(p, n, v, expected)` | `obj.set_at_if(p, n, v, expected)` | `set_at_if(p, n, v, expected)` | | List property, child and verb names | `obj.shape()`, `obj.shapeAt(p)` | `obj.shape()`, `obj.shape_at(p)` | `shape()`, `shape_at(p)` | | Page of objects under a prefix | `obj.query(opts)` | `obj.query(prefix=…, order_by=…, limit=…, cursor=…)` | `query(…)` | | The object’s own path | `obj.path()` | `obj.path()` | `path()` | Reads of an absent or unreadable property return `undefined` (JavaScript), `None` (Python, Rust) and are indistinguishable from each other. A write the caller may not make throws `forbidden`. Only actions may write: a `kind=function` verb that calls a write is refused with `403` and `forbidden-side-effect`. ### Find the object’s own path `obj.path()` returns the concrete path the verb was invoked on, never the route pattern it was deployed at. A verb deployed at `ws/{id}/pages/{id}:render` and called on `ws/w1/pages/p3` reads `ws/w1/pages/p3`. This lets a verb address itself without the caller passing the path in: 
```js
function invoke(input) {
  const me = obj.path();
  return { path: me, title: obj.getAt(me, "title") };
}
```
 It returns `null` (`None` in Rust) when the verb is not bound to an object, as with an unbound `:verb` route, and the host advertises the feature as `object-self-path`. Call it again inside a composed verb and it reports that verb’s own object, not the caller’s. ### Query a subtree `obj.query` returns one page of objects under a path prefix, each with its readable properties. It is a single call into the host, so it replaces one `getAt` per column per object, and it is a pure read: a `kind=function` verb may use it. The host advertises it as `verb-object-query`. Here is a verb that lists a project’s open tasks, highest priority first. Seed some tasks: 
```sh
for i in 1 2 3 4 5 6 7; do
  curl -s -X PUT -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
    --data "{\"title\":\"Task $i\",\"priority\":\"0$((i%3+1))\",\"status\":\"$([ $((i%2)) = 0 ] && echo done || echo open)\"}" \
    localhost:8080/v1/proj/p1/tasks/t$i
done
```
 top.js 
```js
function invoke(input) {
  const opts = input ? JSON.parse(input) : {};
  const page = obj.query({
    prefix: obj.path() + "/tasks/",
    orderBy: "priority",
    descending: true,
    predicate: { key: "status", value: "open" },
    limit: opts.limit || 2,
    cursor: opts.cursor || null,
  });
  return {
    tasks: page.objects.map(r => ({ path: r.path, title: r.properties.title, priority: r.properties.priority })),
    next: page.cursor,
  };
}
```
 
```sh
curl -gX POST -H 'Content-Type: text/javascript' --data-binary @top.js \
  'localhost:8080/v1/modules?verb=proj/%7Bid%7D:openTasks&kind=function&lang=text/javascript'
curl -g 'localhost:8080/v1/proj/p1:openTasks'
```
 
```json
{"tasks":[{"path":"proj/p1/tasks/t5","title":"Task 5","priority":"03"},{"path":"proj/p1/tasks/t7","title":"Task 7","priority":"02"}],"next":{…}}
```
 Pass `next` back unchanged as `cursor`; it is an opaque value sealed by the host. A `null` `next` means there are no more rows. A cursor the host did not issue for the same query and caller fails the invocation with `422 invalid-input`: 
```sh
NEXT=$(curl -sg 'localhost:8080/v1/proj/p1:openTasks' | jq -c .next)
curl -g -G --data-urlencode "input={\"limit\":2,\"cursor\":$NEXT}" \
  'localhost:8080/v1/proj/p1:openTasks'
```
 
```json
{"tasks":[{"path":"proj/p1/tasks/t1","title":"Task 1","priority":"02"},{"path":"proj/p1/tasks/t3","title":"Task 3","priority":"01"}],"next":null}
```
 The options, all optional: | Option | Meaning | | ------------ | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `prefix` | Only objects whose path starts with this string. Default: the whole store. | | `orderBy` | A property name to sort by. Default: path order. A property declared `shared`, or whose read ACL does not admit the caller on every object (an `owner` or `prop:` ACL, or one that excludes them), is ignored, as if no `orderBy` were given: the page is in path order and each row’s `orderValue` is its path. A `shared` declaration by any verb counts. | | `descending` | `true` to reverse the order. | | `predicate` | `{key, value}`: keep objects whose `key` property equals `value`. | | `limit` | Page size. The host clamps it to its per-call maximum. | | `cursor` | The `cursor` of the previous page. | A string value sorts as a string, so zero-pad numeric strings (`"02"`, not `"2"`) when you order by them; stored numbers order after strings, then booleans, arrays and objects. Each row has `path` and `properties` (a plain object of the readable properties). System fields such as `$policy` never appear in `properties`. ### Update safely under contention `setAtIf(path, name, value, expected)` writes only if the property still equals `expected`, and throws `precondition-failed` otherwise; pass `null` to mean “only if absent”. The [JavaScript guide](/docs/guides/javascript/#handle-errors) has a complete retry loop. The check and write are atomic on the target object, and the write commits at once. The host advertises it as `object-cas`. ### Remove a property `remove` and `remove_at` are authorized exactly as `set` and `set_at` of the same name, so a verb never removes what its caller could not. Removing a property that is not set succeeds and changes nothing. A `final` property that is set fails with `property-final`. `remove` on the bound object is held until the verb returns and dropped if it fails, and gives `not-found` when the bound object holds no data; `remove_at` commits at the call, as `set_at` does, and fails with `not-found` when no object is stored at the path, because a removal never creates one. Verbs cannot touch `$` attributes, so a verb cannot remove a policy. A caller who may not read the object gets `not-found` in place of `forbidden`. The host advertises the verb functions as `object-remove`. ## Make an invocation atomic By default, a verb’s writes to its own object are held until it returns and dropped if it fails, but each `setAt` to another object commits at the moment it is called. A verb that fails halfway therefore leaves its earlier `setAt` writes in place. Start the host with `RESTACT_CONSISTENCY=atomic-invocation` and the whole write set of one invocation, on the bound object and on other objects through `setAt` and `removeAt`, commits together when the verb returns, and is discarded if it fails. This transfer verb writes the source account first and then checks for enough funds: transfer.js 
```js
function invoke(input) {
  const t = JSON.parse(input);
  const from = obj.getAt(t.from, "balance");
  const to = obj.getAt(t.to, "balance");
  obj.setAt(t.from, "balance", from - t.amount);
  if (from - t.amount < 0) throw new Error("insufficient funds");   // after the first write
  obj.setAt(t.to, "balance", to + t.amount);
  return { from: from - t.amount, to: to + t.amount };
}
```
 
```sh
curl -X PUT -H 'Content-Type: application/json' --data '{"balance":100}' localhost:8080/v1/acct/a
curl -X PUT -H 'Content-Type: application/json' --data '{"balance":50}'  localhost:8080/v1/acct/b
curl -gX POST -H 'Content-Type: text/javascript' --data-binary @transfer.js \
  'localhost:8080/v1/modules?verb=bank:transfer&kind=action&lang=text/javascript'
curl -gX POST --data '{"from":"acct/a","to":"acct/b","amount":30}' localhost:8080/v1/bank:transfer
curl -gX POST --data '{"from":"acct/a","to":"acct/b","amount":500}' localhost:8080/v1/bank:transfer
curl localhost:8080/v1/acct/a
```
 With `atomic-invocation`: 
```json
{"from":70,"to":80}
{"detail":"Error: insufficient funds\n    at invoke (eval_script:7:38)\n","restact_code":"script-error",…}
{"balance":70}
```
 The failed transfer left account `a` at `70`. Under the default level, the same request leaves it at `-430`, because the first `setAt` had already committed. Two things change at this level. `setAtIf` is refused with `unsupported-consistency` (`501`), because a compare-and-swap commits immediately and cannot join a buffered set, and the host stops advertising `object-cas`. Check `consistency` in the [discovery document](/docs/concepts/discovery/) to see which level a host runs. ## Next steps * [Compose verbs](/docs/guides/composition/): one verb calling another with the caller’s authority. * [Lifecycle hooks](/docs/guides/lifecycle-hooks/): run a verb when an object is created, changed or deleted. * [HTTP API](/docs/reference/http-api/#data-plane-the-noun): every data-plane rule. * [Authoring reference](/docs/guides/authoring-verbs/): the object capability from compiled verbs.

# Write verbs in Python

> Upload a Python file and call it over HTTP. The host does not compile it: it stores the source and runs it with monty, a Python interpreter written in Rust, inside the same sandbox and limits as…

Upload a Python file and call it over HTTP. The host does not compile it: it stores the source and runs it with monty, a Python interpreter written in Rust, inside the same sandbox and limits as every other verb. monty runs a subset of Python. This guide shows the working path first, then lists exactly what the subset includes and leaves out. For the cross-language reference, see the [authoring reference](/docs/guides/authoring-verbs/). ## Prerequisites * A running restactd with the Python tier enabled. [Install](/docs/start/install/) shows how to build the runner; a host without it answers `415` to a Python upload. * `curl`. Commands use `localhost:8080`, and `-g` keeps curl from treating `{id}` as a glob. Check that the host accepts Python: 
```sh
curl -s localhost:8080/.well-known/restact | grep -o '"acceptedSourceLanguages":\[[^]]*\]'
```
 
```text
"acceptedSourceLanguages":["application/wasm","text/javascript","text/x-python"]
```
 ## A function you can call with GET 1. Write the verb. It defines `invoke(input)`, where `input` is the request body or `?input=` value as a string. hello.py 
   ```python
   def invoke(input):
       name = input.strip() or "world"
       log("greeting " + name)
       return {"greeting": f"Hello, {name}!", "length": len(name)}
   ```
 2. Deploy it as a function. `lang=text/x-python` names the language. 
   ```sh
   curl -gX POST -H 'Content-Type: text/x-python' --data-binary @hello.py \
     'localhost:8080/v1/modules?verb=py:hello&kind=function&lang=text/x-python'
   ```
 The `201` body lists the imports the verb ended up with and its content hash. 3. Call it. 
   ```sh
   curl -g 'localhost:8080/v1/py:hello?input=Ada'
   ```
 
   ```json
   {"greeting":"Hello, Ada!","length":3}
   ```
 What just happened: the return value is turned into a response body. A `str` passes through unchanged, `bytes` pass through as bytes, `None` is an empty body, and a `dict`, `list`, number or `bool` is serialized as JSON. A tuple serializes as `{"$tuple":[...]}`, so return a list when a client will read it. Replace a verb’s source with `PUT /v1/py:hello/code` and the same `Content-Type`; the response is `204`. `GET` on the same path with `Accept: text/x-python` returns the stored source. A script that does not parse is refused at upload. Unsupported syntax is caught there too, with the interpreter’s own message: 
```sh
curl -gX POST -H 'Content-Type: text/x-python' --data-binary 'def invoke(i):
    yield i' 'localhost:8080/v1/modules?verb=py:bad&kind=function&lang=text/x-python'
```
 
```json
{"detail":"Traceback (most recent call last):\n  File \"verb\", line 2, in <module>\n    yield i\n    ~~~~~~~\nNotImplementedError: The monty syntax parser does not yet support yield expressions","restact_code":"invalid-source","restact_verb":":bad","status":422,"title":"Unprocessable Entity","type":"about:blank"}
```
 ## What the Python subset supports Input and output are text. The interpreter version string is `3.14.0 (Monty)`. These work: * `def`, `lambda`, closures, recursion (capped at 1000 frames), default arguments, `*args` and unpacking such as `a, *b = [1, 2, 3]`. * `if`, `for`, `while`, `try`/`except`/`raise`, `assert`. * List, dict and set comprehensions, slicing (`s[::-1]`), f-strings including format specs (`f"{x:.2f}"`). * `class` definitions, `%` string formatting and `str.format`. * `eval()` and `exec()`, described below. * `str`, `int` (arbitrary size), `float`, `bool`, `list`, `dict`, `set`, `tuple`, `bytes`, and builtins such as `len`, `sorted`, `sum`, `range`, `isinstance` and `type`. * `async def` helpers driven from a plain `invoke` with `asyncio.run(...)`, such as `return asyncio.run(double(21))`. * `match` statements and dataclass `field`. * `import json`, `re`, `math`, `sys`, `typing`, `asyncio`, `collections`, `itertools`, `functools`, `datetime` (values, not the clock), `random` (after `random.seed(n)`), `hashlib` (digests; not `file_digest` or `scrypt`), `csv`, `bisect`, `tomllib`, `decimal` and the string functions of `os.path`. These do not work, and fail at upload (`422`), fail when the line runs, or return an unusable value: * Generators (`yield`) and other unsupported statements (`del`, class inheritance, `async for`/`async with`): refused at upload. * The clock and sleep (`time.time()`, `time.sleep()`, `datetime.datetime.now()`, `datetime.date.today()`), `random` without an explicit `random.seed(...)`, `open()`, directory listing (`os.listdir`, `os.scandir`, `os.walk`, `Path.glob`, `Path.rglob`, `Path.iterdir`), `os.path` existence and stat checks, path resolution (`os.path.realpath`), and environment reads fail with `unsupported operation (os/filesystem)`. A verb has no clock or entropy of its own. * `socket`, `subprocess` and other modules not listed above raise `ModuleNotFoundError`, which a script can catch with `except ImportError`. * `async def invoke`: the host calls `invoke` directly, so it must be a plain `def`. A coroutine comes back as `{"$repr":"<coroutine object invoke>"}` instead of a result. `print()` does not reach the response; use `log()`. `eval(source)` and `exec(source, namespace)` run inside the same sandbox, with the script’s own names and the same limits, and add no reach the script does not already have. Never pass caller input to them. The code would run with the verb’s authority, including `runs_as=owner`, so a caller who controls the string controls what the verb does. ## An action that uses object data An entity verb is bound to an object. Inside the script, `obj` reads and writes that object’s data. visit.py 
```python
def invoke(input):
    n = (obj.get("visits") or 0) + 1
    obj.set("visits", n)
    log("visit " + str(n))
    return {"visits": n, "properties": obj.shape().properties}
```
 
```sh
curl -gX POST -H 'Content-Type: text/x-python' --data-binary @visit.py \
  'localhost:8080/v1/modules?verb=guests/%7Bid%7D:visit&kind=action&lang=text/x-python'
curl -gX POST localhost:8080/v1/guests/g1:visit
curl -gX POST localhost:8080/v1/guests/g1:visit
curl localhost:8080/v1/guests/g1
```
 
```json
{"visits":1,"properties":[]}
{"visits":2,"properties":["visits"]}
{"visits":2}
```
 Property values are typed. `obj.get` returns `None` when the property is absent or the caller may not read it. A `kind=function` verb may read but not write; a write is refused with `403` and `forbidden-side-effect`. **Typed values.** A property holds a string or a JSON value, and the runner maps them at the boundary: | Python | Stored as | Read back as | | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------- | | `str` | `text` | the same `str` (`"5"` stays a `str`) | | `int`, `float`, `bool`, `list`, `dict` | `json` | the same type (an integral float is stored as an int) | | `None` | refused: `invalid-input` (`422`); use `obj.remove` | n/a | | `tuple`, `set`, `frozenset`, `bytes`, a dict with a non-`str` key, `nan`/`inf`, dates, paths, exceptions, functions, class instances (at any depth; `None` is allowed only inside a list or dict) | refused: `invalid-input` (`422`) before the call reaches the host | n/a | | an `int` outside ±(2^53−1) (incl. big ints) | refused by the runner: `invalid-input` before the call reaches the host | n/a | | a `float` of magnitude 2^53 or more, or nesting deeper than 64 levels | refused by the host: `invalid-input` | n/a | For `obj.set_at_if`, `expected` maps the same way, and `None` or leaving it out means “absent”. Query rows carry the same typed `.properties` and `.order_value`. | Call | Does | | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `obj.get(name)`, `obj.set(name, value)` | Read or write a property of the bound object. | | `obj.get_at(path, name)`, `obj.set_at(path, name, value)` | Read or write a property of another object, checked against the caller. | | `obj.remove(name)`, `obj.remove_at(path, name)` | Remove a property of the bound object or of another object, checked against the caller like a write. Removing an unset property succeeds. `remove` raises `not-found` when the bound object holds no data, and `remove_at` raises `not-found` when no object is stored at `path`. | | `obj.shape()`, `obj.shape_at(path)` | An object with `.properties`, `.children` and `.verbs` lists. | | `obj.get_bytes()`, `obj.get_bytes_at(path)` | The binary value of the bound or another object as `(content_type, data)`, or `None` when there is none. | | `obj.path()` | The concrete path the verb was invoked on, for example `pages/home`, or `None` for an unbound verb. | | `obj.set_at_if(path, name, value, expected=None)` | Write only if the current value equals `expected` (`None` means absent). Raises an error carrying `precondition-failed` otherwise. | | `obj.query(prefix=None, order_by=None, descending=False, predicate=None, limit=None, cursor=None)` | A page of objects under a path prefix. See below. | `get_bytes` returns a `BinaryValue` named tuple, so `content_type, data = obj.get_bytes()` unpacks. A value over 6 MiB (a quarter of the script heap) raises `RuntimeError` with the code `memory-limit`; read it from a [Rust verb](/docs/guides/rust/). Return `bytes` to send them back unchanged. `obj.query` returns a page with `.objects` and `.cursor`. Each row has `.path`, `.properties` (a dict of the properties the caller may read) and `.order_value`. `predicate` is a dict `{"key": ..., "value": ...}`. `.cursor` is `None` once the result is exhausted; otherwise pass it straight back as `cursor=` to read the next page: 
```python
def invoke(input):
    paths = []
    cursor = None
    while True:
        page = obj.query(prefix=obj.path() + "/", order_by="title", limit=200, cursor=cursor)
        for row in page.objects:
            paths.append(row.path)
        cursor = page.cursor
        if cursor is None:
            return paths
```
 The host clamps `limit` to a per-call maximum. See [Store and query object data](/docs/guides/object-data/) for ordering, predicates and the cursor. ## Ask a model `llm.complete(request)` makes one text completion through a connector. The verb needs the `llm` grant and cannot be a `function`. The request is one dict: 
```python
def invoke(input):
    r = llm.complete({
        "connector": "ws/1/connectors/anthropic",
        "system": "Answer in one sentence.",
        "messages": [{"role": "user", "content": input}],
        "max_output_tokens": 200,
    })
    return {"text": r.text, "tokens": r.usage.output_tokens}
```
 `connector` and `messages` (a non-empty list of `role`/`content` dicts, where `role` is `"user"` or `"assistant"` and the host refuses any other) are required; `system`, `model`, `max_output_tokens` and `temperature` are optional, and `None` counts as left out. The result has `.text`, `.stop_reason`, `.model` and `.usage` (`input_tokens`, `output_tokens`, `cache_read_tokens`, `estimated_usd_micros`, `meter_tier`). A bad field raises `RuntimeError` with the code `invalid-input` and the field named. Treat `.text` as untrusted input. ## Read the caller 
```python
def invoke(input):
    return {
        "subject": caller.subject(),
        "anonymous": caller.is_anonymous(),
        "roles": caller.roles(),
        "scopes": caller.scopes(),
        "groups": caller.groups(),
        "dept": caller.claim("dept"),
        "actor": caller.actor(),
        "invoker": caller.invoker(),
    }
```
 `caller.actor()` is the delegated client’s id when the request runs under a delegated token, else `None`. `caller.invoker()` is the principal on whose behalf the whole call tree runs; it is `None` unless the verb was deployed with `?invoker=1`. On a host with no authentication, a call returns `{"subject":null,"anonymous":true,"roles":[],"scopes":[],"groups":[],"dept":null,"actor":null,"invoker":null}`. With a bearer token, the subject and claims come from the token. [Add authentication](/docs/guides/authentication/) shows how to set that up, and [Identity and authority](/docs/concepts/identity-and-authority/) explains why the verb always runs as the caller. ## Call another verb `compose(verb, input)` runs another verb and returns its output as a string. List every verb you call in `calls=` at deploy time: 
```python
def invoke(input):
    shouted = compose("util:shout", "hello " + input)
    return shouted + "!"
```
 
```sh
curl -gX POST -H 'Content-Type: text/x-python' --data-binary @loud.py \
  'localhost:8080/v1/modules?verb=py:loud&kind=action&lang=text/x-python&calls=util:shout'
curl -gX POST --data-binary ada localhost:8080/v1/py:loud
```
 
```text
HELLO ADA!
```
 This assumes a `util:shout` verb exists, such as the one in [Write verbs in JavaScript](/docs/guides/javascript/#call-another-verb). Verbs can call across languages. [Compose verbs](/docs/guides/composition/) covers budgets, errors and cycles. ## Handle errors An uncaught exception becomes a `422` with `restact_code: script-error`, and the traceback goes into `detail`. Callers see that text, so keep secrets out of messages. A failure in a host capability, such as a denied write or an undeclared `compose`, raises `RuntimeError`. The structured code is inside the message, between `\u0001` characters: 
```python
def host_code(e):
    m = str(e)
    return m.split("\u0001")[2] if m.startswith("\u0001restact-host-error\u0001") else None


def invoke(input):
    try:
        compose("util:shout", "x")      # not listed in calls=
    except RuntimeError as e:
        return {"code": host_code(e)}
    return "no error"
```
 
```json
{"code":"not-declared"}
```
 Rethrow what you do not handle: an uncaught host error reaches the caller with its original status (`403` for `forbidden`), while a replacement exception keeps the code only if its message carries the tag, and otherwise becomes a generic `script-error`. In a composed call, `verb-not-deployed`, and only that code, means nothing is deployed at the route; any other code, `not-found` included, is a refusal. See [Distinguishing host errors](/docs/guides/authoring-verbs/#distinguishing-host-errors-both-scripting-tiers). ## Limits Each invocation runs under per-request limits that discovery reports under `defaultCapabilities`: 200 million compute units, 2 seconds and 32 MiB by default. The runner caps its own heap at 24 MiB. * A loop that never ends, or a very large comprehension, stops with `429` and `restact_code: cpu-budget`. * Unbounded recursion stops with a Python `RecursionError` traceback in a `script-error`. * Source is parsed on every invocation, out of the run fuel, so keep hot scripts small and give a large source a higher `?fuel=` (see [Capabilities and budgets](/docs/concepts/capabilities-and-budgets/#metering)). ## Editor completion and type checks `obj`, `caller`, `llm`, `log`, `compose` and `publish` are installed by the runner, so an editor reports them as undefined. [`sdk/python/restact.pyi`](https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/blob/main/sdk/python/restact.pyi) and [`sdk/python/__builtins__.pyi`](https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/blob/main/sdk/python/__builtins__.pyi) declare them for Pyright and Pylance; mypy needs a `TYPE_CHECKING` import. [`sdk/README.md`](https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/blob/main/sdk/README.md#editor-types-for-javascript-and-python-verbs) has the setup. The stubs list only what the Python runner provides. `publish` is described in [Write verbs: Python](/docs/guides/authoring-verbs/#python-server-side-no-toolchain). ## Next steps * [Store and query object data](/docs/guides/object-data/): the data plane and what verbs can do with it. * [Compose verbs](/docs/guides/composition/): shared budgets and run-as-caller through a call chain. * [Language tiers](/docs/concepts/language-tiers/): how the interpreted tiers differ from compiled verbs. * [Authoring reference](/docs/guides/authoring-verbs/): every global and the compiled-verb path.

# Write verbs in Rust

> Build a verb as a WebAssembly component with the restact-sdk crate, upload the .wasm file, and call it over HTTP. Compiled verbs run without an interpreter, accept and return binary payloads, and…

Build a verb as a WebAssembly component with the `restact-sdk` crate, upload the `.wasm` file, and call it over HTTP. Compiled verbs run without an interpreter, accept and return binary payloads, and can read binary object values. This guide builds two verbs: a function that turns text into a URL slug, and an action that counts hits on an object. It then shows composition. ## Prerequisites * A checkout of the restactd repository: the SDK is not published to crates.io, so a verb depends on it by path. * Rust 1.88 or newer with the `wasm32-wasip2` target: `rustup target add wasm32-wasip2`. The repository’s [Install](/docs/start/install/) page covers the pinned toolchain. * A running restactd (`localhost:8080` below). ## A function: slugify text 1. Create a library crate. The verb is a `cdylib`, and the SDK is the only dependency. Cargo.toml 
   ```toml
   [package]
   name = "slug"
   version = "0.1.0"
   edition = "2021"


   [dependencies]
   restact-sdk = { path = "/path/to/restactd/sdk/rust" }


   [lib]
   crate-type = ["cdylib"]


   [workspace]
   ```
 2. Implement the `Verb` trait and export it with one macro. src/lib.rs 
   ```rust
   use restact_sdk::{export_verb, log, Error, ErrorExt, LogLevel, Payload, Verb};


   struct Slug;


   impl Verb for Slug {
       fn invoke(input: Payload) -> Result<Payload, Error> {
           let text = String::from_utf8(input)
               .map_err(|_| Error::invalid_input("input is not UTF-8"))?;
           if text.trim().is_empty() {
               return Err(Error::invalid_input("send some text to slugify"));
           }
           log(LogLevel::Debug, "slugifying");
           let slug = text
               .to_lowercase()
               .split(|c: char| !c.is_alphanumeric())
               .filter(|w| !w.is_empty())
               .collect::<Vec<_>>()
               .join("-");
           Ok(slug.into_bytes())
       }
   }


   export_verb!(Slug);
   ```
 `Payload` is a `Vec<u8>`. The input and output are raw bytes, so a verb can handle any media type; this one treats them as UTF-8 text. 3. Build with plain cargo. The `wasm32-wasip2` target produces a component directly, so `cargo-component` is not needed. 
   ```sh
   cargo build --release --target wasm32-wasip2
   ```
 The component is `target/wasm32-wasip2/release/slug.wasm`. 4. Deploy it. A component upload uses `Content-Type: application/wasm`. 
   ```sh
   curl -gX POST -H 'Content-Type: application/wasm' \
     --data-binary @target/wasm32-wasip2/release/slug.wasm \
     'localhost:8080/v1/modules?verb=text:slug&kind=function'
   ```
 The `201` response lists the component’s imports, which are the capabilities it can reach. This one imports only `rest-act:plugin/host` (logging) beside the WASI base. 5. Call it. 
   ```sh
   curl -g -G --data-urlencode 'input=Hello, REST:ACT World!' localhost:8080/v1/text:slug
   ```
 
   ```text
   hello-rest-act-world
   ```
 An empty input returns the error the verb built, with the HTTP status the host maps it to: 
   ```sh
   curl -g 'localhost:8080/v1/text:slug?input=%20'
   ```
 
   ```json
   {"detail":"send some text to slugify","restact_code":"invalid-input","restact_verb":":slug","status":422,"title":"Unprocessable Entity","type":"about:blank"}
   ```
 What just happened: the SDK exported your `Verb` as the `rest-act:plugin` component interface. The host read the component’s imports at upload and granted exactly those capabilities, nothing more. `Error::not_found(...)` maps to `404`. Reserved codes keep their statuses (`forbidden` is `403`, `conflict` is `409`), and any other code you pass to `Error::new(code, message)` surfaces as `422`. ## Choose capabilities with Cargo features The SDK’s features decide which world, and so which host capabilities, the component imports. A verb that does not enable a feature cannot reach that capability at all. | Features | Capabilities the verb can reach | | ------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | none | `log` | | `compose` | `log`, `restact_sdk::compose` | | `object` | `log`, object data (`restact_sdk::object`), caller identity (`restact_sdk::caller`) | | `compose`, `object` | all of the above | | `event` | everything in `object`, plus `restact_sdk::publish`; implies `object` | Keep the verb crate a leaf in its dependency graph. Cargo unifies features across a build, so a dependency that turned on `compose` would widen your verb’s world. ## An action with object data Enable the `object` feature and bind the verb to an object route: 
```toml
[dependencies]
restact-sdk = { path = "/path/to/restactd/sdk/rust", features = ["object"] }
```
 
```rust
use restact_sdk::object::PropertyValue;
use restact_sdk::{caller, export_verb, object, Error, ErrorExt, Payload, Verb};


struct Hits;


impl Verb for Hits {
    fn invoke(_input: Payload) -> Result<Payload, Error> {
        let who = caller::subject().unwrap_or_else(|| "anonymous".to_string());
        let hits: u64 = match object::get("hits") {
            Some(PropertyValue::Text(n) | PropertyValue::Json(n)) => n.parse().unwrap_or(0),
            _ => 0,
        } + 1;
        object::set("hits", PropertyValue::Json(hits.to_string()))?;
        object::set("last", PropertyValue::Text(who.clone()))?;
        let path = object::path()
            .ok_or_else(|| Error::not_found("verb is not bound to an object"))?;
        Ok(format!("{path}: {hits} hits, last by {who}").into_bytes())
    }
}


export_verb!(Hits);
```
 
```sh
curl -gX POST -H 'Content-Type: application/wasm' \
  --data-binary @target/wasm32-wasip2/release/hits.wasm \
  'localhost:8080/v1/modules?verb=pages/%7Bid%7D:hit&kind=action'
curl -gX POST localhost:8080/v1/pages/about:hit
curl -gX POST localhost:8080/v1/pages/about:hit
curl localhost:8080/v1/pages/about
```
 
```text
pages/about: 1 hits, last by anonymous
pages/about: 2 hits, last by anonymous
{"hits":2,"last":"anonymous"}
```
 With authentication configured, `last` holds the caller’s subject. The `object` module also has `get_at`, `set_at`, `set_at_if`, `remove`, `remove_at`, `shape`, `shape_at`, `query` and `path`, and `get_bytes` for binary values; see [Store and query object data](/docs/guides/object-data/). Reads and writes are checked against the caller’s permissions, and `object::set` fails with `forbidden` where the caller could not write. ## Compose another verb With the `compose` feature, `restact_sdk::compose(route, input)` runs another verb. The repository ships a pair you can deploy as-is: `examples/word-count-sdk` (a function) and `examples/summarize-sdk` (an action that calls it). 
```rust
use restact_sdk::{compose, export_verb, log, Error, ErrorExt, LogLevel, Payload, Verb};


const COUNT: &str = "wcsdk:count";


struct Summarize;


impl Verb for Summarize {
    fn invoke(input: Payload) -> Result<Payload, Error> {
        log(LogLevel::Info, "summarize-sdk composing wcsdk:count");
        let counted = compose(COUNT, &input)?;
        let n = String::from_utf8(counted)
            .map_err(|_| Error::new("compose-bad-output", "callee output not UTF-8"))?;
        Ok(format!("words={n}").into_bytes())
    }
}


export_verb!(Summarize);
```
 
```sh
cd examples/word-count-sdk && cargo build --release --target wasm32-wasip2 && cd ../..
cd examples/summarize-sdk  && cargo build --release --target wasm32-wasip2 && cd ../..


curl -gX POST -H 'Content-Type: application/wasm' \
  --data-binary @examples/word-count-sdk/target/wasm32-wasip2/release/word_count_sdk.wasm \
  'localhost:8080/v1/modules?verb=wcsdk:count&kind=function&exec=public'
curl -gX POST -H 'Content-Type: application/wasm' \
  --data-binary @examples/summarize-sdk/target/wasm32-wasip2/release/summarize_sdk.wasm \
  'localhost:8080/v1/modules?verb=wcsdk:summarize&kind=action&calls=wcsdk:count'


curl -gX POST --data-binary 'one two three four' localhost:8080/v1/wcsdk:summarize
```
 
```text
words=4
```
 The `calls=` list is the declared call graph; the host refuses any edge that is not on it. See [Compose verbs](/docs/guides/composition/). ## Other ways to produce a component * `cargo-component`: an alternative, with a generated `bindings` module instead of the SDK. [`examples/word-count`](https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/blob/main/examples/word-count/src/lib.rs) and [`examples/summarize`](https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/blob/main/examples/summarize/src/lib.rs) use it, and `scripts/build-examples.sh` builds all the examples. * Any other language whose toolchain can emit a WebAssembly component for one of the `rest-act:plugin@0.1.0` worlds in [`wit/`](https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/tree/main/wit) can upload with `Content-Type: application/wasm`. The host checks the component’s imports against the world, so it accepts any component that stays inside it. The repository ships and tests the Rust path; other toolchains are not covered here. * No toolchain at all: [JavaScript](/docs/guides/javascript/) and [Python](/docs/guides/python/) verbs. ## Next steps * [Store and query object data](/docs/guides/object-data/): everything the `object` module can do. * [Compose verbs](/docs/guides/composition/): shared budgets and run-as-caller. * [Capabilities and budgets](/docs/concepts/capabilities-and-budgets/): why a verb can only reach what it imports. * [Authoring reference](/docs/guides/authoring-verbs/): the worlds, in detail.

# Schedules and subscriptions

> A schedule runs an action verb at a fixed interval. A subscription runs an action verb whenever an object publishes an event. Both are registered over HTTP as resources, so you create, list and…

A schedule runs an action verb at a fixed interval. A subscription runs an action verb whenever an object publishes an event. Both are registered over HTTP as resources, so you create, list and delete them with the same requests you use for everything else. ## Prerequisites * A running restactd with the JavaScript tier (see [Install](/docs/start/install/)). The examples use `http://localhost:8080` with no authentication configured. * `curl`. ## 1. Deploy the action to run The target of a schedule or subscription must be an action. This one counts how many times it has run, on the object `heartbeat`. Save it as `tick.js`: 
```js
function invoke(input) {
  const n = Number(obj.get("count") ?? "0") + 1;
  obj.set("count", String(n));
  return String(n);
}
```
 
```sh
curl -gs -o /dev/null -w '%{http_code}\n' -X POST \
  -H 'Content-Type: text/javascript' --data-binary @tick.js \
  'http://localhost:8080/v1/modules?verb=heartbeat:tick&kind=action&lang=text/javascript'
```
 
```text
201
```
 ## 2. Register a schedule 
```sh
curl -i -X POST -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
  -d '{"target":"heartbeat:tick","intervalSeconds":10}' \
  http://localhost:8080/v1/schedules
```
 
```http
HTTP/1.1 201 Created
location: /v1/schedules/sch-0-AdKFRNknAN1IU5xghK6QBA


{"createdAt":1792341560,"id":"sch-0-AdKFRNknAN1IU5xghK6QBA","intervalSeconds":10,"kind":"schedule","lastFiredAt":1792341560,"owner":"anonymous","target":"heartbeat:tick"}
```
 Wait half a minute and read the counter: 
```sh
curl http://localhost:8080/v1/heartbeat
```
 
```json
{"count":"3"}
```
 `lastFiredAt` on the schedule record advances each time it runs. List schedules with `GET /v1/schedules` and remove one with `DELETE /v1/schedules/<id>`, which answers `204`. Once that answers `204` (or an operator reset returns), the schedule never fires again and a restart does not bring it back; a run already under way may finish. Registration is validated up front: | Request | Answer | | --------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------ | | `intervalSeconds` below the floor (10 seconds by default) | `400`, `intervalSeconds=1 is below the server floor 10` | | a `target` that is not a deployed verb | `400`, `target 'nope:x' does not resolve to a deployed verb` | | a `target` that is a function | `400`; only actions can be scheduled | ## 3. Register a subscription A subscription binds a topic, which is an object path, to an action. Any change to that object fires the action. 
```sh
curl -X PUT -H 'Content-Type: application/json' -d '{"color":"red"}' http://localhost:8080/v1/widget/7


curl -X POST -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
  -d '{"topic":"widget/7","target":"heartbeat:tick"}' \
  http://localhost:8080/v1/subscriptions
```
 
```json
{"createdAt":1792341641,"id":"sub-1-W-KbLeSraeTVuCvFrcuXLQ","kind":"subscription","lastFiredAt":1792341641,"owner":"anonymous","target":"heartbeat:tick","topic":"widget/7"}
```
 Change the widget and read the counter again: 
```sh
curl -X PUT -H 'Content-Type: application/json' -d '{"color":"teal"}' http://localhost:8080/v1/widget/7
curl http://localhost:8080/v1/heartbeat
```
 The count goes up by one. The topic must exist and be readable by the person registering. Registering on a missing or unreadable object answers `404`, with the same answer in both cases. ## What the action receives The action’s input is a JSON document describing why it ran. A schedule sends: 
```json
{"event":"schedule","firedAt":1792341663,"schedule":"sch-0-AdKFRNknAN1IU5xghK6QBA","target":"heartbeat:tick"}
```
 A subscription sends the event that fired it. `data` is the event body as a JSON string. The host adds a `$publisher` member to events it publishes itself, such as this lifecycle event: 
```json
{"data":"{\"$publisher\":{\"kind\":\"host\"},\"event\":\"onChange\",\"object\":\"widget/7\",\"subject\":null}","event":"onChange","seq":17,"subscription":"sub-1-W-KbLeSraeTVuCvFrcuXLQ","topic":"widget/7"}
```
 ## What just happened The host keeps a timer for each schedule and runs its target through the normal invoke path, including limits and quotas. A subscription is delivered from the same stream that feeds server-sent events: every data-plane write publishes an event on the object’s path, and the host runs each subscription registered for that topic. Both kinds of firing run as the person who registered them. They carry that person’s subject and nothing else, so a target must be executable by the registrant’s subject alone. Registering a target the registrant cannot execute is refused with `403`, because the registration would never fire. ## Restarts Registrations are stored with your data, so they survive a restart. A restarted host resumes each schedule from its recorded `lastFiredAt`. If one or more intervals were missed while the host was down, it fires once to catch up and then returns to the normal rhythm; it does not replay every missed interval. To check it, register the 10-second schedule above, stop the host for about 35 seconds, and start it again over the same data directory. The counter goes up by one after the restart, not by three. ## Who can see a registration Once you configure authentication ([Add authentication](/docs/guides/authentication/)), an anonymous caller cannot register or delete anything (`401`). Schedules and subscriptions are owned by the caller who made them: 
```sh
curl -H "Authorization: Bearer $BOB" http://localhost:8080/v1/schedules
```
 
```json
{"schedules":[]}
```
 Bob’s listing leaves out Alice’s schedules. A `GET` or `DELETE` by id on one of them answers `404` for Bob. ## Limits and the kill switches | Variable | Default | Effect | | ------------------------------------ | ------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `RESTACT_SCHEDULE_MIN_INTERVAL_SECS` | `10` | Smallest accepted `intervalSeconds`. | | `RESTACT_MAX_SCHEDULES` | `32` | Live schedules per tenant. | | `RESTACT_MAX_SUBSCRIPTIONS` | `32` | Live subscriptions per tenant. | | `RESTACT_SUB_MIN_GAP_MS` | `1000` | Minimum gap between two firings of one subscription. This bounds a loop where a subscribed action writes the object it subscribes to. | | `RESTACT_DISABLE_SCHEDULES` | off | `1` turns the schedule timer off. Registration answers `501` and discovery omits the `schedules` feature. | | `RESTACT_DISABLE_EVENTS` | off | `1`/`true`/`on`/`yes` turns eventing off. Subscription registration answers `501`, existing subscriptions stop firing, and discovery omits `verb-subscriptions`. It also stops the events the host publishes on each data-plane write and the server-sent event stream. Lifecycle hooks still fire. | `RESTACT_DISABLE_SCHEDULES` does not affect subscriptions, and `RESTACT_DISABLE_EVENTS` does not affect schedules. The full list is in [Configuration](/docs/reference/configuration/#eventing-lifecycle--object-features). ## Next steps * [Lifecycle hooks](/docs/guides/lifecycle-hooks/) run a verb on every create, change and delete without registering anything per object. * [Add authentication](/docs/guides/authentication/) explains the identities that own registrations.

# Run with Docker

> The repository's Dockerfile builds restactd, the example verbs and both scripting-tier runners into a small non-root image. This page covers building it, running it, persisting data, and a…

The repository’s `Dockerfile` builds restactd, the example verbs and both scripting-tier runners into a small non-root image. This page covers building it, running it, persisting data, and a single-node deployment with authentication and TLS. Releases publish images to `ghcr.io/stackumi/restactd` (see [Released images](#released-images)). You can also build the image from a checkout of the repository. ## Build the image 
```sh
git clone https://github.com/stackumi/restactd
cd restactd
make docker-build IMAGE=restactd   # tags restactd:dev, with the JavaScript and Python tiers
```
 The first build compiles the host, the QuickJS runner (it downloads a WASI SDK and compiles QuickJS from C) and the Python runner, so expect a long build and a few GB of cache. Later builds reuse the layers. | Target | Result | | ------------------------ | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `make docker-build` | Host, example verbs, JavaScript tier and Python tier. | | `make docker-build-core` | Host and example verbs only (`WITH_JS=false`, `WITH_PY=false`). The image has no runner, so a JavaScript or Python upload (`lang=text/javascript`, `lang=text/x-python`) answers `415`. | | `make docker-build-pg` | Adds the PostgreSQL backend (`WITH_PG=true`). Needed for [active-active clusters](/docs/operate/storage/#postgresql-active-active). A released `-full` image already has it. | The Dockerfile also takes `--build-arg WITH_OPENBAO=true` to compile the OpenBao secrets backend. `make help` lists every target. `IMAGE` and `TAG` set the image name and tag (`TAG` defaults to `dev`). This page uses `IMAGE=restactd`; pass the same `IMAGE` to the other targets, or run `docker build -t restactd:dev .` yourself. ## Released images Releases publish two images to `ghcr.io/stackumi/restactd`, built from the release binaries rather than from source. | Flavour | Tags | Contents | | ------- | -------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | slim | `X.Y.Z`, `X.Y`, `latest` | Without PostgreSQL. It refuses to start with `RESTACT_DATABASE_URL` set and names the `-full` image. | | full | `X.Y.Z-full`, `X.Y-full`, `full` | Adds the PostgreSQL, OpenBao and OpenTelemetry features. | A release candidate gets only `X.Y.Z-rc.N` and `X.Y.Z-rc.N-full`. Both flavours run as uid 65532, keep data in `/data`, and install `restactd` at `/usr/local/bin/restactd` with the JavaScript and Python runners under `/usr/local/share/restactd/runners/`. They carry no `/app` directory, site or docs content. Pin the image you deploy by digest, not by tag, so a re-pushed `latest` or `X.Y` cannot change what runs. The release notes list the digest of each image; the registry gives the same value: 
```sh
VERSION=0.1.0                            # the release you want
docker buildx imagetools inspect ghcr.io/stackumi/restactd:$VERSION
docker run --rm -p 127.0.0.1:8080:8080 -v restact-data:/data ghcr.io/stackumi/restactd@sha256:<digest>
```
 `gh attestation verify oci://ghcr.io/stackumi/restactd:$VERSION --repo stackumi/restactd --deny-self-hosted-runners` checks an image’s build provenance. ## Run it 
```sh
docker run -d --name restactd -p 8080:8080 -v restact-data:/data restactd:dev
curl -s localhost:8080/healthz
```
 `docker run --rm restactd:dev --version` prints the version and the commit the image was built from, then exits. An image built by `make docker-build` reports `dev` as the version; a release image reports its version number. `make docker-build` passes the commit; a hand-run `docker build` needs `--build-arg RESTACT_BUILD_COMMIT=$(git rev-parse --short=12 HEAD)`, since the build context has no `.git`. The image sets these variables, so it works without any: | Variable | Value in the image | Why | | ----------------------- | ------------------ | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `RESTACT_BIND` | `0.0.0.0:8080` | A container must listen on all interfaces to be reachable. | | `RESTACT_DATA_DIR` | `/data` | The [filesystem store](/docs/operate/storage/#filesystem) lives on the `/data` volume. | | `RESTACT_ALLOWED_HOSTS` | `localhost` | The host answers only to these `Host` names. See below. | The container runs as uid and gid 65532 on a distroless base with no shell. A fresh, empty named volume (`-v restact-data:/data`) inherits the right ownership, because Docker copies it only into an empty volume. A bind mount (`-v ./data:/data`), or a named volume that already holds files owned by another uid, must be owned by uid 65532, or the container refuses to start: an unwritable data directory is a fatal error, never a silent fallback to memory. Fix a bind mount with `sudo chown -R 65532:65532 ./data` and a named volume, which needs no shell in the image, with: 
```sh
docker run --rm -v restact-data:/data busybox:1.38.0@sha256:fd7dc98638c8e305f4dc34e979f1c0fdfdcaeb0fbf8fcff77ae834b6da3d7e6e chown -R 65532:65532 /data
```
 The image’s entrypoint is `restactd`, so its other commands run as `docker run --rm <image> <command>`. To snapshot a stopped host’s volume, or restore one, use a one-off container on the same volume while the server’s container is stopped; the data directory is locked while a server runs. The full procedure, including a running host, is in [Backup and restore](/docs/operate/storage/#backup-and-restore). 
```sh
docker run --rm -v restact-data:/data restactd:dev checkpoint
docker run --rm -v restact-data:/data restactd:dev restore --from /data/snapshots/<name>
```
 The image has a `HEALTHCHECK` that runs `restactd healthcheck` every 30 seconds. The command requests `/healthz` on the address in `RESTACT_BIND` (`--ready` requests `/readyz` instead), prints nothing on success and exits 1 with one line on stderr otherwise. The image carries no `curl`. Caution A host with no authentication configured is open: any caller can deploy, replace and delete verbs. `docker run` as shown is fine on your machine. Before you publish the port anywhere else, set `RESTACT_JWT_SECRET` (or OIDC) as in the example below. ### Allowed host names The host rejects any request whose `Host` header is not `localhost`, `127.0.0.1`, `[::1]` or listed in `RESTACT_ALLOWED_HOSTS`. This blocks DNS-rebinding attacks. Requests from another machine to `http://my-server:8080` fail with `403` until you list the name: 
```sh
docker run -d -p 8080:8080 -v restact-data:/data \
  -e RESTACT_ALLOWED_HOSTS=restact.example.com,restactd \
  restactd:dev
```
 List the public name your clients use and any in-network service name. `/healthz` and `/readyz` are exempt, so probes by IP keep working. See [`RESTACT_ALLOWED_HOSTS`](/docs/reference/configuration/#core). ### Configure with environment variables Pass any variable from the [configuration reference](/docs/reference/configuration/) with `-e` or `--env-file`. The built-in website and playground are off by default; `make docker-run` turns them on with `RESTACT_BUILTIN_SITE=1` (plus `RESTACT_EXAMPLES=1` for the example content), which you should leave off in production. The image itself seeds no example content. `make docker-run` runs the image in the foreground on port `PORT` (default `127.0.0.1:8080`) with the volume `DATA_VOL`; `make docker-stop` removes the container. ### Debug a running container The runtime image has no shell and no package manager, so `docker exec <container> sh` fails. The restactd commands still work, because the entrypoint binary is in the image: 
```sh
docker exec <container> restactd token mint --sub alice --ttl 1h
docker exec <container> restactd healthcheck --ready
```
 `token mint` reads `RESTACT_JWT_SECRET` from the container’s environment. To get a shell beside a running container, start a throwaway one that shares its process and network namespaces: 
```sh
docker run --rm -it --pid=container:<container> --network=container:<container> \
  busybox:1.38.0@sha256:fd7dc98638c8e305f4dc34e979f1c0fdfdcaeb0fbf8fcff77ae834b6da3d7e6e
```
 For a shell inside the image itself, `make docker-build-debug` builds a local-only variant, `$(IMAGE):dev-debug` (`restactd:dev-debug` with this page’s `IMAGE=restactd`), on the distroless `debug-nonroot` base, which adds busybox. It is never published; use it on a workstation only: 
```sh
docker run --rm -it --entrypoint sh restactd:dev-debug
```
 ### Extend the image To add files, such as a verb library or a certificate bundle, copy them in from another stage. There is no shell, so a `RUN` instruction (and `apt-get`) cannot run in the final stage: 
```dockerfile
FROM scratch AS extra
COPY ./my-files /my-files


FROM restactd:dev
COPY --from=extra --chown=65532:65532 /my-files /app/my-files
```
 Do any package installs or file preparation in an earlier stage and `COPY` the result across. ### Trust a private CA The image carries the public CA roots. For an OTLP collector (`OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT`) behind a corporate CA, mount a PEM bundle and point `SSL_CERT_FILE` at it. The file replaces the trust roots rather than adding to them, so it must hold every CA the exporter needs: 
```sh
docker run -d -v ./corp-ca.pem:/etc/ssl/corp-ca.pem:ro -e SSL_CERT_FILE=/etc/ssl/corp-ca.pem ... restactd:dev
```
 ## A single-node deployment This Compose file runs restactd with HS256 authentication, process isolation, per-tenant quotas and a persistent volume, with Caddy in front terminating TLS. restactd is reachable only on the Compose network. Save it as `compose.yaml` in the repository checkout, next to the `Dockerfile`: 
```yaml
services:
  restactd:
    build: .
    restart: unless-stopped
    environment:
      RESTACT_ALLOWED_HOSTS: restact.example.com
      RESTACT_JWT_SECRET: ${RESTACT_JWT_SECRET:?set RESTACT_JWT_SECRET in .env}
      RESTACT_ISOLATION: process
      RESTACT_QUOTA_FUEL: "1000000000"
      RESTACT_QUOTA_CALLS: "10000"
      RESTACT_QUOTA_WINDOW_SECS: "86400"
      RESTACT_ACCESS_LOG: "1"
    volumes:
      - restact-data:/data
    expose:
      - "8080"


  caddy:
    image: caddy:2
    restart: unless-stopped
    ports:
      - "80:80"
      - "443:443"
    volumes:
      - ./Caddyfile:/etc/caddy/Caddyfile:ro
      - caddy-data:/data
    depends_on:
      - restactd


volumes:
  restact-data:
  caddy-data:
```
 The `Caddyfile` next to it: 
```text
restact.example.com {
    reverse_proxy restactd:8080
}
```
 Create the secret and start the stack: 
```sh
echo "RESTACT_JWT_SECRET=$(openssl rand -base64 32)" > .env
chmod 600 .env
docker compose up -d --build
```
 Clients send `Authorization: Bearer <HS256 JWT>` signed with that secret; see [Authentication](/docs/reference/configuration/#authentication--authorization) for the claims. With authentication on, anonymous callers can read and invoke public verbs but cannot deploy or write data (`401`). Quota values are in fuel units and calls per tenant per window; see [Storage and scaling](/docs/operate/storage/) and the [production checklist](/docs/operate/production/) for how to size them. ## Compose files in the repository Both files are local demonstrations, not production templates. ### `docker-compose.postgres.yml` 
```sh
docker compose -f docker-compose.postgres.yml up --build
```
 Starts one PostgreSQL 16 container and two restactd nodes built with `WITH_PG=true`, both pointing at the same database through `RESTACT_DATABASE_URL`. Node A listens on `localhost:8080` and node B on `localhost:8081`. A write, deploy, schedule or event on one node is visible on the other. The demo runs without authentication and sets `RESTACT_PG_ALLOW_PLAINTEXT=1` because the stock `postgres:16` image has no TLS; in production use `sslmode=verify-full` and drop that variable. `down -v` removes the database volume. ### `docker-compose.openbao.yml` 
```sh
docker compose -f docker-compose.openbao.yml up --build
```
 Starts a throwaway OpenBao server in dev mode, a one-shot container that writes a token file readable only by the restactd user, and one restactd node built with `WITH_OPENBAO=true` and pointed at it through `RESTACT_SECRETS_URL` and `RESTACT_SECRETS_TOKEN_FILE`. It exists to develop against the OpenBao secrets backend. The dev-mode server is unsealed, in-memory and uses a fixed root token, so never reuse it outside local development. ## Related * [Storage and scaling](/docs/operate/storage/): the data volume, PostgreSQL and backups. * [Production checklist](/docs/operate/production/) * [Troubleshooting](/docs/operate/troubleshooting/)

# Observability

> restactd writes logs, serves two health endpoints, and can export traces, metrics and logs over OpenTelemetry. This page covers each and what to alert on.

restactd writes logs, serves two health endpoints, and can export traces, metrics and logs over OpenTelemetry. This page covers each and what to alert on. ## Logs Log events go to stderr, one event per line. The boot summary (the `store:`, `auth:` and similar lines printed at startup) goes to stdout as plain text. Lines starting `telemetry:`, `quota:`, `compile cache:` and `warning:` go to stderr; the rest of the summary goes to stdout. 
```text
telemetry: off (no OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT)
restactd dev commit=2c46581a1b2c target=x86_64-unknown-linux-gnu profile=release features=none
store: filesystem (durable) at /var/lib/restact
auth: HS256 bearer tokens
…
REST:ACT host on http://127.0.0.1:8080
```
 The first stdout line is the build: version, the commit it was built from (`unknown` when the build could not tell), target, profile and compiled features. `restactd --version` prints the same line and exits without starting anything, so quote it in bug reports. Read the boot summary after every deployment: it states the store, the authentication mode, the quotas (`quota: fuel=off calls=3 storage_bytes=off objects=off window=lifetime`) and which optional surfaces are on. A `warning:` line there means the host started with a feature disabled, such as a missing runner component. | Variable | Effect | | ---------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `RESTACT_LOG` | A `tracing` filter directive such as `info`, `debug` or `restact.egress=debug`. The default admits restactd’s own events at `info` and everything else at `warn`. | | `RESTACT_LOG_FORMAT=json` | One JSON object per line with the keys `ts`, `level`, `target`, `trace_id`, `span_id`, `message` and `fields`. | | `RESTACT_ACCESS_LOG=1` | One `access` line per data-plane request, collection listing, SSE subscribe, registration create and delete, token mint, grant endpoint, token exchange and MCP `tools/call`: method, path, subject and status. Object values are never logged. The [configuration reference](/docs/reference/configuration/#observability) lists the exact coverage. | | `RESTACT_ACCESS_LOG_INVOCATIONS=1` | With the access log on, also one line per verb invocation, with the `:verb` in the path. | With `RESTACT_ACCESS_LOG_INVOCATIONS=1` also set, an invocation line looks like this in text mode, and the same fields appear under `fields` in JSON mode. It carries the method, path, subject and status, and no error code: 
```text
access method=GET path=/v1/demo:run subject=alice status=405
```
 The access log is the complete per-route record. Metric route labels fold into an overflow series when a host has very many distinct routes, and the access line does not. Values that callers control (paths, subjects, error text from a verb) are escaped before they are logged, so one request cannot forge a second log line. Verb `log` output is rate-limited per tenant and in total (`RESTACT_GUEST_LOG_RATE_PER_SEC`, `RESTACT_GUEST_LOG_BURST`, `RESTACT_GUEST_LOG_CEILING_PER_SEC`). ## Health and readiness 
```sh
curl -i localhost:8080/healthz    # 200 ok
curl -i localhost:8080/readyz     # 200 ready
```
 * `/healthz` answers `200` with `ok` as long as the process is serving HTTP. It checks nothing else. Use it as the liveness probe. * `/readyz` answers `200` with `ready` when the backing store responds. On PostgreSQL it runs `SELECT 1`; if the database is unreachable it answers `503` and logs a warning. Memory and filesystem stores are always ready. Use it as the readiness probe so a node that lost its database is taken out of rotation. Both are exempt from the `RESTACT_ALLOWED_HOSTS` check, so a probe that addresses the container by IP works. The Docker image’s built-in `HEALTHCHECK` uses `/healthz`. ## OpenTelemetry Export is off unless two things are true: the binary was built with the `otel` Cargo feature, and an endpoint is set. With no endpoint nothing is built and no socket opens. The released `-full` images are built with it. The local `Dockerfile` has no build argument for the `otel` feature, so a locally built image with export needs a custom build. From source: 
```sh
cargo build -p restactd --release --features otel
OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT=http://otel-collector:4318 cargo run -p restactd --release --features otel
```
 With an endpoint set, a binary built without the feature says so on its first line: `telemetry: off (otel feature not compiled)`. With export on it prints, for example: 
```text
telemetry: otlp http://otel-collector:4318 traces,metrics,logs sampler=1.0 enduser=raw export=ok
```
 ### Configuration | Variable | Meaning | | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT` | Base URL of an OTLP collector that accepts `http/json`. The host appends `/v1/traces`, `/v1/metrics` and `/v1/logs`. | | `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_TRACES_ENDPOINT`, `…_METRICS_ENDPOINT`, `…_LOGS_ENDPOINT` | A full URL for one signal, used as given. | | `OTEL_SDK_DISABLED=true` | Build no exporter, whatever else is set. | | `OTEL_SERVICE_NAME`, `OTEL_RESOURCE_ATTRIBUTES` | The exported resource. | | `OTEL_TRACES_SAMPLER` | `always_on` (default), `always_off` or `traceidratio`. Any other value is a fatal startup error when an exporter would be built. | | `OTEL_TRACES_SAMPLER_ARG` | The ratio for `traceidratio`, a number from 0 to 1. | Rules the host enforces at startup, each as a `fatal:` line that names the variable: * `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_HEADERS` (and its per-signal forms) must not be set. Credentials for your telemetry backend belong on the collector, not on the host. * An endpoint must be a plain `http://` or `https://` URL with no user name, password, query string or fragment. `http://` endpoints are plaintext. Point them at a collector on your own network. `https://` is verified against the system CA certificates, so a slim container image needs `ca-certificates`. The standard `HTTP_PROXY`, `HTTPS_PROXY` and `NO_PROXY` variables apply. On shutdown the host flushes pending data and waits at most 2 seconds for the collector. `RESTACT_LOG` controls stderr only. What is exported is fixed: the host’s own spans and log records at `info` and above, and never output from verbs. ### Traces The sampling decision is always made by the host. An incoming `traceparent` header from an authenticated caller becomes the parent of the request’s trace; from an anonymous caller it is recorded as a link only, so an unauthenticated client cannot choose your trace ids. | Span | Covers | | ----------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | | `http.server` | One HTTP request. | | `restact.auth` | Resolving the caller’s identity. | | `restact.policy` | An authorization decision. | | `restact.invoke` | A verb invocation, including composed calls. | | `restact.worker` | A process-isolation worker. | | `restact.store.op` | A store operation, labeled with the backend. | | `restact.verb.deploy`, `restact.verb.property`, `restact.verb.rollback` | Management writes. | | `restact.object.delete` | An object delete and its cascade. | | `restact.egress.call` | A mediated outbound call. | | `restact.schedule.fire`, `restact.subscription.deliver` | Timer and subscription work. | | `restact.async.continuation` | The background part of a `202` request; linked to the request that started it. | | `restact.mcp.call_tool` | A tool call through the `/mcp` facet. | Spans carry the caller’s subject as `enduser.id`. ### Metrics Metrics are exported on the same endpoint settings. None of them carries a subject or a concrete object path. | Instrument | Use it to watch | | --------------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `http.server.request.duration`, `http.server.active_requests` | Latency and load at the HTTP layer. | | `restact.invoke.duration`, `restact.invoke.fuel`, `restact.invoke.inflight` | Verb latency, compute used, and concurrency. | | `restact.worker.spawn.duration` | The cost of process isolation. | | `restact.store.op.duration` | Store latency. | | `restact.events.subscribers`, `.published`, `.dropped` | Event delivery, including dropped events. | | `restact.schedule.fired` | Schedule ticks. | | `restact.verb.lifecycle` | Management operations on verbs: upload, code, policy, properties, rollback and delete. | | `restact.egress.calls`, `.duration`, `.bytes` | Mediated outbound calls. | | `restact.llm.calls`, `.tokens` | Calls to configured model connectors. | | `restact.auth.decisions`, `restact.policy.decisions` | Authentication and authorization outcomes, including denials. | | `restact.guest.log.dropped` | Verb log lines dropped by the rate limiter. | The three instruments that carry a verb route label stop adding new label sets after 500; further sets fold into one point with `otel.metric.overflow=true`. Use the access log for per-route accounting on a host with many routes. ## Metering signals Each invocation is metered for fuel (compute), wall-clock time, memory and call count. These are enforced limits that also show up as status codes: | Signal | Status | `restact_code` | | ------------------------------------------------ | ------ | ------------------- | | Per-verb fuel exhausted | `429` | `cpu-budget` | | Per-tenant call, fuel or storage quota exhausted | `429` | `quota` | | Wall-clock deadline | `408` | `timeout` | | Memory limit | `507` | `memory-limit` | | Composition depth, fan-out or cycle | `508` | `composition-cycle` | Count `429` and `507` responses in your proxy or from the access log to see which tenants hit limits; the `restact_code` is in the response body, not in the access line. See [Troubleshooting](/docs/operate/troubleshooting/#budget-exhaustion) for what each means and how to raise it. ## What to alert on * `/readyz` failing for more than a few probe intervals. * A rising rate of `5xx`, and of `429` with `restact_code: quota`. * `restact.events.dropped` above zero, and `restact.guest.log.dropped` climbing. * `restact.store.op.duration` and `restact.worker.spawn.duration` p99. * On PostgreSQL, the log line `restact.pg.quota read_failed fail_open=true`: quota enforcement is degraded while the database errors. ## Related * [Production checklist](/docs/operate/production/) * [Configuration reference](/docs/reference/configuration/#observability)

# Performance

> This page gives measured latency, throughput, cold-start and memory figures for restactd, the command that produced them, and what to size from them. The numbers describe one machine on one day.…

This page gives measured latency, throughput, cold-start and memory figures for restactd, the command that produced them, and what to size from them. The numbers describe one machine on one day. Treat them as an order of magnitude and run the benchmark on your own hardware before you plan capacity. ## What was measured * **Hardware:** AMD Ryzen 7 5800X (8 cores, 16 threads), 31 GB RAM, NVMe SSD, Linux 7.2. * **Build:** `restactd` release build, no optional features, Rust 1.99.1. Filesystem store. The data directory and the compile cache were on tmpfs (RAM), under `/tmp`, so disk speed is not part of these figures. * **Load generator:** `oha` 1.16 on the same machine, so the figures include loopback HTTP and exclude any network. * **The verb:** every tier runs the same small job, counting the words of a 43-byte body. It is deployed as an action and called with `POST`, so no response is served from a cache. The compiled verb is the Rust SDK `word-count-sdk` example; the JavaScript and Python verbs are one-line `invoke` functions. * **Shape of a run:** 20,000 requests at concurrency 8 after a 500-request warm-up. Each figure is the median of 3 runs. Other sessions were using the machine while these runs went on. The one-minute load average, sampled with every measurement, ranged over these bands: 7.6 to 8.5 for the in-process latency runs, 13.3 to 23.4 for the process-isolation latency runs, 4.6 to 8.4 for the cold-start runs (repeated on a quieter machine than the other sections), and 9.6 to 34.2 in process and 20.8 to 44.5 under process isolation for the memory runs. A quiet machine should do better, and the process-isolation rows and the memory peaks are the most affected. ## Results ### Per-call latency and throughput Concurrency 8, one verb at a time. Latency is measured by the client, from request sent to response read. | Tier | Execution | p50 | p99 | Requests per second | | --------------- | --------------------------- | ------- | ------- | ------------------- | | Compiled (Rust) | in process | 0.20 ms | 0.28 ms | 39,500 | | JavaScript | in process | 0.90 ms | 1.4 ms | 9,000 | | Python | in process | 0.45 ms | 0.72 ms | 17,300 | | Compiled (Rust) | `RESTACT_ISOLATION=process` | 16 ms | 28 ms | 490 | | JavaScript | `RESTACT_ISOLATION=process` | 26 ms | 58 ms | 310 | | Python | `RESTACT_ISOLATION=process` | 92 ms | 184 ms | 82 | In process, a call costs well under a millisecond for every tier. The JavaScript verb is slower than the Python one in this test; both run inside a shared runner component, and the measurement does not say why. A verb that does real work, calls objects or composes other verbs costs more than this trivial one. Process isolation starts a worker process for every request, and the worker loads its components before it runs. That start-up dominates the figures above. Python is likely the slowest because its runner component is the largest, 5.9 MB against 1 MB for JavaScript. This is the price of the isolation boundary; see [Storage and scaling](/docs/operate/storage/) for when to pay it. ### Cold start A fresh upload is a new content hash, so the host has not seen it before. The compile cache is the on-disk cache described under `RESTACT_COMPILE_CACHE_DIR` in the [configuration reference](/docs/reference/configuration/). | Measure | Compile cache on | Compile cache off | | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------ | ------------------------------ | | Upload of a new Rust component (`POST /v1/modules`) | 23 ms | 22 ms | | Upload of new JavaScript or Python source | 6 ms | 6 ms | | First call after upload, in process (Rust / JavaScript / Python) | 0.6 / 1.0 / 0.7 ms | 0.7 / 1.1 / 0.7 ms | | First call after upload, process isolation (Rust / JavaScript / Python) | 21 / 26 / 56 ms | 31–92 / 188–374 / 720–1,572 ms | | Host start to `/healthz` answering, in process | 56 ms | 880 ms | | Host start to `/healthz` answering, process isolation | 55 ms | 1.0–2.2 s | | First call of a stored verb after a restart, in process (Rust / JavaScript / Python) | 1.7 / 1.3 / 1.6 ms | 16 / 1.3 / 1.4 ms | | First call of a stored verb after a restart, process isolation (Rust / JavaScript / Python) | 21 / 26 / 56 ms | 36–89 / 203–464 / 732–1,796 ms | In process, the first call after an upload is within 0.3 ms of the second. The cache matters in two places. It cuts host start-up in process from about 0.9 s to about 0.06 s, and under process isolation it saves each worker from compiling the runner: with the cache off, a JavaScript call took 190 to 370 ms and a Python call 0.7 to 1.6 s in our runs, on every request. With process isolation, leave the cache on. ### Memory Resident memory of the host process, in process, with a fresh host for every sample. Peak is the highest value seen sampling every 20 ms while the load generator held the stated number of connections open for 6 seconds. | Tier | Idle | Peak at 1 connection | Peak at 8 | Peak at 64 | Added per extra concurrent call, at 64 | | --------------------------- | ----- | -------------------- | --------- | ---------- | -------------------------------------- | | Bare host, nothing deployed | 38 MB | | | | | | Compiled (Rust) | 40 MB | 42 MB | 59 MB | 67 MB | 0.4 MB | | JavaScript | 41 MB | 42 MB | 54 MB | 78 MB | 0.6 MB | | Python | 39 MB | 40 MB | 49 MB | 93 MB | 0.8 MB | The last column is the peak at 64 minus the peak at 1, divided by 63. Memory does not grow evenly: a few concurrent calls cost more each than many, because the first calls set up buffers the later ones reuse. These verbs allocate almost nothing of their own. A verb that holds a large input, builds a large result or raises its `mem_mb` costs that much more. ## Reproduce Install `oha`, build the JavaScript and Python runners if you want those tiers, then run the benchmark from a checkout: 
```sh
cargo install oha --locked
./scripts/build-js-runner.sh
./scripts/build-py-runner.sh
RESTACT_TEST_PORT=9420 BENCH_OUT=bench.tsv ./scripts/bench-invoke.sh 20000 8 3
```
 The arguments are the request count, the concurrency and the number of runs. The script builds the release host and the Rust verb, starts its own host on `RESTACT_TEST_PORT`, runs the three sections (latency, cold start, memory) in process and under process isolation, writes every sample with the one-minute load average to `BENCH_OUT`, and prints the medians. A tier whose runner component is missing is skipped. Run it on an idle machine; the load column in the raw file tells you when it was not. ## Sizing * **Memory, in process.** Start from the idle host, about 40 MB. The measured peaks at 8 concurrent calls were 59 MB (Rust), 54 MB (JavaScript) and 49 MB (Python), which is 10 to 17 MB above idle, or about 1.2 to 2 MB a call. At 64 calls the peaks were 67, 78 and 93 MB, about 0.4 to 0.8 MB for each call beyond the first. Per tier, a rule that covers the 8-call peaks is 40 MB plus 2 MB for each call you expect to run at once for compiled verbs (59 MB at 8), and 1.5 MB for JavaScript (54 MB) and Python (49 MB); add whatever your own verbs allocate. The per-verb `mem_mb` limit is a ceiling for a single call, not a reservation: `RESTACT_MAX_VERB_MEMORY_MB` sizes address space, not resident memory. Set a container memory limit well above the figure you compute, since the peaks are sampled and short-lived. * **Memory, process isolation.** Every concurrent call is a worker process of its own, on top of the host. A Python worker was seen at up to about 50 MB while it ran. That is an upper bound, because the pages a worker shares with the host are counted twice. Multiply by your concurrency and add the host before you set a memory limit; this page has no clean measurement of it. * **Concurrency.** `RESTACT_MAX_INFLIGHT` (default 256) caps requests in flight across the host. Raise it only after you have confirmed that memory and the request timeout hold at the new value. * **Process isolation, throughput.** In this test a call cost 16 ms (Rust), 26 ms (JavaScript) or 92 ms (Python) at the median, and the host served 490, 310 and 82 calls a second at concurrency 8. Size for the tier you use, not for a single figure. If you need more, run more hosts against a shared database rather than raising the in-flight cap; see [Storage and scaling](/docs/operate/storage/). `RESTACT_WORKER_TIMEOUT_MS` should stay well above the cold figures in the cold-start table. * **Disk.** The compile cache is bounded by `RESTACT_COMPILE_CACHE_MAX_BYTES` (2 GiB by default). Put it on a persistent volume (these runs used tmpfs) so a restart does not recompile the runners. * **Fuel and wall time.** Per-verb `fuel` and `wall_ms` limits bound the work a single call may do. The figures above are for calls that finish in microseconds, so they say nothing about where those limits should sit for your verbs. ## Caveats * One machine, one workload, one small body. Verbs that read or write objects, compose other verbs, call out over the network or return large bodies behave differently, and the store matters: these runs used the filesystem store and no authentication. * The load generator shared the machine with the host. Latency under a separate client and a real network will be higher. * Host start-up is timed from just before the process is launched to the first healthy answer to a `curl` poll every 10 ms, so it includes up to one poll. The cache-on figures held within about 25% across three repeats of the cold-start set. The cache-off figures under process isolation varied two to three times with machine load, so they are given as the range of the three repeat medians. The advice does not change: keep the compile cache on. * Other work was running on the machine, as stated above. The process-isolation latency rows were taken at a load average of 13 to 23. * Memory under process isolation is not tabulated. Workers live for a single request, and the 20 ms sampling misses most of them, so the peaks it produced were not stable enough to publish. Measure it on your own deployment with your own verbs. * The PostgreSQL store, OpenTelemetry and authentication add cost that this page does not measure.

# Production checklist

> Work through this list before a restactd host takes traffic from anyone you do not control. Each item says what to set and links to the page that explains it. The full variable list is in the…

Work through this list before a restactd host takes traffic from anyone you do not control. Each item says what to set and links to the page that explains it. The full variable list is in the [configuration reference](/docs/reference/configuration/). A host with no authentication configured is intentionally open: every caller is the same `anonymous` principal and owns every verb and object, so anyone can replace or delete anything. That is fine on localhost and wrong anywhere else. ## Version * [ ] Pin what you run. Deploy an exact release (`VERSION` from a release archive, or a container image by digest such as `ghcr.io/stackumi/restactd@sha256:<digest>`), not `latest`. Verify the download against `checksums.txt` first. See [Install](/docs/start/install/) and [Released images](/docs/operate/docker/#released-images). * [ ] Confirm it. `restactd --version` prints the build line (version and commit, and the runner hashes a release build pins) without starting the server; the same line is the first thing a running host prints. ## Network and identity * [ ] Bind deliberately. `RESTACT_BIND` defaults to `127.0.0.1:8080`. Set `0.0.0.0:8080` (the Docker image already does) only when something in front of the host controls who can reach it. See [Run with Docker](/docs/operate/docker/#run-it). * [ ] List the host names clients use. A non-loopback bind makes `RESTACT_ALLOWED_HOSTS` mandatory; without it the host refuses to start. List exactly the public name and any in-network service name. Avoid `*` unless the proxy in front pins the `Host` header. See [Allowed host names](/docs/operate/docker/#allowed-host-names). * [ ] Terminate TLS in front. restactd speaks plain HTTP.
