Put a reverse proxy or load balancer in front that serves HTTPS and forwards the public `Host`. The [single-node Compose example](/docs/operate/docker/#a-single-node-deployment) uses Caddy. * [ ] Configure authentication. Set `RESTACT_JWT_SECRET` (HS256 bearer tokens) or all three of `RESTACT_OIDC_ISSUER`, `RESTACT_OIDC_JWKS_URL` and `RESTACT_OIDC_AUDIENCE`. All three OIDC variables are required: setting only some of them (or any to an empty value) refuses boot and names what is missing. The JWKS URL must be `https://` (plain `http://` only to a loopback address). A blank or shorter-than-32-byte `RESTACT_JWT_SECRET`, or a trusted sibling header without `RESTACT_TRUSTED_IDENTITY_HEADER`, also refuses boot. Confirm the boot line `auth: OIDC/JWKS verification enabled …`. Once any authentication is configured, anonymous callers cannot deploy or write (`401`); they can still read and invoke verbs that are public. See [Authentication](/docs/reference/configuration/#authentication--authorization). * [ ] If a filtering proxy inspects request bodies, make it read them as restactd does. See [Running behind a filtering proxy](#running-behind-a-filtering-proxy). * [ ] Use a trusted identity header only behind its proxy. `RESTACT_TRUSTED_IDENTITY_HEADER` takes the header’s value at face value with no signature check. Enable it only when restactd is reachable exclusively through the proxy that sets the header and strips any client-supplied copy. Prefer OIDC where you can. See [Trusted-header mode](/docs/reference/configuration/#authentication--authorization). * [ ] OIDC egress. With OIDC, the host fetches the issuer’s JWKS over HTTPS. Allow outbound 443 to the issuer, or every token gets `401`. * [ ] One identity source per subject namespace. If you enable both HS256 and OIDC, make sure their subjects cannot collide: they share one flat namespace. * [ ] Keep the built-in site off. `RESTACT_BUILTIN_SITE` is unset by default; leave it unset. Turning it on also serves repository documentation from the working directory. * [ ] CORS only if browsers call the API. `RESTACT_CORS_ALLOW_ORIGIN` is unset by default (same-origin only). Name exact origins; avoid `*`. ## Who can define and reach what * [ ] Object binding. On a host where more than one principal deploys verbs, leave `RESTACT_OBJECT_BINDING` at its default `strict` (or `auto-create`). Create the collection roots your wildcard-bound verbs use, with credentials, before users arrive. See [Who may define a verb where](/docs/reference/configuration/#who-may-define-a-verb-where). * [ ] Declare ACLs on object data. Undeclared object properties are public. Add `$policy` or per-property ACLs to anything that should not be world-readable or writable. * [ ] Keep the admin routes narrow. `POST /admin/reset`, `/admin/checkpoint` and `/admin/reown` do not exist until you name who may call them. Name a dedicated operator subject (`sub:ops-1`), never `public`, on an exposed host. * [ ] Scripting tiers. The JavaScript and Python tiers are on when their runner components are present. Remove the runner (or build the image with `make docker-build-core`) if you do not want callers to upload source verbs. Pin a runner’s hash with `RESTACT_JS_RUNNER_SHA` or `RESTACT_PY_RUNNER_SHA` so the host refuses a swapped file. ## Isolation and limits * [ ] Process isolation for shared hosts. Set `RESTACT_ISOLATION=process` when untrusted tenants share the host. It needs a durable store. See [Pick an executor](/docs/operate/storage/#pick-an-executor). * [ ] Quotas with a window. Set `RESTACT_QUOTA_FUEL` and `RESTACT_QUOTA_CALLS`, and `RESTACT_QUOTA_WINDOW_SECS` (for example `86400`) so the caps reset. Without the window they are lifetime totals. Add `RESTACT_QUOTA_STORAGE_BYTES` if callers can upload binary values. Set `RESTACT_QUOTA_OBJECTS` (for example `10000`) when untrusted principals can write; without authentication every caller shares one count. * [ ] Review the concurrency and time limits. `RESTACT_REQUEST_TIMEOUT_MS`, `RESTACT_MAX_INFLIGHT`, `RESTACT_MAX_WASM_INFLIGHT`, `RESTACT_WORKER_TIMEOUT_MS`, and the per-verb ceilings `RESTACT_MAX_VERB_FUEL`, `RESTACT_MAX_VERB_MEMORY_MB`, `RESTACT_MAX_VERB_WALL_MS`. See [Limits](/docs/operate/storage/#limits-that-bound-storage-and-load). * [ ] Egress interlock. If you set `RESTACT_EGRESS_ALLOW`, the host also needs the secrets mount and `RESTACT_EGRESS_CONNECTOR_PREFIX` or it refuses to start. A loopback entry in the allowlist is fatal unless `RESTACT_EGRESS_ALLOW_LOOPBACK` is set; do not set that in production. Like `RESTACT_PG_ALLOW_PLAINTEXT`, it takes `1`, `true`, `on` or `yes` for on and `0`, `false`, `off` or `no` for off; any other value refuses boot. Set the `RESTACT_QUOTA_EGRESS_*` caps. See [Mediated egress](/docs/reference/configuration/#mediated-egress) and [Troubleshooting](/docs/operate/troubleshooting/#egress-boot-refusals). ## Storage * [ ] Durable store. Set `RESTACT_DATA_DIR` (a volume, writable by the server’s user) or `RESTACT_DATABASE_URL`. Without either, every restart loses everything. See [Pick a store](/docs/operate/storage/#pick-a-store). * [ ] PostgreSQL over TLS. Use `sslmode=verify-full` with a CA. Do not set `RESTACT_PG_ALLOW_PLAINTEXT` (it takes `1`, `true`, `on` or `yes` for on and `0`, `false`, `off` or `no` for off; any other value refuses boot). * [ ] Postgres sizing for a cluster. `max_connections` at least N × (`RESTACT_PG_POOL_SIZE` + `RESTACT_PG_LOCK_POOL_SIZE` + a few), PgBouncer in session mode for large fleets. See [PostgreSQL active-active](/docs/operate/storage/#postgresql-active-active). * [ ] Backups, and a tested restore. Filesystem: checkpoints copied off the machine. PostgreSQL: `pg_dump` or snapshots. See [Backup and restore](/docs/operate/storage/#backup-and-restore). ## Monitoring * [ ] Probes. `/healthz` as liveness, `/readyz` as readiness. See [Health and readiness](/docs/operate/observability/#health-and-readiness). * [ ] Logs collected. Send stderr to your log system; set `RESTACT_LOG_FORMAT=json` for structured lines and `RESTACT_ACCESS_LOG=1` for an access record. See [Logs](/docs/operate/observability/#logs). * [ ] Metrics and traces if you use OpenTelemetry: build with `--features otel` and point `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT` at a collector on your own network. See [OpenTelemetry](/docs/operate/observability/#opentelemetry). * [ ] Alerts on `429` and `5xx` rates and `/readyz` failures, from your proxy or access log. ## Running behind a filtering proxy A WAF, API gateway or proxy rule that inspects request bodies has to read them the way restactd does. A filter that reads them differently can allow something restactd then treats as a different request. Rely on such a filter only for decisions restactd does not make itself; authorization stays with restactd. * **A data-plane write body carries a media type.** A non-empty `PUT` to `/v1/<object>` is JSON when its `Content-Type` is `application/json`, `text/json` or any `+json` type such as `application/ld+json`. Any other single `type/subtype` stores the bytes as an opaque value. The type is matched ignoring case and parameters. A non-empty body with no `Content-Type`, a blank or malformed one, or more than one `Content-Type` line is refused with `415` and writes nothing; a control character in the type gets `422`. The exception is a path that an `:update` accessor verb serves, its own or an ancestor’s: the accessor receives the body unparsed and the media-type checks do not apply; the repeated-name check still runs first on a body with no type or a JSON type. Verb and management endpoints name their expected type in the [HTTP API reference](/docs/reference/http-api/). * **Names and strings are compared after escape decoding.** A member name written with `\u` escapes is the same name as its plain spelling, so a rule that matches raw bytes sees something different from restactd. * **A repeated member name is refused.** A JSON body the host parses that repeats a name in any object, at any depth, gets `400`. Do not depend on which copy a filter or another parser would pick. Give the filter the same view of the request as the host: forward `Content-Type` unchanged, and do not rewrite or re-encode the body. ## Related * [Security model](/docs/architecture/security-model/) * [Troubleshooting](/docs/operate/troubleshooting/)

# Storage and scaling

> restactd keeps deployed verbs, object data, registrations and operation state in a store. You pick the store, and separately pick how verbs execute: in the host process or in a worker process per…

restactd keeps deployed verbs, object data, registrations and operation state in a store. You pick the store, and separately pick how verbs execute: in the host process or in a worker process per request. This page covers both choices, running several nodes against one PostgreSQL, size limits and backups. ## Pick a store | Store | Select with | Survives restart | Nodes | | ---------- | ----------------------------------- | ---------------- | ----- | | Memory | nothing set | No | 1 | | Filesystem | `RESTACT_DATA_DIR=/var/lib/restact` | Yes | 1 | | PostgreSQL | `RESTACT_DATABASE_URL=postgres://…` | Yes | Many | With neither variable set, everything lives in memory and is lost when the process exits. That is the right setting for development and tests, and the wrong one for anything you intend to keep. If both variables are set, PostgreSQL wins. ### Filesystem 
```sh
RESTACT_DATA_DIR=/var/lib/restact cargo run -p restactd --release
```
 The store is content-addressed: verb components and binary values are kept by hash, and deployments, registrations and object data sit beside them. The directory must exist or be creatable and be writable by the server’s user. If the directory cannot be opened (not writable, or a corrupt `deployments.json`, `registrations.json` or `format.json`), the host refuses to start. It never falls back to memory, because that would lose every write at the next restart. The filesystem store belongs to one host process. The host takes an exclusive lock on `locks/data-dir.lock` in the data directory before it opens the store and holds it until it exits, so a second host on the same directory refuses to start: 
```text
fatal: data directory /data is in use (restactd server, pid 812, since 1792512000)
```
 The text in that file only names the holder; the lock decides. Process-isolation workers inherit the lock, so the directory stays locked until the last worker of a killed host has exited. If the filesystem cannot lock (some network filesystems), the host logs one warning and serves without the lock, except under `RESTACT_ISOLATION=process`, where the host refuses to start. Use PostgreSQL to run more than one node. ### PostgreSQL PostgreSQL cannot hold U+0000, so the host refuses it in every write on every store (`400 invalid-input`), and the file store behaves the same. Data a file store already holds keeps loading. The PostgreSQL backend is optional and compiled in with a Cargo feature. Build the binary or image with it: 
```sh
cargo build -p restactd --release --features postgres
# or
make docker-build-pg
```
 A binary built without the feature that is given `RESTACT_DATABASE_URL` refuses to start, so a missing feature never leaves nodes on isolated stores. 
```sh
RESTACT_DATABASE_URL='postgres://restact:PASSWORD@db.internal:5432/restact?sslmode=verify-full&sslrootcert=/etc/ssl/db-ca.pem' \
  cargo run -p restactd --release --features postgres
```
 The connection is encrypted by default. The URL’s `sslmode` defaults to `require`, and the modes that can silently downgrade to plaintext (`disable`, `allow`, `prefer`) are refused: 
```text
fatal: cannot connect to RESTACT_DATABASE_URL: RESTACT_DATABASE_URL permits a plaintext/downgradeable TLS connection (sslmode=disable|allow|prefer); set sslmode=require or verify-full, or set RESTACT_PG_ALLOW_PLAINTEXT=1 to allow it (loopback/dev only)
```
 For a database that is reached over an untrusted network use `sslmode=verify-full` with a CA file. Set `RESTACT_PG_ALLOW_PLAINTEXT=1` only for a loopback or throwaway database such as the one in [`docker-compose.postgres.yml`](/docs/operate/docker/#docker-composepostgresyml). A bad URL is fatal at startup. A transient connect failure (a database that is still starting) is retried up to `RESTACT_PG_CONNECT_ATTEMPTS` times (default 5) within `RESTACT_PG_CONNECT_TIMEOUT_SECS` (default 180) so a node does not crash-loop. Tuning variables, with defaults: | Variable | Default | Meaning | | --------------------------------- | -------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `RESTACT_PG_POOL_SIZE` | 16 | Query connections per node. | | `RESTACT_PG_LOCK_POOL_SIZE` | 16 | Connections that hold object-write locks; the ceiling on concurrent object writes per node. | | `RESTACT_PG_STATEMENT_TIMEOUT_MS` | 30000 | Server-side `statement_timeout`; bounds a stuck write or a hot-key lock wait. | | `RESTACT_PG_ACQUIRE_TIMEOUT_MS` | 10000 | How long a writer waits for a pooled connection before failing. | | `RESTACT_PG_PRUNE_INTERVAL_SECS` | 300 | How often the leader node prunes idempotency keys and settled operations. The event log is bounded on insert instead. | | `RESTACT_PG_RETENTION_SECS` | 3600 | Age after which settled idempotency keys and async operations are pruned. | | `RESTACT_PG_RESYNC_INTERVAL_SECS` | 60 | How often a node reconciles its verb registry with the stored deployments and policies (1 to 86400; no off switch). Bounds how long a lost notification can leave a stale verb or policy. | | `RESTACT_PG_EVENT_LOG_MAX_BYTES` | 67108864 | Most bytes the event log holds. Every publish drops the oldest events until the new one fits, so the bound holds at every instant. Boot refuses a value below the largest event a verb can publish, and junk, `0` or a value above `i64::MAX`. | | `RESTACT_PG_EVENT_LOG_CAP` | 10000 | Most recent events kept for SSE replay, across all topics. A hard row cap checked at every insert beside the byte bound, with the same refusals at boot. | All of them are listed under [Clustering](/docs/reference/configuration/#clustering--horizontal-scaling-postgresql). ## PostgreSQL active-active With `RESTACT_DATABASE_URL` set, you can run any number of identical nodes behind a load balancer. They share one database, and these behaviors span the cluster: * Object writes are serialized with advisory locks, so concurrent writers on different nodes do not lose updates. * A schedule fires once cluster-wide, and another node takes over if the firing node dies. * Idempotency keys and asynchronous operations (`202` plus a poll URL) work from any node. * Server-sent events and subscriptions are delivered through the database. The event log is bounded in bytes and rows at every insert (migration `0010_event_log_bounds.sql` adds the ledger and the per-topic trim marks). A subscriber whose cursor points at trimmed events gets a `gap` event, as does one that resumes across a `POST /admin/reset`. Set both bounds to the same values on every node, since each publish applies its own node’s. Subscription delivery is claimed through one cursor per subscription (migration `0011_subscription_cursors.sql`), so that state is bounded by the number of live subscriptions and nothing prunes it. A subscription is delivered or skipped at most once per event, in order, across the cluster; once any node has decided an event, no node delivers an earlier one for that subscription. An event published before the subscription was registered is never delivered, and a node attempts only events published after it connected, so a restart delivers nothing published before it. Delivery is live-only: an event that no live node attempts is not delivered later. Every node needs the same authentication settings. For HS256, set the same `RESTACT_JWT_SECRET` on all of them so a token issued for one node validates on the others. The fuel and call quotas are shared through the database. The storage quota (`RESTACT_QUOTA_STORAGE_BYTES`) and the egress quotas are tallied in each process, so on N nodes their effective cap is N times the configured value. Behavior when the database misbehaves: * An idempotency claim fails closed: the request gets `409`. * The cross-node quota read fails open, and the host logs `restact.pg.quota read_failed fail_open=true`. * `/readyz` runs a `SELECT 1`. Use it as the readiness probe so a node that lost its database link is taken out of rotation. Use `/healthz` as the liveness probe. See [Observability](/docs/operate/observability/#health-and-readiness). Listings and `obj.query` scan a path prefix as a byte range (`object_path ~>=~ $lo AND object_path ~<~ $hi`), and the built-in `object_attrs_path_pattern` index (`text_pattern_ops`) serves it. Do not add a partial index of the form `... WHERE object_path LIKE 'notes/%'`. The range bounds and the ordering property arrive as bind parameters, and PostgreSQL may switch a prepared statement to a generic plan after a few executions. Under a generic plan the planner cannot prove a literal index predicate from those parameters, so a partial index cannot be relied on. An expression index on the ordering property (`attrs->>'published'`) fails the same way, because the property name is bound too. Both were checked with `EXPLAIN` on PostgreSQL 16 under `plan_cache_mode = force_generic_plan`: every plan used `object_attrs_path_pattern`. Size `max_connections` on the database for the whole fleet: at least N × (`RESTACT_PG_POOL_SIZE` + `RESTACT_PG_LOCK_POOL_SIZE` + a few connections for event listening), plus up to `RESTACT_MAX_WASM_INFLIGHT` more per node under process isolation. For a large fleet put PgBouncer in front in **session** mode; transaction mode breaks the session advisory locks. ## Pick an executor The store decides where state lives. The executor decides where verb code runs. Both executors run the same components with the same limits, and the full conformance suite passes on each. | | In-process (default) | Process isolation | | ----------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | Select with | nothing | `RESTACT_ISOLATION=process` | | Where a verb runs | A Wasmtime instance inside the host process | A fresh worker process per request, the same binary re-executed | | Protects against | Everything the sandbox enforces: no ambient authority, capability confinement, fuel, wall-clock and memory limits | The above, plus a separate address space per request: one request cannot read another request’s memory or the host process’s | | Costs | Lowest latency | A process spawn and engine start per request, no worker pool | | Needs | Nothing | A durable store: `RESTACT_DATA_DIR` or `RESTACT_DATABASE_URL`. Without one, the host refuses to start (exit 1) and names `RESTACT_ISOLATION`, `RESTACT_DATA_DIR` and `RESTACT_DATABASE_URL` | Choose process isolation when untrusted tenants share the host. Choose in-process when verbs come from people you trust or when per-request latency matters more than the extra boundary. Details of process isolation: * The worker re-opens the same store as the parent, so composition, shared budgets and capability attenuation all behave as they do in-process. The parent debits quota and fuel and kills a worker that passes `RESTACT_WORKER_TIMEOUT_MS` (default 30000). * Workers cache compiled components on disk so they do not recompile on every request. The cache lives in `RESTACT_COMPILE_CACHE_DIR` (default under `$XDG_CACHE_HOME/restactd` or `$HOME/.cache/restactd`), each entry is authenticated with an HMAC before loading, and `RESTACT_COMPILE_CACHE_MAX_BYTES` (default 2 GiB) caps it. In a container, mount a volume there or accept a cold cache after each restart. See [Isolation](/docs/reference/configuration/#isolation). * Workers write diagnostics to stderr only; the parent captures the response from stdout. ## Limits that bound storage and load | Variable | Default | What it bounds | | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | | `RESTACT_MAX_BLOB_BYTES` | 8388608 (8 MiB) | One binary value. A larger `PUT` gets `413`. | | `RESTACT_QUOTA_STORAGE_BYTES` | unset | Total binary-value bytes per tenant. A `PUT` over the cap gets `429` before anything is written. | | `RESTACT_QUOTA_CALLS` / `RESTACT_QUOTA_FUEL` | unset | Invocations and compute per tenant. | | `RESTACT_QUOTA_OBJECTS` | unset | Objects one owner holds. A creation over the cap gets `429` with no `Retry-After`; updates and deletes are never refused. On PostgreSQL the count is the cluster’s and lives in the `object_counts` table, which the boot migrations create. A filesystem host rebuilds it at every start. | | `RESTACT_QUOTA_WINDOW_SECS` | unset | Turns the calls and fuel caps into a rolling per-window budget. Without it they are lifetime totals and an active tenant eventually trips permanently. Storage bytes and the object count are levels, not rates, and are never windowed. | | `RESTACT_MAX_INFLIGHT` | 256 | Concurrent HTTP requests. | | `RESTACT_MAX_WASM_INFLIGHT` | 256 | Concurrently executing verbs. | | `RESTACT_REQUEST_TIMEOUT_MS` | 60000 | One request, including its body transfer (`408`). | | `RESTACT_MAX_VERB_FUEL`, `RESTACT_MAX_VERB_MEMORY_MB`, `RESTACT_MAX_VERB_WALL_MS` | 1e11, 512, 30000 | The ceiling a deployer’s per-verb `fuel`, `mem_mb` and `wall_ms` requests are clamped to. | A verb that exceeds its own limit fails with `429` (fuel), `408` (wall clock) or `507` (memory); see [Troubleshooting](/docs/operate/troubleshooting/#budget-exhaustion). ## Backup and restore A filesystem store is backed up by taking a snapshot: a directory that is itself a valid store root. PostgreSQL is backed up with the database’s own tools. Both are covered below, along with what a snapshot leaves out and why you should only restore snapshots you trust. ### Filesystem store: take a snapshot `restactd checkpoint` writes a verified snapshot. It reads the same `RESTACT_*` variables as the server, so run it with the same `RESTACT_DATA_DIR`: /var/lib/restact/snapshots/checkpoint-1792512000-0 
```sh
restactd checkpoint
```
 The snapshot path is the only thing on stdout (`--json` prints `{"snapshot": ..., "mode": "live"|"offline", "durationMs": ...}` instead). The command checks the data directory’s lock to decide how to work: * With no server running, it copies the store itself and holds the lock while it does. Nothing else writes meanwhile, so the snapshot is consistent. This needs no configuration. * With a server running, it asks that server to do the copy by sending `POST /admin/checkpoint` to `RESTACT_BIND`. The server must have `RESTACT_ADMIN_CHECKPOINT` set (otherwise the command exits 3), and `RESTACT_BIND` must be an IP address and port such as `127.0.0.1:8080` or `[::1]:8080`, because the bearer token is sent to exactly one address (`0.0.0.0` and `[::]` map to loopback). Under authentication the command needs a bearer token on stdin, never on the command line: 
```sh
restactd token mint --sub ops --ttl 5m | restactd checkpoint --token-stdin
```
 With OIDC, pipe in a token from your issuer instead. The command verifies what the server wrote before it reports success. `--out DIR` puts the snapshot in `DIR`, which must not exist yet and must not lie inside the data directory’s store files. `--timeout` bounds the live request (default 15 minutes, a whole number followed by `s`, `m`, `h` or `d`). After a timeout the server still finishes the snapshot and announces it on `@host/checkpoint`. | Exit | Meaning | | ---- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | 0 | Snapshot written and verified. | | 1 | Failed: an I/O error, a snapshot that fails verification, a server error. | | 2 | Usage or configuration: a bad flag, `--out` already exists, no `RESTACT_DATA_DIR`. | | 3 | Not available: PostgreSQL is configured, the server has no checkpoint endpoint, or the filesystem cannot lock. | | 4 | The server refused the bearer token (401 or 403). | | 5 | A server holds the directory but nothing answers at `RESTACT_BIND`, or `--timeout` passed. | | 6 | Busy: another restactd command holds the directory, or the server is running a reset or another checkpoint. | You can also call the endpoint yourself, for example from a cron job on another machine. It is described under [HTTP API](/docs/reference/http-api/#durable-checkpoint-post-admincheckpoint). From another machine, `restact admin checkpoint` makes the same request (see [the command line reference](/docs/reference/cli/)). #### Interval checkpoints and retention A host can snapshot itself on a timer, with or without the HTTP endpoint: 
```sh
RESTACT_CHECKPOINT_INTERVAL_SECS=3600 \
RESTACT_CHECKPOINT_KEEP=24 \
  cargo run -p restactd --release
```
 `RESTACT_CHECKPOINT_INTERVAL_SECS` has a minimum of 30 seconds. `RESTACT_CHECKPOINT_KEEP` keeps the newest N snapshots (default 8, `0` keeps all). The command applies the same retention when it writes into the checkpoint directory, but not when you use `--out`. Snapshots land in `<data dir>/snapshots/checkpoint-<unix-time>-<seq>`. Set `RESTACT_CHECKPOINT_DIR` to put them elsewhere. A stopping host finishes a checkpoint that is already copying, with no cap; see [Containers and Kubernetes](#containers-and-kubernetes) for the grace period. A live snapshot is a crash-stop image of a running store, the same state the host would recover to after a crash. That directory is on the same disk as the store by default, so copy snapshots off the machine on a schedule. Two hosts must not share one `RESTACT_CHECKPOINT_DIR`: each sweeps the directory’s `.partial-*` entries when it boots, and each prunes the other’s snapshots. #### Copy by hand With the host stopped, copying the data directory (or the store files in it: `blobs/`, `state/`, `deployments.json`, `objects.json`, `registrations.json`, `grants.json` and `format.json`) gives you a valid snapshot. Do not copy a running host’s directory with `cp` or `rsync`: the copy races every write. A damaged `objects.json` is never read as an empty index. The host refuses to start on one and names the file, and a running host answers any request that needs the index (a listing, a query, a write of a new object, a delete) with a `503` `unavailable` problem, leaving the file as it is. A verb’s `obj.query` fails the same way instead of returning an empty page. Under process isolation, any verb write is refused while the index is damaged, not only a write of a new object. Under `RESTACT_CONSISTENCY=atomic-invocation`, a verb’s write set is discarded and the failure logged, as that mode already does for a failed commit. Restore the file from a snapshot, or repair it, before starting the host again. A missing `objects.json` is an empty index. ### Filesystem store: restore Stop the host first. `restactd restore` refuses to run while a server or another restactd command holds the data directory (exit 6). /var/lib/restact/pre-restore-1792512100 
```sh
restactd restore --from /var/lib/restact/snapshots/checkpoint-1792512000-0
```
 The command copies the snapshot into a staging directory inside the data directory and verifies it. It checks that every file parses, that every blob matches its hash, and that every blob a deployment or object refers to is present. Only then does it move the current store aside into `pre-restore-<unix-time>` and move the snapshot’s files into place. The printed path is that `pre-restore-<unix-time>` directory, which is itself a complete store, so `restactd restore --from` that path undoes the restore. The command never deletes it; remove it when you no longer need it. A snapshot that fails verification leaves the data directory untouched (exit 1). If a restore is cut off partway, the data directory holds `RESTORE-IN-PROGRESS.json` and the host refuses to start. Run `restactd restore --from` with the same snapshot again: it rolls back the interrupted run and restores afresh. `restore` creates a missing data directory, so it also restores onto a fresh volume. It refuses (exit 2) a directory that holds files that are not part of a store, so a wrong `RESTACT_DATA_DIR` is not overwritten. `--from` may not be the data directory or lie inside its store files. The same exit codes as `checkpoint` apply, minus 4 and 5. To restore by hand, stop the host and either set `RESTACT_DATA_DIR` to the snapshot directory, or empty the data directory’s store files and copy the snapshot’s over them. Keep the old files until the restored host answers. Because a snapshot is a valid store root, the first option works as it stands, but the host then writes into the snapshot, so copy it first if you want to keep it. Test a restore before you need it. Restore a snapshot into a scratch directory, start a host against it on another port, and check that `GET /v1` and a verb you care about answer. ### Containers and Kubernetes In the image the server is the main process, so restore runs in a one-off container on the same volume while the server’s container is stopped. The same goes for an offline checkpoint: 
```sh
docker stop restactd
docker run --rm -v restact-data:/data restactd:dev restore --from /data/snapshots/checkpoint-1792512000-0
docker start restactd
```
 On Kubernetes, scale the Deployment to zero, run a Job that mounts the same PersistentVolumeClaim and runs `restactd restore --from /data/snapshots/<name>`, then scale back up. The image entrypoint is `restactd`, so the Job needs only `args: ["restore", "--from", "/data/snapshots/<name>"]`. A running pod can be checkpointed with `kubectl exec -i <pod> -- restactd checkpoint --token-stdin`, provided the pod has `RESTACT_ADMIN_CHECKPOINT` set and you pipe a bearer token in if it uses authentication. A bind mount must be writable by uid 65532 (see [Run with Docker](/docs/operate/docker/#run-it)). A stopping host finishes a checkpoint that is already copying, with no cap, and starts no new interval checkpoint. If the grace period is shorter than the copy, the container is killed mid-copy: that snapshot is lost, the store is untouched, and the next boot removes the `.partial-*` directory. Size the grace period for your store: `docker stop -t <seconds>`, Compose `stop_grace_period`, or Kubernetes `terminationGracePeriodSeconds`. ### What a snapshot leaves out A snapshot holds the store: deployed verbs and their records, objects, registrations (including schedules and subscriptions) and grants. It does not hold: * `page-token.key`, the key that seals page tokens and query cursors. A restore makes a new one, so outstanding `pageToken`s and any query cursor a verb stored stop working. Nothing else depends on it. To rotate the key on purpose, stop every node and delete the file. Never delete it while a node runs: that node’s isolation workers fail every invocation until it restarts. The data directory must support hard links, or the host refuses to boot. * The compile cache (`RESTACT_COMPILE_CACHE_DIR`). Verbs recompile once after a restore. * `locks/`, which belongs to the directory and not to its contents. JavaScript and Python verbs come back bound to the runner build recorded in their deployment record, and schedules come back with their stored last-fire time, so a schedule fires at most once to catch up. ### A snapshot is authority A snapshot carries owners, `$policy` objects, grants, deployment records (with their `runs_as` and capability settings) and verb code. Restoring one trusts whoever wrote it. Verification shows that a snapshot is intact and consistent, not that it is harmless. Restore only snapshots you took yourself, and keep them where only operators can write. Restored verbs still run inside the sandbox; what their records grant is the question of trust. ### PostgreSQL `restactd checkpoint` and `restactd restore` exit 3 when `RESTACT_DATABASE_URL` is set, without connecting to the database, and point here. `POST /admin/checkpoint` is also not available on PostgreSQL. Use the database’s own tools: `pg_dump` and `pg_restore`, WAL archiving, or your provider’s snapshots. Back up the whole database; restactd keeps nothing important outside it. 
```sh
pg_dump --format=custom --file=restact.dump "$RESTACT_DATABASE_URL"
```
 To restore, stop every restactd node first. Restore into an empty database (`pg_restore --clean --if-exists --dbname=...`), then start the nodes. A node that keeps running against a database you are restoring serves stale caches and can write into the half-restored data. The page-token key is the `host_keys` row named `page-token`; it travels with the dump. To rotate it, stop every node and delete the row. ## Audit a store for ancestor claims `restactd audit ancestry` reads a store and lists what the ancestor-claim guard would leave in place. It reads the same `RESTACT_*` variables as the server (`RESTACT_DATA_DIR`, or `RESTACT_DATABASE_URL` on a PostgreSQL build, the auth, `RESTACT_OBJECT_BINDING` and `RESTACT_POLICY_INHERITANCE` settings, `RESTACT_ADMIN_REOWN`, and the bootstrap file settings), prints `ancestry-audit:` lines on stdout and exits. Run it with the server’s environment and working directory: the environment decides what is in scope, and the bootstrap files (`docs/`, `site/examples/`, the example `.wasm` files) are found by the same relative paths the server uses. 
```sh
restactd audit ancestry
# ancestry-audit: claimed ancestor area/x owner alice class after: 3 descendants, 2 other owners, hosts 1 verbs; remedy: POST /admin/reown on area/x
# ancestry-audit: done (1 claimed ancestors, 0 precedence changes, 0 orphan definitions)
```
 It reports three things: * **Claimed ancestors.** A stored object with at least one descendant owned by someone else. The line names the ancestor and its owner, the descendant and other-owner counts, and whether the ancestor hosts verbs. `class` says whether the ancestor was created `after` its descendants, `before` them, or `unknown` (a missing stamp, the same second, or a mix). It rests on one-second wall-clock stamps, so treat it as a hint. An ancestor whose owner matches `RESTACT_ADMIN_REOWN` is listed as `exempt` and is neither counted nor reflected in the exit code, since an operator-owned ancestor is not a squat. * **Precedence changes.** Same-level pattern pairs for one verb, such as `px/{id}:v` and `{id}/leaf:v`, where the pattern with the leftmost literal wins but literal count alone would tie or pick the other pattern. The line names the deploy-order, route-order and leftmost-literal winners; the leftmost-literal winner is the pattern served. * **Orphan definitions.** A verb definition whose anchor object is absent or owned by someone other than the definition’s owner. Claims and orphans belong to the ancestor-creation guard, so they are listed and counted only when the guard is on in the audit’s own environment: authentication configured, and either a coupled `RESTACT_OBJECT_BINDING` mode (`strict` is the default under any configured authentication) or `RESTACT_POLICY_INHERITANCE`. Orphans are in scope only under a coupled mode. When the guard is off the audit prints one `ancestry-audit: note:` line saying so. Precedence changes are always listed and counted. With `RESTACT_EXAMPLES` unset the bootstrap writes no example content, so nothing is classed `host-bootstrap`. What this host’s own startup bootstrap writes is listed with class `host-bootstrap` and is not counted: the backing objects, each `docs/<name>` with exactly the title and document the docs directory gives, and the bootstrap verbs whose stored record equals what the bootstrap deploy writes: the route and file hash, and also the kind, limits, allowed calls, policy, grants, `produces`, `runs_as` and paused settings, with no declared data property. The match is exact. An extra anonymous object under `docs/`, an edited doc, a verb the bootstrap did not deploy or deployed with other options, a different hash, or a file the audit cannot read is counted, and its line names the first item that differs. A claim is also listed `host-bootstrap` when its ancestor is exactly such an object and every foreign descendant is a direct child of an object a bootstrap action is bound to by `{id}` (`counters/<id>` under `counters`), and that bound verb is stored exactly as the bootstrap deploys it; a tampered ancestor, a deeper descendant or a redeployed verb is counted. The last line is always `ancestry-audit: done (<claims> claimed ancestors, <pairs> precedence changes, <orphans> orphan definitions)`. | Exit | Meaning | | ---- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | | 0 | No findings. | | 3 | At least one finding. | | 1 | The audit could not complete: the data directory holds an interrupted restore (`RESTORE-IN-PROGRESS.json`; finish it with `restactd restore`), or the store could not be read. | | 2 | Usage error; `RESTACT_DATA_DIR` is missing, empty, unset, has no `format.json` naming a format this binary reads, or lacks its `blobs/` or `state/` directory; or the environment is one the server refuses to start on. | The audit writes nothing, by construction: it never creates or stamps a store or a directory, uses the in-process coordinator whatever `RESTACT_ISOLATION` says (so it creates no `locks/`), keeps its own page-token key in memory (so a snapshot, a restored store or one without `page-token.key` or the `host_keys` row audits like any other), takes no data-directory lock and opens no listener. It can run beside a live server, and it can run from a one-off container or Job before a new binary starts serving. On PostgreSQL, run it from one node or one Job. Warnings the store and the registry load raise while it reads print to stderr. A host serving a store with changed pairs prints one stderr line once serving has started, `warning: N same-level pattern pairs are won by the leftmost literal where literal count alone would tie or pick the other route; run restactd audit ancestry to list them`. To fix a finding, see [Recovering from `ancestor-claim`](/docs/operate/troubleshooting/#recovering-from-ancestor-claim); the remedy for a claim or an orphan is `POST /admin/reown`, or [`restactd reown`](#move-ownership-offline) when no server is running. ## Move ownership offline `restactd reown` moves the verbs and objects that one subject owns, under one path, to another subject. It is the offline door to [`POST /admin/reown`](/docs/reference/http-api/#operator-ownership-recovery-post-adminreown) and runs the same code, so the result is the same. It needs no running server, no bearer token and no `RESTACT_ADMIN_REOWN`. Use it when an owner has left and the server is down, or when no operator subject is set up. 
```sh
restactd reown --from alice --to bob --path area/x --subtree --dry-run
# verb area/x/{id}:render runs-as-cleared=true invoker-cleared=false paused=false
# object area/x
# done from=alice to=bob path=area/x subtree=true dry-run=true verbs=1 objects=1 secret-slots-skipped=0
restactd reown --from alice --to bob --path area/x --subtree --yes
```
 It reads the same `RESTACT_*` variables as the server: `RESTACT_DATA_DIR` or `RESTACT_DATABASE_URL`, and the secrets settings. It reads no authentication setting. A real move on a terminal selects first and asks `Move N verbs and M objects that alice owns under area/x to bob? [y/N]`; off a terminal it refuses with exit 2 unless you pass `--yes`. A dry run never asks. The server skips a verb’s secret slot when the slot lies under its secrets mount, so the command needs the same mount. Set `RESTACT_SECRETS_URL` and `RESTACT_SECRETS_MOUNT` as the server has them, or pass `--no-secrets` if the server runs without one. With neither, the command exits 2 before it opens the store, a dry run included. It never opens the secrets backend. **Filesystem.** A real move takes the data-directory lock, so it exits 6 while a server or another command holds the directory. Stop the server first, or send the move through the running server with `restact admin reown`. The change goes live when a server next starts on the directory and loads the records. A dry run takes no lock and runs beside a live server. A directory with an interrupted restore is refused (exit 1) and a directory that is not a store root is refused (exit 2). **PostgreSQL.** The command connects as one more node beside live servers, checks that the database schema matches this build (exit 2 if it does not), and tells the other nodes about each moved verb. It takes no data-directory lock. A reown on another node, or a deploy racing the command on a single node, can leave a window the command does not cover. Run `--dry-run` again afterwards: it selects nothing when the move held. A stopped cluster has no such windows. Stdout has one line per moved item, verbs first and then objects, and a final `done` line; `--json` prints the HTTP route’s `200` body on one line instead. Stderr carries a note naming the secrets mount in effect and, for a real move, the audit line `warning: admin reown by 'restactd:reown': ...`. Moved objects are stamped in their provenance with the subject `restactd:reown` and no actor. A verb’s record does not record the move. | Exit | Meaning | | ---- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | | 0 | Done, including a dry run and a run that matched nothing. | | 1 | The move could not complete (a store error part-way, an interrupted restore, an unreachable database, or stdout could not be written). Committed items are listed on stdout when it can be written and the audit line ends `(stopped part-way)`; run it again to finish. If stdout fails only after a complete move, when the `done` line or the `--json` body is written, the exit is still 1 but the audit line says the move completed, and a re-run then reports that nothing matched. | | 2 | Usage or configuration: a refused value; no confirmation, or a declined prompt; no secrets setting, a half secrets pair, or `--no-secrets` beside a set secrets variable; no store configured; a directory that is not a store root; a PostgreSQL URL the server’s TLS rule refuses; or a database schema this build does not match. | | 3 | The filesystem cannot lock. | | 6 | Busy: a server or another restactd command holds the directory. | ## Related * [Run with Docker](/docs/operate/docker/) * [Production checklist](/docs/operate/production/) * [Observability](/docs/operate/observability/)

# Troubleshooting

> Find the symptom, read the cause, apply the fix. Errors from the HTTP API are application/problem+json documents; the restact_code member names the exact cause, and the examples below show the real…

Find the symptom, read the cause, apply the fix. Errors from the HTTP API are `application/problem+json` documents; the `restact_code` member names the exact cause, and the examples below show the real bodies. Startup failures print one `fatal:` or `error:` line to stderr and exit non-zero. ## Startup ### The system glibc is too old 
```text
./restactd: /lib/x86_64-linux-gnu/libc.so.6: version `GLIBC_2.28' not found
```
 The Linux release binaries need glibc 2.28 or later. Run them on a newer distribution, or use the [container image](/docs/operate/docker/#released-images), which brings its own libc. `ldd --version` prints the glibc version of the machine. ### macOS refuses to open the binary The macOS binaries are unsigned and not notarized, so Gatekeeper reports that the developer cannot be verified or that the file is damaged. If the archive came through a browser, clear the quarantine flag on the unpacked directory and run it again: 
```sh
xattr -dr com.apple.quarantine restactd-v0.1.0-aarch64-apple-darwin   # the directory you unpacked
```
 ### Address already in use 
```text
Error: Address already in use (os error 98)
```
 Another process holds the port; the default is `127.0.0.1:8080`. Find and stop it, or bind elsewhere: 
```sh
lsof -tiTCP:8080 -sTCP:LISTEN          # who owns the port
RESTACT_BIND=127.0.0.1:9180 restactd   # or pick another port
```
 Stop an old restactd by the port it listens on. Matching by process name also kills hosts you did not mean to stop. ### Refuses to start on a non-loopback address 
```text
fatal: RESTACT_BIND=0.0.0.0:8080 is not a loopback address; set RESTACT_ALLOWED_HOSTS to the host names clients use (comma-separated), or * to accept any Host
```
 A host bound beyond loopback must say which `Host` names it answers to. Set `RESTACT_ALLOWED_HOSTS=restact.example.com`. See [Allowed host names](/docs/operate/docker/#allowed-host-names). ### Cannot open the data directory 
```text
fatal: cannot open RESTACT_DATA_DIR=/data: Permission denied (os error 13)
```
 The directory is not writable by the server’s user, or its `deployments.json`, `registrations.json` or `format.json` is corrupt. The host will not fall back to memory. In the Docker image the user is uid 65532, so the data directory must be owned by it: a fresh, empty named volume is, and a bind mount or a volume holding files from another uid needs the `chown` in [Run it](/docs/operate/docker/#run-it). ### The data directory is in use 
```text
fatal: data directory /data is in use (restactd server, pid 812, since 1792512000)
```
 Another restactd holds the lock on `<data-dir>/locks/data-dir.lock`. Stop it, or point this host at its own directory. Under Docker, Kubernetes or systemd a restart policy retries until the old process has exited. A host killed with `SIGKILL` under `RESTACT_ISOLATION=process` leaves its workers holding the lock until they finish. See [Filesystem](/docs/operate/storage/#filesystem). If the lock file itself cannot be opened (wrong permissions, or a directory or symlink where the file should be), the host exits 1 with `fatal: cannot open RESTACT_DATA_DIR=<dir>`. It does not serve unlocked. Only a filesystem whose `flock` fails makes the host warn that the lock is not held, and then serve; under `RESTACT_ISOLATION=process` the cross-process lock probe refuses that filesystem and the host exits 1. A directory with a `RESTORE-IN-PROGRESS.json` file also refuses to start, because an earlier `restactd restore` did not finish. Run `restactd restore --from <source>`, with the snapshot directory the interrupted restore used, to complete or roll it back. The refusal message names that directory. ### Refuses to start over an authentication setting A set-but-unusable authentication setting stops the host before it serves, and the message names the variable. The cases: * Only some of `RESTACT_OIDC_ISSUER`, `RESTACT_OIDC_JWKS_URL` and `RESTACT_OIDC_AUDIENCE` are set, or any `RESTACT_OIDC_*` variable is set while one of the three is missing or blank. OIDC errors start with `fatal: OIDC configuration:`. * `RESTACT_OIDC_JWKS_URL` is a remote `http://` URL or does not parse, `RESTACT_OIDC_JWKS_TTL_SECS` is not a whole number, or `RESTACT_OIDC_JWT_HEADER` is not a valid header name. * `RESTACT_JWT_SECRET` or `RESTACT_TRUSTED_IDENTITY_HEADER` is set but empty or blank. * `RESTACT_JWT_SECRET` is shorter than 32 bytes. The message reads `RESTACT_JWT_SECRET is too short`. Generate a secret with `openssl rand -base64 32`. * `RESTACT_TRUSTED_ROLES_HEADER`, `RESTACT_TRUSTED_SCOPES_HEADER` or `RESTACT_TRUSTED_GROUPS_HEADER` is set without `RESTACT_TRUSTED_IDENTITY_HEADER`. * Any of these variables holds a value that is not valid UTF-8. Set the missing value, or unset the variable you did not mean to set. The boolean switches `RESTACT_PG_ALLOW_PLAINTEXT` and `RESTACT_EGRESS_ALLOW_LOOPBACK` refuse boot too when they hold anything other than `1`, `true`, `on`, `yes`, `0`, `false`, `off` or `no`. ### PostgreSQL refuses `sslmode=disable` `RESTACT_DATABASE_URL` connections use TLS by default, and the modes `disable`, `allow` and `prefer` are refused at startup: 
```text
fatal: cannot connect to RESTACT_DATABASE_URL: RESTACT_DATABASE_URL permits a plaintext/downgradeable TLS connection (sslmode=disable|allow|prefer); set sslmode=require or verify-full, or set RESTACT_PG_ALLOW_PLAINTEXT=1 to allow it (loopback/dev only)
```
 The fix is to use TLS (`sslmode=require`, or `verify-full` with a CA). For a throwaway local database only, set `RESTACT_PG_ALLOW_PLAINTEXT=1`. See [PostgreSQL](/docs/operate/storage/#postgresql). If instead the boot log says the database URL is set but the binary was built without the `postgres` feature, the host refuses to start rather than fall back to another store: 
```text
fatal: RESTACT_DATABASE_URL is set but this binary was built without the `postgres` feature; use the `restactd-full` download or the `-full` image, or build with `--features postgres`, or unset RESTACT_DATABASE_URL
```
 Use the `restactd-full` download or the `-full` image, build with `--features postgres`, or unset the variable. ### Egress boot refusals Setting `RESTACT_EGRESS_ALLOW` turns on mediated outbound calls, and the host checks the surrounding configuration before it starts. Three messages, in the order you will meet them: 
```text
error: RESTACT_EGRESS_ALLOW is set but no secrets mount is in service — a destination allowlist with nothing to splice is a misconfiguration. Configure RESTACT_SECRETS_URL + RESTACT_SECRETS_MOUNT, or unset RESTACT_EGRESS_ALLOW.
```
 Configure the secrets mount: `RESTACT_SECRETS_URL=file:/etc/restact/secrets.json` and `RESTACT_SECRETS_MOUNT=ws/{ws}/secrets/{name}` (or an OpenBao URL on a build with the `openbao` feature). The secrets file must be mode `0600` in a directory that is not group- or world-writable, or the host reports `fatal: cannot open RESTACT_SECRETS_URL=…`. 
```text
error: RESTACT_EGRESS_ALLOW is set but RESTACT_EGRESS_CONNECTOR_PREFIX is not — the host must know where connectors live before it will call through one. Set it to the positional prefix connectors are created under, e.g. 'ws/{ws}/connectors'.
```
 Set `RESTACT_EGRESS_CONNECTOR_PREFIX=ws/{ws}/connectors`. 
```text
error: RESTACT_EGRESS_ALLOW names the loopback destination(s) [127.0.0.1] — the only path by which a non-public destination is ever reachable, and never the default. Set RESTACT_EGRESS_ALLOW_LOOPBACK to acknowledge it (deterministic test harnesses only), or remove the entry.
```
 Remove the loopback entry from the allowlist. `RESTACT_EGRESS_ALLOW_LOOPBACK` exists for test harnesses only. ### Export is configured but nothing arrives The first boot line tells you: 
```text
telemetry: off (otel feature not compiled)
```
 The binary was built without `--features otel`. See [OpenTelemetry](/docs/operate/observability/#opentelemetry). ## Requests ### `403` on every request from another machine 
```json
{"detail":"the request's Host is not allowed; set RESTACT_ALLOWED_HOSTS to the host names clients use","restact_code":"forbidden","status":403,"title":"Forbidden",…}
```
 The `Host` header names something not in `RESTACT_ALLOWED_HOSTS`. Add the name clients use, including the public name behind a proxy, and make the proxy forward the original `Host`. `/healthz` and `/readyz` are exempt. ### `401` on every write after enabling auth 
```json
{"detail":"authentication required to deploy on this server","restact_code":"unauthorized","status":401,"title":"Unauthorized",…}
```
 Once any authentication is configured, anonymous callers cannot deploy, change verbs, or write object data. Send a bearer token: 
```sh
curl -X POST -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" --data-binary @verb.wasm \
  'localhost:8080/v1/modules?verb=mine:hi&kind=function'
```
 If you do send a token and still get `401`, the body says why: 
```json
{"detail":"invalid bearer token: bad signature encoding","restact_code":"unauthorized",…}
```
 Check that the token is signed with the host’s `RESTACT_JWT_SECRET` (the same value on every node of a cluster), has not expired, and, for OIDC, that the issuer, audience and JWKS URL match. Custom routes give a token that fails verification the same `401` as `/v1`; it is never served as anonymous. ### `409 object-binding` when deploying a verb 
```json
{"detail":"object 'mine' does not exist; create it (PUT /v1/mine) before deploying a verb on it (strict object-binding)","restact_code":"object-binding",…}
```
 With authentication on, `RESTACT_OBJECT_BINDING` defaults to `strict`: a verb can bind only to an object that already exists. Create the object first, then deploy: 
```sh
curl -X PUT -H "Authorization: Bearer $TOKEN" -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
  -d '{"name":"mine"}' localhost:8080/v1/mine
```
 For a wildcard route such as `ws/{id}:action`, create the collection root (`ws`) first. Property values on the data plane are typed, so `{"a":1}` is stored as a number; a value outside the rules (a number beyond ±(2^53−1) such as `1e21`, nesting past 64 levels, a non-string `$owner`) is refused with `400` `invalid-input`. ### Recovering from `ancestor-claim` 
```json
{"detail":"cannot create 'area/x': objects or verb definitions at or below it might belong to another principal, …","restact_code":"ancestor-claim",…}
```
 With the ancestor-creation guard on, nobody may create an object above something another principal owns. The refusal is not retryable. The host’s operator log names the first foreign object or definition that blocked it (`ancestor-claim: refused create of …`). A store written before the guard can already hold such claims. 1. List them with `restactd audit ancestry` (see [Audit a store for ancestor claims](/docs/operate/storage/#audit-a-store-for-ancestor-claims)). It runs beside a live server and changes nothing. Run it with the server’s environment and working directory, so it applies the same guard settings and finds the same bootstrap files; bootstrap state shows as `host-bootstrap` and is not counted (the host seeds example content only with `RESTACT_EXAMPLES=1`, and the audit follows the same variable). 2. For a refused create, pick one remedy: use a different path; have the descendants’ owners delete first; or have the operator create the object directly (an `RESTACT_ADMIN_REOWN` subject sends `X-Restact-Operator-Exempt: ancestor-claim`), then reown it, without `subtree`, to the intended owner. 3. For a claimed ancestor or an orphan definition in the audit, `POST /admin/reown` the ancestor to its rightful owner. With the server stopped, or no `RESTACT_ADMIN_REOWN` subject, run `restactd reown` instead (see [Move ownership offline](/docs/operate/storage/#move-ownership-offline)). An orphan definition is reowned with `subtree: true`, or by reowning its anchor path, which also selects the deployments anchored there. Reown pauses the object’s lifecycle hooks and withdraws runs-as-owner. 4. If a same-level pattern pair is listed, accept the new winner (the leftmost literal) or delete one of the two verbs. A deleted root has to be re-created before its owner’s descendants can be reached again. The only exemption source is `RESTACT_ADMIN_REOWN`. A writer who owns 10,000 or more objects or verb definitions at or below an absent path is always refused at that path, and the operator log says `check limit reached`; the operator creates the path, then reowns it (not `subtree`) to the writer. ### `500 verb-unlinkable` when invoking a verb 
```json
{"detail":"the stored verb 'docs/1:summarize' no longer links against this host (import rest-act:plugin/retired@0.1.0); redeploy it against this host or delete it","restact_code":"verb-unlinkable",…}
```
 The stored component imports an interface this host no longer provides, or provides with a different signature, usually after a host upgrade. The verb never ran. The host also writes one line per verb to stderr, `verb-unlinkable: route=<route> import=<import>`: at startup when the stored import names show a removed interface, and on the first call when only linking shows a changed signature. The verb stays in discovery. Redeploy it built against this host, or delete it. ### `405` when invoking a verb 
```json
{"detail":"verb is a Action; invoke it with POST","restact_code":"method-not-allowed",…}
```
 A `function` verb is invoked with `GET`, an `action` with `POST`. Match the method to the verb’s kind. ### `415` on a JavaScript or Python upload 
```json
{"detail":"no runtime for source language 'text/javascript'","restact_code":"unsupported-language","status":415,…}
```
 The host started without the scripting tier because the runner component was not found. With `RESTACT_EXAMPLES=1` you will also see warnings like `could not bootstrap JS example 'hello:greet': unsupported source language: text/javascript`. Build the runner and point the host at it: 
```sh
./scripts/build-js-runner.sh      # QuickJS runner, needs cargo-component
./scripts/build-py-runner.sh      # Python runner
```
 From a release archive, keep `share/restactd/runners/` next to `bin/` (see [Install](/docs/start/install/#unpack-and-run)); a `restactd` copied out on its own has no runners and starts without the scripting tiers. An installed binary (one whose directory has a sibling `share/restactd/`) looks only under `share/restactd/runners/` and never in its working directory. A binary run from a source checkout looks at `examples/js-runner/target/wasm32-wasip2/release/js_runner.wasm` and `examples/py-runner/…/py_runner.wasm` relative to its working directory. `RESTACT_JS_RUNNER_WASM` and `RESTACT_PY_RUNNER_WASM` override the paths; the first boot line (`layout: installed (share …)` or `layout: checkout (cwd …)`) says which rule applied, and a `javascript:` or `python:` line says what happened to each tier. The Docker image includes both unless built with `make docker-build-core`. A tier can also be off because a runner was refused. If the log says a runner’s sha256 does not match its pin, the file at the path is not the one the pin names. A release build pins the runners it shipped with, so a replaced file under `share/restactd/runners/` leaves the tier off: restore the shipped file, or set both `RESTACT_JS_RUNNER_WASM` (or `RESTACT_PY_RUNNER_WASM`) and the matching `RESTACT_JS_RUNNER_SHA` (or `RESTACT_PY_RUNNER_SHA`) to run a different runner. The warning reads: 
```text
warning: JS runner at <path> sha256 is <actual> but this release build pins <expected>; refusing to register (JavaScript tier disabled); to run a different runner set both RESTACT_JS_RUNNER_WASM and RESTACT_JS_RUNNER_SHA
```
 A refused or unreadable runner withdraws the tier: discovery drops the language and a new deploy returns `415`, while verbs already deployed keep running with the runner they were deployed with. That holds on the filesystem store and on PostgreSQL alike. The boot line is `javascript: off (<reason>)` when the tier is gone and `javascript: stored runner kept (<reason>)` only when no pin is set, the default file is absent or cannot be registered, and a stored runner still serves it. If the layout line reads `checkout` for a binary you installed, the executable could not be resolved at start (for example it was replaced while running); the warning above the line gives the reason, and restarting the process fixes it. ### `422 invalid-source` on a JavaScript or Python upload The script failed the syntax check at deploy time: 
```json
{"detail":"Error: invalid property name\n    at verb:1:19\n","restact_code":"invalid-source","status":422,"title":"Unprocessable Entity",…}
```
 The `detail` carries the interpreter’s message and position. Fix the script and upload again. Runtime errors appear later, at invocation, as `script-error`. ### `501` feature not supported 
```json
{"detail":"server schedules are disabled (RESTACT_DISABLE_SCHEDULES)","restact_code":"unsupported-feature","status":501,…}
```
 The request needs a feature this host has turned off or does not have. The `detail` names the switch. Check what the host advertises before you rely on a feature: 
```sh
curl -s localhost:8080/.well-known/restact | jq '.service.features'
```
 If the feature is missing from the list, the host does not support it. If it is disabled by an environment variable the message names, remove that variable. ### curl changes the `{id}` in a route 
```sh
curl -X POST --data-binary @verb.wasm 'localhost:8080/v1/modules?verb=things/{id}:poke&kind=function'
```
 This deploys the verb as `things/id:poke`. curl treats `{id}` as a glob set and sends only `id`. The same happens when you call `GET /v1/things/{id}:poke`. Pass `-g` (globoff), or URL-encode the braces as `%7B` and `%7D`: 
```sh
curl -g -X POST --data-binary @verb.wasm 'localhost:8080/v1/modules?verb=things/{id}:poke&kind=function'
```
 Remove the wrongly named verb with `DELETE /v1/things/id:poke/code` (answers `204`), then deploy again with `-g`. ## Deploy validation errors `POST /v1/modules` rejects a bad upload with a specific code: | Status | `restact_code` | Cause | | ------ | ---------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `400` | `invalid-component` | The body is not a WebAssembly component (`not a valid WebAssembly component: failed to parse WebAssembly module`), or it lacks the `invoke` export of the verb world (the detail names the export). Build with `cargo component build --release`, or with the SDK’s `cargo build --target wasm32-wasip2`. | | `400` | `invalid-input` | A bad query parameter. Examples: `exec must be 'public' or 'owner' (got 'nobody')`; `verb name 'b_x' is not a valid identifier (camelCase: [a-z][a-zA-Z0-9]*)`. | | `400` | (none) | The `verb` query parameter is missing: `Failed to deserialize query string: missing field `verb“. | | `401` | `unauthorized` | Anonymous deploy on a host with auth configured. | | `403` | `disallowed-import` | The component imports something outside the sandbox, such as sockets or filesystem access beyond the allow-list, or an interface the host does not provide (the detail names it). Remove the import. | | `409` | `object-binding` | Strict binding and the object does not exist; see above. | | `412` | `precondition-failed` | You sent `If-None-Match: *` and the route already holds a verb. | | `413` | `module-too-large` | The upload is over the 10 MiB module limit (`module is N bytes; limit is 10485760`). A request body over 12 MiB is refused earlier by the HTTP layer with a plain-text `413`, not a problem document. | | `415` | `unsupported-language` | No runtime for that source language; see above. | | `422` | `invalid-source` | The script has a syntax error. | Verb names are camelCase identifiers: letters and digits, starting with a lowercase letter, no underscores or hyphens. ## Budget exhaustion Every invocation runs under fuel, wall-clock, memory and quota limits. The status and `restact_code` tell you which one: | Status | `restact_code` | Body `detail` | Meaning and fix | | ------ | ------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `429` | `cpu-budget` | `CPU budget exhausted` | The verb used its fuel. It was deployed with a low `fuel=`, or the verb needs more. Redeploy with a higher `fuel=`, up to `RESTACT_MAX_VERB_FUEL`. A composed call tree shares one budget (`shared request compute budget exhausted`). | | `429` | `quota` | `per-tenant calls quota exhausted` | The caller used up a cap; the `detail` names the dimension. For calls and fuel (`RESTACT_QUOTA_CALLS`, `RESTACT_QUOTA_FUEL`), `RESTACT_QUOTA_WINDOW_SECS` makes the cap a per-window budget that resets with the window. Without a window the cap is a lifetime total: on the in-memory and filesystem stores it clears when the host restarts, on PostgreSQL it persists in the database until the state is reset. Storage bytes and the object count (`RESTACT_QUOTA_STORAGE_BYTES`, `RESTACT_QUOTA_OBJECTS`) are never windowed; delete objects or blobs to free budget. | | `408` | `timeout` | `wall-clock deadline exceeded` | The verb ran past its `wall_ms` (ceiling `RESTACT_MAX_VERB_WALL_MS`, default 30 seconds). The same code covers `RESTACT_REQUEST_TIMEOUT_MS` and a request body that arrives too slowly. | | `507` | `memory-limit` | `memory limit exceeded` | The verb needed more than its `mem_mb` (ceiling `RESTACT_MAX_VERB_MEMORY_MB`, default 512). | | `508` | `composition-cycle` | | A verb called itself, or composition went too deep or too wide. | | `503` | `unavailable` | `too many concurrent event subscriptions`, `backing store is unreachable` | `RESTACT_MAX_SSE_STREAMS` reached, or `/readyz` reporting the store down. Retry later; the first case clears when a subscriber disconnects. | The per-verb limits are set at deploy time, with `fuel`, `mem_mb` and `wall_ms` query parameters on `POST /v1/modules`. The host clamps each to its ceiling, so a request above the ceiling is silently lowered, not refused. A real example, a verb deployed with `fuel=1`: 
```json
{"detail":"CPU budget exhausted","restact_code":"cpu-budget","restact_verb":":b1","status":429,"title":"Too Many Requests",…}
```
 and with `mem_mb=1`: 
```json
{"detail":"memory limit exceeded","restact_code":"memory-limit","restact_verb":":b4","status":507,"title":"Insufficient Storage",…}
```
 ## Still stuck Turn on more logging and look at the line for your request: 
```sh
RESTACT_LOG=debug RESTACT_ACCESS_LOG=1 RESTACT_ACCESS_LOG_INVOCATIONS=1 restactd
```
 Then see [Observability](/docs/operate/observability/). For a suspected vulnerability, report it privately as described in [Reporting a vulnerability](/docs/architecture/security-model/#reporting-a-vulnerability), not in a public issue.

# Changelog

> All notable changes to this project are recorded here. The format follows Keep a Changelog.

All notable changes to this project are recorded here. The format follows [Keep a Changelog](https://keepachangelog.com/en/1.1.0/). ## \[Unreleased] First public release of restactd, the reference host for the REST:ACT protocol, with the `restact` client, the `restact mcp` bridge, the Rust SDK, the conformance runner and the agent plugins. The project is pre-1.0 and the HTTP surface may still change. ### Added * A client calls a function with `GET /v1/<object>:<verb>` and an action with `POST /v1/<object>:<verb>`, reads and writes an object’s data with `GET` and `PUT /v1/<object>`, and learns what a server offers from the discovery document at `/.well-known/restact`. Discovery and `/openapi.json` list only the verbs the caller may read, invoke or manage. Errors are problem documents. Optional features are advertised in discovery and a client uses only what it finds there. A long action can run asynchronously when the client sends `Prefer: respond-async`, and a presentation verb returns a declarative UI document (`application/vnd.restact.ui+json`) that a client renders. The host serves a generated OpenAPI 3.1 document of its live surface at `/openapi.json` and can merge an operator-written base document into it. * The reference host, `restactd`, is written in Rust. Each call runs in Wasmtime with 200,000,000 fuel, 2 seconds of wall-clock time and 32 MiB of memory by default. A verb can ask for more at deploy time, up to the operator’s ceilings. A call that runs out of fuel ends with `429`, one that runs out of time with `408`, and one that runs out of memory with `507`. Calls compose under one shared budget and run as the caller unless the verb is deployed to run as its owner. Two executor backends are available, in-process and a per-request worker process (`RESTACT_ISOLATION=process`). * Verbs come in three tiers. A WebAssembly component for `wasm32-wasip2` can come from any toolchain that targets the published WIT package. JavaScript verbs are uploaded as source and interpreted by a shared QuickJS runner component, and Python verbs by a shared monty runner component, with no per-upload compilation. Both runners ship in the host archive. A verb can declare input and output schemas that discovery and the OpenAPI document carry, and a caller with write access to a verb can read its history and roll it back over HTTP. * A verb reaches only the interfaces it imports. `identity` reads the caller. `object` reads and writes the data of the object the verb is bound to and of other objects by path, and runs a paged, ACL-filtered `query`. `compose` calls another verb, `event` publishes to a topic, and `llm` asks for one bounded model completion through an owner-governed connector. A JavaScript or Python verb is granted `llm` only on a host that offers `llm-connector`. No interface opens a socket. Outbound calls go only to destinations on the operator’s allowlist, with credentials held by the host. * Object data is a noun of its own. Properties can hold any JSON value, objects inherit properties and carry a per-object policy, accessor verbs (`:get`, `:list`, `:update`, `:set`, `:create`, `:delete`) can take over an object’s reads and writes, and binary values are stored as content-addressed blobs. The data plane supports conditional requests with `ETag`, a `null` member in a `PUT` removes a property, and objects can fire lifecycle hooks (`onCreate`, `onChange`, `onDelete`). Schedules and topic subscriptions are registered as resources, events stream over server-sent events, and an optional consistency mode commits a call’s writes together. * The filesystem backend is durable, holds an exclusive lock on its data directory and supports checkpoints. The PostgreSQL backend, in the `-full` build, lets several nodes share one database and refuses plaintext connections unless an operator opts in. Without either, data lives in memory. `restactd checkpoint` and `restactd restore` snapshot and replace a filesystem store, `restactd reown` moves one subject’s verbs and objects to another, `restactd audit ancestry` reports claimed ancestors and orphaned definitions, and `restactd healthcheck` probes `/healthz` or `/readyz` for a container health check. * Authentication accepts HS256 bearer tokens, OIDC with JWKS verification (RS256 and ES256) and a trusted identity header for a proxy that sets one. Once any of them is configured, an anonymous caller cannot deploy or manage verbs or write object data directly. Reads and public actions stay open, and an action deployed to run as its owner can still write on an anonymous caller’s behalf. Roles, scopes, groups and claims reach verbs through `identity`, and a delegation model covers sub-tokens, grants and token exchange. `restactd token mint` signs an HS256 token with the `RESTACT_JWT_SECRET` in its environment. * The host checks the `Host` header against an allowlist, refuses cross-origin requests for unsafe methods and `/mcp`, cuts off slow request bodies, and binds to loopback by default. Operators can set per-tenant quotas and an object cap per owner, turn on an access log, and probe `/healthz` and `/readyz`. The `-full` build adds OpenTelemetry export and an OpenBao secrets backend. * The `restact` command line client keeps named server contexts, deploys, calls and removes verbs, reads and writes object data, lists what a server holds, follows events, shows a verb’s history and rolls it back, and runs admin operations. * `restact mcp` is an MCP server over stdio or Streamable HTTP that turns a server’s discovered verbs into tools, with built-in deploy, admin and blob tools. The host can also mount an MCP endpoint at `/mcp` with `RESTACT_MCP=1`. * The `restact-sdk` Rust crate lets a verb be written as a trait implementation and built with plain `cargo build --target wasm32-wasip2`. The repository has example verbs in Rust, JavaScript and Python, and editor types for script verbs. * restactd is also a Rust library. A server that embeds it adds native verbs and custom HTTP routes with `HostBuilder::register_native_verb` and `register_route`. The crate is not on crates.io, so depend on it by git URL or path. * `restact-client` and `restact-builder` are Claude Code plugins in this repository’s marketplace, and the same skills install for Codex CLI and Cursor. * `restact-conformance` is a black-box test runner. It reads a server’s discovery document and checks the required behavior plus each optional feature the server advertises, against any compliant server. * Each release publishes archives of `restactd`, `restactd-full`, `restact` and `restact-conformance` for Linux (x86-64 and arm64) and Apple-silicon macOS, a runners archive, a documentation archive, `checksums.txt`, and slim and `-full` container images at `ghcr.io/stackumi/restactd`. * restactd is licensed under Apache-2.0, and contributions need a Developer Certificate of Origin sign-off. * U+0000 is refused on every write, on every store. A string or member name holding it in a property value, a JSON request body, a property name, an object path or a deploy parameter is `400` `invalid-input` (`invalid-input` in a verb), a URL path containing `%00` is `400` on every method, and a grant is refused for a credential whose roles, groups, scopes or claims hold it. All three stores refuse it the same way. A U+0000 in an async operation’s recorded error is shown as U+FFFD. Data a store already holds keeps loading. ### Compatibility * The `rest-act:plugin@0.1.0` WIT package is frozen from this release. New capabilities arrive as additions to the same package, so a component built against it keeps linking. A breaking change needs a new package version. ### Known limits * The specification is an Internet-Draft. It has not been submitted to the IETF and can change, so the protocol core is not stable yet. * The full test suite runs on x86-64 Linux. The arm64 Linux and macOS archives are built on their own platforms, and the host archives are smoke-tested there. Other platforms, Intel Macs included, build from source untested, and Windows is not supported. * Nobody outside the project has audited the sandbox. The host, Wasmtime, the two runner components and any native verbs an embedder registers are in the trusted base, and process isolation is not a second sandbox. The limits page in the documentation (`docs/project/limits.md` in the source tree) has the details. * Storage and egress quotas are counted per process, so with several nodes the effective cap is the configured value times the node count.

# Contributing

> restactd welcomes bug reports, fixes, new examples and documentation changes. This page covers how to propose a change and what a change has to satisfy before it merges.

restactd welcomes bug reports, fixes, new examples and documentation changes. This page covers how to propose a change and what a change has to satisfy before it merges. ## Report a problem or propose a change Open an issue at [github.com/stackumi/restactd/issues](https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/issues). A bug report is most useful with the request you sent, the response you got, the response you expected, and the `GET /.well-known/restact` document of the host. For a larger change, open an issue first and describe the problem before the solution, so the design can be agreed before you write code. Report a security problem privately, not in a public issue. See [`SECURITY.md`](https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/blob/main/SECURITY.md). ## Set up [Development setup](/docs/project/development/) lists the toolchain, the `make` targets and the test suites. The short version: 
```sh
git clone https://github.com/<you>/restactd   # your fork
cd restactd
git remote add upstream https://github.com/stackumi/restactd
./scripts/bootstrap.sh     # Rust toolchain, wasm target, cargo-component and friends
make build-all             # host, example verbs, and both scripting-tier runners
make run                   # serves http://127.0.0.1:8080
```
 `./scripts/bootstrap.sh` also installs or checks the tools `make ci` and your first commit need (gitleaks, cargo-deny, cargo-nextest, shellcheck, node, actionlint, zizmor, PyYAML, jsonschema). Without gitleaks, the first `git commit` fails in the secret-scan hook that bootstrap enables. Rust is pinned to an exact version in `mise.toml`, `rust-toolchain.toml`, the workspace `rust-version` and the Dockerfile builder stage’s tag and digest. `make check-toolchain-pin` fails if they disagree. ## Formatting The repository has no auto-formatting, and `rustfmt.toml` turns `cargo fmt` into a no-op. Match the style of the code around your change; `.editorconfig` records the indentation. Clippy with warnings as errors is the style gate. ## Branch and pull request 1. Fork the repository and do your work on a branch of your fork, not on its `main`. 2. Keep a pull request to one concern. A change to behavior comes with its tests and its documentation in the same pull request. 3. In the description, name the issue the change touches: `Closes #N` for a full fix, `Part of #N` for a partial one. 4. CI runs on the pull request. A change that touches only Markdown pages directly in `docs/` (including `docs/README.md`), any file under a `docs/` subdirectory other than `docs/guides/rust.md` (which `make check-rust-snippets` compiles), `docs-site/`, most design records in `spec/decisions/`, or top-level Markdown and licence files skips the heavy jobs. A change to Markdown elsewhere in the tree (for example under `cli/`), to a non-Markdown file directly in `docs/`, or to a file such as `Makefile` or `Cargo.toml`, still runs them. ## Licence and sign-off restactd is licensed under the [Apache License, Version 2.0](https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/blob/main/LICENSE), and a contribution is licensed under the same terms (section 5 of the licence).
