Every commit must carry a `Signed-off-by:` line with your name and email, certifying the [Developer Certificate of Origin 1.1](https://developercertificate.org/): that you wrote the change, or otherwise have the right to submit it under the project’s licence. `git commit -s` adds the line; `git rebase --signoff $(git merge-base HEAD upstream/main)` adds it to the commits already on your branch, where `upstream` is this repository. ## Tests are the source of truth When you change observable behavior, extend the tests in the same change: * `./scripts/spike-smoke.sh` is the fast black-box suite for the security spine (invocation, limits, routing, upload allow-list, composition, auth). * `./scripts/conformance-test.sh` runs the [conformance suite](/docs/reference/conformance/). * The other `scripts/*-test.sh` suites cover one area each: auth, persistence, quotas, isolation, lifecycle hooks, schedules, egress and more. [Development setup](/docs/project/development/#test) lists them. Both the smoke suite and the conformance suite must pass on both executor backends, the in-process executor and the process-isolation worker. `make ci` runs most of the gates CI runs (about 9 to 17 minutes on an unchanged tree with a warm build directory, about 25 after a change to the host crate, and about 30 from a cold build directory; see [Development setup](/docs/project/development/#how-long-make-ci-takes)): clippy with warnings as errors, unit tests, dependency checks, the secrets scan and the lint for the agent plugins. It leaves out the black-box suites (`make suites`) and the otel, PostgreSQL and plugin end-to-end jobs (`make ci-otel`, `make postgres-test`, `make plugins-e2e`). ## Rules that protect compatibility * The WIT package `rest-act:plugin@0.1.0` is not frozen until outside adoption (the first public release, or earlier if the maintainer declares it), but a change to anything under `wit/` needs the maintainer’s approval first. Add a capability as a new interface or world in the same package, so compiled verbs keep linking. A change to an existing declaration touches every component built against the package and lands as one change set. After the freeze, existing declarations are never edited. See [WIT interfaces](/docs/reference/wit/#stability). * Every optional feature is advertised in discovery and works only when advertised. A new feature gets its own token, and the host answers `501` for a verb that requires an unsupported feature. See [Feature flags in discovery](/docs/reference/features/). * The `x-restact` JSON Schema in `openapi/x-restact-extension.schema.json` is strict. Extend it with a new `$defs` entry, then run `python3 openapi/scripts/validate.py`. * In the process-isolation worker, only the response goes to stdout. Write diagnostics with `eprintln!`. * The protocol itself lives in the [Specification repository](https://github.com/stackumi/restact). A change to the wire format, to a registry, or to the meaning of a feature token is a pull request there, and it needs agreement before the implementation follows. ## Secrets A pre-commit hook runs [gitleaks](https://github.com/gitleaks/gitleaks) over staged changes. Enable it once per clone with `make bootstrap` or `git config core.hooksPath .githooks`. CI also scans the full history (`make secrets-scan`). If the hook flags a real secret, unstage it and rotate it. If it flags a false positive, mark the line with `gitleaks:allow` and say why in the pull request. ## Design decisions Non-obvious design decisions are recorded as numbered Architecture Decision Records in the `spec/decisions/` directory, with an index in its README. A maintainer writes the record when a change needs one, so you do not need to draft an ADR with your pull request; the reviewer will say if one is needed. `make adr-check` validates the format of the records in the tree. ## Documentation These pages live under [`docs/`](https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/tree/main/docs) and are plain Markdown, readable on GitHub. If you change the HTTP surface, update [HTTP API](/docs/reference/http-api/) and, for configuration, [Configuration](/docs/reference/configuration/). The agent skills under `plugins/` carry cheat-sheets distilled from these pages; `make plugins-check` tells you when one needs a review.

# Development

> How to build, run, test and extend restactd. If you are new to the code, read Architecture first.

How to build, run, test and extend restactd. If you are new to the code, read [Architecture](/docs/architecture/overview/) first. ## Toolchain Rust is pinned to an exact version (currently 1.99.1). [`mise.toml`](https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/blob/main/mise.toml) is the source of truth; [`rust-toolchain.toml`](https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/blob/main/rust-toolchain.toml) mirrors it (plus the `wasm32-wasip2` target and `rustfmt`/`clippy`) for rustup-only callers and CI, and the workspace `rust-version` in `Cargo.toml` is its major.minor; the Dockerfile builder stage’s `rust:` tag and digest is the fourth site. `make check-toolchain-pin` fails if they disagree. To bump Rust, edit all four together and fix what the new clippy reports. CI installs the pin via `.github/actions/rust-toolchain`, never “newest stable”. The one-time setup: 
```sh
./scripts/bootstrap.sh
```
 This installs (idempotently, without sudo): the Rust toolchain and wasm target, the WebAssembly component tooling (`cargo-component` at the version CI uses and `wasm-tools` at the version `mise.toml` pins), and the git hooks. The tools `make ci` and your first `git commit` need are pinned in `mise.toml`: gitleaks (the pre-commit secret scan), cargo-deny, cargo-nextest (parallel `host-tests`), shellcheck, node, actionlint and zizmor. When [mise](https://mise.jdx.dev) is installed the script runs `mise install` for them; without mise it lists what is missing and where to get it. A tool counts as present only when it is on `PATH`, because `make` runs it from there: if mise installed it but your shell cannot see it, the script says so and exits non-zero. Add `eval "$(mise activate bash)"` to your shell rc (or put mise’s shims directory on `PATH`) and open a new shell. The Python gates (`openapi-validate`, `plugins-check`) need PyYAML and jsonschema, which the script installs with `pip` when they are absent. ### Formatting The repository has no auto-formatting. The Rust code is not rustfmt-formatted, and `rustfmt.toml` sets `disable_all_formatting = true` so `cargo fmt` and an editor’s format-on-save change nothing. A change matches the style of the code around it. `.editorconfig` records the indentation in use (four spaces for Rust, Python and WIT, two for shell, JavaScript, YAML and JSON, tabs in the Makefile). Clippy with `-D warnings` is the enforced style gate. ### How long `make ci` takes As a rough guide, on a machine with six CPUs and a warm build directory, the parts of `make ci` take: 1.5 minutes for the static guards, 1 to 5 for clippy and the unit tests, about 5 for restactd’s own suite (`host-tests`, plus about 3 for the runner and example builds it needs), 1 for cargo-deny and the secrets scan, and under 1 for the plugin lint. (`host-tests` runs the integration binaries concurrently under cargo-nextest.) That is about 9 to 17 minutes on an unchanged tree, and about 25 after a change to the host crate, which rebuilds the test binaries. From a cold build directory, expect about 30 minutes. A machine with fewer cores, including a standard GitHub-hosted runner, takes longer. `make suites`, the black-box suites, add about 35 minutes on top. ### Gotchas (read these first) * The host binds `127.0.0.1:8080`, so run one instance at a time. Restart it by killing whatever is listening on that port (`lsof -tiTCP:8080 -sTCP:LISTEN | xargs -r kill`), never `pkill -f target/debug/restactd`, which matches by binary path and kills every restactd on the machine. Run it in the background when it must persist across steps. The black-box suites take `RESTACT_TEST_PORT=<free port>` (default 8080; `RESTACT_CONFORMANCE_PORT` is an alias for `conformance-test.sh`) and clean up only a restactd listening on that port. * The workspace members are `host`, `host/cwasm`, `conformance` and `cli` (the `restact` client and its MCP bridge). `examples/` and `sdk/` are excluded: they build for `wasm32-wasip2` (with `cargo-component`, or plain `cargo build --target wasm32-wasip2` for the SDK), not the host toolchain. * The JavaScript tier is interpreted, not compiled. Build the trusted QuickJS runner component once with `scripts/build-js-runner.sh` (needs `cargo-component` + the `wasm32-wasip2` target; the `rquickjs` build fetches a WASI SDK and compiles QuickJS from C). A checkout that skips it just starts without the JS tier (discovery omits it, and an upload with `?lang=text/javascript` returns `415`). * With `curl` and bound-verb routes, pass `{id}` with `-g` (globoff) or URL-encoded as `%7Bid%7D`; otherwise curl’s glob expansion mangles the path. ## Make targets A top-level [`Makefile`](https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/blob/main/Makefile) wraps every command on this page: the cargo invocations, the `scripts/` suites, the CI gates, and the Docker build/run. Run `make` (or `make help`) for the grouped, self-documenting list. The raw commands below still work; the targets are just shortcuts. | Target | Runs | | --------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | | `make build` | `cargo build -p restactd`, then `cargo build -p restact` (the client), debug | | `make build-release` | `cargo build -p restactd --release` | | `make build-all` | host + the example verbs + both scripting-tier runners | | `make run` | `RESTACT_EXAMPLES=1 RESTACT_BUILTIN_SITE=1 cargo run -p restactd`, the host with the example content and the built-in site (`make kill` stops a running instance) | | `make test` / `make clippy` | workspace unit tests / the clippy lint gate | | `make ci` | most of the CI gate set locally: the static guards, the unsafe-code guard, clippy (default features plus `openbao`, `otel` and `postgres`, and `make clippy-guests` for the SDK and both runners), unit and host tests, cargo-deny, the secrets scan and the plugin checks. The black-box suites are `make suites` | | `make check-rust-snippets` | compiles the verb templates and the whole-crate Rust blocks of `docs/guides/rust.md` against the in-tree SDK (needs the `wasm32-wasip2` target). Mark a whole-crate block in that guide with `<!-- verb-crate: FEATURES -->` on the line before its fence | | `make smoke` / `make conformance` | the headline black-box suites | | `make suites` | the CI `suites` job: builds the runners and the example verbs, then restactd’s own tests, the black-box suites (the lane manifest, serial) and the MCP bridge suites; smoke and conformance run with the JS/Python tiers **required** | | `make docker-build` / `make docker-run` | build and run the container image (see [Docker](#docker)) | Variables are overridable, e.g. `make docker-run PORT=127.0.0.1:9090`. ## Build / run 
```sh
cargo build -p restactd          # or: make build
cargo run   -p restactd          # serves the API on :8080; `make run` adds the site
                                      # and the example content (RESTACT_BUILTIN_SITE=1, RESTACT_EXAMPLES=1)
```
 Build `restactd` and `restact` with separate commands. A single `cargo build -p restactd -p restact` merges the two packages’ dependency features, so the `cargo run -p restactd` after it recompiles about twenty shared crates. Build an example verb (from its own directory, since it’s outside the workspace): 
```sh
cd examples/word-count && cargo component build --release
```
 `make build-all` builds the host, the example verbs, and the JS/Python runners in one step, after which `make run` has the demo, docs, and both scripting tiers available. ## Test Tests are the source of truth. Most black-box scripts start their own copy of the host, so stop any running instance first. | Command | Covers | | ------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `./scripts/spike-smoke.sh` | The security spine end-to-end (\~290 assertions): invocation, limits, routing/taxonomy, the upload allow-list, composition, auth, idempotency, async ops, discovery/OPTIONS, data properties. Fast, local. | | `./scripts/conformance-test.sh` | The full [conformance suite](https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/tree/main/conformance), run against **both** the in-process and process-isolation backends. | | `./scripts/auth-test.sh` | HS256 bearer auth, `public`/`owner` policy matchers, runs-as-caller. | | `./scripts/persistence-test.sh` | An uploaded verb + its module survive a host restart (durable store). | | `./scripts/quota-test.sh` | Per-tenant invocation/compute quotas, fail-closed. | | `./scripts/isolation-test.sh` | The per-request process-isolation backend. | | `./scripts/egress-test.sh` | The mediated outbound call (feature `mediated-egress`): the boot-time configuration checks, a credentialed round trip, and that the secret never reaches the caller, the logs or stored objects. | | `./scripts/transport-test.sh` | The Host allowlist, the cross-origin (CSRF) check and the non-loopback bind interlock. | | `./scripts/body-deadline-test.sh` | The total request-body deadline: a trickled body is cut with 408. | | `./scripts/postgres-test.sh` | The optional PostgreSQL active-active backend: two nodes + a worker over one Postgres prove the cross-node guarantees. Needs the `postgres` feature + docker (or `RESTACT_DATABASE_URL`). | | `./scripts/bench-invoke.sh` | Informational micro-benchmark of the hot path: latency, cold start and memory per tier, with `oha` (see [Performance](/docs/operate/performance/)). **Not** pass/fail. | | `python3 openapi/scripts/validate.py` | The `x-restact` JSON Schema + every example + docs. | The conformance suite is the executable definition of “compliant”; the others target the reference host specifically. **Both must stay green on both executor backends.** ### Against an arbitrary server The `conformance-test.sh` wrapper builds and starts its own host on localhost and pins the runner to `http://127.0.0.1:$RESTACT_TEST_PORT` (default 8080), so it ignores an externally-set `RESTACT_BASE_URL`. To test an external server, run the runner binary directly (it honors `RESTACT_BASE_URL`): 
```sh
cargo build -p restact-conformance
RESTACT_BASE_URL=https://your-host ./target/debug/restact-conformance
# required: RESTACT_FIX_DEMO/_WC/_NET (fixture component paths the runner deploys; it exits 2
# if one is unset or unreadable)
# optional: RESTACT_CONFORMANCE_TOKEN=<bearer> (the default client), RESTACT_TOKEN_ALICE/BOB
# (the AUTHZ checks; empty → skipped), RESTACT_CONFORMANCE_NO_SETUP=1 (pre-deployed fixtures),
# RESTACT_FIX_COUNTER (JavaScript fixture; the check is skipped without it).
```
 ### Coverage Coverage is informational (no CI gate) and reported as two separate numbers, because a single percentage would conflate code that is verifiable in two different ways: | Command | What it measures | | -------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `make coverage` | Unit line coverage of the host crate with default features, from the in-process `cargo test` suite (`http_tests.rs` and the per-module `#[cfg(test)]` tests). | | `make coverage-functional` | Functional line coverage: the host binary is instrumented (`cargo llvm-cov show-env`) and driven through `postgres-test.sh` + `spike-smoke.sh`, capturing the running binary and its worker subprocesses. | The optional PostgreSQL backend (`host/src/pg/*.rs`) and the cross-node wiring in `lib.rs` (`open_backends` / `readyz` / the LISTEN-loop spawners / `build_worker_executor`) are not unit-testable in-process, because they need a real database or a separate worker process. A real-database “unit” test would exercise Postgres, not the type under test. So the PostgreSQL backend is excluded from the unit number (`make coverage` builds default features, so `pg/*.rs` isn’t compiled) and earns its confidence as a functional percentage instead. A module with thin or zero unit coverage must carry a correspondingly higher functional number. `pg/*.rs` should be near-fully covered by `make coverage-functional` (`postgres-test.sh` exercises it end to end), while a unit-heavy module like `policy.rs` leans on the unit number. When you add code that can only be reached cross-node or through a real database, add the assertion to the relevant black-box suite rather than reaching for a unit test that can’t exist. ## Docker The repo ships a self-contained multi-stage [`Dockerfile`](https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/blob/main/Dockerfile). The builder compiles the host plus the example verbs and both scripting-tier runners; the runtime image is a slim, non-root Debian that binds `0.0.0.0:8080` and persists to a `/data` volume. The image sets `RESTACT_ALLOWED_HOSTS=localhost`, so it answers only to `localhost`, `127.0.0.1` and `[::1]` until you override it with the names clients use. 
```sh
make docker-build                    # docker build -t ghcr.io/stackumi/restactd:dev . (IMAGE=restactd for a plain local name)
make docker-run                      # -p 127.0.0.1:8080:8080 (loopback; PORT=0.0.0.0:8080 exposes it), persisting to the `restact-data` volume
# raw equivalents:
docker build -t restactd:dev .
docker run --rm -p 127.0.0.1:8080:8080 -v restact-data:/data -e RESTACT_BUILTIN_SITE=1 restactd:dev
curl localhost:8080/healthz          # -> ok
```
 * The two scripting tiers are on by default. For a faster, lighter build that omits them, use `make docker-build-core` (`--build-arg WITH_JS=false --build-arg WITH_PY=false`); the host then starts without the JS/Python tiers, and `?lang=text/javascript` or `?lang=text/x-python` uploads return `415`. * The first (cold) build is slow: the JS runner downloads a WASI SDK and compiles QuickJS from C. * Configure the container with the same environment variables as a native run; see [Configuration](/docs/reference/configuration/#examples) for a Docker example and the full variable reference. * Release archives are built by the release workflow. `make release-local VERSION=0.1.0-dev` (a `*-dev` label, or exactly the workspace version) builds the same tarballs into `dist/`, smoke-tests them and runs the verify checks, and `make release-images VERSION=0.1.0-dev` builds the two images from them without pushing. The maintainer steps for cutting a release are in [Releasing](https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/blob/main/docs/project/releasing.md). * The `Dockerfile` build above is for development, and it targets `linux/amd64`. [`.github/workflows/docker.yml`](https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/blob/main/.github/workflows/docker.yml) runs it on demand as a build and boot check; it publishes nothing. Released images are assembled from the release tarballs by [`release/image.Dockerfile`](https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/blob/main/release/image.Dockerfile); see [Releasing](https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/blob/main/docs/project/releasing.md). ## Pull requests Before you open a pull request, run `make ci` and the suites that cover your change. A draft pull request runs only the light CI jobs: the static guards, the plugin lint and the supply-chain scans. The docs-site build ([`docs-site.yml`](https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/blob/main/.github/workflows/docs-site.yml)) also runs when the change touches its inputs. A pull request that is ready for review also runs the heavy jobs: clippy and the unit tests first, then restactd’s own tests and the black-box suites. Each later push runs them again, so a draft is a cheap place for work in progress. In the public repository every CI job runs on GitHub-hosted runners: pushes, scheduled and manual runs, and pull requests, forks included. The self-hosted runners serve only a private repository and are not registered to the public one. That registration is the control; the workflow files enforce nothing, because a pull request runs its own copy of them. The `runs-on` expression in [`ci.yml`](https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/blob/main/.github/workflows/ci.yml) only picks the runner for cost and speed, each self-hosted job opens with a step that fails if it finds itself on a non-hosted runner outside that private repository (a guard against our own misconfiguration), and `make check-runner-routing` keeps the expression and that step in place. The hosted runners are smaller than the self-hosted ones, so a timing-sensitive test can fail on a hosted run where it passes locally; re-run once before treating it as a regression. A workflow run from a fork waits for a maintainer to approve it, for every outside collaborator. Read the diff, including `.github/`, `scripts/` and `Makefile`, before approving: an approved run executes that code on a GitHub-hosted runner with a read-only token and no secrets. A ready pull request skips the heavy jobs when every file it changes is on the skip-list in [`scripts/ci/classify-changes.sh`](https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/blob/main/scripts/ci/classify-changes.sh): files under a `docs/` subdirectory (except `docs/guides/rust.md`, which `make check-rust-snippets` compiles) or `docs-site/`, top-level `docs/*.md` pages, most design records in `spec/decisions/`, and top-level Markdown and licence files. Test hosts seed fixture pages rather than the live ones, so prose changes need no heavy run. Markdown elsewhere in the tree (for example under `cli/`, which the bridge embeds) and non-Markdown files directly in `docs/` are not on the list. The otel, PostgreSQL and plugin end-to-end jobs, and the MCP bridge suites inside the `suites` job, run only when the change touches what they test. [`scripts/ci/classify-jobs.sh`](https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/blob/main/scripts/ci/classify-jobs.sh) decides this from the changed paths and, for otel and PostgreSQL, from whether a changed file contains `feature = "otel"` or `feature = "postgres"`. A push to `main` runs every CI job. The otel job runs only what the feature adds, not the whole suite a second time. [`scripts/ci/otel-tests.sh`](https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/blob/main/scripts/ci/otel-tests.sh) runs: * the lib tests that exist with `--features otel` and not without it (the diff of two `--lib -- --list` runs, computed each time, so a new otel-only test is picked up by construction and an empty diff fails the job), plus the `telemetry::` and `spans_tests::` modules whole; * each `host/tests` binary (about 69) whose source mentions `otel` in any case, every `adr0100_*` binary, and every binary that uses a helper module (a dir with no `main.rs`, such as `support/`) that mentions it. Binaries in `scripts/ci/thread-pins.sh` keep their thread pin. Every other test already runs without the feature in `host-tests.sh`, and `clippy --features otel --all-targets` compiles every target under it. `make ci-otel` runs the same script, and `make otel-tests-test` (a static gate) self-tests the binary selection. ### Suite lanes The `suites` job runs every black-box suite through one script, [`scripts/ci/lanes.sh`](https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/blob/main/scripts/ci/lanes.sh), from one manifest, [`scripts/ci/lanes.txt`](https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/blob/main/scripts/ci/lanes.txt); `make suites` runs the same pair, so local and CI runs cover the same list. `make check-lanes` (a static gate) fails when a `scripts/*-test.sh` suite is missing or listed twice, a fixed-port or shared-binary suite (the `restact mcp` suites, `cli-test.sh`, `plugins-e2e.sh`) is outside lane C, or the lane port blocks overlap. A new suite goes into the manifest or, with a reason, into the exclusion list in `scripts/ci/test-lanes.sh`. | Setting | Meaning | | --------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `RESTACT_LANES=1` (default) | every entry serially, in manifest order, on port 9171. | | `RESTACT_LANES=3` | the `warmup` entries first (the cargo builds, spike-smoke, the `restact` build variants; a failure stops the run), then lanes A, B and C concurrently on port blocks 9181, 9191 and 9201, each with its own `TMPDIR` under `<base>/<lane>/tmp`; `<base>` is `$RUNNER_TEMP/lanes` in CI and a fresh `mktemp -d "$TMPDIR/lanes.XXXXXX"` (printed) locally. | | repository variable `RESTACT_CI_LANES` | CI’s value for `RESTACT_LANES` (unset means 1). | | `RESTACT_LANES_ONLY=a,b`, `RESTACT_LANES_DRY_RUN=1` | run only those labels; print the plan (lane, port, `TMPDIR`, command) and exit. | | `RESTACT_GATE_MCP`, `RESTACT_GATE_PLUGINS_E2E` | `false` skips a gated entry. MCP runs unless `false` (CI passes the classifier’s result). The plugins-e2e entry runs only when its gate is `true` (default off, so `make suites` does not start it; CI runs `plugins-e2e` as a job of its own). | | `RESTACT_LANES_TIMEOUT` | per-suite timeout in seconds (default 1200, sized for a cold debug build in warmup). | No suite stops its lane at the first failure; the step fails if any suite failed or did not run. Each suite’s log is replayed in a collapsible group, a failure adds an error annotation with its last `FAIL` lines, the step summary lists wall time and CPU-throttling per suite, and a `FAIL` line mentioning 408 adds a warning (a likely sign that the host was starved of CPU). The lane logs (`*.log`, `*.rc`, `*.secs`; no data directories) are uploaded as the `suite-lanes` artifact for 7 days when the step fails, is cancelled or times out. Serial mode streams live. Local runs use ports 9171-9174, 9181-9184, 9191-9194 and 9201-9204, and the cleanup after each suite SIGTERMs a restactd listening on them, so do not hand-run a suite on those ports meanwhile. `RESTACT_LANES_PORTS="warmup=N A=N B=N C=N"` moves the blocks (the runner self-test uses 39171 and up). A suite that turns flaky only under lanes is moved to another lane or given a looser wall-clock margin, never retried. To try lanes on CI without changing the variable, run `ci.yml` by hand with its `lanes` input (`gh workflow run ci.yml -f lanes=3`); the input wins over the variable for that run only. ## Conventions & constraints * The WIT package `rest-act:plugin@0.1.0` ([`wit/`](https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/tree/main/wit)) becomes frozen at the first public release, or earlier if the maintainer declares it. Until then a change needs the maintainer’s approval, and a breaking one lands as a single change set across every component built against the package, including `host/wit/`, the packaged copy the host compiles against; `make check-host-wit` fails when it differs from `wit/`. New capabilities arrive as new, individually gated worlds and interfaces. See [WIT interfaces](/docs/reference/wit/#stability). * [`openapi/x-restact-extension.schema.json`](https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/tree/main/openapi) is `additionalProperties: false`. Extend it through new `$defs`, then run the validator. * Design decisions are recorded in `spec/decisions/`, indexed in its README. When you make a non-obvious design decision, add or extend a record. Deferred work is a [GitHub issue](https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/issues). * In the `RESTACT_WORKER` subprocess path, only the response goes to stdout. All diagnostics use `eprintln!` (stderr); never `println!` on that path. * Optional features are discovery-gated. Advertise them in `service.features`; a verb may require a feature, and the host returns `501` if it is unsupported. Keep new features optional and advertised. * Extend `scripts/spike-smoke.sh` (fast, local) and the conformance suite whenever observable behavior changes. ## Extending the host | You want to… | Start in… | | -------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------- | | Add or change a route | `host/src/lib.rs` (the axum router + `handle_v1` dispatch). | | Change how verbs resolve/bind | `host/src/registry.rs`. | | Add a capability or execution behavior | `host/src/wasm_exec.rs` (and a new gated WIT world in `wit/`). | | Add an executor backend | implement the `Executor` trait in `host/src/executors.rs`. | | Change storage | `host/src/store.rs` (the `Store` trait). | | Change auth/policy/quota | `host/src/auth.rs` / `policy.rs` / `quota.rs`. | | Add a conformance check | `conformance/src/` (gate it on the discovery document). | ## Repository layout | Path | Contents | | ------------------------ | ------------------------------------------------------------- | | `host/` | the `restactd` server crate | | `wit/` | the WIT worlds verbs are written against | | `sdk/` | `restact-sdk`, the Rust SDK for verbs | | `examples/` | example verbs and the JavaScript and Python runner components | | `site/` | the built-in static site and playground | | `conformance/` | the black-box conformance suite | | `cli/` | the `restact` client, including the `restact mcp` bridge | | `plugins/` | agent skills for Claude Code, Codex and Cursor | | `openapi/` | the `x-restact` OpenAPI extension schema and examples | | `spec/` | maintainer design notes | | `scripts/` | black-box test suites and build scripts | | `Makefile`, `Dockerfile` | the build, test and image entry points | ## Where things live * Concepts and the why: [`concepts.md`](https://github.com/stackumi/restact/blob/main/concepts.md) * The normative wire format: [`draft-byrd-restact-protocol-00.md`](https://github.com/stackumi/restact/blob/main/draft-byrd-restact-protocol-00.md) * Deferred work: [GitHub issues](https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/issues)

# FAQ

> Short answers to the questions people ask first, with a link to the page that has the detail.

Short answers to the questions people ask first, with a link to the page that has the detail. ## Is restactd production ready? Not yet. The HTTP surface can still change before 1.0. The WIT package that verbs compile against freezes at outside adoption, the first public release or earlier if the maintainer declares it. See [Status and roadmap](/docs/project/status/) for what freezes and what can still change, and the [Production checklist](/docs/operate/production/) if you run it anyway. ## What is a verb? A small WebAssembly program, or a JavaScript or Python script that the host interprets, uploaded over HTTP and called at a URL. `GET /v1/docs:summarize` calls a function, which cannot change state. `POST /v1/pages/home:visit` calls an action, which can. See [The model in one page](/docs/concepts/overview/). ## How is this different from serverless functions? A serverless platform deploys code for its operator, usually through a separate build and release pipeline, and the function runs with its own service identity. In restactd the client uploads the verb through the same HTTP API it calls, the verb is bound to a path in the object tree, and it runs as the caller: it can read and write only what the caller could. The comparison with other approaches is in the [specification repository](https://github.com/stackumi/restact#compared-with-other-approaches). ## How is it different from Spin or wasmCloud? Spin and wasmCloud run WebAssembly components that an operator deploys and routes. restactd is a server for the REST:ACT protocol: verbs are uploaded by API clients at runtime, addressed as `object:verb`, tied to stored object data, and subject to per-object access policy, one shared budget across composed calls, and discovery-advertised features. Pick by who deploys the code and who it runs as. ## How is it different from Extism? Extism is a plug-in system for an application: the application’s developers pick the integration points and its users or customers supply the plug-ins, which the application loads through an Extism host SDK. restactd is a standalone server with that upload path built in. Clients upload verbs over HTTP, and each verb is bound to an object path, runs as the caller and shares one budget when verbs call each other. Extism gives you a plug-in runtime to embed; restactd gives you a protocol and a server. ## How is it different from PostgREST or Supabase? PostgREST exposes Postgres functions over HTTP: `POST` runs any function, `GET` runs one that does not modify the database. That is close to restactd’s function and action split. The code is different, though. A PostgREST function is a Postgres function created in the database schema by someone with database access, and access follows database roles. A restactd verb is uploaded over HTTP at runtime in WebAssembly, JavaScript or Python, runs in a sandbox with fuel, time and memory limits, and sees only the capabilities its world grants. Supabase builds on PostgREST, and its own path for custom server code is Edge Functions: TypeScript on Deno, deployed with the Supabase CLI or dashboard and running on Supabase’s Edge Runtime, outside the database. Use PostgREST when the database is the application; use restactd when clients should be able to add behavior to a running server. ## How is it different from Hasura actions? A Hasura action is a GraphQL field backed by a REST endpoint you provide, whether your own server, a public API or a serverless function. Hasura routes the call; your service runs the code. In restactd the host itself runs the code, in its sandbox, and the code arrives over the same HTTP API that calls it. ## How is it different from PocketBase hooks? PocketBase hooks are JavaScript files, `*.pb.js`, that the operator places in a `pb_hooks` directory beside the executable. They run in an embedded interpreter as the application’s own code. restactd verbs are uploaded by API clients at runtime, bound to object paths and confined by capabilities, and the JavaScript tier is one of three language tiers. ## How is it different from Cloudflare Workers for Platforms? Workers for Platforms lets a platform run its customers’ code, each customer in its own isolated Worker on Cloudflare’s network. The problem is close to restactd’s, running code you do not trust, but it is a hosted service built around separate Workers per customer. restactd is software you run yourself, where uploaded verbs live in one object tree, run as the calling user, and can compose under one shared budget. ## Which languages can I write verbs in? * Rust, with the `restact-sdk` crate, compiled to a `wasm32-wasip2` component. See [Write verbs in Rust](/docs/guides/rust/). * JavaScript, uploaded as source and run by an embedded QuickJS runner. See [Write verbs in JavaScript](/docs/guides/javascript/). * Python, uploaded as source and run by an embedded interpreter. See [Write verbs in Python](/docs/guides/python/). * Any other language that can produce a component for the `rest-act:plugin` WIT package. See [WIT interfaces](/docs/reference/wit/). [Language tiers](/docs/concepts/language-tiers/) compares them. ## Can a verb make network calls? No. No world gives a verb sockets or a usable filesystem, and an uploaded verb cannot make an HTTP request. The one outbound path a verb has is the `llm` capability: the verb asks for a text completion, and the host sends it through an operator-configured connector that holds the credential. General outbound HTTP calls with stored credentials are available to Rust code that embeds restactd. See [Outbound calls and secrets](/docs/guides/egress/). ## How is untrusted code contained? Each call runs in a Wasmtime sandbox that can reach only the host functions its world imports. Fuel, wall-clock time and memory are limited per request; the defaults are 200,000,000 fuel units, 2 seconds and 32 MiB, and an uploader can ask for less or more only up to the operator’s ceilings. Optionally each request runs in a separate worker process. See [Capabilities and budgets](/docs/concepts/capabilities-and-budgets/) and the [Security model](/docs/architecture/security-model/). ## What does a call cost? Every call has a budget and is metered, so cost is bounded and visible. Composed calls draw from one shared budget, and per-tenant quotas can cap invocations and compute over a window. A call that runs out returns `429` (fuel or quota), `408` (time) or `507` (memory); see [Errors and status codes](/docs/reference/errors/). Metrics and traces are covered in [Observability](/docs/operate/observability/). ## Does it scale horizontally? Yes, with the PostgreSQL backend. Several stateless nodes behind a load balancer share one database. Without it the host keeps state in memory or in a local directory, which suits one node. A binary needs the `postgres` Cargo feature for this. See [Storage and scaling](/docs/operate/storage/). ## Where does it store data? In memory by default, which is lost on restart. Set `RESTACT_DATA_DIR` for a durable local store, or `RESTACT_DATABASE_URL` for PostgreSQL. See [Configuration](/docs/reference/configuration/). ## Can I embed it in my own Rust application? Yes. The host is a library as well as a binary. You can add your own trusted native verbs and HTTP routes next to the uploaded ones. See [Embed restactd in a Rust app](/docs/guides/embedding/). ## How do AI agents use it? Through the same HTTP API, through a built-in MCP endpoint at `/mcp` that the operator can turn on, or through the `restact mcp` bridge, which turns a host’s discovery document into MCP tools. An agent can deploy a verb, call it, and read its descriptor. See [Use restactd from AI agents](/docs/guides/ai-agents/). ## How do I know a server behaves correctly? Run the conformance suite against it. It reads the discovery document, checks the mandatory core, and checks each optional feature the server advertises. See [Conformance suite](/docs/reference/conformance/). ## Can I run a different server that speaks REST:ACT? Yes. restactd is the reference implementation of the protocol, and the protocol is specified separately in the [Specification repository](https://github.com/stackumi/restact). A client should rely only on what discovery advertises. ## How do I report a bug or contribute? See [Contributing](/docs/project/contributing/).

# Limits, and when not to use restactd

> restactd suits a particular job: letting clients add small sandboxed programs to a server that also holds the data those programs act on. This page lists where it stops being the right tool.

restactd suits a particular job: letting clients add small sandboxed programs to a server that also holds the data those programs act on. This page lists where it stops being the right tool. ## What it is not restactd is not a general function-as-a-service platform, a container runtime or a database. A verb is a WebAssembly component, or JavaScript or Python source that a shared runner interprets. It cannot start a process, open a socket or read a file. If you need a long-running service, arbitrary native code or a query planner, use something else and call it from your own code. See [Introduction](/docs/start/introduction/). ## One node or several The memory and filesystem stores belong to a single host process; two nodes must not share a data directory. To run several nodes behind a load balancer, use PostgreSQL. Even then the storage quota and the egress quotas are counted in each process, so with N nodes the effective cap is N times the configured value. Fuel and call quotas are shared through the database. Two other things to plan for with several nodes: every node needs the same authentication settings, and you back up PostgreSQL with the database’s own tools, because the checkpoint endpoint and `restactd checkpoint` are refused on PostgreSQL. The memory store writes a checkpoint dump, but only a filesystem checkpoint can be restored. The details are in [Storage and scaling](/docs/operate/storage/). ## What the sandbox does not cover The sandbox confines an uploaded verb. It does not protect against a defect in what the sandbox relies on: * The host itself, including its HTTP layer, policy engine and executor. * Wasmtime, the WebAssembly runtime. * The two runner components, QuickJS and monty. Every script verb shares one of them, so a defect there reaches all script verbs. * Native verbs that an embedding program registers. They run as trusted host code. No third party has audited restactd. The [security model](/docs/architecture/security-model/) describes the intended guarantees; treat them as claims that still need outside review. The operator still has to set up authentication, TLS in front of the server, the list of allowed host names, quotas and a durable store. With no authentication configured the server is open by design and meant for loopback. The [Production checklist](/docs/operate/production/) covers each of these. ## What process isolation buys With `RESTACT_ISOLATION=process`, each verb invocation runs in its own worker process, so one invocation cannot read another’s memory or the host’s. The worker is the same binary and opens the same store, and it applies the same limits and the same capability checks. It costs a process spawn and an engine start for every invocation, because there is no worker pool, and it needs a durable store: set `RESTACT_DATA_DIR` or `RESTACT_DATABASE_URL`, or the host refuses to start. It does not replace the sandbox. It also leaves things exposed. The worker opens the same store with the same credentials, runs as the same operating-system user and has no syscall filtering. A Wasmtime escape inside a worker is cut off from other invocations’ memory, but not from the store, the database credentials or anything else that user can read. Use it when untrusted tenants share the host, and skip it when the verbs come from people you trust and latency matters more. ## Default ceilings An uploaded verb gets 200,000,000 fuel units, 2 seconds of wall clock and 32 MiB of memory unless it asks for more, and the operator’s ceilings cap what it can ask for. A large JavaScript or Python source can use up the default fuel just loading, so it needs a higher `?fuel=`. A binary value is limited to 8 MiB by default. See [Capabilities and budgets](/docs/concepts/capabilities-and-budgets/#metering) and [Storage and scaling](/docs/operate/storage/#limits-that-bound-storage-and-load). ## Outbound calls A verb has no network access. The one outbound path an uploaded verb has is the `llm` interface, which the host sends through a connector, to a destination on the operator’s egress allowlist. Calls with stored credentials to other services are available to Rust code that embeds restactd. See [Outbound calls and secrets](/docs/guides/egress/). ## Telemetry restactd sends no telemetry. The server makes outbound connections only to places the operator configures: an OIDC issuer’s key set, an OpenBao secrets server, an OTLP collector, a PostgreSQL database, an `llm` connector’s endpoint and the egress allowlist. If none is configured, it makes none. The optional built-in website, off by default, loads its fonts from Google Fonts in the visitor’s browser.

# Stability and versioning

> restactd is pre-1.0. This page says which parts you can build on and which can still change. Status and roadmap gives the short version.

restactd is pre-1.0. This page says which parts you can build on and which can still change. [Status and roadmap](/docs/project/status/) gives the short version. ## What does not break ### Compiled verbs The WIT package `rest-act:plugin@0.1.0` freezes at outside adoption: the first public release, or earlier if the maintainer declares it. From then on an existing declaration is never removed or changed. A new capability arrives as an addition in the same package, and the host binds the superset of every world, so a verb compiled today keeps linking on a later host. The guarantee runs one way. A verb that imports a newer interface will not link on an older host, so check the feature token first. The rules are on the [WIT interfaces](/docs/reference/wit/#stability) page. ### Discovery An optional behaviour is advertised as a token in `service.features`, and a new capability gets a new token. A client checks for the token before it relies on the behaviour, so a host that lacks a feature is never mistaken for one that has it. The tokens are listed in [Feature flags in discovery](/docs/reference/features/). ## What can change before 1.0 * The HTTP management and admin routes. * The shape of verb descriptors, except the fields the draft requires: `route`, the verb’s `name` and `kind`, `binding`, `callGraph` and `capabilities` where the verb declares them, and `runsAs` when a verb runs as its owner. Other descriptor fields can change. * The Rust embedding API that [Embed restactd in a Rust app](/docs/guides/embedding/) describes. * Environment variable names and defaults in [Configuration](/docs/reference/configuration/). The core (the addressing grammar, the function and action methods, the data plane, problem documents and discovery) follows the draft specification and is checked by the [conformance suite](/docs/reference/conformance/). The draft itself is not an adopted standard and can change. ## Stored data and upgrades ### Filesystem store The data directory holds a `format.json` file naming the layout, currently `restact-fsstore/1`. A host refuses to open a directory whose stamp names another format or cannot be parsed. A directory with data but no stamp is adopted and stamped on first open. ### PostgreSQL The schema migrations are embedded in the binary and run at boot, once, under a database lock, so several nodes can start together. They only move forward: there are no down migrations. ### Downgrades Downgrades are not supported. Take a backup before you upgrade (see [Storage and scaling](/docs/operate/storage/#backup-and-restore)), and restore it if you need to go back to an older release. ## Which releases get fixes Before 1.0, only the latest release gets fixes. Until the first release is tagged, that means the `main` branch. Report a vulnerability as [SECURITY.md](https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/blob/main/SECURITY.md) describes.

# Status and roadmap

> restactd is pre-1.0 software. The WIT contract that compiled verbs depend on freezes at outside adoption, the first public release or earlier if the maintainer declares it; until then it changes…

restactd is pre-1.0 software. The WIT contract that compiled verbs depend on freezes at outside adoption, the first public release or earlier if the maintainer declares it; until then it changes rarely and only with the maintainer’s approval. The HTTP surface and the optional features are still moving. ## What freezes at release * The WIT package `rest-act:plugin@0.1.0`. After the freeze (the first public release, or earlier if the maintainer declares it) its declarations do not change, and new capabilities are added beside it, so a verb compiled then keeps linking. Before that, a change needs the maintainer’s approval. See [WIT interfaces](/docs/reference/wit/#stability). See also [Stability and versioning](/docs/project/stability/), [Limits, and when not to use restactd](/docs/project/limits/) and the [Glossary](/docs/reference/glossary/). ## What is checked The HTTP core follows the draft specification and is checked by the conformance suite: the `/v1/<object>:<verb>` addressing grammar, the function and action methods (`GET` and `POST`), the data plane for objects, problem-document errors, and the discovery document at `/.well-known/restact`. No release before 1.0 promises it. ## What can still change * The details of the HTTP surface outside the core, including management routes and the shape of descriptors. * The optional features. Each is advertised by a token in discovery, and a client must not assume one it has not seen advertised. The [feature table](/docs/reference/features/) says which are on by default and which an operator turns on. * Configuration names and defaults in [Configuration](/docs/reference/configuration/). ## The specification REST:ACT is specified in `draft-byrd-restact-protocol-00`, written as an Internet-Draft and not yet submitted to the IETF, in the [Specification repository](https://github.com/stackumi/restact) together with the registries (feature tokens, verb property names, capability interfaces, source languages). The draft is not an adopted standard and can change. restactd is the reference implementation, and the conformance suite is the executable form of the draft’s requirements. ## Releases and versioning Each `v` tag runs the release workflow, which publishes the archives, `checksums.txt` and two images; see [Install](/docs/start/install/). ## Roadmap Planned work is tracked as GitHub issues in the [restactd repository](https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/issues). Issues carry a type label and an area label. Search there for open work, or open an issue to propose something. See [Contributing](/docs/project/contributing/).

# The restact command line

> restact is the client for any REST:ACT server. It reads the server's discovery document, builds the URL for you, and turns the server's answer into an exit code. It never expands braces in a route,…

`restact` is the client for any REST:ACT server. It reads the server’s discovery document, builds the URL for you, and turns the server’s answer into an exit code. It never expands braces in a route, so `items/{id}:get` needs no `curl -g`. The server side is `restactd`, which has its own commands (see [Configuration](/docs/reference/configuration/) and [Operate](/docs/operate/production/)). Build it from source: 
```sh
cargo build -p restact          # target/debug/restact
cargo run -p restact -- --help  # or run it in place
```
 The examples below use a host on the default address, `http://127.0.0.1:8080`. ## Commands | Command | What it does | | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `restact context add NAME --url URL [--token-stdin] [--use] [--allow-plaintext]` | Add or update a named server. | | `restact context use NAME` | Make a context current. | | `restact context ls` | List contexts. | | `restact context rm NAME` | Remove a context and its stored token. | | `restact context show [NAME]` | Show a context (the current one by default). | | `restact discover [--raw]` | Show the server’s version, binding mode, features, accepted languages and verbs. `--raw` prints the discovery document. | | `restact deploy FILE --verb ROUTE:NAME [options]` | Deploy a verb from a `.wasm`, `.js` or `.py` file. | | `restact call ROUTE:VERB [options]` | Call a verb. A function is a `GET`, an action a `POST`, taken from discovery. | | `restact ls verbs [PREFIX]` | List the verbs the server advertises. | | `restact ls objects [--prefix P] [--filter K=V] [--limit N]` | List stored objects, following every page. | | `restact get PATH [-o FILE]` | Read an object, or a verb property such as `docs:summarize/policy`. | | `restact put PATH [FILE] [--content-type T] [--if-match ETAG]` | Store a file (or stdin) as an object. | | `restact rm PATH [--yes]` | Remove an object, or a verb when the path is `ROUTE:VERB`. | | `restact history ROUTE:VERB` | List a verb’s superseded versions. | | `restact rollback ROUTE:VERB --to N\|HASH [--yes]` | Restore an earlier version of a verb’s code. | | `restact events tail PATH [--after ID]` | Follow an object’s event stream. | | `restact admin reset --yes` | Wipe all tenant state. | | `restact admin checkpoint` | Ask the server for a durable checkpoint. | | `restact admin reown --from S --to S [--path P] [--subtree] [--dry-run] [--yes]` | Move the verbs and objects one subject owns to another. | | `restact mcp` | Serve the MCP bridge. See [below](#restact-mcp). | | `restact --version` | Print the version line. | `restact COMMAND --help` prints the options of each command. ### Global flags These work on every command. | Flag | Meaning | | ----------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `--url URL` | The server for this command only. No stored token is sent. | | `--context NAME` | Use this named context. | | `--json[=BOOL]` | Print one JSON document on stdout. A failure prints `{"error": {"exit": N, "message": "..."}}`, with `status` and `problem` added when the server answered. `events tail` prints one JSON event per line. | | `-v`, `--verbose` | Log each request line and response status on stderr. The token is never logged. | | `--timeout SECS` | Whole-request timeout, 30 by default. For `events tail` it is the idle timeout. | | `--max-bytes N` | Largest body, 64 MiB by default. It caps request bodies, response bodies and the total of a listing. | ### Deploy 
```sh
restact deploy hello.js --verb greeter:hello --kind function
restact deploy save.js --verb 'notes/{id}:save' --kind action
```
 The language comes from the file extension: `.wasm` is a compiled component, `.js` is `text/javascript`, `.py` is `text/x-python`. Source files are checked against the server’s accepted languages first, and a server that does not take that language gives exit 3. Any other extension is a usage error. | Option | Meaning | | --------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `--kind function\|action` | Left out, the server’s default applies. | | `--calls ROUTE:VERB` | A verb this one composes. Repeat it or separate the values with commas. | | `--produces TYPE`, `--description TEXT` | The output media type, and a description for people and models. | | `--input-schema FILE`, `--output-schema FILE` | A JSON Schema file for the verb’s input or output, sent compact as `input_schema`/`output_schema`. The file is read and parsed first (subject to `--max-bytes`; not JSON is exit 2). A server that does not advertise `verb-schemas` is refused with exit 3. Not allowed with `--update`. | | `--exec public\|owner` | Who may invoke the verb. The default is `owner`. | | `--deny CAP` | Withhold a capability: `object`, `compose`, `event` or `llm`. Repeatable. | | `--fuel N`, `--mem-mb N`, `--wall-ms N` | Per-call limits. | | `--runs-as owner` | Run with the owner’s authority. | | `--lib FILE` | Put a library file in front of a `.js` or `.py` source. Repeatable; each file is followed by a newline. | | `--update` | Replace the code of an existing verb. | | `--create-only` | Fail with `412` if the verb already exists. The server must advertise `verb-conditional-requests`. | `--update` and `--create-only` cannot be combined, and `--update` replaces only the code, so it refuses the descriptor options (`--kind` through `--runs-as`) with exit 2. `--lib` still applies. A route with `{id}` is passed as written. ### Call 
```sh
restact call greeter:hello --query input=Ada
restact call notes/todo:save --input '{"text":"call Sam"}'
restact call greeter:hello -o greeting.txt
```
 The method comes from the server’s discovery document, matched against the route the way a client would. A host lists a verb in discovery only to a caller who may read or invoke it, so a route missing from the listing may still be deployed. For such a route the command sends a credential-free `OPTIONS` and reads `Allow`: `GET` means a function and `POST` an action, and the call then goes out with your token, so a caller who may not use the verb gets the host’s own 401 or 403 (exit 4). A 404 to the probe is a not-found failure (exit 1). When the probe gives no single kind (a 405, 501 or an `Allow` naming both) or discovery lists the route with conflicting kinds, the command stops with exit 2 and asks for `--get` or `--post`; either flag skips the probe. A `GET` takes no body: pass parameters with `--query K=V`. For an action, the input is `--input TEXT`, `--input-file FILE`, or `-` for stdin. `--accept TYPE` and `--idempotency-key KEY` set the matching headers, and `-o FILE` writes the response body to a file. When stdout is piped or redirected, the response body goes out byte for byte, with no newline added. On a terminal, control characters are replaced, a newline is added if the body lacks one, and a binary body is refused unless you give `-o`. ### Objects 
```sh
restact put notes/x data.json
restact get notes/x
restact ls objects --prefix notes/ --limit 20
restact rm notes/x --yes
```
 `put` takes its type from `--content-type`; without it, a `.json` file is `application/json` and anything else is `application/octet-stream`. `rm` asks for confirmation on a terminal and needs `--yes` everywhere else. `ls objects` needs the server to advertise `object-listing`, `--prefix` also needs `object-listing-prefix`, and `--filter` needs `object-query`; otherwise the command exits 3. ## Choosing the server The target comes from the first of these that is set: 1. `--url` 2. `--context` 3. `RESTACT_URL` 4. `RESTACT_CONTEXT` 5. the config file’s `current-context` 6. `http://127.0.0.1:8080`, which gets no environment token (`RESTACT_TOKEN`, `RESTACT_MCP_BEARER`); `restact mcp` with `RESTACT_MCP_LISTEN` refuses to start on it A **context** is a name for a URL, with an optional stored token: 
```sh
restact context add prod --url https://restact.example.com --use
echo "$TOKEN" | restact context add prod --url https://restact.example.com --token-stdin
restact --context prod ls verbs
```
 Contexts live in `config.toml`, at `$RESTACT_CONFIG` if that is set, else `$XDG_CONFIG_HOME/restact/config.toml`, else `~/.config/restact/config.toml` (on macOS too). The tokens live in `credentials.toml` in the same directory, created with mode 0600. The directory is created 0700. There is no config file inside a project directory, so a cloned repository cannot point the CLI at another server. The config is TOML: 
```toml
current-context = "prod"


[contexts.prod]
url = "https://restact.example.com"


[contexts.lab]
url = "http://10.0.0.5:8080"
allow-plaintext = true
```
 ## Tokens A token comes from `RESTACT_TOKEN` first, then from the stored token of the selected context. There is no `--token` flag, because command-line arguments are visible to every local user. * An environment token goes only to a server someone chose. When nothing names one (no `--url`, `--context`, `RESTACT_URL`, `RESTACT_CONTEXT` or current context), the built-in `http://127.0.0.1:8080` gets no `RESTACT_TOKEN`: another local process may be listening there. The command runs without a token and prints one line on stderr naming `RESTACT_TOKEN` and saying to set `RESTACT_URL`. Writing `--url http://127.0.0.1:8080` or `RESTACT_URL=http://127.0.0.1:8080` is a chosen server and gets the token. * A stored token goes only to its own context’s URL. With `--url` or `RESTACT_URL`, only `RESTACT_TOKEN` is used, so `restact --url https://other.example get x` cannot leak the current context’s token. * The CLI refuses to send a token over plain `http://` to a host that is not loopback (`localhost`, `127.0.0.0/8`, `[::1]`). The refusal is exit 2. A context sets `allow-plaintext = true` (or `context add --allow-plaintext`) to accept the risk for that server. * `credentials.toml` is refused (exit 2) when other users can read or change it, when another user owns it, or when it is a symlink. * When a token would be sent to a URL that came from the config file, the config file must belong to you or to root and must not be writable by group or others. Otherwise whoever can edit it could redirect your token. The refusal is exit 2 and names the `chmod` to run. * Redirects are not followed. A `3xx` is reported with its `Location` and exits 1. * A token is never printed. `context show` says `token: stored`, and `-v` leaves out the `Authorization` header. To make an HS256 token for a server you operate, use `restactd token mint` (see [Authentication](/docs/guides/authentication/)). Pipe it into the context: 
```sh
restactd token mint --sub alice --ttl 1h | restact context add local --url http://127.0.0.1:8080 --token-stdin
```
 ## Exit codes | Code | Meaning | | ---- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | | 0 | Success. | | 1 | The server refused or failed the request (4xx or 5xx not listed below), or a 3xx was not followed. A verb that answers with an error also exits 1. | | 2 | Usage or local configuration error: a bad flag, a bad config file, an unsafe credentials file, a refused plaintext token, a missing `--yes`. | | 3 | The server does not support what you asked: a required feature is not advertised, a `501`, or a `404`/`405` where absence is not proof (deploy, verb properties), or a source language the server does not accept. | | 4 | Authentication or authorization: `401` or `403`. | | 5 | Transport: connect, TLS, timeout, a response over `--max-bytes`, or an unreadable response. A request body over `--max-bytes` (`put`, `deploy --lib`, stdin) is a usage error, exit 2. | | 130 | Interrupted. | The server’s problem document is printed on stderr, or inside the `--json` error object. ## Environment variables The client reads these. The server’s own `RESTACT_*` variables are in [Configuration](/docs/reference/configuration/); the two sets share no name. | Variable | Meaning | | ----------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | | `RESTACT_URL` | Server URL, below `--url` and `--context` in precedence. A target set this way gets no stored token. | | `RESTACT_TOKEN` | Bearer token. Beats the stored token. Not sent to the built-in default `http://127.0.0.1:8080`; set `RESTACT_URL` to send it. | | `RESTACT_CONTEXT` | Context name, below `--url`, `--context` and `RESTACT_URL`. | | `RESTACT_CONFIG` | Path of `config.toml`. `credentials.toml` sits next to it. | | `RESTACT_MCP_*` | The bridge’s settings, listed in the [bridge README](https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/blob/main/cli/docs/mcp/README.md#configuration). | `restactd` reads none of these. The one similar name `restactd` reads is `RESTACT_MCP` (exactly that name), which switches on the server’s own MCP facet and is documented in [Configuration](/docs/reference/configuration/). `restactd checkpoint` takes its bearer from `--token-stdin`, never from `RESTACT_TOKEN`. ## restact mcp `restact mcp` runs the MCP bridge: it serves MCP over stdio, or Streamable HTTP when `RESTACT_MCP_LISTEN` is set, and turns a REST:ACT server’s verbs into MCP tools. It takes `--url` and `--context` like every other command. When `RESTACT_MCP_HOST_URL` and `RESTACT_MCP_HOSTS` are unset, the bridge uses the selected context’s URL and its stored token. Under `RESTACT_MCP_LISTEN` the context supplies the URL only, because a token there would become the identity of every client that connects without its own. Every other `RESTACT_MCP_*` setting is described in the [bridge README](https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/blob/main/cli/docs/mcp/README.md). The bridge checks its configuration before it serves anything. A bad target URL, listen address, number, switch or local OpenAPI file exits 2 with nothing on stdout, and a message that never repeats a credential. The five switches (`RESTACT_MCP_ALLOW_PLAINTEXT`, `RESTACT_MCP_ALLOW_DEPLOY`, `RESTACT_MCP_ALLOW_DATA_WRITES`, `RESTACT_MCP_ALLOW_VERB_ADMIN`, `RESTACT_MCP_ALLOW_REGISTRATIONS`) take `1`, `true`, `on` or `yes` to turn on and `0`, `false`, `off` or `no` to turn off, in any case; any other value is an error. `RESTACT_MCP_FILE_ROOT` is not checked at startup. Each log and warning line the bridge writes to stderr starts with `restact mcp:`. A bridge refusal prints `restact mcp: <reason>` and exits with the CLI’s usual code, like every other command. The bridge ignores the global `--json`, `--timeout` and `--max-bytes`: stdout is the MCP wire, and `RESTACT_MCP_REQUEST_TIMEOUT_SECS` sets the request timeout. The MCP server still reports its name as `restact-mcp`, and the `restact-mcp:` resource URIs keep that prefix. ## Related * [HTTP API](/docs/reference/http-api/) for the requests `restact` sends. * [Quickstart](/docs/start/quickstart/) for the commands in use.

# Configuration

> The reference host is configured entirely through environment variables. This document lists every variable, explains the security posture, and gives a production checklist. The variables the…

The reference host is configured entirely through **environment variables**. This document lists every variable, explains the security posture, and gives a production checklist. The variables the `restact` client reads are in [the command line reference](/docs/reference/cli/#environment-variables). ## Environment variables ### Core | Variable | Default | Effect | | --------------------------- | ---------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | | `RESTACT_BIND` | `127.0.0.1:8080` | Bind address. Set `0.0.0.0:8080` to expose beyond localhost — **only with auth enabled** (see [Security](#security)). A non-loopback address also needs `RESTACT_ALLOWED_HOSTS`, or the host refuses to start. Use an IP literal (`127.0.0.1:8080`, `[::1]:8080`): a host name that resolves to more than one address, such as `localhost` on a dual-stack machine, is refused at boot. `restactd healthcheck` reads this variable to find the host to probe. | | `RESTACT_ALLOWED_HOSTS` | *(unset)* | Host names the server answers to, beyond the built-in `localhost`, `127.0.0.1` and `[::1]`, which are always accepted. A comma-separated list of DNS names or IP literals (IPv6 in brackets), matched case-insensitively against the request’s `Host` on any port. Any other `Host` gets `403 forbidden`, on every route and on `/mcp`, except `/healthz` and `/readyz`. This is the DNS-rebinding defense. An entry with a port, scheme, path, leading dot or wildcard is a boot error. `*` alone accepts any `Host` and prints a boot warning; use it only when a proxy in front pins `Host`. **Required when `RESTACT_BIND` is not a loopback address**: without it the host refuses to start. The container image sets `localhost`. A host behind a reverse proxy that forwards the public `Host` must list the public name. Deployment config, not advertised in discovery. | | `RESTACT_CORS_ALLOW_ORIGIN` | *(unset)* | Cross-origin access for browser clients. **Absent/empty → CORS off** (same-origin only — the bundled site/playground call same-origin and need nothing). Set to an exact origin (e.g. `http://localhost:8090`, or a comma-separated list) or `*` to emit `Access-Control-*` headers + answer preflight `OPTIONS`, so a browser client on another origin can call the API. Allowed methods GET/POST/PUT/PATCH/DELETE/OPTIONS; request headers `authorization`/`content-type`/`idempotency-key`/`if-match`/`if-none-match`/`prefer`/`last-event-id`; exposes `etag`/`location`/`restact-read-set`/`restact-read-set-coarse`. A configured origin list also admits `Origin: null` for a path-scoped sub-token’s cross-origin read. **No credentials reflected** (bearer-token auth, not cookies) — so `*` is legal and CORS grants no new authority. The same list drives the cross-origin check on `POST`/`PUT`/`PATCH`/`DELETE` and on every `/mcp` request: a browser request from another origin is refused with `403` unless its origin is listed here, or this is `*` and the request needed a CORS preflight (a JSON body or another non-simple `Content-Type`, or a method other than `POST`; an `Authorization` header does not count, since a browser or proxy can attach one without a preflight). A cross-site form or `text/plain` POST is refused even under `*`. Clients that send no `Origin` (curl, SDKs, servers) are unaffected. Deployment config, not advertised in discovery. | | `RESTACT_SITE_DIR` | `site` | Directory the static website + playground are read from when the site is on, and where the example-verb bootstrap finds `examples/` when `RESTACT_EXAMPLES=1`. An installed layout seeds the example verbs only when this variable is set. | | `RESTACT_BUILTIN_SITE` | *(unset)* | **Stock `restactd` binary only.** Set `1`/`true` (trimmed, case-insensitive; anything else, including a typo, stays off) to mount the built-in static website + playground and the `repo_doc` in-repo-docs fallback. **Off by default** — the reason is the same working-directory disclosure `repo_doc` serves (see [Security](#security)). Several playground panels and the docs page call the example verbs and `docs/` objects, so they need `RESTACT_EXAMPLES=1` as well and return 404 without it. Read only in `configure_host()`, never by `run()`/`serve()`, so it has **no effect on an embedding binary**, which uses `HostBuilder::with_builtin_site()` in code instead. | | `RESTACT_MCP` | *(off)* | Set `1`/`true` to mount the remote MCP facet at `/mcp` and advertise the `mcp-facet` discovery feature; unset, `/mcp` does not exist. Streamable HTTP, stateless (`GET /mcp` returns `405`). Every request authenticates through the ordinary identity chain, so an anonymous-open host serves the facet anonymous-open too; path-scoped sub-tokens and delegated tokens are refused. The tool set is fixed: `invoke_verb`, `list_verbs`, `list_objects`, `get_object` (no raw writes, no deploy). Each tool runs through the same dispatch, policy and quota path as the equivalent HTTP request. `/mcp` enforces `RESTACT_ALLOWED_HOSTS` and refuses a cross-origin `Origin` on every method, as described under `RESTACT_CORS_ALLOW_ORIGIN`. Implemented in `host/src/mcp_facet.rs`. | | `RESTACT_DATA_DIR` | *(unset)* | Enables the durable content-addressed `FsStore` at this path. **Absent → in-memory, ephemeral** (everything is lost on restart). **Set, but opening the store or loading its `page-token.key` fails for any reason** (an unwritable directory, or a corrupt `deployments.json`/`registrations.json`/`format.json`) **is a FATAL boot refusal** — matching a bad `RESTACT_DATABASE_URL` below; it never silently (or loudly) falls back to an ephemeral store, which would lose every write at the next restart. **One server per directory:** the host takes an exclusive lock on `<data-dir>/locks/data-dir.lock` before opening the store and holds it until it exits (isolation workers inherit it). A second host on the same directory refuses to start, exit 1, naming the holder. A filesystem that cannot lock (some network filesystems) logs one warning and the host serves unlocked, except under `RESTACT_ISOLATION=process`, where the host refuses to start. A directory holding `RESTORE-IN-PROGRESS.json` also refuses to start. | | `RESTACT_DATABASE_URL` | *(unset)* | Enables the optional **PostgreSQL active-active backend** — N stateless nodes behind a load balancer sharing one Postgres. Takes precedence over `RESTACT_DATA_DIR`. **Requires the `postgres` Cargo feature** (a binary built without it refuses to start). A bad URL is fatal. See [Clustering](#clustering--horizontal-scaling-postgresql) for the full `RESTACT_PG_*` knobs. | | `RESTACT_OPENAPI_SPEC` | *(unset)* | Path to an operator-authored OpenAPI 3.1 **base document** that `GET /openapi.json` merges with the live generated parts on every request: `info."x-restact"` and each verb’s `x-restact-verb` always stay live, a path/method the base already documents keeps the operator’s own operation object (only its descriptor is refreshed), and everything else — notably the data plane, which the generator never documents on its own — passes through untouched. `.yaml`/`.yml` parses as YAML, anything else as JSON. **Absent → the fully generated document.** A missing file or a syntax error is fatal at startup. See `openapi/examples/library.openapi.yaml` for a worked example. | ### Clustering / horizontal scaling (PostgreSQL) The default host is single-node — all shared state lives in-process. Setting `RESTACT_DATABASE_URL` (on a binary built with the `postgres` feature: `cargo build -p restactd --features postgres`) points N equal nodes at one PostgreSQL so object writes (advisory-lock-serialized — no lost updates), schedules (fire once cluster-wide, with failover), idempotency + async operations, quota, and SSE/events all span the cluster. When unset, the host uses the filesystem or in-memory store; PostgreSQL is a secondary backend, not a replacement. It is a storage/deployment choice and is **not** advertised in discovery. **Connection security is fail-safe (no silent plaintext).** The DB connection defaults to `sslmode=require`; the downgradeable modes (`disable`/`allow`/`prefer`) are **refused** unless `RESTACT_PG_ALLOW_PLAINTEXT=1` (the loopback/dev opt-out — a throwaway `postgres:16` has no TLS). Use `sslmode=verify-full` (with a CA) for MITM protection in production. **Degradation on a DB error.** The idempotency claim **fails closed** (`409`), and the cross-node quota read **fails open** (logged as `restact.pg.quota read_failed fail_open=true`). The prune is leader-elected: one node per tick, via `pg_try_advisory_lock`. The released `-full` image and a source build with `WITH_PG=true` (`make docker-build-pg`) include the PostgreSQL backend, and `scripts/postgres-test.sh` verifies the backend. Postgres has no analogue of the in-memory FIFO caps. The `event_log` is bounded on every insert, in bytes and in rows, and a background prune (leader-elected — one node per tick) bounds the idempotency and operations tables; the `RESTACT_PG_*` knobs below tune them and the connection pools. Set the event log bounds to the same values on every node. | Variable | Default | Effect | | --------------------------------- | ----------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `RESTACT_PG_ALLOW_PLAINTEXT` | *(unset)* | Boolean. `1`/`true`/`on`/`yes` (trimmed, case-insensitive) permit a plaintext/downgradeable DB link (`sslmode=disable`/`allow`/`prefer`); unset or `0`/`false`/`off`/`no` refuse it; any other value, including an empty one, **refuses boot** naming the variable. The loopback/dev/CI opt-out; never set it for a DB reached over an untrusted network. | | `RESTACT_PG_POOL_SIZE` | `16` | Query connection pool size (per node). | | `RESTACT_PG_LOCK_POOL_SIZE` | `16` | Advisory-lock-holder pool size — the **per-node concurrent-object-write ceiling** (each write holds one lock connection for its critical section). Raise it for write-heavy clusters. | | `RESTACT_PG_RUNTIME_THREADS` | `4` | Worker threads on the backend’s dedicated Tokio runtime. | | `RESTACT_PG_RESYNC_INTERVAL_SECS` | `60` | Whole seconds, 1 to 86400. How often a node reconciles its verb registry with the stored deployments and policies, whatever its LISTEN connection did; it bounds how long a node can serve a stale verb or policy after a lost notification. A tick reads the deployments table once, plus one single-row read per in-memory verb the table lacks, plus the code of a verb whose code changed. A second timer on the same interval reloads the schedule and subscription registrations from the database, holding the live registration table’s write lock across the read so a registration created while it runs is kept. A node also reconciles whenever its LISTEN connection is re-established, registrations included. Before each schedule or subscription firing a PostgreSQL node reads that registration’s row by primary key (one indexed read per firing, not per sweep), so a registration deleted on a peer node does not fire once the delete has returned 204. Any other value, `0` included, **refuses boot** naming the variable; there is no off switch. | | `RESTACT_PG_STATEMENT_TIMEOUT_MS` | `30000` | Server `statement_timeout` — bounds a stuck write body OR a hot-key advisory-lock wait. `0` disables the server-side bound (the `with_locks` calling thread is still bounded by `RESTACT_PG_OP_TIMEOUT_MS`, but a contended lock can then tie up a lock-pool connection until it drops — **not recommended**). | | `RESTACT_PG_ACQUIRE_TIMEOUT_MS` | `10000` | Pool-acquire timeout (the Nth concurrent writer past the lock pool fails fast instead of parking). | | `RESTACT_PG_OP_TIMEOUT_MS` | *(stmt timeout + 5000)* | Hard ceiling on how long a sync store call parks the calling thread waiting on the dedicated runtime/DB.
