Also backstops the `with_locks` advisory-lock acquire (the one PG path that doesn’t flow through the generic sync bridge). | | `RESTACT_PG_CONNECT_ATTEMPTS` | `5` | Bounded retry on a **transient** startup connect (a slow/restarting/failing-over DB), so a node doesn’t crash-loop. A bad URL / disallowed TLS mode is still fatal immediately. | | `RESTACT_PG_CONNECT_TIMEOUT_SECS` | `180` | Overall connect budget across the retry schedule. | | `RESTACT_PG_PRUNE_INTERVAL_SECS` | `300` | How often the leader node prunes idempotency keys and settled operations. The event log is not pruned here; it is bounded on insert, and subscription claim state is one row per subscription, so it needs no pruning. | | `RESTACT_PG_RETENTION_SECS` | `3600` | Age after which settled idempotency keys + async operations are pruned. | | `RESTACT_PG_EVENT_LOG_MAX_BYTES` | `67108864` (64 MiB) | Most bytes the shared `event_log` holds, counted as `RESTACT_EVENT_RING_MAX_BYTES` counts. Every publish deletes the oldest events until the new one fits, so the log never exceeds the larger of this value and the newest event. A positive whole number of bytes no larger than `i64::MAX` and no smaller than the largest event a verb can publish; any other value refuses to start, naming the variable. Not `RESTACT_EVENT_TOTAL_MAX_BYTES`, which sizes each node’s memory. | | `RESTACT_PG_EVENT_LOG_CAP` | `10000` | Most `event_log` rows kept for SSE replay, a **global** window across all topics: a hard cap checked at every insert alongside the byte bound, whichever binds first (a high-volume topic can evict a quiet topic’s backlog, which the quiet topic’s next resume reports as a `gap`). It also caps the per-topic bookkeeping that tells a resume what was trimmed. A positive whole number no larger than `i64::MAX`; `0`, junk or a larger value refuses to start, naming the variable. | > **Process isolation composes with PostgreSQL.** `RESTACT_ISOLATION=process` needs no data dir under PG: the worker subprocess inherits `RESTACT_DATABASE_URL` and shares the same database (a single lazy connection), so its `object.set`/`set-at` commits serialize cluster-wide through the same advisory locks; the parent owns the prune loop, the event transport and the quota debit. Size `max_connections` accordingly (see the checklist). ### Authentication & authorization restact resolves the caller’s identity through an **ordered chain**: a trusted forwarded-identity header (if configured) is consulted first, then a cryptographically-verified OIDC/JWKS bearer JWT (if configured), then an `Authorization: Bearer` HS256 token, then the anonymous fallback. The first source that yields a non-anonymous principal wins. | Variable | Default | Effect | | ----------------------------------- | --------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `RESTACT_TRUSTED_IDENTITY_HEADER` | *(unset)* | **Enable switch** for trusted forwarded identity (no signature check). Name of the request header carrying the subject (an email or user id) → `Principal.subject`. Trusted **at face value**; see the caveat below. Absent → feature off; set but blank or not valid Unicode refuses boot. | | `RESTACT_TRUSTED_ROLES_HEADER` | *(unset)* | Optional. Header with comma/whitespace-separated roles → `Principal.roles`. Needs `RESTACT_TRUSTED_IDENTITY_HEADER`; set without it, boot is refused. | | `RESTACT_TRUSTED_SCOPES_HEADER` | *(unset)* | Optional. Header with whitespace-separated scopes → `Principal.scopes`. Needs `RESTACT_TRUSTED_IDENTITY_HEADER`; set without it, boot is refused. | | `RESTACT_TRUSTED_GROUPS_HEADER` | *(unset)* | Optional. Header with comma/whitespace-separated groups → `Principal.groups` (for the `group:` policy matcher). Needs `RESTACT_TRUSTED_IDENTITY_HEADER`; set without it, boot is refused. | | `RESTACT_RESERVED_SUBJECT_PREFIXES` | *(unset)* | Comma-separated subject prefixes (entries trimmed, empty ones ignored), for example `agent:`. A subject from OIDC or the trusted identity header that starts with one is refused `401`; an HS256 bearer or sub-token may use it, which is how your own services authenticate inside the namespace. Combined with prefixes an embedder reserves through `HostBuilder::reserve_subject_prefix`; neither source can remove the other’s, and `RESTACT_EXTERNAL_SUBJECT_ALLOW` cannot lower them. End each prefix with its separator (`agent:`, not `agent`). An entry holding whitespace or a control character refuses boot (`fatal:`). | | `RESTACT_EXTERNAL_SUBJECT_ALLOW` | *(unset)* | Comma-separated tokens from `colon` and `whitespace` that relax the default character rules for subjects from OIDC and the trusted identity header. By default such a subject is refused `401` when it contains `:` (or the fullwidth colon U+FF1A), whitespace, or a Unicode format character. `colon` lifts the `:` rule (set it when your IdP issues colon subjects: GitHub Actions, GitLab CI or Kubernetes workload tokens, SPIFFE, Tailscale tsidp, Keycloak federation without import); `whitespace` lifts the whitespace rule. Control characters and format characters are always refused, and reserved prefixes still apply. An unknown token refuses boot. The boot log states the rules in force and each refusal logs its mechanism and rule class (never the subject). | | `RESTACT_OIDC_ISSUER` | *(unset)* | Expected `iss` claim. One of the three variables that enable OIDC/JWKS verification (`RESTACT_OIDC_ISSUER`, `RESTACT_OIDC_JWKS_URL`, `RESTACT_OIDC_AUDIENCE`); all three are required. Setting any `RESTACT_OIDC_*` variable, even to an empty value, without all three of issuer, JWKS URL and audience non-blank refuses boot and names the missing variables, so a host never starts with OIDC silently off. Confirm the boot line `auth: OIDC/JWKS verification enabled …`. | | `RESTACT_OIDC_JWKS_URL` | *(unset)* | URL of the issuer’s JWKS (signing keys). It must be `https://`: a remote plaintext URL refuses boot, because the JWKS is the trust root and a plaintext fetch can be tampered with. Plain `http://` is accepted only to a loopback host (`localhost`, any `127.0.0.0/8` address, or `::1`) for dev and test, and a URL that does not parse refuses boot. Redirects are not followed, so this must be the final URL. The key set is fetched with a size cap and cached (TTL plus a throttled refetch on an unknown `kid`); a failed fetch rejects tokens. | | `RESTACT_OIDC_AUDIENCE` | *(unset)* | Expected `aud` claim. **Audience validation is mandatory** — there is no way to disable it. | | `RESTACT_OIDC_JWT_HEADER` | `Authorization` | Optional. Request header carrying the token (a leading `Bearer `is stripped). An invalid header name refuses boot. Custom headers (e.g. a proxy-forwarded `X-Id-Token`) carry the raw JWT. | | `RESTACT_OIDC_SUBJECT_CLAIM` | `sub` | Optional. Claim mapped to `Principal.subject`. | | `RESTACT_OIDC_JWKS_TTL_SECS` | `3600` | Optional. JWKS cache TTL in seconds. A value that is not a whole number (empty included) refuses boot. | | `RESTACT_JWT_SECRET` | *(unset)* | Enables HS256 bearer-token auth and the `owner`/`public` policy. **Absent → anonymous-only** (see warning below); set but empty, blank, not valid Unicode or shorter than 32 bytes (the HS256 hash size; counted in bytes) refuses boot. Once ANY auth is configured (this, the `RESTACT_OIDC_*` set or `RESTACT_TRUSTED_IDENTITY_HEADER`), an anonymous caller may not mutate — deploy, `:verb` management writes and data-plane `PUT`/`PATCH`/`DELETE` answer `401` (so an unauthenticated caller cannot match the `owner` matcher of an anonymously-owned or bootstrapped resource and seize it); reads and execute-policy-gated action invocation stay open. **The subject `anonymous` is reserved**: an absent `$owner` resolves to `anonymous`, so a credential carrying that exact, case-sensitive subject (an HS256 bearer or sub-token, an OIDC token, the trusted identity header, the remote MCP facet, an exchange `actor_token`) would own every ownerless object and pass the anonymous-mutation rule above; it is refused `401` on every path (and a stored delegation grant whose subject is `anonymous` never yields a delegated principal), whether or not delegation is on. The bearer and OIDC refusals carry the same body as an invalid credential on that path; the trusted-header refusal carries its own plain “credential is not accepted” detail. Near-misses such as `Anonymous` stay ordinary subjects. A subject containing a control character (NUL, newline, tab, DEL, C1) is refused `401` on every ordinary path, HS256 included, so that every store treats a subject alike. Idempotency replay and async-operation ownership are honored only for an authenticated caller: anonymous callers share one label, so an anonymous `Idempotency-Key` is ignored and its operation ids carry an unguessable random suffix. `restactd token mint` reads it too, with the same checks, to sign tokens. | > **OIDC/JWKS verification** cryptographically verifies a forwarded RS256/ES256 JWT against the issuer’s published keys — the production alternative to trusting a header (you switch from a demo to real verification by configuration alone). The verification algorithm is **pinned from the JWK** (`RSA`→RS256, `EC P-256`→ES256), never from the token header, so an alg-confusion or `alg:none` token is rejected; `iss`/`aud`/`exp`/`nbf`/`iat` are all validated, fail-closed (including a zero/unavailable wall clock). When both OIDC and HS256 are configured on the same header, OIDC wins on success. Only a token that is not an OIDC token (not a JWT, or no `kid`) falls through to HS256, so HS256 service tokens still authenticate; a token that fails OIDC verification (bad signature, unknown `kid`, expired, wrong `aud`, JWKS unreachable) is `401`. A present-but-invalid token never silently becomes anonymous. Like the other auth modes this is deployment config — it is **not** advertised in discovery and is not a conformance feature. Validate a deployment with `scripts/oidc-test.sh` (`make oidc-test`). > > **Operational notes.** > > * **Key rotation window.** A JWKS refetch (triggered by an unknown `kid`) is throttled to at most once per \~60s to bound a bogus-`kid` flood. So for up to \~60s after the issuer rotates signing keys, a token signed with the brand-new key may intermittently `401` until the cache refreshes — it **fails closed and self-heals**. Keep `RESTACT_OIDC_JWKS_TTL_SECS` comfortably below your issuer’s key-rotation cadence. > * **Blocking pool.** OIDC verification runs on the same blocking pool as verb execution; keep `RESTACT_MAX_INFLIGHT` enabled (the default) — it bounds how many concurrent verifications (and thus pool threads) an OIDC-header flood can consume. > * **Outbound egress.** With OIDC enabled, restact makes **outbound HTTPS** to `RESTACT_OIDC_JWKS_URL` (the IdP’s JWKS endpoint) — a new egress requirement for a host that otherwise makes no outbound calls. In egress-restricted deployments (k8s `NetworkPolicy`, locked-down containers, air-gapped), **allow egress to the issuer host on 443**; a blocked JWKS fetch fails closed (all OIDC tokens → `401`). The Mozilla CA roots are compiled into the binary (`webpki-roots`), so no OS CA store is required. > * **One identity source per subject namespace.** Principals from OIDC and HS256 share a flat subject namespace (no issuer prefix), so an HS256 token with `sub:"alice"` and an OIDC token for `alice` are the same principal for ownership/ACLs. If you enable both, ensure their subject namespaces don’t overlap (or rely on one source); a leaked HS256 secret could otherwise impersonate an OIDC subject. To keep a namespace for subjects you mint yourself over HS256, list its prefix in `RESTACT_RESERVED_SUBJECT_PREFIXES`: OIDC and the trusted header can then never produce it. Independently, a subject from those two sources may not contain `:`, whitespace or a format character unless `RESTACT_EXTERNAL_SUBJECT_ALLOW` relaxes the first two. Issuers that put a colon in `sub` on purpose (GitHub Actions, GitLab CI, Kubernetes service accounts, SPIFFE, tsidp) need `RESTACT_EXTERNAL_SUBJECT_ALLOW=colon`. Plain overlapping names such as `alice` still rely on the one-source rule. Caution **Trusted-header mode trusts the header at face value — only enable it when restact is *exclusively* reachable through a trusted proxy that sets this header and strips any client-supplied copy.** With it on, restact performs **no** signature check (OIDC/JWKS verification does that): if a client can reach restact directly, it can spoof any identity by sending the header. The proxy authenticates the user (Cloudflare Access, oauth2-proxy, Authelia, Keycloak, Tailscale Serve, Traefik forward-auth, …) and forwards the result; restact stays vendor-neutral. When both a trusted header and a bearer token are present, **the trusted header wins** (precedence above); an absent/empty header falls through to the bearer path, so service tokens still authenticate there. On startup the host logs which header is trusted (`auth: trusted identity header '<name>' enabled …`) so the mode is never a silent surprise. An operator plugs in whatever header their proxy emits, e.g. `Cf-Access-Authenticated-User-Email` (Cloudflare Access), `X-Auth-Request-Email` (oauth2-proxy), or `Remote-User` (Authelia). > **Verb policy matchers.** A verb’s policy (`GET`/`PUT /v1/<obj>:<verb>/policy`) gates `read`/`execute`/`write`, each an OR-list of canonical matchers — `public`, `owner`, `sub:<id>`, `role:<r>`, `group:<g>`, `scope:<s>`, `claim:<k>=<v>` (the same vocabulary as per-property ACLs and object `$policy`). A single matcher may be written/read as a bare string; a list is a JSON array. A verb uploaded through `POST /v1/modules` starts owner-only for every action (see [Deploy a verb](/docs/reference/http-api/#deploy-a-verb)); `exec=public` opens `execute`. ### Isolation | Variable | Default | Effect | | --------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | | `RESTACT_ISOLATION` | *(unset)* | Set to `process` for per-request worker-process isolation; only an unset variable runs in-process, and any other value, an empty one included, refuses to start (exit 1) naming the variable. Needs `RESTACT_DATA_DIR` *or* `RESTACT_DATABASE_URL` (the worker re-opens whichever durable backend the parent uses); without one the host refuses to start (exit 1). Under process isolation discovery omits `targeted-invalidation`, because a worker’s reads never reach the parent. | | `RESTACT_WORKER_TIMEOUT_MS` | `30000` | Kill deadline for a process-isolation worker. The worker itself stops running wasm a little earlier (5% of the value, between 100 ms and 1 s, never more than half of it), so a run cut off at its time limit is still metered and charged to its caller; once that moment has passed no further verb, composed call or hook starts. A worker killed after it wrote its whole response is metered from that response; one killed before that reports nothing, so hook compute from that run is lost and the caller is charged the verb’s whole fuel limit. | | `RESTACT_COMPILE_CACHE_DIR` | `$XDG_CACHE_HOME/restactd` (when that is an absolute path), else `$HOME/.cache/restactd` | Where compiled verb components are cached between runs and shared with process-isolation workers. Each entry is sealed with an HMAC and checked before it is loaded; an entry that fails the check is recompiled, never loaded. `off` (any letter case) disables the disk cache, and then every worker compiles each component it loads. The directory must be owned by the server’s user and not group- or world-writable, or the cache is disabled with a warning. A root that is a symbolic link turns the cache off with a warning. With none of the directory variable, an absolute `XDG_CACHE_HOME` and `HOME` set, the cache is off and the host logs a boot warning. wasmtime’s own `~/.cache/wasmtime` and `~/.config/wasmtime/config.toml` are not used. | | `RESTACT_COMPILE_CACHE_KEY_FILE` | `<cache dir>/key` (generated, mode 0600) | The HMAC key for cache entries: a file of 32 to 4096 bytes, used as-is. Point it at a read-only secret on a different filesystem from the cache, so that someone who can write the cache cannot forge entries. A set but missing, unreadable or wrongly sized file refuses boot. | | `RESTACT_COMPILE_CACHE_MAX_BYTES` | `2147483648` (2 GiB) | Size cap for the running engine’s cache entries, enforced at boot by deleting the oldest entries down to three quarters of the cap. The same sweep removes the directories of other engine configurations whose newest entry is over 30 days old. A value that is not a number falls back to the default, with a boot warning. | ### Quotas & rate limiting | Variable | Default | Effect | | --------------------------------- | -------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `RESTACT_QUOTA_FUEL` | *(unset)* | Per-tenant cumulative **compute** (fuel) cap. Enforced on **both** backends — fuel is debited under process isolation too. Every deploy-time validation is charged to the caller, whatever the outcome: a deploy, a code replace, a rollback, or a policy write that re-validates the stored code is debited the flat deploy cost, or the fuel the validation consumed when that is more, whether it succeeds or is refused (bad component, source that does not compile, a validation timeout, a failed precondition or a write refusal decided after the upload was checked). A request refused before any validation runs costs nothing: no credential (`401`), over quota (`429`), a malformed request or precondition header (`400`), an unsupported language (`415`), a bad verb name, accessor kind or call graph, a source over the size cap, a body over the module limit (`413`). A refusal decided after the upload was validated (`403` on a verb the caller may not write, `412` on a failed precondition) is charged. | | `RESTACT_QUOTA_CALLS` | *(unset)* | Per-tenant cumulative invocation cap. | | `RESTACT_QUOTA_WINDOW_SECS` | *(unset)* | Accounting **window** for the fuel/calls quotas: a tenant’s tally expires this many seconds after its window opened, turning the lifetime circuit breaker into a per-window budget. `86400` gives each tenant a **rolling daily budget measured from its first debit** (each tenant rolls over independently — not a global midnight reset), and a full-budget burst at a window boundary can briefly reach \~2× the cap (the soft-cap overshoot semantics). Unset/`0` → lifetime cumulative (a tripped tenant stays tripped until a restart or admin reset). The reset is **lazy** (an expired tally reads as zero; the next debit restarts the window) — no sweeper. On PostgreSQL the rollover is judged by the **DB clock**, so all nodes agree. **Storage is not windowed** — stored bytes are a level, not a rate. A windowed 429 carries an honest `Retry-After` (the tenant’s actual remaining window) and a `detail` naming the tripped dimension; startup echoes the resolved posture and **warns if the value doesn’t parse** (whole seconds only — `1h` is not accepted) instead of silently falling back to lifetime. | | `RESTACT_QUOTA_STORAGE_BYTES` | *(unset)* | Per-tenant cumulative **storage** cap for binary object values. A separate dimension from fuel/calls — blobs live off the size-capped attribute map, so without this a loop of media `PUT`s is unmetered storage (denial-of-wallet). Checked **before** a blob `PUT` writes (an over-cap `PUT` → `429`, not a rollback); `RESTACT_MAX_BLOB_BYTES` separately bounds a *single* blob. **Note:** the tally is in-process **per node**, so on a PostgreSQL cluster the effective cap is N× the configured value (like the documented best-effort fuel/calls degradation under a DB error); there is no cross-node-exact storage accounting. | | `RESTACT_QUOTA_OBJECTS` | *(unset)* | Per-owner cap on stored objects. Unset, empty or `off` means no cap; a whole number is the cap, and `0` is a cap of zero. Any other value refuses boot, naming the variable. Isolation workers receive it from the parent. It counts the objects each owner (`$owner`) holds, across the whole cluster on PostgreSQL; a filesystem host rebuilds the counts at every boot, so a restore that leaves a stale counts file is corrected on the next start. A creation over the cap answers `429` with code `quota` and no `Retry-After`, because the count frees only when the owner deletes. Updates and deletes are never refused, and an owner change (reown) moves the count and is never refused. With no authentication configured every caller is anonymous and shares one count, so the cap is deployment-wide and one caller can use it up for everyone; per-user caps need authentication. The boot summary ends its quota line with `objects=off` or the cap. | | `RESTACT_REQUEST_TIMEOUT_MS` | `60000` | Outer HTTP request timeout (`408` on elapse). `0` disables both this timeout and the body deadline below. `POST /admin/checkpoint` is exempt too, so a long copy answers with its snapshot instead of a `408`; its request body keeps the body deadline. The SSE subscribe path (a bare-object `GET` with `Accept: text/event-stream`) is exempt and is bounded instead by `RESTACT_SSE_MAX_SECS`; it is exempt only while eventing is on (with `RESTACT_DISABLE_EVENTS` set it is an ordinary request). The exemption is scoped to the real subscribe path, so a verb invocation that spoofs the `Accept` header cannot bypass the deadline. The same value is also a hard deadline on the whole request-BODY transfer on every route, the SSE subscribe shape included (`408` `timeout` problem when it elapses mid-body), so a client that declares a large `Content-Length` and trickles bytes cannot hold a `RESTACT_MAX_INFLIGHT` permit past it. | | `RESTACT_MAX_INFLIGHT` | `256` | Global in-flight request cap. `0` disables. | | `RESTACT_MAX_WASM_INFLIGHT` | `256` | Global cap on concurrently-EXECUTING wasm invocations, held until the wasm finishes (independent of the HTTP request). `0` disables. | | `RESTACT_MAX_VERB_FUEL` | `100000000000` | Server **ceiling** an uploader-requested per-verb `?fuel=` is clamped to, so a malicious upload can’t self-grant unbounded compute. High by default — the wall-clock ceiling is the real CPU-time bound. The JS runner’s built-in loop bound sits above this default, so the ceiling stays authoritative; raise the variable far past its default and the runner’s bound can fire first, with the same `cpu-budget`/`429` (or `500` if the script resumes after a builtin swallows the abort). | | `RESTACT_MAX_VERB_MEMORY_MB` | `512` | Server ceiling each uploader-requested per-verb `?mem_mb=` is clamped to; an overflowing `mem_mb` is rejected `400`. Also sizes the pooling allocator’s per-slot memory reservation (virtual address space, not resident memory), so every grant the clamp approves is one the pool can back; a ceiling too large for the pool to reserve falls back to the on-demand allocator with a startup warning. | | `RESTACT_MAX_VERB_WALL_MS` | `30000` | Server ceiling each uploader-requested per-verb `?wall_ms=` is clamped to — the real per-frame CPU-time bound. | | `RESTACT_SOURCE_VALIDATE_WALL_MS` | `5000` | Operator-only wall window for the compile-only syntax check of an uploaded JS/Python source (never uploader-set). Valid range `100` to the effective `RESTACT_MAX_VERB_WALL_MS`; the unset default is clamped to that max. A bad value refuses boot. A check that hits the window is `503` `unavailable` with `Retry-After` and stores nothing, not `422`. The check’s fuel is fixed at 5500 per byte of the 256 KiB source cap. Validation (upload, `PUT :verb/code`, `PUT :verb/policy` on a source verb, rollback) also holds a `RESTACT_MAX_WASM_INFLIGHT` permit. | ### Eventing, lifecycle & object features | Variable | Default | Effect | | ------------------------------------ | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `RESTACT_DISABLE_EVENTS` | *(off)* | Set `1`/`true`/`on`/`yes` (trimmed, case-insensitive; `0`/`false`/`off`/`no` leave eventing on) to turn eventing OFF; any other value refuses to start. One parse serves discovery, the event bus and lifecycle auto-publish — the complete kill-switch: the discovery `event-subscriptions`, `verb-subscriptions` and `targeted-invalidation` tokens are omitted, host auto-publish, the guest `event.publish` capability publishes nothing (its input checks still apply) and the subscription-delivery loop goes inert (subscription registration answers `501`), and the SSE endpoint falls through to the data-plane representation. | | `RESTACT_DURABLE_EVENTS` | *(off)* | Set `1`/`true` to persist a bounded global event log to the durable store (needs `RESTACT_DATA_DIR`), so the SSE replay ring survives a restart; absent → in-memory only. **Ignored under `RESTACT_DATABASE_URL`** — the PostgreSQL `event_log` is the cross-node transport, so durability is always-on. | | `RESTACT_EVENT_MAX_DATA_BYTES` | `65536` (64 KiB) | Cap on one published event’s data, counted as stored, so the `$publisher` stamp the host adds is included. `event.publish` (from any verb tier or a native verb) refuses a larger event with `413` `object-too-large`; nothing is stored or broadcast. A positive whole number of bytes; zero, negative, fractional, empty or non-numeric refuses to start. | | `RESTACT_EVENT_RING_MAX_BYTES` | `4194304` (4 MiB) | Byte budget of each topic’s replay ring, alongside the 256-event count cap; the oldest events go first. The durable log (`RESTACT_DURABLE_EVENTS`) obeys the same bound, as one global log rather than per topic. The budget counts each event’s data, name, topic and delete snapshot plus a small fixed charge. It is also the most one invocation (its whole call tree, lifecycle hooks its writes fire included, on either executor backend) may publish in total: the publish that would pass it answers `413` `object-too-large` and is not stored. A subscriber resuming with a `Last-Event-ID` older than the oldest retained event receives a `gap` event (with `missed`, the number of events evicted after its cursor; an upper bound when more than 4096 have been evicted or when the topic was dropped and recreated since; omitted on PostgreSQL when a replay query failed and the count is unknown) before the replay, on every backend (on PostgreSQL the replay itself reads at most the newest 256 events within this budget). A host that restarts without the durable log has no memory of what it dropped and sends none. On PostgreSQL the shared log is bounded by `RESTACT_PG_EVENT_LOG_MAX_BYTES` and `RESTACT_PG_EVENT_LOG_CAP`, and the figure counts what was trimmed from the topic itself, except that folded bookkeeping makes it an upper bound. A positive whole number of bytes; any other value refuses to start. | | `RESTACT_EVENT_TOTAL_MAX_BYTES` | `67108864` (64 MiB) | Byte budget across every topic’s replay ring, counted as for `RESTACT_EVENT_RING_MAX_BYTES`. Each topic entry also costs the budget a fixed amount. Past the budget the globally oldest events are evicted, and a topic left empty with no subscriber is dropped, so neither the retained bytes nor the number of topics can grow the host’s memory without bound. A topic’s broadcast channels are created on subscribe and released when its last subscriber leaves; their memory is not part of this budget (evicting rings would not free it) and is bounded instead by `RESTACT_MAX_SSE_STREAMS`, so leave that cap set. A verb may publish to a topic of at most 1024 bytes. A positive whole number of bytes; any other value refuses to start. The shared PostgreSQL log has its own bound, `RESTACT_PG_EVENT_LOG_MAX_BYTES`. | | `RESTACT_DISABLE_SCHEDULES` | *(off)* | Kills the schedule timer: schedule registration answers `501` and the `schedules` discovery token is omitted. Set `1`/`true`/`on`/`yes` (trimmed, case-insensitive). Topic subscriptions are switched off by `RESTACT_DISABLE_EVENTS`, not by this. | | `RESTACT_SCHEDULE_MIN_INTERVAL_SECS` | `10` | Registration floor for a schedule’s `intervalSeconds`. A value that does not parse as a whole number falls back to the default. | | `RESTACT_MAX_SCHEDULES` | `32` | Per-tenant cap on live schedule registrations. | | `RESTACT_MAX_SUBSCRIPTIONS` | `32` | Per-tenant cap on live topic-subscription registrations. | | `RESTACT_SUB_MIN_GAP_MS` | `1000` | Minimum gap between two firings of ONE subscription — the bound on a publish→fire→publish loop. | | `RESTACT_CONSISTENCY` | `conditional` | Advertised and enforced consistency level. `conditional` is per-key last-writer-wins. `atomic-invocation` buffers a verb invocation’s WHOLE write set (bound instance, bound shared, and foreign `set-at` targets) and commits it together under sorted per-key locks at frame success; a trap or any commit-time refusal discards all of it. Any other value falls back to `conditional`. Reaches the isolation worker through the environment, so both backends commit identically. Crash atomicity (an `FsStore` journal) is not implemented. At `atomic-invocation` the host refuses `set-at-if` (`unsupported-consistency`, no compare, no write) and withdraws the `object-cas` discovery token, because a synchronous CAS commit cannot join a buffered frame. | | `RESTACT_MAX_SSE_STREAMS` | `256` | Cap on concurrent SSE subscriptions; past the cap a new subscribe is refused (`503`). `0` → unbounded. | | `RESTACT_SSE_MAX_SECS` | `3600` | Max lifetime of one SSE stream before it ends and the client reconnects with `Last-Event-ID`. | | `RESTACT_LIFECYCLE_HOOKS` | *(on)* | Set `off`/`0`/`false`/`no` to make a deployed `onCreate`/`onChange`/`onDelete` hook inert (suppresses server-initiated invocation). `PUT` fires `onCreate` (new object) or `onChange` (existing), `DELETE` fires `onDelete`; hooks fire on a data-plane write and on an in-verb `object.set`/`set-at`. Reserved hook names must be **actions**, resolved up the ancestry/`{id}` chain, and run under **registrant authority**: as the hook’s OWNER, charged to the owner’s quota on either executor backend, including a hook cut off at its time limit or whose code is removed mid-run (an over-quota owner’s hook does not run, and under process isolation an owner who reaches the cap partway through an invocation gets no further hook runs in it), with the owner’s identity and `object` capabilities rather than the triggering caller’s (the triggering subject rides in the payload as information only). Invocation goes through the normal path (limits, owner quota, execute policy), is synchronous and best-effort (a hook error is logged and the mutation kept), and a hook→write→hook chain is bounded at depth 4 (`MAX_HOOK_DEPTH`). Advertised as `service.lifecycleHooks` and the `lifecycle-hooks` feature. | | `RESTACT_OBJECT_BINDING` | `strict` when authentication is configured (`RESTACT_JWT_SECRET`, the `RESTACT_OIDC_*` set or `RESTACT_TRUSTED_IDENTITY_HEADER`), otherwise `implicit` | Object↔verb coupling: `implicit` (independent planes), `auto-create` (deploying on a missing object creates it, caller-owned), or `strict` (`409`). **The coupled modes are also what authorizes *defining* a verb on a multi-tenant host** — see [Who may define a verb where](#who-may-define-a-verb-where); under `implicit` any authenticated caller may define a verb at any not-yet-registered route, including under another principal’s object paths. An authenticated host that sets `implicit` explicitly boots with a `warning:` naming this (and noting that the ancestor-creation guard is then on only with `RESTACT_POLICY_INHERITANCE`); an unrecognized value on an authenticated host falls back to `strict`. In the coupled modes, deploying a verb on an *existing* object requires owning it (verb owner ⊆ object owner; anonymous-open is transparent), and `DELETE /v1/<obj>` cascades to every definition anchored at the object (its bound verbs, and the `{id}` patterns beneath it) and to their shared state. The delete is all-or-nothing: if any anchored definition is not the caller’s to remove, the whole delete is refused `403`. Advertised in discovery as `service.objectBinding`. | | `RESTACT_SUB_TOKENS` | *(on)* | Operator switch for minting path-scoped sub-tokens. Unset, `on`, `1`, `true` or `yes` mounts `POST /v1/tokens/sub`, and advertises `path-scoped-tokens` only when `RESTACT_JWT_SECRET` is also set; `off`, `0`, `false` or `no` leaves the route unmounted (every method on `/v1/tokens/sub` answers `404`) and withholds the feature token. Minting needs `RESTACT_JWT_SECRET`: without it an authenticated OIDC or trusted-header caller gets `503`. The switch gates minting only: a sub-token issued earlier still verifies and stays confined to its prefix until it expires. Any other value refuses boot, naming the variable. | | `RESTACT_DELEGATION` | *(on)* | Delegated tokens. Unset or `on` (trimmed, case-insensitive) leaves delegated tokens enabled; `off` disables them AND revokes every stored grant before the first request (booting `on` again revives none). Any other value (`0`, `false`, `no`, …) **refuses boot** naming the variable. With the switch on and `RESTACT_JWT_SECRET` set, the host advertises `delegated-tokens` and `delegated-client-actors` (with `serviceConfig.delegation`), mounts the grants API, `POST /v1/tokens/exchange` and `POST /v1/tokens/grants/redeem`, and recognises delegated tokens; with the switch `off`, or with no signing secret, all of these are off together (the routes answer 404 and a delegated token is a 401). While `delegated-client-actors` is advertised, an ordinary credential (bearer, sub-token, trusted identity header, OIDC token) whose subject begins `client:` is refused 401, so a principal cannot pose as a client actor. The exchange and redeem rate limits key on the TCP peer: a router served without connect info (in-crate construction only; the stock host always supplies it) rate-limits every caller as one source. On a multi-node deployment a node running `off` also treats every grant as not live, even one another node created after it booted: mixed settings fail closed. A node with no signing secret is paused, not revoked: with the switch unset it advertises and mounts nothing, and it also treats every grant as not live, so stamped actions, hooks and registration firings stop running on it (`403 delegation-bound` for a stamped request); grants are left untouched and run again when the node boots with a secret. One computed value (switch not `off` AND a signing secret) feeds all of these, and a `RESTACT_ISOLATION=process` worker is told it explicitly (the parent sets \`RESTACT_DELEGATION=on | | `RESTACT_MAX_ROUTE_BYTES` | `1024` | Ceiling on a verb route’s length in UTF-8 bytes (the longer of the decoded route and its stored `{id}` form): `POST /v1/modules?verb=` and `PUT :verb/code` / `PUT :verb/policy` at a longer concrete route are refused `400 invalid-input` (`route exceeds N bytes (got M)`) before any side effect; stored verbs, reads and `DELETE` are not checked. A positive integer; set but empty, `0` or not an integer is a fatal boot refusal (exit 1). | | `RESTACT_MAX_BLOB_BYTES` | `8388608` (8 MiB) | Per-blob size ceiling for **binary object values**: a non-JSON `PUT /v1/<obj>` stores the body as a content-addressed blob (a `$blob` reference in the object’s attrs); a `GET` serves the bytes with the stored Content-Type + a content-hash ETag. An upload over the cap is `413`. The bytes live in the blob store, independent of the per-object attribute-map cap; advertised as the `binary-values` feature. | | `RESTACT_ACCESSOR_DISPATCH` | *(on)* | Set `off`/`0`/`false`/`no` so a bare-object CRUD request always serves stored data instead of dispatching to a defined accessor verb. Candidates are tried in preference order and the first deployed one wins: `GET`→`:get` then `:list` (collection enumeration), `PUT`→`:update`, `PATCH`→`:set` (partial write) then `:update`, `POST`→`:create`, `DELETE`→`:delete`. When on but no accessor resolves, CRUD serves stored data unchanged. Advertised as `service.accessorDispatch` and the `active-properties`, `bare-path-accessor-dispatch` and `set-list-accessors` features. Also an owner-settable object `$policy` (`{read,write,delete}`, canonical matchers; default public-read, owner-write, owner-delete; feature `object-policy`) gates CRUD, and a per-property `inherit` flag opts a property into value inheritance up the object ancestry (feature `property-inheritance`). | | `RESTACT_POLICY_INHERITANCE` | *(off)* | Set `1`/`true` to enable prefix-inherited object policy: an object lacking its own `$policy` resolves its effective policy from the nearest ancestor `$policy` marked `inherit:true`, and a first write under such an anchor is admission-gated. Unset → the resolver never walks ancestors, and behavior and discovery match a host without it; the `object-policy-inheritance` feature token is advertised only when on. The same variable reaches the isolation worker, so both backends resolve identically. | | `RESTACT_GOVERNED_CREATION` | *(off)* | Set `1` or `true` (trimmed) to require that a creating write outside any governed anchor establishes an explicit `$policy`; a create that does not is refused `403`. Requires `RESTACT_POLICY_INHERITANCE=1`, or the host refuses to start. Advertised as the `governed-creation` feature. On a host shared by several tenants, pair it with anchors and seeded top-level containers; see [Who owns a new object](/docs/concepts/identity-and-authority/#who-owns-a-new-object). | | `RESTACT_ACCESS_LOG` | *(off)* | Set `1`/`true` to log access to stderr (method, path, subject, status; never object values). **What it covers**, precisely: the object data plane (`GET`/`PUT`/`DELETE` and accessor dispatch), the `GET /v1` collection listing, SSE subscribe (logged with the synthetic method `GET-SSE`), registration create (`POST /v1/{noun}`) and delete, the sub-token mint (logged at `/tokens/sub`, without the `/v1` prefix the other lines carry), the delegated-grant endpoints and token exchange (`/v1/tokens/grants`, `/v1/tokens/grants/redeem`, `/v1/tokens/exchange`), and an MCP `tools/call` (logged with the synthetic method `MCP`, `path` the tool name, and `status` **always `200`**, fixed at dispatch time before the tool call runs, so it never reflects the tool’s outcome). `path` and `subject` are escaped — every ASCII control byte (tab included), the space, and the backslash become a `\xNN` escape — so one event is exactly one line and each field is exactly one token: a value contains none of those bytes, so a parser may split the line on single ASCII spaces and read each field at its first occurrence and get the right answer. Non-ASCII whitespace (U+00A0, U+2028, U+3000) is not escaped and passes through a value verbatim, so a parser must split on the ASCII space specifically — a Unicode-aware tokenizer (`str.split()`, `/\s+/`, `split_whitespace`) can still be shadowed by one of those. `path` is the once-decoded matched route parameter and never the raw URI, which is why the line carries no query string. Verb invocations are **not** logged unless `RESTACT_ACCESS_LOG_INVOCATIONS` is also set. | | `RESTACT_ACCESS_LOG_INVOCATIONS` | *(off)* | With `RESTACT_ACCESS_LOG` on, extends the same `access …` line to verb invocations (one line per invocation, same grammar, `path` including the `:verb` suffix). Inert unless `RESTACT_ACCESS_LOG` is set. This is the unsampled per-route record of last resort: metric route labels fold into an overflow series past their cardinality limit, and this line does not. | | `RESTACT_LOG` | `warn,restactd=info,restact.=info` | `tracing`’s `EnvFilter` directive (`info`, `debug`, `restact.egress=debug`, …). It filters stderr only; OpenTelemetry export has its own fixed `INFO` ceiling (see [Observability](#observability)). The default applies when the variable is unset, empty or whitespace-only. It admits the host’s own targets at `info` (`restactd=info` for module-path targets and `restact.=info` for the dotted custom targets such as `restact.access` and `restact.worker`) and everything else at `warn`, so third-party `WARN` and `ERROR` events still reach stderr. An explicit value is taken verbatim. See [Filter pitfalls](#filter-pitfalls) before narrowing it. | | `RESTACT_LOG_FORMAT` | *(unset — text)* | `json` switches stderr to one JSON object per line with the top-level keys `ts`, `level`, `target`, `trace_id`, `span_id`, `message` and `fields`. `span_id` is the current span’s id (empty outside a span); `trace_id` is the OpenTelemetry trace id of the current span when the host was built with `--features otel` and is exporting (see [Observability](#observability)), and an empty string otherwise. The event’s own fields nest under `fields` and cannot shadow a canonical key. Every host diagnostic is a `tracing` event and is JSON-wrapped, except the lines printed before logging starts (the `telemetry: …` boot line, `fatal: telemetry initialization failed: …` and two early route-registration warnings) and the few lines written to stdout, which this stderr-only variable never touches. Unset, each line is exactly the event’s message plus a newline. | | `RESTACT_GUEST_LOG_CEILING_PER_SEC` | `200` | Process-wide ceiling on verb `host.log` lines reaching stderr. | | `RESTACT_GUEST_LOG_RATE_PER_SEC` | `20` | Per-tenant sustained rate inside that ceiling, keyed on the invoking (root) principal — never on an elevated verb’s switched owner. | | `RESTACT_GUEST_LOG_BURST` | `50` | Per-tenant burst. | | `RESTACT_GUEST_LOG_MAX_BYTES` | `4096` | Per-line cap, measured on the **whole escaped line** (the `[guest <route> log <level>] `prefix included, not just the message). An over-cap line is dropped whole, never truncated. | A dropped line is announced on stderr at most once per window **per (tenant, route, reason)** — up to four lines per (tenant, route), since `reason` (`rate`/`size`) is part of the key — as `telemetry: guest log dropped route=… n=… reason=rate|size window=…s`, where `route` is the host-minted deployed route pattern, itself escaped the same way `path`/`subject` are above (a deployed pattern needs no control byte to carry fake `n=`/`reason=` text) — the dropped line itself never reaches stderr, so this marker is where the attribution lives. No subject, no principal id, no guest bytes. The window is a fixed **60 seconds**, not one of the four knobs above: a window yields at most two markers — one at the end of the invocation that first dropped (carrying the drops so far) and, only if more drops followed inside the window, one closing marker once the window has expired (carrying the carried remainder) — counts are carried forward, never discarded, and are announced on the next flush of **any** tenant, not only the one that dropped. #### Filter pitfalls * `EnvFilter` reads an unrecognised value as a target directive, not a level. `RESTACT_LOG=inf` (a typo for `info`) enables only a target literally named `inf` and leaves no global default, so the `access` line disappears with no diagnostic while the host looks healthy. A genuinely invalid directive (for example `=debug`) refuses to boot with `fatal: telemetry initialization failed: invalid RESTACT_LOG filter: …` and exit code 1. * A directive that turns a target off also silences the boot refusals that log on it. The egress interlock refusal is on `restact.egress`; the database URL, lock-establishment and governed-creation refusals are on `restact.boot`; the secrets-backend refusals are on `restact.secrets`. For example `RESTACT_LOG=off` or `info,restact.egress=off` makes the process exit `1` with no reason on stderr. The default filter never does this. The plaintext-OpenBao `warning:` is a `WARN` event on `restact.secrets`, so `RESTACT_LOG=error` hides it while the `ERROR`-level refusals keep printing. * Target directives are prefix matches. `RESTACT_LOG=info,restact.guest=off`, the natural way to quiet the verb log, also drops every `restact.guest.audit.*` line (the egress, `llm` and secrets audit lines), every `restact.guest.wasm` line (host diagnostics that can carry a caller-chosen object path) and every `restact.guest.boot` line. To keep them, add the sub-targets back: `…,restact.guest=off,restact.guest.audit=info,restact.guest.wasm=warn,restact.guest.boot=warn`. * `restact.invoke`, `restact.worker` and `restact.auth` each name both an event target and a span. A directive selects the event target only, so `RESTACT_LOG=restact.invoke=debug` does not affect the span, and `RESTACT_LOG=restact.auth=off` silences the OIDC-rejection event but not the span. * An explicit `RESTACT_LOG=info` also admits third-party session logs such as `rmcp`’s, and the level gates the `restact.sse` event (`debug`) and the `restact.worker` event (`warn`) as well as the `access` line. ### Mediated egress The mediated outbound call (feature `mediated-egress`) is an embedder-facing `RouteContext::mediated_call` that splices a stored credential into a request to an allowlisted destination. | Variable | Default | Effect | | ----------------------------------------- | -------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `RESTACT_EGRESS_ALLOW` | *(unset — feature absent)* | Operator destination allowlist: a comma-separated list of `host[:port]` entries, where `host` is a DNS name, a `*.`-prefixed suffix, or an address literal. **Unset → the feature does not exist**: no `mediated-egress` discovery token, and `mediated_call` returns `None` (an embedder’s route answers `404 mediated-egress-unavailable`), and the host behaves as if the feature did not exist. The boot interlock is asymmetric: set WITHOUT the secrets mount in service, or without `RESTACT_EGRESS_CONNECTOR_PREFIX`, the process **refuses to start** with an `error:` line naming the missing variable; the mount without the allowlist boots unchanged. | | `RESTACT_EGRESS_CONNECTOR_PREFIX` | *(unset)* | **Required when `RESTACT_EGRESS_ALLOW` is set.** The positional prefix connector objects live under, e.g. `ws/{ws}/connectors` (`*` and `{seg}` each match one segment). It is a rule about where connectors are **born**, not a call-time allowlist: a data-plane write that CREATES an object under it must carry its own `$policy` with `write` and `delete` each exactly `["owner"]`, else the write is refused `422 connector-birth-ungoverned`. Updates of existing objects are untouched, and connectors outside the prefix stay callable, governed at call time (`egress-connector-ungoverned`). The host learns one prefix and nothing about any individual connector. | | `RESTACT_EGRESS_ALLOW_LOOPBACK` | *(unset)* | Boolean acknowledgement (`1`/`true`/`on`/`yes`; unset or `0`/`false`/`off`/`no` is off; any other value refuses boot) for **deterministic test harnesses only**: a `127.0.0.1`/`::1`/`localhost` allowlist entry is honored only under it, and is a FATAL boot error without it (never silently dropped). Boot prints one `warning:` line naming the override. | | `RESTACT_EGRESS_MAX_INFLIGHT` | `8` | Process-wide concurrent mediated calls. Exhaustion refuses `egress-busy` rather than queueing. | | `RESTACT_EGRESS_MAX_REQUEST_HEADERS` | `64` | Host constant a connector’s `limits` cannot raise: the most request headers this host will frame. Over it → `egress-bounds-exceeded`, before a socket exists. | | `RESTACT_EGRESS_MAX_REQUEST_HEADER_BYTES` | `16384` | Same, for the total request-header bytes. | | `RESTACT_EGRESS_MAX_REQUEST_BYTES` | `1048576` (1 MiB) | Host ceiling a connector’s own request-body `limits` is clamped to, never raised past. | | `RESTACT_EGRESS_MAX_RESPONSE_BYTES` | `2097152` (2 MiB) | Host ceiling for the response-body limit. | | `RESTACT_EGRESS_MAX_WALL_MS` | `30000` | Host ceiling for a call’s wall-clock limit. | | `RESTACT_QUOTA_EGRESS_CALLS` | *(unset)* | Per-principal cap on mediated calls, checked before a call runs with the call itself counted. Unset → the dimension is metered but never gates. Subject to `RESTACT_QUOTA_WINDOW_SECS`. | | `RESTACT_QUOTA_EGRESS_BYTES` | *(unset)* | Per-principal cap on mediated-call bytes; a principal at or over it is refused. Unset → metered, never gating. | | `RESTACT_QUOTA_EGRESS_WALL_MS` | *(unset)* | Per-principal cap on cumulative mediated-call wall-clock milliseconds. Unset → metered, never gating. | | `RESTACT_SECRETS_URL` | *(unset)* | Where the secrets backend lives, set together with `RESTACT_SECRETS_MOUNT`. `file:<path>` (or `file://<path>`) opens a local secrets file; `http://` or `https://` selects OpenBao, which needs a build with the `openbao` Cargo feature. Setting only one of URL and mount, an unknown scheme, an OpenBao URL without that feature, or an OpenBao mount path with an empty, `.` or `..` segment, a control character or any of `?#%` is a fatal boot error. Both unset means no secrets mount. | | `RESTACT_SECRETS_MOUNT` | *(unset)* | The route pattern of the objects that hold secrets, for example `ws/{ws}/secrets/{name}`. A `{…}` segment matches any single segment and the segment count must match exactly, so the mount names one object shape, not a subtree. Set together with `RESTACT_SECRETS_URL`. A `PUT` to a slot whose concrete path is not printable ASCII, contains a space or any of `:?#%\`, or has a `.` or `..` segment is refused `400` (`invalid-input`) before anything is stored, because a connector’s `secretRefs` could never name it. A slot never serves binary content: a read answers the metadata representation, and `get-bytes` finds nothing. | | `RESTACT_SECRETS_TOKEN_FILE` | *(unset)* | Path to a file holding the OpenBao token. Required when `RESTACT_SECRETS_URL` selects OpenBao; the token is never read from an inline variable. | The numeric variables fall back to their default when the value does not parse. The three `RESTACT_QUOTA_EGRESS_*` tallies reset together when `RESTACT_QUOTA_WINDOW_SECS` expires, and they are kept in-process per node (never in PostgreSQL), so on a cluster each cap is effectively N×. ### Observability **Export is off unless the host is built with `--features otel` and an endpoint is configured.** With no endpoint set, no exporter is built and no socket is opened; a build without the `otel` feature never exports. With an endpoint set, such a binary says so on its first line (`telemetry: off (otel feature not compiled)`). | Variable | Default | Effect | | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | | `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT` | *(unset — export off)* | Base URL of an OTLP collector speaking `http/json`. The host appends the signal path itself, as a path segment, to EVERY signal it enables: `http://collector:4318` exports traces to `http://collector:4318/v1/traces`, metrics to `…/v1/metrics`, and logs to `…/v1/logs`; a trailing `/` is trimmed first (no `//v1/traces`) and a base path is kept (`http://collector:4318/base` → `…/base/v1/traces`). | | `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_TRACES_ENDPOINT` / `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_METRICS_ENDPOINT` / `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_LOGS_ENDPOINT` | *(unset)* | A per-signal endpoint, used **verbatim** — no path is appended. Wins over the base URL for that signal; `…_LOGS_ENDPOINT` configures the log signal (below), same as the other two. | | `OTEL_SDK_DISABLED` | *(unset)* | `true` builds no exporter, whatever else is set. | | `OTEL_SERVICE_NAME` / `OTEL_RESOURCE_ATTRIBUTES` | *(SDK defaults)* | The exported resource. | **Three boot refusals**, each checked before any exporter is built, each exiting non-zero with a `fatal:` line that names the variable and never prints its value, and each applying only when an exporter would be built (an endpoint set, `OTEL_SDK_DISABLED` not `true`): * `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_HEADERS`, or any of its per-signal spellings (`…_TRACES_HEADERS`, `…_METRICS_HEADERS`, `…_LOGS_HEADERS`), is set. The exporter library would send those headers on every request, so the host refuses rather than carry a backend credential — that belongs on the collector. * An endpoint variable (the base URL or any per-signal one) carries URL userinfo (`http://user:password@…`), or a query string or fragment (`?…` / `#…`). An endpoint is a plain URL; no credential may ride anywhere in it. * An endpoint variable’s scheme is neither `http://` nor `https://`. Enforced on the base URL and every per-signal variable. **`RESTACT_LOG` does not gate export.** `RESTACT_LOG` is attached to the stderr layer ONLY. The OTel trace/log layers carry their OWN fixed filter: an allowlist of the host’s own targets plus a **fixed `INFO` level ceiling**, independent of `RESTACT_LOG` in EITHER direction. **Under `RESTACT_LOG=warn`**, the INFO `http.server`/`restact.auth` request spans still export, and an admitted log record still carries its trace/span ids and attributes exactly as at the default filter (`enduser.id` **raw**, since `enduser=raw` is the default); lowering stderr’s verbosity narrows stderr only. **Under `RESTACT_LOG=debug`**, a DEBUG-level event on an admitted target reaches stderr but does NOT export (`restact.sse`’s subscribe marker is exactly this case, which is why it never exports); raising stderr’s verbosity widens stderr only. The only ways to stop export of an admitted target are `OTEL_SDK_DISABLED=true`, `RESTACT_WORKER`, or removing the endpoint, never `RESTACT_LOG`. **The sampling decision is always local.** `OTEL_TRACES_SAMPLER` accepts exactly `always_on`, `always_off`, `traceidratio`, or unset (equivalent to `always_on`, ratio 1.0) — any other value (`parentbased_*`, `jaeger_remote`, `xray`, or an unrecognized string) is a `fatal:` boot refusal naming the variable, but **only when an exporter would be built**; with no endpoint, `OTEL_SDK_DISABLED=true`, or inside a `RESTACT_WORKER` child, a bad value is never refused (the same shape as the headers refusal above). `OTEL_TRACES_SAMPLER_ARG` (used only by `traceidratio`) must parse as a finite number in `[0, 1]`, or unset (default `1.0`) — anything else (out of range, `NaN`/`inf`, or unparseable) is a `fatal:` boot refusal naming that variable, under the same exporting-only condition. The ratio is never applied to an INBOUND trace id, by construction — an unauthenticated caller therefore holds no amplification primitive over a host running at a low ratio. **The `metrics` signal exports** on the same `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_METRICS_ENDPOINT`/base-URL wiring as the other two signals. The instruments: two semconv HTTP-layer ones (`http.server.request.duration`, `http.server.active_requests`); the invoke path (`restact.invoke.duration`, `restact.invoke.fuel`, `restact.invoke.inflight`); process-isolation spawn (`restact.worker.spawn.duration`); the store (`restact.store.op.duration`); events (`restact.events.subscribers`, `.published`, `.dropped`); schedules (`restact.schedule.fired`, counting a schedule **tick** only — never a subscription delivery); verb lifecycle (`restact.verb.lifecycle`); egress and LLM calls (`restact.egress.calls`/`.duration`/`.bytes`, `restact.llm.calls`/`.tokens` — never a connector id or a model name, both unbounded caller-chosen values); auth (`restact.auth.decisions`, never counting an anonymous request); policy (`restact.policy.decisions`, counted once at every authorizing decision, data-plane included); and the guest-log limiter (`restact.guest.log.dropped`, reason only, never a route). Three instruments — the two invoke ones plus `restact.verb.lifecycle` — carry a verb route label bounded by an explicit **cardinality limit of 500 attribute *sets*** (not 500 routes: a set is the route combined with every other label on that instrument, so a legitimate host can fold well under 500 distinct routes in ordinary traffic). Past the limit the SDK folds every further set into one synthetic point carrying `otel.metric.overflow=true` and no route — permanent until restart, which is why the **access log** (below) stays the record of last resort once that happens. Span-derived rates (the collector’s `spanmetrics` connector, with an explicit `dimensions` allowlist) are sampling-dependent below ratio 1.0: they undercount by the sampling ratio, so use the metrics above for rates. No metric anywhere carries `enduser.id` or a concrete object path. The server prints one boot line naming every signal that actually exports, e.g. `telemetry: otlp http://otel-collector:4318 traces,metrics,logs sampler=1.0 enduser=raw export=ok` for a base-URL endpoint (which drives every signal it enables at once), or, with only `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_LOGS_ENDPOINT` set, `telemetry: otlp http://collector:4318 logs …` — a logs-only host never prints `none`. `export=` is **always `ok`**: the boot-time flush runs before anything has been queued, so it sends nothing and says nothing about whether the collector is reachable (the boot line does not check reachability). The host boots and serves either way. See [Observability](/docs/operate/observability/). ### Admin | Variable | Default | Effect | | -------------------------------------------- | ------------------------ | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `RESTACT_ADMIN_RESET` | *(unset — endpoint OFF)* | Enables the operator clean-reset, `POST /admin/reset`: a comma-separated OR-list of the canonical subject matchers (`role:admin`, `sub:ops-1`, `group:…`, `scope:…`, `claim:k=v`, `public`) admitted to trigger it. Unset → the route answers `404` and discovery omits `admin-reset`. `owner`/`prop:`/unparseable tokens disable the feature (fail-closed). `public` suits the intentionally-open localhost host; with ANY auth configured, anonymous is still denied. A reset wipes ALL tenant state and re-runs the startup bootstrap — equivalent to a restart over a fresh data dir; the event `seq` survives, so SSE cursors stay valid. **Name a dedicated operator subject** here: the gate matches the caller’s subject, so any full bearer minted under it (for example a plugin’s minted user bearer) carries the same power; only delegated and path-scoped tokens are refused. The same holds for `RESTACT_ADMIN_REOWN`. On a host with auth configured this matcher also decides who may deploy a verb at the service root or on a leading-wildcard route; naming `public` there makes every signed-in user the operator. | | `RESTACT_ADMIN_CHECKPOINT` | *(unset — endpoint OFF)* | Enables the durable checkpoint, `POST /admin/checkpoint`: the same matcher vocabulary and fail-closed parsing as `RESTACT_ADMIN_RESET`. Each checkpoint copies the durable store to a `checkpoint-<ts>-<seq>` snapshot directory and publishes `checkpoint-started`/`checkpoint-finished` on the reserved `@host/checkpoint` topic (subscribable, never guest-publishable). **Not supported on the PostgreSQL backend** — use the database’s own tooling (`pg_dump`, WAL archiving, managed snapshots); discovery omits `admin-checkpoint` under PG even if this is set. `restactd checkpoint` calls this endpoint when a server holds the data directory, and needs this variable set on the server (and, under auth, a bearer on stdin: `restactd token mint --sub ops --ttl 5m \| restactd checkpoint --token-stdin`). The live path sends the request to one address, so `RESTACT_BIND` must be an IP literal (`127.0.0.1:PORT` or `[::1]:PORT`; an unspecified address maps to its loopback); a host name is exit 2. It is taken only while the lock file reads `server`. With the server stopped, `restactd checkpoint` copies the store itself and needs no variable. | | `RESTACT_ADMIN_REOWN` | *(unset — endpoint OFF)* | Enables the operator ownership recovery, `POST /admin/reown`: a comma-separated list of **`sub:<id>` matchers only**. The route is on only when the value is non-empty, every token is a `sub:` matcher, none is `sub:anonymous`, and authentication is configured; anything else (`public`, `role:`, `group:`, `scope:`, `claim:`, `owner`, `prop:`, an unparseable token, or an open host) leaves it off and prints a boot `warning:` line. On, the boot prints `admin reown: on for [sub:…] (POST /admin/reown; X-Restact-Operator-Exempt for direct creates)`; off, `admin reown: off (…)`. Off → the route answers `404` and discovery omits `admin-reown`. Name a **dedicated operator subject**: a full bearer minted under that subject (for example a plugin’s minted user bearer) carries the same power; only delegated and path-scoped tokens are refused. The same subjects may send `X-Restact-Operator-Exempt` on a direct create (next row). Without the route, or with the server stopped, `restactd reown` is the offline fallback and needs none of this ([Move ownership offline](/docs/operate/storage/#move-ownership-offline)). | | `X-Restact-Operator-Exempt` (request header) | *(none)* | Lets one of the `RESTACT_ADMIN_REOWN` subjects create an object above another principal’s object or verb definition. The value must be exactly `ancestor-claim`; any other value gives no exemption, and a caller who is not a listed subject gets none either. It is honoured only on a direct request: a delegated token, a path-scoped sub-token and the bridge’s tool calls get no exemption, even when they send the header, and a write made by a verb, an accessor or a lifecycle hook is never exempt, whoever triggered it. The boot prints `operator exemption: [sub:…] (RESTACT_ADMIN_REOWN)` or `none (RESTACT_ADMIN_REOWN unset)`. An exempt write is logged. An orphan definition still refuses: one whose anchor object is absent or owned by someone other than the definition’s owner. | | `RESTACT_ANCESTRY_GUARD` | *(internal)* | Set by the host on its isolation workers (`off`, `objects` or `coupled`), like `RESTACT_DELEGATION`; never set it by hand. The host derives it from its own configuration: the guard is on when authentication is configured and either a coupled `RESTACT_OBJECT_BINDING` mode or `RESTACT_POLICY_INHERITANCE` is on. While on, creating an object that has a stored descendant, or (under a coupled binding mode) a verb definition, owned by another principal is refused `409` with code `ancestor-claim`; the boot prints `ancestor-creation guard: on (…)` or `off (…)` and discovery advertises `ancestor-creation-guard`. | | `RESTACT_CHECKPOINT_DIR` | `<data-dir>/snapshots` | Where checkpoint snapshots land. Snapshots of a filesystem store are themselves valid store roots (`restactd restore --from <snapshot>` replaces a stopped host’s store with one). Survives `POST /admin/reset`. `restactd checkpoint` reads it with the same default, and without `--out` writes there with the same naming and `RESTACT_CHECKPOINT_KEEP` retention. A copy is written into `.partial-<name>` and renamed once it is stamped and flushed, so a `checkpoint-*` directory is always whole; a filesystem-store server removes leftover `.partial-*` directories at boot, and the offline command at its next run. Two hosts must not share one `RESTACT_CHECKPOINT_DIR`: each sweeps the directory’s `.partial-*` entries at boot. | | `RESTACT_CHECKPOINT_INTERVAL_SECS` | *(unset — no loop)* | Interval checkpoints, floored at 30 s. Runs even when `RESTACT_ADMIN_CHECKPOINT` is unset (operator config, not a caller-reachable mutation); configuring it advertises `admin-checkpoint` in discovery. A stopping host starts no new tick and finishes a copy already running, with no cap (see the grace period in the storage guide). | | `RESTACT_CHECKPOINT_KEEP` | `8` | Retention: keep the newest N `checkpoint-*` snapshots, pruning older ones after each run. `0` = keep everything. | ### Bootstrap / examples | Variable | Default | Effect | | ------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `RESTACT_EXAMPLES` | *(off)* | Set `1`/`true` (trimmed, case-insensitive; anything else, including a typo, stays off and the host starts) to seed the example content at startup and on `POST /admin/reset`: the demo verbs, the JavaScript and Python example verbs from `site/examples` with their backing objects, the `docs/<page>` objects and the `docs:renderHTML` / `docs:renderPDF` renderers. The JavaScript and Python runners register whenever their files exist, with or without it. Turning it off deletes nothing already seeded. `make run` and `make docker-run` set it. The image sets nothing. | | `RESTACT_DEMO_WASM` | `examples/demo/target/wasm32-wasip1/release/demo.wasm` | Path to the demo component seeded when `RESTACT_EXAMPLES=1`; if absent, the demo verbs are skipped. An installed layout seeds it only when this variable is set. | | `RESTACT_DEMO_NATIVE` | *(off)* | Set `1`/`true` to register demo **native verbs**: `sys:objects`, a function listing the caller’s readable object paths via the native `query` capability (run-as-caller), negotiating `application/activity+json` vs `application/json` on `Accept`; `sys:query`, a native query verb; the accessor `notes/{id}:get`; and the action `ctr/{id}:bump`. For exercising the native-verb path end-to-end (incl. under process isolation, which the worker inherits); the stock host registers no native verbs. The same switch also registers two demo custom routes, neither present on a default host: `GET /.well-known/demo-webfinger` and `POST /demo/egress`, which — unlike the inert read routes — performs a **credentialed** outbound call on the caller’s own principal (see [Mediated egress](#mediated-egress)). | | `RESTACT_JS_RUNNER_WASM` | installed: `<share>/runners/js_runner.wasm`; checkout: `examples/js-runner/target/wasm32-wasip2/release/js_runner.wasm` | The shared QuickJS runner component for the `text/javascript` tier. Unset or empty uses the layout’s default: an installed layout (the executable’s directory has a sibling `share/restactd/`) looks only under that share directory and never in the working directory, and a checkout looks under the working directory. `off` (any case) turns the tier off. Any other value is that path only, with no fallback, and an explicit path that cannot be registered (missing, unreadable, not a valid component, or failing the sha256 pin) logs a warning naming this variable and withdraws the tier, even if an earlier boot stored a runner. With the tier off or withdrawn, or no runner found, the host starts **without** the JavaScript tier (discovery omits it; an upload with `?lang=text/javascript` returns `415`); verbs already deployed keep running. The boot prints `layout: installed (share …)` or `layout: checkout (cwd …)`, then `javascript: <path> sha256 <12 hex>…`, `javascript: off (<reason>)` when the tier is gone, or `javascript: stored runner kept (<reason>)` when the default-path runner is absent or cannot be registered, no pin is set, and the runner stored with an earlier verb still serves the tier. Built by `scripts/build-js-runner.sh`. | | `RESTACT_JS_RUNNER_SHA` | *(unset)* | Optional sha256 **pin** for the JavaScript runner (the untrusted-JS TCB). If set, the runner’s content hash must match or the host refuses to register it, and the JavaScript tier is withdrawn: discovery drops `text/javascript`, a new deploy or redeploy of it answers `415`, and verbs already deployed keep their runner and still run; unset → register whatever is at the path and print its sha to pin. A release build bakes in the hash of the runners it ships with (`RESTACT_BUILD_{JS,PY}_RUNNER_SHA`); that hash applies to whichever file is chosen unless this variable names another, so a swapped runner leaves the tier off (the warning says which two variables run a different runner). | | `RESTACT_PY_RUNNER_WASM` | installed: `<share>/runners/py_runner.wasm`; checkout: `examples/py-runner/target/wasm32-wasip2/release/py_runner.wasm` | The shared monty runner component for the `text/x-python` tier, looked up like `RESTACT_JS_RUNNER_WASM` (layout default, `off`, or an explicit path with no fallback; an explicit path that cannot be registered withdraws the tier). With the tier off or withdrawn, or no runner found, the host starts **without** the Python tier (discovery omits it; an upload with `?lang=text/x-python` returns `415`); verbs already deployed keep running. The boot line is `python: <path> sha256 <12 hex>…`, `python: off (<reason>)` when the tier is gone, or `python: stored runner kept (<reason>)` as for JavaScript. Built by `scripts/build-py-runner.sh`. | | `RESTACT_PY_RUNNER_SHA` | *(unset)* | Optional sha256 **pin** for the Python runner (the untrusted-Python TCB). If set, the runner’s content hash must match or the host refuses to register it, and the Python tier is withdrawn: discovery drops `text/x-python`, a new deploy or redeploy of it answers `415`, and verbs already deployed keep their runner and still run; unset → register whatever is at the path and print its sha to pin. A release build bakes in the hash of the runners it ships with (`RESTACT_BUILD_{JS,PY}_RUNNER_SHA`); that hash applies to whichever file is chosen unless this variable names another, so a swapped runner leaves the tier off (the warning says which two variables run a different runner). | | `RESTACT_DOCS_DIR` | `docs` | Directory the docs pages are read from and seeded as `docs/<page>` object data when `RESTACT_EXAMPLES=1`. Only these top-level files are seeded: `README`, `architecture`, `authoring-verbs`, `building-applications`, `cli`, `configuration`, `development` and `http-api` (`.md`). A listed page that is missing is skipped, and any other `.md` file in the directory is never seeded.
