An installed layout has no default and uses it only when this variable is set (see `RESTACT_DOCS_DIR`). | | `RESTACT_RENDER_PDF_WASM` | checkout: `examples/render-pdf/target/wasm32-wasip1/release/render_pdf.wasm`; installed: *(none)* | Optional PDF renderer deployed on `docs:renderPDF` when `RESTACT_EXAMPLES=1`; if absent, that route is skipped. An installed layout has no default and deploys it only when this variable is set. | ## Build-time variables These are read by the build, never by the running server. | Variable | Effect | | ----------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `RESTACT_BUILD_COMMIT` | The commit `restactd --version` and the first boot line report. 7 to 40 lowercase hex digits, optionally followed by `-dirty`; the build keeps the first 12 digits and fails on any other value. Set it when building where git history is not available, such as the Docker build (`make docker-build` passes it; by hand, `--build-arg RESTACT_BUILD_COMMIT=$(git rev-parse --short=12 HEAD)`). Unset, the build reads the commit from the crate’s packaging data (`.cargo_vcs_info.json`, as in a build from crates.io), then from git in any profile; with neither it reports `commit=unknown`. | | `RESTACT_RELEASE_VERSION` | The version `restactd --version` and the first boot line report. Unset or empty, the version is `dev`. A release build sets it to the tag without its `v` (`0.1.0`, `0.1.0-rc.1`); the build fails unless it is `MAJOR.MINOR.PATCH` with an optional `-prerelease` and equals the workspace version in the root `Cargo.toml`, so a tag cannot ship a binary that reports another version. The Docker build takes it as `--build-arg RESTACT_RELEASE_VERSION=<version>`; the release workflow (`.github/workflows/release.yml`) sets it from the tag for the binaries it builds. | | `RESTACT_BUILD_JS_RUNNER_SHA` | The sha256 of the JavaScript runner a release build trusts. 64 lowercase hex digits; the build fails on any other value, and unless `RESTACT_BUILD_PY_RUNNER_SHA` is set too. Unset or empty, the build has no baked pin (source builds, `cargo install`). A stamped build adds `js-runner=<hash>` to the `--version` line and checks every JavaScript runner it loads against it, unless `RESTACT_JS_RUNNER_SHA` names a different hash. | | `RESTACT_BUILD_PY_RUNNER_SHA` | The same for the Python runner: 64 lowercase hex digits, set together with `RESTACT_BUILD_JS_RUNNER_SHA` or not at all. A stamped build adds `py-runner=<hash>` to the `--version` line. | ## Security Caution **The default configuration is open: no access control.** With **no `RESTACT_JWT_SECRET`** set, the host runs **anonymous-only**: every request is the `anonymous` principal, every anonymously-uploaded verb is owned by `anonymous`, and any caller is therefore its owner. That means **anyone can upload, replace the code of, re-policy, or delete any verb** via the management plane (`PUT`/`DELETE` on `:verb/code|policy|props`). This is intentional for local development; the default bind is `127.0.0.1`. **Do not expose a host beyond localhost without setting `RESTACT_JWT_SECRET`.** The threat model is **untrusted, possibly malicious uploads from day one**. The defenses, layered: * **Sandbox.** WASI 0.2 components with no ambient authority; a verb reaches only what its WIT world imports. No world offers `wasi:sockets`, and `wasi:filesystem` is linked with no directories. Disallowed imports are rejected at upload (`403`). * **The built-in site is opt-in.** `RESTACT_BUILTIN_SITE` (unset by default, see above) also gates the `repo_doc` fallback, which serves `docs/`, `openapi/`, `examples/`, `conformance/` and `wit/` from the process’s own working directory. That is a working-directory disclosure surface, so it stays off unless the site is explicitly enabled. It never serves `spec/`: `/spec/…` answers `404`. A symlink that resolves outside the directory it was requested under answers `404` too, as does any of those directories when it resolves outside the working directory or to the working directory itself. * **Resource limits.** Epoch (wall-clock → `408`), fuel (CPU → `429`), memory (`507`) on every invocation, on both executor backends. * **Host and origin checks.** Every request’s `Host` must be a loopback name or listed in `RESTACT_ALLOWED_HOSTS`, which stops DNS-rebinding pages from reaching a local host; a non-loopback bind without that list refuses to start. A browser `POST`/`PUT`/`PATCH`/`DELETE`, and any `/mcp` request, from another origin is refused unless `RESTACT_CORS_ALLOW_ORIGIN` admits it, which stops cross-site request forgery through a cookie-session proxy or against an open host. * **Authorization.** HS256 bearer auth + the `owner`/`public` policy, runs-as-caller — a verb can never exceed the caller’s authority. * **Anonymous mutation.** Once ANY auth is configured (`RESTACT_JWT_SECRET`, the `RESTACT_OIDC_*` set or `RESTACT_TRUSTED_IDENTITY_HEADER`), an anonymous caller may not mutate: deploy, `:verb` management writes and data-plane `PUT`/`PATCH`/`DELETE` answer `401`. Reads and execute-policy-gated action invocation stay open. With no auth configured the deployment is intentionally open. * **Quotas.** Per-tenant fuel/call caps, fail-closed — lifetime cumulative, or a per-window budget with `RESTACT_QUOTA_WINDOW_SECS`. `RESTACT_QUOTA_OBJECTS` caps the objects each owner holds; with no auth configured all callers share one anonymous count. * **Composition safety.** Declared call graph, single shared budget, depth/fan-out/cycle bounds, attenuation. * **Process isolation.** `RESTACT_ISOLATION=process` runs each request in a worker subprocess for cross-tenant memory isolation. * **Admin surface is deny-by-default.** The operator clean-reset (`POST /admin/reset`) does not exist until `RESTACT_ADMIN_RESET` names who may trigger it; configuring `public` on an exposed host hands every visitor a wipe-the-world button — gate it on a role/subject in any multi-tenant deployment. * **Log hygiene.** Every caller-, guest- and deployer-chosen value the host writes into a log line is escaped first, so a route, object path, principal label or a guest’s own error text cannot forge a second line on stderr. Operator-configured URLs have credentials removed before they appear in a log line or error. See [Log hygiene](#log-hygiene) below. * **OpenBao settings.** `RESTACT_SECRETS_URL` (feature `openbao`) must be the final address. Redirects are not followed, because every request carries the backend token in the `X-Vault-Token` header and a followed redirect would resend it to a host the configuration never named. Each call refuses any status outside its allowlist as a secrets-backend error: `resolve`, `meta` and `ping` accept `200` only, `delete` and `put` accept `200` or `204`. A `404` means “not found” for `resolve` and `meta` and “already purged” for `delete`. This is the same posture as `RESTACT_OIDC_JWKS_URL`. A plaintext `http://` `RESTACT_SECRETS_URL` to a non-loopback host boots with one `warning:` line naming the variable, the scheme and the host (never the query string or mount path). Userinfo (`user:password@`) in the URL is refused at boot, because the credential always comes from `RESTACT_SECRETS_TOKEN_FILE`. A query string or fragment is refused at boot too; both checks run on the raw string before it is parsed, so a value that does not parse cannot skip them. Every remaining error, warning and boot line that mentions the URL redacts a query string or fragment as well. Caution **Object data plane.** The data plane is **first-writer-owns**: the first writer of an object owns it (`$owner`) and only the owner may write its *undeclared* properties. But under the anonymous-open default **every caller is `anonymous`**, so they all share ownership — treat an exposed, unauthenticated host’s undeclared object data as world-writable, and **enable auth**. Declared properties follow their `acl`/`final`; the reserved `@`/`$` namespaces are unaddressable; shared properties aren’t writable via the data plane; per-object size + `RESTACT_QUOTA_CALLS` bound growth. Verbs that emit `text/html` (e.g. `render-html`) render attacker-controlled data: the host sends `nosniff` + a strict CSP, but don’t serve such output same-origin with privileged cookies. ### Log hygiene Escaping applies to every value a caller, a guest or a deployer can choose and the host writes into a log line: routes, object paths, principal labels and a guest’s own thrown error text. The escapers cover ASCII control and newline bytes, the C1 control range (U+0080 to U+009F) and the Unicode line breaks U+0085, U+2028 and U+2029, so one event is always one line. Unicode format characters (category Cf: bidi controls, zero-width characters, the soft hyphen) are escaped too, because they reorder or hide text in a log viewer. Ordinary non-ASCII, combining marks and single-code-point emoji pass through unchanged; a ZWJ emoji sequence is split at each joiner, and a tag-sequence flag and a ZWNJ are escaped, since all three are Cf. When an error embeds more than one caller-controlled value (a governed-tree denial that names both the caller’s path and an ancestor), the whole rendered text is escaped as one unit. Secret and stored content is never quoted back. Operator-configured URLs (`RESTACT_OIDC_JWKS_URL`, `RESTACT_SECRETS_URL`) have `user:password@` or `user@` userinfo stripped before they appear in a boot line, an error or an OpenBao status message. A value with no `@` prints unchanged. A value that fails to parse, or whose `@` does not introduce real userinfo, prints as the fixed literal `<unparseable url>`, never any byte of the input. Otherwise the userinfo is cut from the operator’s own spelling and the result is checked against the parsed URL, falling back to the same placeholder on any mismatch. Two limits follow from this. An ordinary `@` outside the authority (`https://host/a@b`, `?tenant=a@b`) looks like misplaced userinfo and also prints as `<unparseable url>`. And a credential placed in the query string of `RESTACT_OIDC_JWKS_URL` prints unchanged, so keep credentials out of it. `RESTACT_SECRETS_URL` is stricter: a query string or fragment is refused at boot and redacted wherever the URL is printed. Other diagnostics follow the same rule. A JWK’s key material is never logged, only its kind (`oct`, `OKP` or unrecognized). A corrupted on-disk object is reported by line and column, never by quoting the record. A PostgreSQL row with the wrong shape reports one fixed message naming the row’s kind, for example `invalid deployments/object/registration record (schema mismatch)`. A present but unparseable filesystem `deployments.json` or `registrations.json` is never read as empty, because the next write would serialize an empty map over the file and destroy every earlier record. `FsStore::open` refuses to load such a file and reports its path, the parse error category and the line and column, with the remediation (restore from backup, or move the file aside to start with no deployments or registrations). Each later deployment or registration write re-reads the manifest first and refuses, leaving the file untouched, if it no longer parses. A missing file still means no records. At boot, any failure to open `RESTACT_DATA_DIR` (an unwritable directory, a corrupt `format.json`, a corrupt manifest, or a `page-token.key` that is not a 32-byte regular file owned by the server’s user and readable and writable by the owner only) is a fatal refusal: one `fatal:` line on stderr and exit `1`, never a fallback to an ephemeral store. The page-token key file is hard-linked into place, so a data directory on a filesystem without hard links also refuses boot. A refused write on a running host answers `500 store-error`. ### Who may define a verb where Two different questions, with two different answers — conflating them is how a multi-tenant deployment ends up with a hole: * **Replacing an existing verb** is governed by that verb’s own `write` ACL, on every interface — `POST /v1/modules` at an already-registered **exact** route is the same operation `PUT :verb/code` performs, and both are gated. An authorized non-owner gets a *code* grant only: the owner and policy are preserved verbatim, `exec=` is refused (`400`), `runs_as=owner` is refused (`403`), and a runs-as-owner verb is replaceable by its owner alone. `PUT :verb/policy` and `DELETE :verb/code` are **owner-only** (`manage`). * **Defining a verb at a route nothing is registered at yet** is *first-deployer-owns* — there is no prior registration whose ACL could be consulted. On a multi-tenant host the authority that answers “may this principal define a verb here?” is **object ownership**, and it is consulted only in the coupled binding modes: | `RESTACT_OBJECT_BINDING` | Fresh route on an object you own | …on someone else’s object | …on an object that doesn’t exist | | ------------------------------------------------ | -------------------------------- | ---------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------- | | `implicit` (default only without authentication) | allowed | **allowed** — planes are decoupled | allowed | | `auto-create` | allowed | `403` | allowed; the object is minted **caller-owned** | | `strict` | allowed | `403` | `409 object-binding` | **Wildcard routes are covered too.** A route with `{id}` segments is gated on its deepest **concrete ancestor** — the literal prefix up to the first wildcard — so `ws/{id}:pageView` and `ws/{id}/pages/{id}/blocks/{id}:cellView` both require ownership of `ws`. A route with a *leading* wildcard (`{id}:v`) owns no object at all and is refused (`409`) in the coupled modes; a truly unbound service-root verb (`:bulkPing`) binds to root, so the ownership gate does not apply to it. Root scope has its own rule, below. **Every verb deployed at root scope is operator-only.** Root scope is the unbound service root (`:bulkPing`, `:get`) or a leading-wildcard route (`{id}:get`). A verb bound there is reachable from every object on the host. A reserved representation accessor (`get`, `list`, `create`, `update`, `set`, `delete`) bound there answers every bare data-plane request that has no more specific accessor, and runs with the *caller’s* authority, so any principal able to plant one could read, or rewrite as another user, every object on the host. Whenever auth is configured, in **every** binding mode (`implicit` included), `POST /v1/modules`, `PUT :verb/code` and `POST :verb/rollback` on a root-scope route answer `403` unless the caller matches `RESTACT_ADMIN_RESET` (the operator; a path-scoped sub-token never qualifies). The rule covers any verb name, the lifecycle hooks (`onCreate`, `onChange`, `onDelete`) included: a root hook sees every creation’s path and creator. With no operator configured nobody may deploy one. A verb bound to an object the caller owns is unaffected, as is an anonymous-open host (no auth). Three companion rules close the same hole. (1) **Root scope is the registry’s own parse**: an object path must be canonical on `POST /v1/modules` and on every `:verb/<prop>` route, so a path with an empty segment or a leading or trailing `/` (`/:get`, `//:get`, `/{id}:get`, `%2F:get`, `a//b:zap`) is `400 invalid-input` for every caller, the operator included; those spellings would otherwise alias root scope and dodge the gate under `implicit` binding. (2) **`PUT :verb/props` at root scope is operator-only** (`403` for everyone else on an auth-on host): a declared property’s ACL is resolved registry-wide, so a declaration on a root-scope verb would reshape every object’s reads and writes: a property ACL only narrows an object’s own policy, but it can still hide a member, wedge it `final` or inject a default everywhere. (3) **The operator may remove any root-scope verb**: on an auth-on host `DELETE /v1/:get/code` (and likewise any other root-scope route) by a caller matching `RESTACT_ADMIN_RESET` succeeds even when the operator does not own the verb; everyone else is unchanged. This is the clean-up for a registration planted earlier; `/admin/reset` wipes the whole host and is not the remedy. **A delegated request is never the operator**, even when its grant’s subject matches `RESTACT_ADMIN_RESET`: it is an ordinary caller at the four operator paths (deploy via `POST /v1/modules`, `PUT :verb/code` and rollback, root `PUT :verb/props`, and the operator root `DELETE`). To find a planted root-scope verb, read the verbs listed in discovery (`GET …/meta` answers `403` to the operator on a verb it does not own, so it cannot be used for this). Deployments are stored under their canonical route (an empty segment is dropped and every `{name}` is spelled `{id}`). A server that finds a record stored under a non-canonical spelling, with or without a row already under the canonical spelling, refuses to boot, names the route and leaves the store untouched. To switch a planted registration off while you remove it, `RESTACT_ACCESSOR_DISPATCH=off` disables accessor dispatch entirely and `RESTACT_LIFECYCLE_HOOKS=off` disables lifecycle hooks (`RESTACT_ACCESSOR_DISPATCH=off` does not stop hooks). Seed your collection roots (`ws` for `ws/{id}:…`) with an operator credential before users can reach the port. The first writer owns a root that does not exist yet, and the owner of a root may define verbs that resolve on every entity beneath it. Three consequences to plan for before switching a live deployment to a coupled mode: 1. `strict` refuses a deploy onto an object that does not exist yet — **create the object first**. 2. The gate reads `$owner`, **not** the object’s `$policy`: granting someone `write` on an object (directly or through a governed anchor) does not let them define verbs on it. 3. Creating an object under an existing collection root accepts that root owner’s verbs, accessors and hooks for it. A create that would sit above objects or verb definitions owned by someone else is refused `409` (`ancestor-claim`) unless one of the `RESTACT_ADMIN_REOWN` subjects makes it directly with `X-Restact-Operator-Exempt: ancestor-claim`. An object’s owner may deploy a more specific verb that takes precedence over an inherited one on that object. Verbs run as the caller, so a visitor who invokes a verb on someone else’s object runs code that object’s owner chose, with the visitor’s authority. Deploying the inherited verb with `?runs_as=owner` does not change this, because the owner’s verb is a separate definition. Treat verb output as untrusted at the client. A visitor who only needs to read can use a short-lived read-only [path-scoped token](/docs/reference/http-api/#schedules-subscriptions-and-tokens) where the host offers one: it reads the stored representation and never runs a verb or an accessor. ## Production checklist Before exposing a host beyond localhost: * [ ] `RESTACT_JWT_SECRET` set (auth on, so the host is no longer anonymous-only). * [ ] If using `RESTACT_TRUSTED_IDENTITY_HEADER`: restact is reachable *only* through the trusted proxy, and that proxy **sets the header and strips any client-supplied copy** (otherwise identity is spoofable). Prefer `RESTACT_OIDC_*` for a cryptographically-verified forwarded token where you can — it needs no such reachability assumption. * [ ] If using `RESTACT_OIDC_*`: all three of `RESTACT_OIDC_ISSUER` / `_JWKS_URL` / `_AUDIENCE` are set (audience validation is mandatory), the JWKS URL is HTTPS, and `RESTACT_OIDC_AUDIENCE` matches the audience your IdP mints for restact. **Egress to the issuer’s JWKS host on 443 is allowed** (a blocked fetch fails closed → all OIDC tokens `401`). * [ ] **Object data ACLs** declared on any object whose data isn’t meant to be world-readable/writable (undeclared = public). `RESTACT_QUOTA_CALLS` set to bound data-plane writes. * [ ] `RESTACT_OBJECT_BINDING` left at its authenticated-host default `strict` (or `auto-create`) on any host where more than one principal deploys verbs — do not override it with `implicit`, which lets any authenticated caller define a verb at any not-yet-registered route, including under another principal’s object paths ([Who may define a verb where](#who-may-define-a-verb-where)). With it set: every **collection root** a wildcard-rooted verb binds to (`ws` for `ws/{id}:…`) is created, credentialed, **before** the host is reachable by users — roots are first-writer-owns. * [ ] `RESTACT_DATA_DIR` or `RESTACT_DATABASE_URL` set (a durable store; process isolation needs one of them). * [ ] `RESTACT_ISOLATION=process` for cross-tenant memory isolation. * [ ] `RESTACT_QUOTA_FUEL` / `RESTACT_QUOTA_CALLS` set (denial-of-wallet defense), plus `RESTACT_QUOTA_WINDOW_SECS` on any long-running host — without it the caps are lifetime cumulative and every active tenant eventually trips permanently. * [ ] `RESTACT_QUOTA_OBJECTS` set (for example `10000`) when the deployment accepts writes from untrusted principals. It needs authentication to be per-user; without it all callers share one count. * [ ] `RESTACT_REQUEST_TIMEOUT_MS` / `RESTACT_MAX_INFLIGHT` / `RESTACT_WORKER_TIMEOUT_MS` reviewed for your load. * [ ] `RESTACT_BIND` set deliberately, behind TLS termination. * [ ] `RESTACT_ALLOWED_HOSTS` lists exactly the names clients use (the public name behind a proxy, plus any in-cluster service name). Avoid `*` unless the proxy in front pins `Host`. * [ ] The management plane is reachable only by trusted principals (policy review). * [ ] **If running active-active (`RESTACT_DATABASE_URL`):** the DB link is encrypted (`sslmode=verify-full` with a CA, **not** `RESTACT_PG_ALLOW_PLAINTEXT`); `/readyz` is wired as the orchestrator **readiness** probe (it does a `SELECT 1`, so a node whose DB link is down is drained) and the static `/healthz` as **liveness**; Postgres `max_connections` ≥ N × (`RESTACT_PG_POOL_SIZE` + `RESTACT_PG_LOCK_POOL_SIZE` + a few LISTEN conns), plus up to `RESTACT_MAX_WASM_INFLIGHT` worker conns under process isolation; a large cluster is fronted by PgBouncer in **session** mode (transaction mode breaks the session advisory locks). ## Examples 
```sh
# Local demo (default): open, ephemeral, localhost only.
cargo run -p restactd


# Durable + authenticated, still localhost.
RESTACT_DATA_DIR=./data RESTACT_JWT_SECRET=replace-with-at-least-32-random-bytes cargo run -p restactd


# Hardened, exposed.
RESTACT_BIND=0.0.0.0:8080 \
RESTACT_ALLOWED_HOSTS=restact.example.com \
RESTACT_JWT_SECRET="$SECRET" \
RESTACT_DATA_DIR=/var/lib/restact \
RESTACT_ISOLATION=process \
RESTACT_QUOTA_FUEL=1000000000 RESTACT_QUOTA_CALLS=10000 RESTACT_QUOTA_WINDOW_SECS=86400 \
  cargo run -p restactd --release


# Behind an authenticating reverse proxy (vendor-neutral): trust the identity the
# proxy forwards. Plug in whatever header your proxy emits — e.g.
# X-Auth-Request-Email (oauth2-proxy), Cf-Access-Authenticated-User-Email
# (Cloudflare Access), or Remote-User (Authelia). ONLY safe when restact is
# reachable solely through that proxy, and the proxy strips client-supplied copies.
# The proxy forwards the public Host, so list it.
RESTACT_BIND=127.0.0.1:8080 \
RESTACT_ALLOWED_HOSTS=restact.example.com \
RESTACT_TRUSTED_IDENTITY_HEADER=X-Auth-Request-Email \
RESTACT_TRUSTED_ROLES_HEADER=X-Auth-Request-Groups \
RESTACT_DATA_DIR=/var/lib/restact \
  cargo run -p restactd --release


# Cryptographically verified OIDC tokens (vendor-neutral).
# Verifies a forwarded RS256/ES256 JWT against the issuer's JWKS — no
# reachability assumption. Point at any OIDC IdP (Auth0, Keycloak, Okta,
# Entra ID, Google, …); audience validation is mandatory.
RESTACT_BIND=0.0.0.0:8080 \
RESTACT_ALLOWED_HOSTS=restact.example.com \
RESTACT_OIDC_ISSUER="https://your-tenant.example.com/" \
RESTACT_OIDC_JWKS_URL="https://your-tenant.example.com/.well-known/jwks.json" \
RESTACT_OIDC_AUDIENCE="restact-api" \
RESTACT_DATA_DIR=/var/lib/restact \
  cargo run -p restactd --release


# Active-active: N nodes behind a load balancer sharing one PostgreSQL.
# Needs a binary built with the `postgres` feature. Wire /readyz as the readiness probe.
RESTACT_BIND=0.0.0.0:8080 \
RESTACT_ALLOWED_HOSTS=restact.example.com,restact.internal \
RESTACT_JWT_SECRET="$SECRET" \
RESTACT_DATABASE_URL="postgres://user:pass@db.internal:5432/restact?sslmode=verify-full&sslrootcert=/etc/ssl/db-ca.pem" \
RESTACT_ISOLATION=process \
RESTACT_QUOTA_FUEL=1000000000 RESTACT_QUOTA_CALLS=10000 RESTACT_QUOTA_WINDOW_SECS=86400 \
  cargo run -p restactd --release --features postgres
```
 A two-node local demo lives in [`docker-compose.postgres.yml`](https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/blob/main/docker-compose.postgres.yml) (`docker compose -f docker-compose.postgres.yml up --build` → node A on `:8080`, node B on `:8081`, sharing one Postgres). ### Container The [`Dockerfile`](https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/blob/main/Dockerfile) image already sets `RESTACT_BIND=0.0.0.0:8080`, `RESTACT_DATA_DIR=/data` and `RESTACT_ALLOWED_HOSTS=localhost`, so it answers at `http://localhost:8080` and refuses any other `Host` until you set the names clients use. Pass any other variable with `-e` and persist `/data` with a volume. Pull a [released image](/docs/operate/docker/#released-images) or build one from a checkout (`make docker-build IMAGE=restactd`; see [Development → Docker](/docs/project/development/#docker)). 
```sh
# Durable + authenticated + process isolation, mapped to host port 8080.
docker run -d -p 8080:8080 \
  -e RESTACT_ALLOWED_HOSTS=restact.example.com \
  -e RESTACT_JWT_SECRET="$SECRET" \
  -e RESTACT_ISOLATION=process \
  -e RESTACT_QUOTA_FUEL=1000000000 -e RESTACT_QUOTA_CALLS=10000 -e RESTACT_QUOTA_WINDOW_SECS=86400 \
  -v restact-data:/data \
  restactd:dev
```
 > A **bind-mounted** data dir (`-v ./data:/data`), or a named volume that already holds files owned by another uid, must be writable by the image’s non-root user (uid 65532), or the container **refuses to boot** (an unwritable `RESTACT_DATA_DIR` is a fatal error, never a silent fallback to in-memory). A fresh, empty **named volume** (`-v restact-data:/data`, as above) gets the right ownership automatically. Fix the others as in [Run with Docker](/docs/operate/docker/#run-it).

# Conformance suite

> restact-conformance is a black-box test runner for REST:ACT servers. It reads a server's discovery document, checks the behavior every server must have, then checks each optional feature the server…

`restact-conformance` is a black-box test runner for REST:ACT servers. It reads a server’s discovery document, checks the behavior every server must have, then checks each optional feature the server advertises. Use it to test restactd, or to test a server you are building. The source is in [`conformance/`](https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/tree/main/conformance). The suite is the executable form of the requirements in the [REST:ACT specification](https://github.com/stackumi/restact), and it also serves as the integration test for the reference host. ## Run it against restactd From a checkout, one script builds the host, the runner and the fixture verbs, then runs the suite against both executor backends, the in-process executor and the per-request process-isolation worker, and then two object-binding legs, `strict` in-process and `auto-create` under process isolation. `RESTACT_CONFORMANCE_LEGS` (`inproc`, `iso`, `objbind` or `all`) picks the legs, and `RESTACT_CONFORMANCE_PORT` sets the port the host binds. 
```sh
./scripts/conformance-test.sh
```
 If port 8080 is taken, set `RESTACT_TEST_PORT` to a free port. The script starts its own host, so stop any running one first. `make conformance` runs the same script. ## Run it against another server Build the runner and point it at the server: 
```sh
./scripts/build-examples.sh
(cd examples/netizen && cargo component build --release --target wasm32-wasip2)
cargo build -p restact-conformance
RESTACT_BASE_URL=https://your-server.example \
RESTACT_FIX_DEMO=examples/demo/target/wasm32-wasip1/release/demo.wasm \
RESTACT_FIX_WC=examples/word-count/target/wasm32-wasip1/release/word_count.wasm \
RESTACT_FIX_NET=examples/netizen/target/wasm32-wasip2/release/netizen.wasm \
./target/debug/restact-conformance
```
 The runner calls `GET /.well-known/restact` first and exits with status 2 if it cannot reach the server. Without `RESTACT_BASE_URL` it uses `http://127.0.0.1:8080`. The runner deploys fixture verbs through `POST /v1/modules` before it checks anything. A server that deploys verbs some other way can pre-deploy the same verbs, named `conf:*`, and set `RESTACT_CONFORMANCE_NO_SETUP=1`. The `setup` function in [`conformance/src/main.rs`](https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/blob/main/conformance/src/main.rs) lists them. | Variable | Purpose | | ------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `RESTACT_BASE_URL` | The server to test. Default `http://127.0.0.1:8080`. | | `RESTACT_CONFORMANCE_TOKEN` | A bearer token for the default client, which deploys the fixtures and drives most checks. | | `RESTACT_CONFORMANCE_NO_SETUP` | Set to `1` to skip fixture deployment and use verbs already deployed. | | `RESTACT_FIX_DEMO`, `RESTACT_FIX_WC`, `RESTACT_FIX_NET` | Paths to the fixture components. Each unset `RESTACT_FIX_*` variable falls back to its file in `../share/restact-conformance/fixtures/` next to the runner’s binary (the layout of the release tarball); a variable that is set always wins. A check that needs one is skipped, with the reason, if the variable is unset (with no such directory) or the file is unreadable. | | `RESTACT_FIX_COUNTER` | A JavaScript fixture, with the same fallback to `counter.js`. Checks that need it are skipped when neither is available. | | `RESTACT_CONFORMANCE_ONLY` | A comma-separated list of check-id prefixes. Only checks whose id starts with one of them run. | | `RESTACT_CONFORMANCE_REQUIRE_FEATURES` | A comma-separated list of feature tokens. A listed feature that the server does not advertise fails instead of being skipped. | The authorization checks need one token per subject, signed so the server accepts them. Without a token, the checks that use it are skipped. | Variable | Subject | | ----------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `RESTACT_TOKEN_ALICE`, `RESTACT_TOKEN_BOB` | `alice` and `bob`. The delegation checks need these literal subjects. | | `RESTACT_TOKEN_OPERATOR` | The operator named for the re-own checks. | | `RESTACT_TOKEN_GROUPS` | Subject `gina` with a `groups` claim that includes `builders`. | | `RESTACT_TOKEN_CLIENT` | A subject that starts with `client:`, for the delegated client-actor checks. | | `RESTACT_TOKEN_DELEG_REG`, `RESTACT_TOKEN_DELEG_DOOR` | Optional separate owner tokens for the two delegated registration checks. Without them those checks reuse the shared token and wait 61 seconds first, to stay under the grant routes’ rate limit of 30 writes per subject per minute. | | `RESTACT_CONFORMANCE_SECRETS_PATH` | A path under the secrets mount, for the secret-value check. | ## How checks are gated Each check has an id, a level and a reference to a section of the specification. | Level | Meaning | | -------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | | Must | The server must behave this way. Every server is checked. | | Should | A recommendation. A failure is reported as a warning. | | Optional | Runs only if the server advertises the named feature token. Otherwise it is skipped. | The core checks cover addressing, verb resolution, the function and action methods, problem-document errors, the three resource limits, and the discovery document itself. Optional checks are tied to the feature tokens in the [features table](/docs/reference/features/): composition, the data plane, conditional requests, async operations, lifecycle hooks, events, schedules, the scripting tiers, delegated tokens, the admin plane and more. A server that advertises nothing optional is checked against the core only. This gating is how the suite stays useful for a small server. A feature the server does not claim is never counted against it, and a feature it does claim has to work. ## Read the results The runner prints the target and the advertised features to stderr, then one line per check on stdout. Example output, with a made-up failure: 
```text
  PASS CORE/METH-03   §taxonomy
  SKIP OPT/ASYNC-02   §Async Operations (feature 'async-operations' not advertised)
  FAIL OPT/COMP-03    §Composition — status 200 (wanted 508); body=…
```
 | Result | Meaning | | ------ | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `PASS` | The behavior matched. | | `FAIL` | A must-level or advertised-feature check did not match. The line gives the status and a body excerpt. | | `WARN` | A should-level check did not match. | | `SKIP` | The server does not advertise the feature, or a token or fixture was not supplied. | The last line is a summary such as `120 passed, 0 failed, 1 warned, 30 skipped`. The exit status is 0 when nothing failed, 1 when any check failed, and 2 when the runner could not start: the server was unreachable, a fixture deploy failed, or two checks shared an id. A skipped or warned check does not change the exit status. The id names the area: `CORE/` for the mandatory core, `OPT/` for an optional feature, and `AUTHZ/` for the authorization checks, which need tokens. A few checks use their own prefix: `ACL/`, `COMPOSE/`, `CONS/`, `DISC/`, `DISCOVERY/`, `IDENT/`, `OPENAPI/`, `REC/` and `REG/`. Each id maps to a section of the specification, so a failing line points at the requirement. ## Limits The suite does not check requirements with no observable HTTP behavior, such as a `GET` never changing state or the use of TLS. It does not validate OpenAPI documents. `python3 openapi/scripts/validate.py` separately checks the `x-restact` extension schema and the example OpenAPI documents in `openapi/examples/`. ## Related * [Feature flags in discovery](/docs/reference/features/) for the tokens that gate the optional checks. * [Development setup](/docs/project/development/#test) for the other test suites. * [Contributing](/docs/project/contributing/) for what a change must pass.

# Errors and status codes

> restactd reports errors as RFC 9457 problem documents with two extension members, restactcode and restactverb. This page lists the response shape, every status code the API returns, and the…

restactd reports errors as RFC 9457 problem documents with two extension members, `restact_code` and `restact_verb`. This page lists the response shape, every status code the API returns, and the `restact_code` values you can match on. ## Response shape An error response has the content type `application/problem+json`. 
```sh
curl -si localhost:8080/v1/nope:verb
```
 
```http
HTTP/1.1 404 Not Found
content-type: application/problem+json


{"detail":"no verb deployed","restact_code":"verb-not-deployed","restact_verb":":verb","status":404,"title":"Not Found","type":"about:blank"}
```
 | Member | Meaning | | -------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `type` | Always `about:blank`. | | `title` | The standard reason phrase for `status`. | | `status` | The HTTP status code. | | `detail` | A human-readable explanation. Safe to show to a user; it never echoes secrets. | | `restact_code` | A stable machine-readable code. Match on this, not on `detail`. | | `restact_verb` | The part of the route from the colon onward, such as `:summarize`. Empty when the request named no verb. | Two headers can come with an error. A `401` carries `WWW-Authenticate: Bearer`. A `408` or a compute-budget `429` carries `Retry-After: 1`, an advisory number of seconds. A quota `429` carries the seconds until the quota window rolls over, and no `Retry-After` when waiting will not help (a lifetime cap or the storage quota). A request that the web framework rejects before routing, such as a `POST /v1/modules` without the required `verb` query parameter, returns a short `text/plain` `400` instead of a problem document. ## Status codes | Status | When | Typical `restact_code` | | ------ | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `400` | The request is malformed: an invalid component (not a component, or one without the `invoke` export), a body that is not a JSON object, a bad parameter, a `filter=` that is not `key=value`, a malformed `Host` header, an `If-Match` or `If-None-Match` on a request whose route cannot evaluate it (a write an accessor verb serves, a verb-route action, an event-stream subscribe) or a malformed one, an invalid call graph, or a `function` verb that imports a capability with side effects. | `invalid-input`, `invalid-component`, `invalid-call-graph`, `forbidden-side-effect`, `invalid-filter` | | `401` | The request needs authentication and has none, or the bearer token is invalid. Once any authentication is configured, an anonymous caller cannot deploy or write. | `unauthorized` | | `403` | The caller is authenticated but not allowed: the object or verb policy refuses, a property write is refused, the verb did not declare the call it made, an upload imports a capability that is not granted or that the host does not provide, the `Host` header is not on the allowlist, or a cross-origin browser request is not permitted. | `forbidden`, `not-declared`, `disallowed-import`, `forbidden-side-effect`, `delegation-bound` | | `404` | No verb, object, property, operation or route matches. A route that resolves to no verb is `verb-not-deployed`. | `not-found`, `verb-not-deployed` | | `405` | The method does not fit the verb: `POST` to a function, `GET` to an action, or any other method on a verb route. | `method-not-allowed` | | `408` | The call ran past its wall-clock limit, or the request timed out. | `timeout` | | `409` | A conflict with current state: an `Idempotency-Key` that is still in progress, a write to a `final` property that is already set, a verb deployed on an object that does not exist under strict object binding, a write that would create an object above one another principal owns, or a clash with the verb already deployed. | `conflict`, `property-final`, `object-binding`, `ancestor-claim` | | `412` | A precondition failed: the `ETag` in `If-Match` is stale, or `If-None-Match: *` found the resource already exists. | `precondition-failed` | | `413` | The input is over a size limit: a module over 10 MiB, an object or value over the per-object cap, a blob over `RESTACT_MAX_BLOB_BYTES`, or a published event over its data cap or an invocation’s published total. | `module-too-large`, `object-too-large` | | `415` | The upload names a source language the host has no runtime for, or, on a path no `:update` accessor serves, a non-empty data-plane `PUT` body carries no usable `Content-Type` (none, blank, or not a single `type/subtype`, or has a comma anywhere in it); more than one `Content-Type` line is refused whatever the body. | `unsupported-language`, `invalid-input` | | `422` | The input is understood but refused: uploaded source that fails its syntax check, or an error a verb returns for bad input. A guest error with an unrecognised code also maps here. | `invalid-source`, `invalid-input`, `script-error` | | `429` | A budget or quota is used up: the fuel budget, a per-tenant quota, a rate limit, or the concurrency cap of a mediated outbound call. | `cpu-budget`, `quota`, `quota-exceeded`, `egress-busy` | | `500` | The host or the verb failed in a way the caller did not cause: a trap, a script runtime fault, a store write error, a host-side failure while serving the request, or a stored verb that no longer links against this host (an interface it imports was removed or changed since it was deployed). The verb stays listed; redeploy it against this host or delete it. | `trap`, `script-runtime`, `script-frame`, `store-error`, `host-error`, `internal`, `verb-unlinkable` | | `501` | The host does not support something the request needs, such as a feature that is switched off. | `unsupported-feature`, `unsupported-consistency` | | `502` | An outbound call or model completion reached an upstream that failed or redirected. | `egress-upstream-error`, `egress-redirect-refused`, `llm-upstream-error` | | `503` | The host cannot serve the request now: the store is unreachable, too many asynchronous operations or event subscriptions are open, an uploaded source’s syntax check ran past its window (`Retry-After`; nothing was stored), or a feature needs configuration that is missing, such as a token issuer. | `unavailable` | | `507` | The call exceeded its memory limit. | `memory-limit` | | `508` | A composed call formed a cycle, or went deeper or wider than the host allows. | `composition-cycle` | Success and cache responses are not errors, but you will see them next to these: `200`, `201`, `202` (an action `POST` with `Prefer: respond-async`; poll the `Location: /operations/{id}`), `204`, and `304` for a matching `If-None-Match`. ## Resource limits A verb runs under three limits. Each has its own status so a client can tell which one it hit. | Limit | Status | `restact_code` | | --------------- | ------ | -------------- | | Wall-clock time | `408` | `timeout` | | Fuel (compute) | `429` | `cpu-budget` | | Memory | `507` | `memory-limit` | Composed calls share one budget, so a callee can exhaust the caller’s fuel. The defaults and the operator ceilings are in [Capabilities and budgets](/docs/concepts/capabilities-and-budgets/). ## Errors from outbound calls The mediated outbound call and the `llm` interface add their own codes. They fall into four groups. * A `403` means the call was refused by policy: the destination is not on the allowlist (`egress-not-allowed`), resolves to a blocked address (`egress-blocked-address`), or a connector or secret is not governed or not readable by the caller (`egress-denied`, `egress-connector-ungoverned`, `secret-denied`, `llm-model-not-allowed`). * A `422` means the connector or request is invalid (`egress-connector-invalid`, `egress-method-refused`, `connector-kind-mismatch`, `llm-provider-unsupported`, `budget-unconfigured`). * A `413`, `429` or `502` means a bound was hit or the upstream failed (`egress-bounds-exceeded`, `egress-busy`, `llm-request-too-large`, `llm-calls-exceeded`, `budget-exhausted`, `egress-upstream-error`). * A `503` means the secret could not be resolved or the call is not available in a worker (`secret-unresolvable`, `egress-unavailable-in-worker`, `typed-call-unavailable-under-isolation`). See [Outbound calls and secrets](/docs/guides/egress/). ## Errors from your own verbs A verb returns an error as an `invocation-error` with a `code` and a `message`; see [WIT interfaces](/docs/reference/wit/). If the code is one of the reserved codes above, the host uses that code’s status. Any other code becomes a `422` with that code in `restact_code`, so a verb can report its own failures, such as `out-of-stock`, without a new status. ## Related * [HTTP API](/docs/reference/http-api/) for the routes that return these errors. * [Troubleshooting](/docs/operate/troubleshooting/) for fixing the common ones. * The [REST:ACT specification](https://github.com/stackumi/restact) for the normative error rules.

# Feature flags in discovery

> A host lists the optional features it supports as strings in service.features of its discovery document. A client reads the list and uses only what it finds there; it never assumes a feature by trial.

A host lists the optional features it supports as strings in `service.features` of its discovery document. A client reads the list and uses only what it finds there; it never assumes a feature by trial. 
```sh
curl -s localhost:8080/.well-known/restact | jq '.service.features'
```
 A host with no configuration advertises 49 tokens, and 50 when a scripting runner is built, because `source-languages` joins the list. Rows marked **default** are among them. Rows marked **opt-in** appear only after the operator sets what the third column names, and the token disappears again when it is unset. The [REST:ACT specification](https://github.com/stackumi/restact) holds the registry of feature tokens and their normative meaning. See [Discovery and optional features](/docs/concepts/discovery/) for how clients use them. Related discovery fields that are not feature tokens: `consistency`, `objectBinding`, `accessorDispatch`, `lifecycleHooks`, `acceptedSourceLanguages` and `openapi`. ## Verb deployment and management | Token | What it enables | When it is on | Guide | | --------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------- | | `code-management` | Read, replace and delete a verb’s code and properties over HTTP (`:verb/code`, `:verb/policy`, `:verb/props`). | **default** | [HTTP API](/docs/reference/http-api/) | | `verb-reference-deployment` | `POST /v1/modules` creates or replaces a verb. | **default** | [HTTP API](/docs/reference/http-api/) | | `verb-private-default` | A new verb is owner-only until the deployer widens its policy. | **default** | [Authoring reference](/docs/guides/authoring-verbs/) | | `verb-conditional-requests` | `ETag` and `If-Match` on verb code, policy and property writes, and `If-None-Match: *` to create without replacing. | **default** | [HTTP API](/docs/reference/http-api/) | | `verb-history` | `GET :verb/history` and `POST :verb/rollback`: the deployment history of a verb and a rollback to an earlier version. | **default** | [HTTP API](/docs/reference/http-api/) | | `verb-schemas` | `POST /v1/modules` takes `input_schema` and `output_schema` (JSON Schema 2020-12, at most 8 KiB each), carried as `inputSchema` and `outputSchema` in discovery and typing `/openapi.json` and the `restact mcp` tools. Advisory; never enforced. | **default** | [HTTP API](/docs/reference/http-api/) | | `source-languages` | At least one source language besides `application/wasm` is accepted on upload. The accepted types are in `acceptedSourceLanguages`. | opt-in: start the host with the JavaScript runner, the Python runner, or both built | [Language tiers](/docs/concepts/language-tiers/) | | `presentation-verbs` | A function verb with `produces=application/vnd.restact.ui+json` returns a declarative UI document. | **default** | [HTTP API](/docs/reference/http-api/) | | `openapi-document` | # Glossary

> Accessor verb. A verb named get, list, create, update, set or delete on an object. A matching request for that object's data runs the verb instead of reading or writing the store directly. See…

**Accessor verb.** A verb named `get`, `list`, `create`, `update`, `set` or `delete` on an object. A matching request for that object’s data runs the verb instead of reading or writing the store directly. See [Verbs, objects and routes](/docs/concepts/verbs-and-objects/#accessor-verbs). **Action.** A verb called with `POST` that may change state. Compare *function*. **Attenuation.** The rule that a callee gets at most the authority of its caller. A composed verb or a gated capability call never gains authority on the way down, except a verb deployed to run as its owner. See [Identity and authority](/docs/concepts/identity-and-authority/). **Binding mode.** The server setting that decides what happens when you deploy a verb on an object that does not exist: `implicit` allows it and leaves object and verb independent, `auto-create` creates the object for the deployer, and `strict` refuses with `409`. Set with `RESTACT_OBJECT_BINDING`; the default is `strict` when authentication is configured and `implicit` otherwise. **Capability.** A host interface a verb may import, such as `object`, `compose`, `event` or `llm`. A verb can call only the capabilities it imports and the host grants. See [Capabilities and budgets](/docs/concepts/capabilities-and-budgets/). **Coupled mode.** The binding modes `auto-create` and `strict`. In them an object and its verbs belong together: you must own an existing object to deploy a verb onto it, and deleting the object deletes its bound verbs. **Epoch.** The Wasmtime mechanism restactd uses for the wall-clock limit. The host advances a counter on a timer, and a verb that is still running when it passes the deadline stops with `408`. **Feature token.** A string in `service.features` of the discovery document that says an optional behaviour is present. See [Feature flags in discovery](/docs/reference/features/). **Fuel.** Wasmtime’s deterministic count of executed instructions, used as the CPU limit. A verb that spends it all stops with `429`. **Function.** A verb called with `GET`. It cannot write, publish or call an action, so its result is safe to cache. Compare *action*. **Lifecycle hook.** A verb named `onCreate`, `onChange` or `onDelete` that runs after a write or delete to its object commits. It runs as its registrant. See [Lifecycle hooks](/docs/guides/lifecycle-hooks/). **Object.** A path in the server’s tree. It holds data and may have verbs bound to it. **Presentation verbs.** An optional extension of REST:ACT in which a function returns a declarative UI document (media type `application/vnd.restact.ui+json`) instead of data. Specified in a separate draft in the [Specification repository](https://github.com/stackumi/restact). **Registrant.** The principal who deployed a lifecycle hook or registered a schedule or subscription. These later run with the registrant’s authority and nothing more. **Run as the caller.** The default identity of a verb: it executes with the authority of whoever called it, not its owner’s or the server’s. A verb can opt into running as its owner, but the caller must still be allowed to execute it. See [Identity and authority](/docs/concepts/identity-and-authority/). **Runner.** The shared component that interprets script verbs: QuickJS for JavaScript and monty for Python. See [Language tiers](/docs/concepts/language-tiers/). **Verb.** A sandboxed program, uploaded over HTTP or registered by the embedding program as a native verb, and called at `/v1/<object>:<verb>`. It is either a *function* or an *action*.

# HTTP API

> The wire surface of the reference host. This is a practical reference; the normative definitions are in the REST:ACT specification, which also covers the conceptual model.

The wire surface of the reference host. This is a practical reference; the normative definitions are in the [REST:ACT specification](https://github.com/stackumi/restact), which also covers the conceptual model. The data and verb API is under `/v1`. The host also serves `/healthz`, `/readyz`, the discovery document, `/openapi.json` and `/operations` at the root, `/mcp` when `RESTACT_MCP=1`, and the operator routes under `/admin` when their variables are set, plus — opt-in via `RESTACT_BUILTIN_SITE=1` — the static site and curated in-repo documentation. > **`curl` + `{id}` patterns.** When a route contains literal braces (bound-verb patterns), pass `-g` (globoff) or URL-encode them as `%7Bid%7D`, or curl mangles the path. ## Addressing REST:ACT defines exactly three addressing forms (the grammar is recursive — a verb is itself an object with properties): 
```text
/v1/<obj>                     the object's data (the "noun")
/v1/<obj>:<verb>              the verb (bare form = invoke)
/v1/<obj>:<verb>/<property>   a property of the verb (code, policy, meta, …)
```
 A verb’s binding determines what `<obj>` looks like: | Binding | Route example | Matches | | -------------------------- | --------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------- | | **Entity** | `docs/{id}:wordCount` | `docs/42:wordCount`, `docs/anything:wordCount` | | **Collection** | `docs:bulkImport` | `docs:bulkImport` | | **Unbound** (service root) | `:bulkImport` | `/v1/:bulkImport` (operator-only to deploy on an auth host) | Invocation **resolves up the path hierarchy**: `:ping` on `docs/42` searches `docs/42`, then `docs`, then the root, and runs the first match — bound to the original target. ## Verb taxonomy | | Pure (no side effects) | Side-effecting | | --------------- | ----------------------- | ------------------------- | | **Name** | Function | Action | | **HTTP method** | `GET` (safe, cacheable) | `POST` | | **Composition** | chains freely | chains only when declared | The host enforces the method: a `POST` to a function, or a `GET` to an action, returns **`405`**. ## Invocation plane ### Run a function 
```sh
GET /v1/<obj>:<fn>
```
 Input may come from the request body or the `?input=` query string (the body wins if both are present): 
```sh
curl -X GET --data-binary 'count a b c'        localhost:8080/v1/text:wc      # body
curl -X GET 'localhost:8080/v1/text:wc?input=count%20a%20b'                   # query string
```
 ### Run an action 
```sh
POST /v1/<obj>:<action>
# with the demo verb seeded (RESTACT_EXAMPLES=1, or `make run`)
curl -X POST --data-binary 'echo hello' localhost:8080/v1/demo:run
```
 The response body is the verb’s output payload (`200`). Send `Prefer: respond-async` on an action `POST` and the host answers `202` with `Location: /operations/{id}` instead (see [Async operations](#async-operations)). ## Data plane (the noun) Besides verbs, an object has **data** — its representation. With the `data-plane` feature, a bare object path (no `:verb`) is that representation: 
```sh
PUT /v1/docs/architecture        # store a JSON object of properties; a null member removes one
GET /v1/docs/architecture        # -> {"document": "# Architecture…", "title": "…"}
```
 
```sh
curl -X PUT -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
     --data-binary '{"document":"# Hello\n\nbody"}' localhost:8080/v1/docs/page
curl localhost:8080/v1/docs/page          # -> {"document":"# Hello\n\nbody"}
```
 `GET` returns the properties the caller may read; `PUT` merges its members, each stored typed (feature `typed-values`: a string, number, boolean, array or object, returned as sent), and a top-level `null` member removes that property (feature `put-null-removes`; a `null` for an unset property changes nothing, `"$policy": null` removes the object’s own policy (below), for `$owner` or any other `$` attribute it is `400`, and for a set `final` property `409` `property-final`). Numbers follow I-JSON: finite, and a whole-number value within ±(2^53−1), so `1e21` is `400` `invalid-input` (an integral double folds to an integer and `-0` to `0`; send a larger value as a string). Nesting deeper than 64 levels is `400`. `$` members keep their shapes: `$policy` may be a JSON object (stored and served as a JSON string via `?meta=policy`), a non-string `$owner` or any other `$` member is `400`. The `ETag` is over the RFC 8785 canonical form of the readable map, so it is unchanged for an all-string object (a non-empty body needs a `Content-Type`: `application/json` or a `+json` type for a merge, else the value’s own media type), honoring per-property write ACL and `final`. A string or member name holding U+0000 (in a value or as a property name) is `400` `invalid-input`, on every store, and nothing is written. `GET` of an object with no stored data is `404`. On a path no `:update` accessor serves, a `PUT` with a non-empty body and no usable `Content-Type` (none, a blank media type, or one that is not a single `type/subtype`, or has a comma anywhere in it) is `415` `invalid-input` and writes nothing; more than one `Content-Type` line is refused the same way whatever the body. An accessor receives the body unparsed. `DELETE` removes the object (`204`). A caller who may not read an existing object gets the answer an absent one gets: `404` from `DELETE`, and from `PUT` the answer the same `PUT` gets at an absent path (the creation rules’ `403` or `404` where they refuse it, else `404`, and nothing is written); a caller who may read it but not change it gets `403`. `POST` and `PATCH` go to an accessor verb (`:create`, or `:set` then `:update`) and answer `405` without one. This is the data that [object-capable verbs](/docs/guides/authoring-verbs/#acting-on-object-data) read and write via the `object` interface. Boundaries: * **First-writer-owns; undeclared properties are public-read.** The first writer of an object becomes its owner (`$owner`); an *undeclared* property is then writable only by that owner (transparent under the anonymous-open default, where everyone is `anonymous`) and readable by anyone. Declare a property with an `acl`/`final` to restrict reads or open writes. **Do not expose a host beyond localhost without auth** (see [Configuration → Security](/docs/reference/configuration/#security)). * **Object policy + ownership.** Two `$`-prefixed fields are client-writable through the data plane (every other `$`- or `@`-prefixed member is in a reserved namespace — `400`, nothing stored, and that includes a `null` member): * `$policy` — a `{read, write, delete}` baseline policy (canonical matchers), gated by the object’s current `write` policy. Read it back with `GET /v1/<obj>?meta=policy` (the **effective** policy + `$owner`, gated on manage authority — i.e. the object’s `write` policy; a denied/missing object is `404`), or `?meta=policy&raw=1` for only the dimensions explicitly set (unset stay empty). `"$policy": null` (feature `put-null-removes`) removes the object’s own policy, so it falls back to its governing anchor’s policy or the defaults. It needs the authority a `$policy` rewrite needs, and is refused on a secrets-mount slot (`422` `invalid-input`), under the connector prefix (`422` `connector-birth-ungoverned`), and with `RESTACT_GOVERNED_CREATION=1` at a path no anchor governs (`403`). * `GET /v1/<obj>?meta=provenance` (feature `object-provenance`) returns `{created, updated}`, each `{subject, actor?, at}` — who created and last wrote the object (every committed mutation, `$policy`/`$owner` and blob writes included), `actor` only for a write made under delegation, **never a grant id**; `at` is RFC 3339 UTC to the second. It is read-gated by the object’s own `read` (a denied or missing object answers as a `GET` does, `404`), never part of the representation. * `$owner` — **ownership is fixed**: no write changes it. `PUT /v1/<obj> {"$owner":"<subject>"}` is admitted only from the object’s current owner **and** only when the value equals that owner (after the usual trim), which is a no-op, so a GET-then-PUT keeps working. Any other value, or any other caller, is `403` and the write changes nothing; an empty or non-string `$owner` is `400`. There is no transfer: *grant* access with `$policy` instead (sharing lends access without moving the owner), and copy an object to give someone their own. An operator can recover ownership with [`POST /admin/reown`](#operator-ownership-recovery-post-adminreown). A creating write by an authenticated caller that carries `$owner` stays `403`, because an absent object’s effective owner is `anonymous`. An object a verb creates with a bound `object.set` records the frame principal as its owner, and no write records an owner on an existing ownerless object. An ownerless object’s `$policy` and `$owner` writes are gated as if `anonymous` owned it (a named caller is refused unless the object’s write policy admits the caller); data-member writes to it claim nothing. A governing anchor’s write policy must admit the caller, and its `owner` matcher means the anchor’s `$owner`. With no governing anchor the write is admitted, except under `RESTACT_GOVERNED_CREATION=1`, which refuses it with `403`. The bound `object.set` and the foreign `set-at` follow the same rule. * **`shared`-scoped properties are not writable here** (`403`) — they’re single-valued on the definition object; write them through a verb’s `object.set`. They’re also excluded from `GET` (they aren’t part of a concrete object’s representation). * **The reserved `@` namespace is not addressable** (`400`): `@shared/<def>` storage keys can’t be read or written through the data plane or `get-at`, and no verb can be deployed or invoked under an `@` segment. A property name beginning `@` is reserved like one beginning `$`: a `PUT` member, a secrets-mount `PUT` member, a verb write and a declaration with such a name are refused, and reads never return one. * **A `.` or `..` path segment is not addressable** (`400 invalid-input`), whether sent literally or percent-encoded (`%2e`, `%2E`). This applies to every data-plane method including `OPTIONS` and `PATCH`, to `:verb` invocation, and to the `prefix` of `GET /v1`; the refusal comes before any `404` or `405` and does not echo the path. The `:verb/<property>` management routes and verb deploy refuse such a path (and an `@` segment) the same way. A verb’s own `object` calls refuse such a path as they refuse an `@` segment, and a compose target with one answers `not-found`; a schedule or subscription `target` with one is `400 invalid-input`. An object path a verb names is never percent-decoded, so `%2e%2e` there is an ordinary name. Other names with dots (`a.b`, `...`, `.hidden`, `x..y`) are valid. * **Per-object caps:** at most 1024 properties and 1 MiB of data per object (`413` beyond), and data-plane writes are metered against the per-tenant quota (`RESTACT_QUOTA_CALLS`). ### Binary object values An object’s value can be **binary** — an avatar, post media — not just JSON properties (features `binary-values` + `object-binary`). A bare-object `PUT` with a **non-JSON `Content-Type`** stores the body in the content-addressed blob store; the object’s representation then *is* those bytes: 
```sh
# store bytes (write-ACL/`final`/size checked like any property write)
curl -X PUT -H 'Content-Type: image/png' --data-binary @avatar.png \
     localhost:8080/v1/users/alice/avatar
# GET serves them back, byte-identical, with the stored Content-Type + a content-hash ETag
curl localhost:8080/v1/users/alice/avatar            # -> the PNG bytes (image/png)
```
 * A `GET` of a binary object **always serves the bytes** (with the stored `Content-Type`, a content-hash `ETag` so `If-None-Match` → `304`, and `nosniff`) — there is no JSON view of it. The response is **privately cacheable**: `Cache-Control: private, max-age=3600` + `Vary: Authorization` (blob reads are ACL-gated per principal, so only a browser cache may hold them, keyed on the bearer token; the content-hash `ETag` makes staleness harmless and the `304` refreshes both headers). Internally the bytes live in the dedup’d blob store and the object keeps a tiny `$blob`/`$blob-type`/`$blob-size` reference, so the per-object size cap bounds the reference, not the media. * **Active content is neutralized.** Because the bytes *and* the `Content-Type` are caller-controlled and served on the host’s own origin (default policy is public-read; the site + API are same-origin), a served blob carries the same restrictive `Content-Security-Policy` as a verb’s HTML output, and a **script-capable** type (`text/html`, `image/svg+xml`, any `*+xml`) is additionally served `Content-Disposition: attachment` — i.e. it **downloads instead of rendering inline**, so a stored HTML/SVG object value can’t run script same-origin. Images (`image/png`, …) still display inline. Conversely, a `Content-Type` in the JSON family (`application/json` or any `+json` suffix, e.g. `application/activity+json`) is **not** binary — it is stored as a queryable JSON noun (property merge), not a blob. * The blob is size-capped by `RESTACT_MAX_BLOB_BYTES` (default 8 MiB → `413`), and a blob `PUT` is metered (bytes-written) against the per-tenant quota. * Verbs read these bytes via the `object` capability’s [`get-bytes`](/docs/guides/authoring-verbs/#acting-on-object-data) (wasm/native, and `obj.getBytes()` / `obj.get_bytes()` in the JS and Python tiers, up to a quarter of the script heap) — the read-time media-transform pattern. Verb-*persisted* binary (`set-bytes`) is not available. ### Object collection (`GET /v1`) and queries `GET /v1` lists the **client-addressable objects with stored data** the caller may read — the object collection (feature `object-listing`). Listing is always ⊆ what the caller could read (ACL-filtered), ordered by path in byte (code-point) order, and paginated: 
```sh
GET /v1                                  # -> {"objects":[{"path":"docs/architecture","properties":2}, …], "nextPageToken":"…"}
GET /v1?pageSize=50&pageToken=<token>    # next page (honest keyset cursor — never silently truncated)
GET /v1?prefix=library/book/             # only objects whose path begins with "library/book/"
GET /v1?filter=kind=note                 # only objects whose readable `kind` property == "note" (a stored string matches byte-for-byte; a non-string matches when its canonical JSON equals the literal, so `filter=n=5` matches both "5" and 5)
GET /v1?read_mask=title,kind             # add a "values" map of just these readable properties to each entry
```
 `nextPageToken` is an opaque, sealed token the server issued for this listing and caller. It is bound to the filter and the caller, so pass it back unchanged on the same request shape. A `pageToken` the server did not issue for that listing and caller (built by hand, altered, from another filter or another subject) answers `400 invalid-input`. An empty `pageToken` means the first page. `pageSize` may change between pages. `prefix` (feature `object-listing-prefix`) keeps only the objects whose path starts with the given string. It is empty or ends in `/`, so `ws/7/` lists everything below `ws/7` at any depth and not `ws/7` itself, which you read with `GET /v1/ws/7`. The value is form-decoded, so send a literal `+` as `%2B`. Two cases answer `400 invalid-input`: a prefix that does not end in `/` (`ws/7`), and one no listable path can begin with (a leading `/`, an empty segment such as `a//`, a `.` or `..` segment, a segment that starts with `@`, or a NUL). The `nextPageToken` is bound to its prefix, so repeat the same `prefix` on every page; a different prefix, or none, is the same `400`. Send `prefix` only when discovery advertises `object-listing-prefix`: a host without the token may ignore it and return the whole listing. On PostgreSQL the prefix is a byte range over the `object_attrs_path_pattern` (`text_pattern_ops`) index, and path order is byte order, the same as on the in-memory and file stores. The `filter=`/`read_mask=` refinement is the **`object-query`** feature (a `filter` that is not `key=value` is `400 invalid-filter`); `GET /v1` itself orders **by path**. The richer **ordered query** primitive (order by an *arbitrary property*, descending, keyset cursor) is reached from verbs through the `object` capability’s `query` (feature `verb-object-query`), or by composing a native query verb the platform exposes (e.g. `feed:candidates`), so a timeline/outbox/search is one indexed query rather than a scan. On the PostgreSQL backend both the listing and that primitive are indexed SQL. See [Embed restactd in a Rust app → the query primitive](/docs/guides/embedding/#the-query-primitive-collections). ### Event streams (SSE) With eventing enabled (the `event-subscriptions` feature), a bare-object `GET` content- negotiated with `Accept: text/event-stream` returns a long-lived **Server-Sent Events** stream of the object’s events instead of its representation: 
```sh
curl -N -H 'Accept: text/event-stream' localhost:8080/v1/rooms/lobby
```
 * **Read-gated** like a data-plane `GET`: a caller who could not read the object receives no stream. * **Replay + resume:** each event carries a monotonic `id`; reconnect with `Last-Event-ID: <id>` (or `?after=<id>`) to replay only events after it. A subscriber that falls behind receives a `gap` event whose data is `{"$publisher":{"kind":"host"},"missed":<n>}`, signalling it should reconnect. A resume whose `Last-Event-ID` is older than the oldest retained event of the topic (evicted by the event-count, per-topic byte or across-topic byte bound, or trimmed from the PostgreSQL log) starts with the same `gap`, `missed` counting the evicted events after the cursor (an upper bound when many have been evicted or when the topic was dropped and recreated meanwhile). On PostgreSQL `missed` is absent when a replay query failed and the count is unknown, and a clean reset also gives a stale cursor a `gap`. A `gap` frame carries no `id:` line, so it never moves the client’s `Last-Event-ID`. A fresh subscribe gets none, and a host that has restarted without the durable log has no memory of what it dropped, so it sends none either. * **Publisher stamp:** every frame’s `data` is a JSON object carrying a `$publisher` member. It is `{"kind":"host"}` for an event the server publishes (data-plane lifecycle events, checkpoint events) and `{"kind":"verb","verb":"<deployed route pattern>"}` for one a verb published, such as `widget/{id}:notify`, never the concrete route. * **Bounded:** concurrent streams are capped (`RESTACT_MAX_SSE_STREAMS`, `503` past the cap) and each stream ends after `RESTACT_SSE_MAX_SECS` so the client reconnects with `Last-Event-ID`. * **Kill-switch:** with `RESTACT_DISABLE_EVENTS` set, the discovery token is omitted and this same request falls through to the normal data-plane representation (the complete kill-switch). A verb publishes to its object’s stream via the gated `event` capability (`event.publish`); the host also auto-publishes data-plane changes. An event name is 1-128 bytes with no ASCII control characters (U+0000-U+001F, U+007F); anything else is refused `invalid-input` at publish (a verb surfaces it as `422`). `onCreate`, `onChange`, `onDelete` and `gap` are the server’s own names: a verb publishing one gets `403 forbidden`. The data is empty or one JSON object with no top-level member whose name starts with `$` (else `invalid-input`); the host adds the `$publisher` member and leaves the rest of the text as the verb wrote it. The topic is at most 1024 bytes (else `invalid-input`, `422` from a verb); the stored data, stamp included, is at most `RESTACT_EVENT_MAX_DATA_BYTES`, and one invocation publishes at most `RESTACT_EVENT_RING_MAX_BYTES` in total, over either being `413 object-too-large` with nothing stored or broadcast. A verb that publishes to a topic others read thereby discloses its deployed route to them. See [Configuration → Eventing](/docs/reference/configuration/#eventing-lifecycle--object-features). ## Management plane ### Deploy a verb 
```text
POST /v1/modules?verb=<route-id>[&kind=function|action][&calls=<a,b,...>][&produces=<media-type>][&description=<text>][&input_schema=<json>][&output_schema=<json>][&exec=public|owner][&object=deny][&compose=deny][&event=deny][&llm=deny][&lang=…][&fuel=<n>][&mem_mb=<n>][&wall_ms=<n>][&runs_as=owner][&invoker=1]
[If-None-Match: *]                     (create-only; feature verb-conditional-requests)
Content-Type: application/wasm        (or another advertised source language)
<component or module bytes>
```
 `description` is deployer-authored prose, for people and models — documentation, not a declaration of behavior; no effect on invocation, authorization, or any other property. UTF-8, ≤ 1024 bytes after decoding; over the bound is `400 invalid-input`, never truncated. (The `verb` route is likewise bounded: 1024 decoded bytes by default, `RESTACT_MAX_ROUTE_BYTES`; over it is `400 invalid-input`, `route exceeds N bytes (got M)`.) Absent or empty → none declared. Reflected verbatim (never redacted) in discovery, `:verb/meta`, `/openapi.json`’s `x-restact-verb`, and the `/mcp` facet’s `list_verbs`. A POST replacement re-declares it (an omitted value clears a previously-declared one); `PUT :verb/code` and a rollback preserve the currently-registered one. The KEY is matched literally — a percent-encoded spelling of the key itself (e.g. `%64escription=…`) is not recognized as `description` and is silently ignored as an unrelated query parameter; only the *value* is percent-decoded. `input_schema` and `output_schema` (feature `verb-schemas`, always advertised) declare the verb’s input and output as JSON Schema 2020-12, each the percent-encoded JSON text of one schema, at most 8192 bytes after decoding. They are documentation for clients and models: the host never validates an invocation against them. A schema must be self-contained: a JSON object or a boolean, `$schema` only at the root and only the 2020-12 URI, no `$id`, `$anchor`, `$dynamicAnchor`, `$dynamicRef` or `$vocabulary`, every `$ref` a `#/...` JSON Pointer that resolves to a subschema inside the document (use `$defs`; legacy `definitions` is refused), at most 64 levels deep, no U+0000, and every standard keyword of the right shape. A violation is `400 invalid-input` naming the parameter and the pointer of the node, and nothing is deployed. They are emitted unredacted as `inputSchema` and `outputSchema` in discovery, `:verb/meta` and `x-restact-verb`, and re-declared by every POST (omitting one clears it); `PUT :verb/code` and a rollback preserve the registered ones. A host without the token ignores the parameters, so clients check `service.features` first. `fuel` is the verb’s per-invocation compute, clamped to the operator ceiling. A JavaScript or Python verb is parsed on every call out of that fuel, so a large source (roughly 73 KB of Python or 185 KB of JavaScript, depending on the source) can exhaust the default and answer `429` unless it is deployed with a higher `fuel`. `produces` declares the verb’s **output media type** (e.g. `text/html`), which the invocation response carries; absent → `application/octet-stream`. `object=deny` / `compose=deny` / `event=deny` / `llm=deny` each withhold their gated capability if the component declares it (a grant can only withhold, never add) — `event` is the event-publish capability; `llm` is the typed-connector capability. Deploy refuses a `function`-kind wasm-component verb that declares `llm` at all. A source-language verb is never refused at deploy time; its effective `llm` grant is withheld instead. Either way `llm=deny` has no effect on a `function`, since it never has the grant `deny` would withhold. Caution `produces: text/html` is attacker-controlled output. A verb that renders object data to HTML is emitting bytes derived from possibly-malicious uploaded data. The host hardens such responses with `X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff` and a strict `Content-Security-Policy`, but the **verb must still** escape raw HTML *and* allow-list URL schemes (reject `javascript:`/`data:` in links/images) — see [`render-html`](https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/tree/main/examples/render-html) for the reference. Don’t serve such output same-origin with privileged cookies. The host validates the upload, checks the import allow-list (rejecting disallowed imports with `403 disallowed-import`), content-addresses and persists it, and registers the route. Returns `201 Created` with a `Location` of `/v1/<verb>` and a body of `verb`, `kind`, `sha256` and `imports`. **Born private (feature `verb-private-default`).** A new verb’s policy admits only its owner (the deployer) for `read`, `execute` and `write`: other principals see it in discovery but cannot invoke it or read its properties until the owner shares it with `PUT …/policy`. `exec` is the one-step shorthand for `execute` only: `exec=public` lets anyone invoke it (its `read` stays `owner`), and `exec=owner` keeps it owner-only. Any other `exec` value, empty included, is `400 invalid-input`. On an anonymous-open host (no auth configured) every caller is the owning anonymous principal, so the born policy makes no observable difference there; strict `exec` and the keep-the-policy replacement rule below apply there too. Without the feature, a new verb is born `read`/`execute` = `public`, `write` = `owner`. **Replacing a verb.** A `POST` at a route that already holds a registration replaces it. A replacement by the owner **keeps the existing policy**; `exec`, when given, overrides `execute` and nothing else. To change any other part of the policy, use `PUT …/policy`. A replacement by a non-owner holding `write` keeps the policy verbatim and refuses `exec` (`400`). **Create-only (feature `verb-conditional-requests`).** Send `If-None-Match: *` to create the verb only if the route is free: when a registration already exists at that exact route (the reference host refuses an object path with an empty segment, `400`, and treats any `{name}` wildcard as `{id}`, exactly as for replacement), the host answers `412 precondition-failed` and changes nothing, or the replacement’s `401`/`403` if you could not have replaced that verb. Of concurrent create-only requests for one route, handled by one host process, at most one succeeds (the serialization is per node; see the paragraph on conditional management writes below). It is the only precondition this endpoint accepts: any other `If-None-Match` value, or an `If-Match`, is `400`. **Discovering the endpoint (feature `verb-reference-deployment`).** A host that advertises it accepts `POST /v1/modules` as described here. A host without the token may still accept it, so a client may try; if the host refuses it as unsupported (`404`, `405` or `501`), it does not accept verb deployment this way; do not retry. 
```sh
curl -X POST --data-binary @verb.wasm 'localhost:8080/v1/modules?verb=docs:summarize&calls=docs:wordCount&exec=public'
# create-only: 412 if docs:summarize already exists
curl -X POST -H 'If-None-Match: *' --data-binary @verb.wasm 'localhost:8080/v1/modules?verb=docs:summarize'
```
 **Source languages.** The `?lang=` query parameter declares a *source* language; the `Content-Type` header alone does not. Without `?lang=`, the body is treated as a WebAssembly component, and a script sent that way is refused `400` (`invalid-component`). With the optional `source-languages` feature the host accepts `lang=text/javascript`: the script is stored as the verb’s source and **interpreted** server-side by the shared QuickJS runner (no client compile, no per-upload compilation).
