The upload is syntax-checked at deploy by a compile-only parse; a script that does not parse is rejected **`422` (`restact_code: invalid-source`)**, carrying the engine’s message — instead of a `201` that only fails at first invoke. A check that outruns the operator’s window (`RESTACT_SOURCE_VALIDATE_WALL_MS`) is not a verdict on the script: it is **`503` `unavailable`** with `Retry-After`, and nothing is stored. Runtime faults still surface at invoke (`script-error`). A `?lang=` value with no registered runtime returns `415` (`unsupported-language`). 
```sh
curl -X POST -H 'Content-Type: text/javascript' --data-binary @verb.js \
     'localhost:8080/v1/modules?verb=docs:summarize&kind=function&lang=text/javascript'   # -> 201
```
 The host likewise accepts `text/x-python` when its monty runner is present; the same deploy shape applies with a `def invoke(input)` script: 
```sh
curl -X POST -H 'Content-Type: text/x-python' --data-binary @verb.py \
     'localhost:8080/v1/modules?verb=docs:summarize&kind=function&lang=text/x-python'   # -> 201
```
 ### Verb properties A verb is an object; its facets are addressed as properties and operated on with ordinary HTTP methods. The conventional (RECOMMENDED, not required) names are below. `PUT …/code` and `PUT …/policy` can create a registration at the request’s concrete route, so a route over `RESTACT_MAX_ROUTE_BYTES` (default 1024 bytes, counting the longer of the decoded route and its stored `{id}` form) is `400 invalid-input` there too; reads, `DELETE`, `PUT …/props` and rollback are not length-checked. | Property | `GET` | `PUT` | `DELETE` | Notes | | ---------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `…/code` | read effective code (`Accept` negotiates source vs. wasm); `ETag` = sha256 of the source (or the component) | upload/replace (`Content-Type` = language) | remove | wrong type → `415`; oversized → `413` | | `…/policy` | read the authorization policy (with its own `ETag`†) | set the actions you send (omitted actions keep their value; fail-closed validation → `400`); owner-only (`manage`); the `owner` member can only restate the current owner | — | `{owner, read, execute, write}`; each action is one matcher or an OR-list | | `…/meta` | `{route, verb}`: the verb descriptor (kind, binding, limits, declared capabilities, call graph); the owner is in `…/policy` | — | — | read-only | | `…/props` | declared data properties (with its own `ETag`†) | replace the declarations (a name beginning `$` or `@` is reserved → `400`) | — | the data-property profile | Each policy action (`read`/`execute`/`write`) is an OR-list of **canonical subject matchers** — `public`, `owner`, `sub:<id>`, `role:<r>`, `group:<g>`, `scope:<s>`, `claim:<k>=<v>` (the same vocabulary as per-property ACLs and object `$policy`), plus, in verb policy only, `readers:<path>` (feature `readers-matchers`): it admits whoever may currently `read` the object at `<path>`, checked on every request, so sharing or unsharing that object shares or unshares the verb. An absent or unreadable object admits no one; `readers:` in object `$policy` or a property ACL is `400`. **Careful:** an object with no `$policy` of its own and no governing anchor is publicly readable by default, so `readers:` pointing at it admits every caller, anonymous included. Point `readers:` at an object with its own `$policy` or under a governing anchor. A single matcher may be written as a bare string and reads back as one; a list reads back as an array. A verb created through `POST /v1/modules` starts owner-only for every action (feature `verb-private-default`); `exec=public` opens `execute`. An action omitted from a `PUT …/policy` keeps its current value, so `{"execute":"owner"}` narrows `execute` and leaves `read` and `write` alone. An unrecognized matcher or an empty list → `400`. A verb’s owner is fixed. The `owner` member of a `PUT …/policy` body is read like this: | `owner` member | Result | | -------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------- | | absent | the owner is kept | | a string exactly equal to the current owner (no trimming, no case folding) | no-op; the rest of the body applies | | a string that differs | `403` `forbidden`; nothing in the body is applied | | present but not a string | `400` `invalid-input` | Re-registration, `PUT …/code`, `PUT …/props` and rollback keep the owner too. An operator can recover ownership with [`POST /admin/reown`](#operator-ownership-recovery-post-adminreown). **History and rollback (feature `verb-history`).** `GET …:verb/history` returns the verb’s current hash triple, owner and timestamps plus a bounded, newest-first list of superseded deployments (never the code bytes); it needs `write` on the verb. `POST …:verb/rollback`, naming a history entry by its full hash triple, rebinds the verb’s code to that version and changes nothing else (owner and policy are kept). It is gated like `PUT …/code`. † **Conditional management (feature `verb-conditional-requests`).** `code`, `policy` and `props` each carry their own `ETag`, and a policy change never invalidates a code tag (or the reverse). `If-Match` is honoured on `PUT`/`DELETE …/code`, `PUT …/policy`, `PUT …/props` and `POST …/rollback` (compared with the current code tag: use the `ETag` of the default, source representation of `GET …/code`, not of an `Accept: application/wasm` read); a stale tag is `412 precondition-failed` and nothing changes. Successful `PUT`s return the new tag. Preconditions are checked only after authorization: a caller without the right gets its `401`/`403`, never a `412`. Within one host process, the reference host runs every write to a verb, and every `POST /v1/modules` to the same exact path pattern and verb name (a write to an instance path of a wildcard verb is keyed on that instance path), in one serialized step, so two conditional writers to one facet cannot both succeed and a policy write is never lost to a concurrent re-upload. That serialization is per node: on a multi-node PostgreSQL deployment it does not span nodes. An `If-Match` or `If-None-Match` value that is not visible ASCII is `400`, never ignored. Without the token, only `PUT …/code` honours `If-Match`. 
```sh
curl localhost:8080/v1/docs:summarize/meta
curl -i -X OPTIONS localhost:8080/v1/docs:summarize/code     # the Allow set for this property
# simple (string) form — owner-only execute:
curl -X PUT -H 'Content-Type: application/json' --data-binary '{"execute":"owner"}' \
     localhost:8080/v1/docs:summarize/policy                  # -> 204
# rich form — execute admits either role (OR-list):
curl -X PUT -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
     --data-binary '{"execute":["role:editor","role:admin"]}' \
     localhost:8080/v1/docs:summarize/policy                  # -> 204
curl -X DELETE localhost:8080/v1/docs:summarize/code          # -> 204
# share with whoever can read workspace ws/1 (feature readers-matchers):
curl -X PUT -H 'Content-Type: application/json' --data-binary '{"execute":["owner","readers:ws/1"]}' \
     localhost:8080/v1/docs:summarize/policy                  # -> 204
# conditional policy edit (feature verb-conditional-requests):
curl -i localhost:8080/v1/docs:summarize/policy               # -> 200, ETag: "<tag>"
curl -X PUT -H 'If-Match: "<tag>"' -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
     --data-binary '{"read":"public"}' localhost:8080/v1/docs:summarize/policy   # -> 204, or 412 if changed
```
 Writing follows the prototype model: `PUT /docs/42:noise/code` defines `noise` *on that entity* (an override); `GET` returns the effective code, resolving up the chain. Which properties exist, and which methods each accepts, is host-defined and discoverable — clients learn it from `OPTIONS` (the `Allow` header) and the discovery manifest, never by guessing. ## Discovery Clients never assume a feature; they ask. 
```sh
GET /.well-known/restact          # the discovery manifest
OPTIONS /v1/<obj>:<verb>          # 204 with Allow: the verb's method and OPTIONS
OPTIONS /v1/<obj>:<verb>/<prop>   # the Allow set for a property
```
 The discovery document advertises the service `version`, `consistency` level, the optional `features` the host supports, `defaultCapabilities`, and the verbs with their kinds, limits, declared capabilities, and call graph. It lists only the verbs the caller may read, invoke or manage, so two callers can see different lists; a credential that fails is refused `401`, and no credential is the anonymous caller. Each response carries `Vary` over the identity headers the host reads, and one computed for a signed-in caller is `Cache-Control: private, no-cache`. `service.acceptedSourceLanguages` lists the upload `Content-Type`s the host accepts — always `["application/wasm"]`, plus each source language with a registered runtime (e.g. `"text/javascript"`). When a non-wasm language is available the host also advertises the `source-languages` feature token. A host started without the JS runner advertises neither, and an upload with `?lang=text/javascript` returns `415`. `service.openapi` (always `/openapi.json` on the reference host, feature token `openapi-document`) points at a generated OpenAPI 3.1 description of the server’s live surface — every verb route the caller may read or invoke as a path (the same filter as discovery), `x-restact` carried through. `GET /openapi.json` serves it unconditionally. A verb that declares `inputSchema`/`outputSchema` has each emitted once under `components.schemas` (named from a hash of the route, so bounded by the schema size, not the route length) and referenced as `{"$ref": "#/components/schemas/<name>"}` from the request body, the `200` response and, for a function, a `?input=` query parameter; a verb that declares none is unchanged. A client (or the MCP bridge, `cli/src/mcp/`) can use it to enrich tool/request schemas instead of guessing from the discovery document alone. An operator MAY additionally set `RESTACT_OPENAPI_SPEC=<path>` to an OpenAPI 3.1 **base document**: the host then MERGES it with the same generated parts on every request instead of serving the bare generated document. `info."x-restact"` and each verb’s `x-restact-verb` always stay live (a stale operator claim can never survive a merge); a path/method the base already documents keeps the operator’s own operation object, refreshing only its descriptor; everything else in the base — notably the bare-object **data plane**, which the generator has no way to document on its own — passes through untouched. The host performs no automated data-plane generation or inference: the base is the only source of that documentation. `openapi/examples/library.openapi.yaml` is a worked example (the book-API demo’s data plane). **Presentation verbs** (feature token `presentation-verbs`, always advertised): a `kind=function` verb deployed with `?produces=application/vnd.restact.ui+json` returns a declarative **UI document** — a JSON tree of components from a fixed catalog (the trust boundary) that a catalog-aware client renders. Such a verb carries `role: "view"` in its descriptor (unless its name is a reserved accessor/lifecycle name, where the `produces` type is the equivalent signal), so a client builds its navigation from discovery: untemplated views (`library:homeView`) are top-level, templated ones (`library/book/{id}:detailView`) are per-object. See the presentation verbs in [the REST:ACT specification](https://github.com/stackumi/restact). ## Cross-cutting semantics A route that resolves to no verb definition answers `404` with `restact_code` `verb-not-deployed` and the fixed detail `no verb deployed`, for every method and every caller. Resolution reads only the deployed definitions (the ones discovery lists), never the object’s stored data or who may read it, so the answer says nothing about which objects exist. Other checks keep their place: a `.` or `..` segment is `400`, a bad credential is `401`/`403`, and a wrong method on a deployed verb is `405`. A route with an empty segment, and a bare path that names no verb, are plain `404 not-found`. A route with an `@` segment is refused `400 invalid-input` at the edge, before resolution (the `@` namespace is reserved). The host advertises the feature `verb-not-deployed` in `service.features`. A verb that ran never produces the code for its caller. When a verb returns `verb-not-deployed`, or passes it up from a call it made, the caller of that verb gets `404 not-found` with the same message. A composing verb can therefore read `verb-not-deployed`, and only that, as “nothing is deployed there”. See [Composition](/docs/guides/authoring-verbs/#distinguishing-host-errors-both-scripting-tiers). ### Errors — RFC 9457 Problem Details Errors use `application/problem+json` with REST:ACT extension members (`restact_code`, `restact_verb`). Status codes reuse standard HTTP: | Code | Meaning | | ------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `200` / `202` | success / accepted (async) | | `400` | bad input (e.g. missing `:verb`, invalid policy); `invalid-component` when an upload is not a valid WebAssembly component or lacks the verb world’s `invoke` export (nothing is stored) | | `401` / `403` | unauthenticated / forbidden (incl. `disallowed-import`, which also covers an import the host does not provide, and `not-declared` for a call edge the verb did not declare) | | `403` (`forbidden`) | also: a `Host` outside `RESTACT_ALLOWED_HOSTS`, or a cross-origin browser request that is not allowed | | `404` | no such property/resolution/object (`not-found`); a route that resolves to no verb is `verb-not-deployed` | | `405` | method/taxonomy mismatch (GET on action, POST on function) | | `408` | wall-clock timeout (epoch), the request timeout, or the request-body transfer deadline (`restact_code: timeout`) | | `409` | conflict: an `Idempotency-Key` request whose first execution is still running, a write to a `final` property (`property-final`), a deploy that violates the object-binding mode (`object-binding`), a create above another principal’s object or verb definition (`ancestor-claim`, when the host advertises `ancestor-creation-guard`), or a concurrent management write | | `412` | precondition (`If-Match` / `If-None-Match`) failed | | `413` | oversized module or input | | `415` | unsupported source language | | `422` | source upload failed the compile-only syntax check (`invalid-source`), or a script raised an error at invoke (`script-error`) | | `429` | quota / rate / fuel budget exhausted | | `500` | the guest trapped or a script failed at run time (`trap`, `script-runtime`), the store failed (`store-error`), or a stored verb no longer links against this host (`verb-unlinkable`) | | `501` | unsupported feature (host can’t satisfy a verb’s requirement) | | `502` | an upstream call failed: mediated egress or the `llm` connector (`egress-upstream-error`, `llm-upstream-error`) | | `503` | the backing store or secrets mount is unreachable (`unavailable`), including a failing `/readyz`, or a source upload’s syntax check timed out (`unavailable` + `Retry-After`, nothing stored; `RESTACT_SOURCE_VALIDATE_WALL_MS`) | | `507` | memory limit exceeded (including the wasm stack) | | `508` | composition cycle / depth / fan-out exceeded | A request whose `Host` is not `localhost`, `127.0.0.1`, `[::1]` or listed in `RESTACT_ALLOWED_HOSTS` gets `403 forbidden` (`/healthz` and `/readyz` are exempt); a duplicate, non-text or malformed `Host` gets `400 invalid-input`. A request that is not `GET`/`HEAD`/`OPTIONS`, or any request to `/mcp`, is refused with `403 forbidden` when a browser marks it cross-origin (`Sec-Fetch-Site`, else `Origin` against the request’s authority) and its origin is not listed in `RESTACT_CORS_ALLOW_ORIGIN`. Clients that send neither header (curl, SDKs, the bridge) are unaffected. The detail names the variable to set and never echoes the value. A URL path containing `%00` (a decoded U+0000) is `400 invalid-input` on every route and method, reads and deletes included, before authentication; the query string is not checked, because verb input travels there. The upload parameters of `POST /v1/modules` (`verb`, `description`, `calls`, the schemas) may not contain `%00` either. A JSON request body the host parses must not repeat a member name in any object, at any depth. Such a body is refused `400 invalid-input`, naming the member, after authentication and before any authorization or lookup, so every authenticated caller gets the same answer. An anonymous mutation on a host with auth configured is `401` first, without the body being parsed; with no auth configured the check runs first. This covers data-plane `PUT` bodies sent as JSON (a non-JSON body is stored as a blob, and an accessor-mediated data-plane `PUT` is checked the same way), verb policy, props and rollback bodies, sub-token mint, grants (create and patch; redeem is unauthenticated and answers `400` to everyone), schedules, subscriptions, `/admin/reown` and the JSON-RPC body of `POST /mcp` (a `400` with a JSON-RPC parse error). A disabled schedule or subscription surface still answers `501`. A string or member name holding U+0000 in those same bodies is refused `400 invalid-input` the same way, before any lookup (the detail never echoes the value); a `$policy` matcher or a secrets-mount `value` holding it is refused too. Verb input is not parsed by the host and is not covered, which includes the `POST` and `PATCH` bodies an accessor verb receives. ### Conditional requests `ETag` + `If-None-Match` / `If-Match` (RFC 7232) for caching and optimistic concurrency: 
```sh
curl -i -X GET -H 'If-None-Match: *' localhost:8080/v1/docs:summarize/code   # -> 304
```
 A failed `If-Match` precondition returns `412`. On the object data plane (`GET` and `PUT /v1/<obj>`) both headers go through one parser: repeated header lines combine as one comma list, `*` cannot be mixed with entity-tags, and a value that is not a quoted entity-tag or contains bytes outside visible ASCII is `400 invalid-input`. `If-Match` compares strongly (a `W/` validator never matches) and `If-None-Match` compares weakly. Preconditions apply only where the response without them would be a success or a `412` (RFC 9110 section 13.2.1). On a `GET`, a well-formed `If-Match` that does not match the current `ETag` of a readable object is `412`, and a matching `If-None-Match` (including `*` on an existing object) is `304`. An object that is absent or not readable by the caller is the plain `404` whatever the headers say, and a malformed header is `400` only for a caller who may read the object. `If-Match` is evaluated first. On a `PUT` the same rule applies to JSON values, binary values and the secrets mount, evaluated inside the write lock after the write-authority check (RFC 9110 section 13.2.1). A caller who may read an object but not write it gets `403`, never a `412` that shows whether its state matched, and never a `400` for a malformed header or for a precondition the secrets mount does not support. A caller who may not read an existing object is answered as the same request is at an absent path, where a write that would create is still refused the same way: the precondition’s outcome (`412` for an `If-Match` that nothing can satisfy, `400` for a malformed header or a secrets-mount precondition) is part of that answer, and `404` comes only after it. `If-None-Match: *` makes the write create-only: it creates the object when none exists and returns `412` when one does, and the check and the write are one atomic step, so of several concurrent first-creates exactly one wins. An `If-None-Match` list of validators returns `412` when one matches the object’s current `ETag` and proceeds otherwise; `If-Match` returns `412` when none matches. For a binary value the `ETag` is the content hash that `GET` serves. With both headers, `If-Match` is evaluated first. A secrets-mount value is never readable and has no `ETag`, so there only `If-None-Match: *` is accepted (`412` when the slot exists, with the backend untouched); a malformed header, `If-Match` or any other `If-None-Match` is `400 invalid-input`, answered after the authority check. `If-Match: *` asks whether a current representation exists, the same test `If-None-Match: *` uses, so a caller who may write an object but not read it can pass it. A list of validators needs a representation the caller can read. `DELETE /v1/<obj>` follows the same rule, evaluated in the delete’s critical section after the existence and delete-authority checks: `If-Match` with a stale validator is `412` and the object stays, a binary value compares against the content hash `GET` serves, repeated `If-Match` lines combine, and `If-None-Match: *` on an existing object is `412`. An absent object is `404`, and a caller who may not delete gets `403` whatever the headers say. In a coupled object-binding mode the caller’s authority over the bound verbs is checked before the precondition, so a caller who may delete the object but not a verb bound to it gets `403`. `DELETE` of a secrets-mount value takes the mount’s rule: the precondition is checked after the authority gates and before the backend is purged, and the purge and the reference removal run in the same critical section, so a refused request leaves the secret in place. Where a verb makes the write, the host has no stored tag to compare. A `PUT`, `PATCH`, `POST` or `DELETE` that an accessor verb serves (`:update`, `:set`, `:create`, `:delete`), and a verb-route action (`POST /v1/<obj>:<action>`, including `Prefer: respond-async`), is refused with `400 invalid-input` when it carries any `If-Match` or `If-None-Match`, well-formed or not. The verb does not run and no idempotency record is written. The authentication, delegation and anonymous-mutation checks answer first, so a caller they refuse gets their `401` or `403`. To guard such a write, put a version field in the body, or use `set-at-if` from a verb. A read that a verb serves evaluates both headers against the sha256 `ETag` of its output: `GET` on an accessor-served object (`:get`, `:list`) and `GET /v1/<obj>:<function>` answer `412` for an `If-Match` that does not match, `304` for an `If-None-Match` that does (a `W/` form, a list or `*`), and `400` for a malformed value, after the verb’s own policy has allowed the call. `GET /v1/<obj>?meta=<facet>` evaluates them after its read or manage check: it has no `ETag`, so a list in `If-Match` is `412` and `*` passes, and `*` in `If-None-Match` is `304`. An event-stream subscribe (`Accept: text/event-stream`) with either header is `400` once the caller may read the topic. With `RESTACT_ACCESSOR_DISPATCH=off` the bare path is the plain data plane described above. On a secrets-mount path no method dispatches to an accessor, whether it is deployed at the slot pattern or at an ancestor: the data plane serves `GET`, `PUT` and `DELETE`, and `PATCH` and `POST` answer `405`. Every `405` carries an `Allow` header listing the same methods `OPTIONS` reports for that path, whether the mismatch is a wrong method on a verb, on a verb property, or on a plain object. A method sent to a verb that is not deployed is `404`, the same answer `OPTIONS` gives. On the verb plane, `If-Match` beyond `PUT …/code` and create-only `If-None-Match: *` on `POST /v1/modules` need the `verb-conditional-requests` feature (see [Verb properties](#verb-properties) and [Deploy a verb](#deploy-a-verb)). ### Idempotency For retry-safe actions, send an `Idempotency-Key` header. A repeat by the same authenticated caller on the same route replays the first result, whatever the body. While the first request is still running, a repeat answers `409`. The key is scoped to the caller and the route, so the same key on another route runs normally. Anonymous callers share one identity, so their key is ignored and the action just runs. ### Async operations An action `POST` that carries `Prefer: respond-async` returns `202 Accepted` with `Location: /operations/{id}`. Poll the operation resource; `DELETE` cancels it: 
```sh
GET    /operations           # the caller's own operations, oldest first
GET    /operations/{id}      # AIP-151 fields: name, done, result or error
DELETE /operations/{id}      # cancel
```
 Both routes are owner-scoped: another tenant’s operations are invisible (404 on the item route, absent from the listing). The listing is unpaginated by design — the table is bounded and owner-filtered (feature token `operations-listing`). A U+0000 in a recorded error’s code or detail is shown as U+FFFD. ## Other endpoints | Route | Purpose | | -------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `GET /healthz` | Liveness probe (`ok`). | | `GET /readyz` | Readiness probe: `200` when the backing store answers, `503` when it does not. | | `POST /admin/reset` | Operator clean-reset (opt-in) — see below. | | `POST /admin/checkpoint` | Durable checkpoint (opt-in) — see below. | | `POST /admin/reown` | Operator ownership recovery (opt-in) — see below. | | `GET /` and the site pages | The static website + in-browser playground — opt-in, `RESTACT_BUILTIN_SITE=1`; off by default. | | In-repo docs | The host serves curated documentation directories (`spec/`, `openapi/`, `examples/`, `conformance/`, `wit/`, `docs/`) read-only, restricted to documentation file types — the same opt-in switch as the site above; off by default. | ### Schedules, subscriptions and tokens These routes are brief here; the guides carry the worked examples. | Route | Purpose | | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `POST`/`GET /v1/schedules`, `GET`/`DELETE /v1/schedules/{id}` | Register a timer that invokes an action every `intervalSeconds`; owner-scoped. See [Schedules and subscriptions](/docs/guides/schedules/). | | `POST`/`GET /v1/subscriptions`, `GET`/`DELETE /v1/subscriptions/{id}` | Register an action to run on each event published to a topic object. See [Schedules and subscriptions](/docs/guides/schedules/). | | `POST /v1/tokens/sub` | Mint a short-lived read-only token limited to a path prefix (feature `path-scoped-tokens`; needs `RESTACT_JWT_SECRET`; `RESTACT_SUB_TOKENS=off` removes the route). See [Hand out a path-scoped sub-token](/docs/guides/authentication/#hand-out-a-path-scoped-sub-token). | | `POST`/`GET /v1/tokens/grants`, `GET`/`PATCH`/`DELETE /v1/tokens/grants/{id}`, `POST /v1/tokens/grants/redeem`, `POST /v1/tokens/exchange` | Delegated-token grants and the token exchange (feature `delegated-tokens`; see `RESTACT_DELEGATION`). Creating or renewing a grant from a credential whose roles, groups, scopes or claims contain U+0000 is `400`. | | `/mcp` | The remote MCP facet, mounted only when `RESTACT_MCP=1` (feature `mcp-facet`). | ### Verb subscriptions are read-gated (`verb-subscriptions-read-gate`) `POST /v1/subscriptions {"topic","target"}` requires `read` on the topic object at registration **and at every delivery**. Registration on a topic the caller cannot read, or that does not exist, is `404 not-found` (the same refusal SSE gives; create the topic object first). A topic the caller can read but the subscription’s subject-only registrant cannot (readable only through a role, group, scope or claim matcher) is `403 forbidden`. Each delivery is re-checked against the registrant’s current read authority, before the min gap and the quota; a refused delivery is skipped, never retried, invisible to the registrant, and logged as one content-free `warn` line. An `onDelete` is checked against the pre-delete snapshot and skipped without one (on PostgreSQL every `onDelete` delivery is skipped). Advertised as `verb-subscriptions-read-gate` alongside `verb-subscriptions`; it cannot be turned off. ### Operator clean-reset (`POST /admin/reset`) Returns a running host to its **post-bootstrap state** — equivalent to stopping the process, deleting the data dir, and starting again, without restarting. Wipes all tenant state: deployed verbs + blobs, object data + listing, schedules/subscriptions, event replay rings + the durable event log, quota tallies, the idempotency cache and operations table — then re-runs the same idempotent startup bootstrap (which seeds the example content only with `RESTACT_EXAMPLES=1`, and always registers the script runners). The SSE event-id counter deliberately survives, so a client reconnecting with its old `Last-Event-ID` replays exactly the post-reset events; live SSE streams end and `EventSource` clients auto-reconnect. **OFF by default.** Enable with `RESTACT_ADMIN_RESET=<matcher-list>` (the canonical subject matchers, e.g. `role:admin`, or `public` for an intentionally-open localhost host — see [Configuration](/docs/reference/configuration/)); unconfigured, the route answers `404` like any unknown path and the `admin-reset` discovery feature token is omitted. With auth configured, an anonymous `POST` is `401` (the anonymous-mutation rule) and a non-matching principal `403`. 
```sh
POST /admin/reset              # → 200 {"reset":true}; 409 `conflict` while a checkpoint or another reset runs
```
 Name a **dedicated operator subject** in `RESTACT_ADMIN_RESET` (and in `RESTACT_ADMIN_REOWN`, below), one that is used for nothing else. The gate matches the caller’s subject, so any full bearer minted under that subject carries the same power, for example a user bearer that a plugin minted for it. Only delegated and path-scoped tokens are refused. ### Operator ownership recovery (`POST /admin/reown`) Ownership is fixed, so nothing an owner has can change hands by an ordinary write. If an owner leaves, their verbs and roots would freeze; this route recovers them. It moves the verbs and objects that one subject owns, under one path, to another subject. **OFF by default.** Enable with `RESTACT_ADMIN_REOWN=<sub:…,sub:…>`: `sub:` matchers only, none of them `sub:anonymous`, and authentication must be configured. Anything else leaves the route off and prints a boot `warning:` line. Unconfigured, the route answers `404` (`OPTIONS` too) and discovery omits the `admin-reown` feature token. Name a **dedicated operator subject** here: a full bearer minted under that subject has the same power (only delegated and path-scoped tokens are refused). Gate order: off `404` (whatever the body size); then the 64 KiB body cap, `413`, **before authentication** (so an anonymous caller or a non-operator also gets `413` for an oversized body); then an invalid credential `401`; a path-scoped sub-token `403`; anonymous `401`; a caller matching no configured subject `403`; then the body, `400` `invalid-input` when invalid. 
```sh
POST /admin/reown   Content-Type: application/json
{ "from": "bob", "to": "carol", "path": "ws/acme", "subtree": true, "dryRun": false }
# → 200 {"from":"bob","to":"carol","path":"ws/acme","subtree":true,"dryRun":false,
#        "verbs":[{"route":"ws/acme/{id}:onCreate","runsAsCleared":false,"invokerCleared":false,"paused":true}],
#        "objects":12,"skipped":{"secretSlots":1}}
```
 A body is `400` when it has an unknown member; when `from` or `to` is missing, not a string, empty after trimming, has leading or trailing whitespace, or contains a control character; when `from == to`; when `to` is `anonymous` or begins `client:`; when `path` is missing or not a data-plane object path (no leading `/`, no `v1/` prefix, no `:`, no `@` segment, no `.` or `..` or empty segment, nothing at or under the reserved delegation paths); or when `subtree` or `dryRun` is not a boolean. `from: "anonymous"` is allowed. When the server is down or the route is off, [`restactd reown`](/docs/operate/storage/#move-ownership-offline) runs the same move offline and prints this `200` body with `--json`. The request body is limited to about 64 KiB: a larger one is `413` (and with the route off, `404`). What moves, and what does not: * Selection is literal and owner-filtered: an object whose path equals `path` (or, with `subtree`, lies under it) **and** whose stored owner is `from`, and a stored verb deployment whose route is anchored at `path` (or, with `subtree`, under it) **and** whose owner is `from`. A route’s anchor is its leading run of literal segments, so `ws/acme/{id}:onCreate` is anchored at `ws/acme`. Native verbs are never selected. * A moved verb takes the new owner, loses `runs_as=owner` and the invoker grant, and, if it is a lifecycle hook (`onCreate`, `onChange`, `onDelete`), is **paused**: it does not fire until its new owner redeploys its code. The new owner re-opts in with their own credential. Policies are kept. * A moved object changes only its owner. No hook fires and no event is published. * **Not moved:** schedules and subscriptions (they keep firing as their registrant), grants and sub-tokens (the subject’s own credentials), and secret-mount slots (skipped and counted in `skipped.secretSlots`). * `dryRun: true` validates, selects and reports, and writes nothing. * Each item is atomic; the call as a whole is not. A part-way store error is `500` `store-error` carrying the `verbs`, `objects` and `skipped` counts already done, and a retry finishes the move. A `PUT …/policy` that races a reown of the same verb answers `409 conflict`. * Each non-dry-run call writes one operator-log line on `restact.mgmt`. A call that stopped part-way (a store error, or a panic in the move) ends its line with `(stopped part-way)`; a completed call’s line never does. The line is written even when the client disconnects mid-call. ### Durable checkpoint (`POST /admin/checkpoint`) Copies the durable store to `<checkpoint-dir>/checkpoint-<ts>-<seq>` — a best-effort point-in-time snapshot (crash-stop consistency). The snapshot of a filesystem store is itself a valid store root: restore by pointing `RESTACT_DATA_DIR` at it (or copying it over a wiped root). Around each run the host publishes `checkpoint-started` / `checkpoint-finished {ok, snapshot, durationMs}` on the reserved **`@host/checkpoint`** topic — guests can subscribe (verb subscriptions, or SSE on `/v1/@host/checkpoint` as any authenticated principal) but can never publish there. **OFF by default.** Enable with `RESTACT_ADMIN_CHECKPOINT=<matcher-list>` (gating exactly as reset: unconfigured → `404` + no `admin-checkpoint` token; anonymous under auth → `401`; non-matching → `403`). `RESTACT_CHECKPOINT_INTERVAL_SECS=<n>` additionally runs an interval loop (floored at 30 s) — that alone also advertises the token. Retention: `RESTACT_CHECKPOINT_KEEP` newest snapshots (default 8). Snapshots survive `POST /admin/reset`. A checkpoint and a reset never overlap: one that finds the other running (or another checkpoint) is refused `409` and never queued, and an interval tick that finds the lock held is skipped. The checkpoint request is exempt from `RESTACT_REQUEST_TIMEOUT_MS`. 
```sh
POST /admin/checkpoint         # → 200 {"checkpoint":true,"snapshot":"checkpoint-…","durationMs":…}; 409 `conflict` while a reset or another checkpoint runs
```

# WIT interfaces

> Every verb is a WebAssembly component that targets one world of the WIT package rest-act:plugin@0.1.0. The world a verb targets lists the only host functions it can call, so it doubles as the verb's…

Every verb is a WebAssembly component that targets one world of the WIT package `rest-act:plugin@0.1.0`. The world a verb targets lists the only host functions it can call, so it doubles as the verb’s capability manifest. The package lives in [`wit/`](https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/tree/main/wit). Check it with `wasm-tools component wit wit/`. ## Worlds A world imports interfaces and exports one function, `invoke`. A host links only the imports a component declares, so a verb that does not import `compose` cannot reach it. | World | Imports | Use | | --------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `plugin` | `host` | The minimal verb: structured logging only. | | `plugin-compose` | `host`, `compose` | Calls other verbs. | | `plugin-identity` | `host`, `identity` | Reads who is calling. | | `plugin-object` | `host`, `object`, `identity` | Reads and writes the data of its object. | | `plugin-object-compose` | `host`, `compose`, `object`, `identity` | Object data and composition. | | `plugin-object-event` | `host`, `object`, `identity`, `event` | Object data and event publishing. | | `plugin-object-compose-event` | `host`, `compose`, `object`, `identity`, `event` | Object data, composition and events. | | `plugin-llm` | `host`, `llm` | Model completions only. | | `plugin-object-llm` | `host`, `object`, `identity`, `llm` | Object data and model completions. | | `plugin-object-compose-event-llm` | `host`, `compose`, `object`, `identity`, `event`, `llm` | Everything above. The reference host binds this world, so it can instantiate a verb built against any of the others. | No world offers `wasi:sockets`, and `wasi:filesystem` is linked with no directories, so a verb has nothing to read or write. Outbound network access goes through the host, not through the guest; see [Outbound calls and secrets](/docs/guides/egress/). ## The entry point Every world exports the same function. 
```wit
export invoke: func(input: payload) -> result<payload, invocation-error>;
```
 `payload` is `list<u8>`. The HTTP layer carries the encoding, which is JSON by default. An `invocation-error` has a stable `code` and a human-readable `message`; the host maps the code to an HTTP status and a problem document. See [Errors and status codes](/docs/reference/errors/). ## Interfaces ### host Ambient: every verb gets it. | Function | Meaning | | --------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `log(level, message)` | Emit a log record. Levels are `trace`, `debug`, `info`, `warn`, `error`. Rate-limited per tenant. | ### compose Gated by the `plugin-compose` family of worlds, and by the call graph declared at deploy time with `?calls=`. | Function | Meaning | | --------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `invoke(verb, input)` | Call another verb by route id, for example `docs:wordCount`. The callee runs as the same caller (or as its owner, when it was deployed with `runs_as=owner`) and draws from the same budget. | See [Compose verbs](/docs/guides/composition/). ### object Gated. Reads and writes the data properties of the object the verb is bound to, and of other objects by path. Every call is checked against the caller’s access, so a verb never reads or writes what its caller could not. | Function | Meaning | Feature | | --------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------- | | `get(name)` | Read a property of the bound object. `none` when absent or not readable. | `object-data-access` | | `set(name, value)` | Write a property of the bound object. Buffered and merged only if the invocation succeeds. | `object-data-access` | | `get-at(path, name)` | Read a property of another object. | `object-data-access` | | `set-at(path, name, value)` | Write a property of another object. Allowed only if the caller could write it through the data plane. | `object-data-access` | | `remove(name)` | Remove a property of the bound object, under the authority `set` needs. Removing an unset property succeeds; `not-found` when the bound object holds no data. Buffered and applied only if the invocation succeeds. | `object-remove` | | `remove-at(path, name)` | Remove a property of another object. Allowed only if the caller could remove it through the data plane. `not-found` when no object is stored at `path`. | `object-remove` | | `set-at-if(path, name, value, expected)` | Write only if the current value equals `expected` (`none` means absent). Atomic compare-and-swap. | `object-cas` | | `shape()` / `shape-at(path)` | List readable property names, readable child paths and invokable verb names. | `object-data-access` | | `get-bytes()` / `get-bytes-at(path)` | Read a stored binary value as a content type and bytes. | `object-binary` | | `query(prefix, order-by, descending, predicate, limit, cursor)` | One ordered, access-filtered, cursor-paginated page of objects under a path prefix. The host caps `limit`. | `verb-object-query` | | `path()` | The concrete path of the object the verb was invoked on, not the route pattern it is deployed at. `none` for an unbound verb, or when the object grant is withheld. | `object-self-path` | A property value is a `property-value` variant (feature `typed-values`): `text(string)` for a JSON string, carried raw, and `json(string)` for any other JSON value as JSON text, which the host emits in RFC 8785 canonical form. A stored string reads as `text`; anything else reads as `json`. On a write, `json` must parse and a number whose value is a whole number must lie within ±(2^53−1) (so `1e21` is refused) and nesting is bounded at 64 levels, else `invalid-input`; `json("null")` is refused with `invalid-input` (a stored property is never null); a `json` payload that parses to a string is stored as that string; the host folds integral doubles to integers and `-0` to `0`. `set-at-if` compares stored forms: `text` byte for byte, `json` by canonical form, and a `text` never equals a `json`. A `query` row’s `properties` and `order-value` carry `property-value` too: the order value is `text` for a string, an absent property (`text("")`) or the path, and `json` otherwise. `set-at-if` is refused under the `atomic-invocation` consistency level, and the host stops advertising `object-cas` at that level. See [Store and query object data](/docs/guides/object-data/) and the [features table](/docs/reference/features/). ### identity Ambient except for `invoker`. A verb already runs as its caller, so reading the caller grants no new authority. | Function | Meaning | Feature | | ---------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | ------------------ | | `subject()` | The authenticated subject, or `none` when anonymous. | `caller-identity` | | `is-anonymous()` | True when there is no subject. | `caller-identity` | | `roles()` | The caller’s roles. | `caller-identity` | | `scopes()` | The caller’s scopes. | `caller-identity` | | `groups()` | The caller’s group memberships. | `caller-groups` | | `claim(name)` | One scalar token claim, stringified, or `none`. | `caller-identity` | | `invoker()` | The subject on whose behalf an authority-switched invocation tree runs. `none` unless the verb was deployed with `?invoker=1`. | `identity-invoker` | | `actor()` | The delegation actor that bounds this frame, or `none`. Never reveals the delegating subject. | `identity-actor` | See [Identity and authority](/docs/concepts/identity-and-authority/). ### event Gated. | Function | Meaning | | ---------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | | `publish(topic, name, data)` | Publish an event on a topic, which is an object path. Subscribers receive it over Server-Sent Events. The name is 1-128 bytes with no ASCII control characters; anything else is refused with `invalid-input`. A `function` verb cannot publish. | Needs `event-subscriptions`. See [Schedules and subscriptions](/docs/guides/schedules/). ### llm Gated. Present only in the `plugin-llm`, `plugin-object-llm` and `plugin-object-compose-event-llm` worlds. | Function | Meaning | | --------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `complete(req)` | One bounded, synchronous, text-only model completion through a connector object. Returns the text, a stop reason, the model that answered and token usage. | A completion request names the connector path, an optional system prompt, a list of role/content messages, and optionally a model, an output-token limit and a temperature. `complete` has side effects, so deploy refuses a `function` verb that imports it. It is also refused under process isolation. Needs `llm-connector`. See [Outbound calls and secrets](/docs/guides/egress/). ## Stability The package is `0.1.0` and is not frozen yet. It freezes at outside adoption: the first public release, or earlier if the maintainer declares it. Until then a change to any file under `wit/` needs the maintainer’s approval, and a change to an existing declaration lands together with every component built against the package. After the freeze `world.wit` (`types`, `host`, `compose`, and the `plugin` and `plugin-compose` worlds) is not edited at all, so components compiled then keep linking. A breaking change after the freeze means a new package version. New capabilities, before and after the freeze, arrive as additions in the same package `0.1.0`, each behind its own feature token: a new interface in its own file, a new world that includes an existing one, or a new method on an existing interface outside `world.wit`. Adding an import never breaks an existing component, because the host binds the superset world. A component that imports a newer method cannot link on a host that predates it. For that reason each such method has its own [feature token](/docs/reference/features/) in discovery, and a client checks for the token before deploying a verb that needs the method. The host checks linking at deploy: an upload that imports something the host does not provide is refused with `403` `disallowed-import` naming the import, and a component that links but lacks the world’s `invoke` export is refused with `400` `invalid-component` naming the export. Nothing is stored for a refused upload. Choose the smallest world that covers what your verb does. The deploy step rejects a component that imports something outside the host’s allow-list or that the host does not provide, and `llm` when `llm-connector` is not enabled. A grant withheld from a verb, such as `?object=deny`, is not checked at deploy; it is enforced at call time.

# Install

> The quickest route is to download a release archive, check it against its checksum and unpack it. You can also run a published container image, or build restactd from a clone of the repository.

The quickest route is to download a release archive, check it against its checksum and unpack it. You can also run a published container image, or build restactd from a clone of the repository. ## Download a release Releases are on [GitHub](https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/releases). Each one carries archives for three platforms, named by Rust target triple: | Platform | Target | | -------------------- | --------------------------- | | Linux, x86-64 | `x86_64-unknown-linux-gnu` | | Linux, arm64 | `aarch64-unknown-linux-gnu` | | macOS, Apple silicon | `aarch64-apple-darwin` | Other platforms, Intel Macs included, build from source untested. Windows is not supported. The examples below use `VERSION` for the release you want, such as `0.1.0`. Substitute your own, and the target for your machine. ### Pick an archive The host comes in two flavours. `restactd` is the default: it keeps data in memory, or on the filesystem when `RESTACT_DATA_DIR` is set, and refuses to start if `RESTACT_DATABASE_URL` is set. `restactd-full` adds the PostgreSQL backend, the OpenBao secrets backend and OpenTelemetry export, so pick it for a PostgreSQL cluster or for traces and metrics. The OpenTelemetry exporter in the `-full` build starts when an OTLP endpoint is set (`OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT` or a per-signal one); setting `OTEL_SDK_DISABLED=true` turns it off whatever else is set. See [Observability](/docs/operate/observability/). | Archive | Contents | | -------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | | `restactd-vVERSION-TARGET.tar.gz` | The host, without PostgreSQL. | | `restactd-full-vVERSION-TARGET.tar.gz` | The host with PostgreSQL, OpenBao and OpenTelemetry. | | `restact-vVERSION-TARGET.tar.gz` | The `restact` client and its MCP bridge. | | `restact-conformance-vVERSION-TARGET.tar.gz` | The conformance runner, for testing another server. | | `restactd-runners-vVERSION.tar.gz` | The JavaScript and Python runner components alone. The host archives already contain them. | | `restactd-docs-vVERSION.tar.gz` | These docs as static files, built for restact.org/docs. A `.sha256` file sits beside it. | | `checksums.txt` | SHA-256 of every file above. | ### Verify Download the archives you want and `checksums.txt` into one directory, then check them: 
```sh
VERSION=0.1.0
TARGET=x86_64-unknown-linux-gnu
base=https://github.com/stackumi/restactd/releases/download/v$VERSION
curl -fLO $base/restactd-v$VERSION-$TARGET.tar.gz
curl -fLO $base/restact-v$VERSION-$TARGET.tar.gz
curl -fLO $base/checksums.txt
sha256sum -c checksums.txt --ignore-missing
```
 On macOS, use `shasum -a 256 -c checksums.txt --ignore-missing`. The release workflow also records a build provenance attestation for each archive, which the GitHub CLI checks: 
```sh
gh attestation verify restactd-v$VERSION-$TARGET.tar.gz --repo stackumi/restactd --deny-self-hosted-runners
```
 ### Unpack and run 
```sh
tar -xzf restactd-v$VERSION-$TARGET.tar.gz
tar -xzf restact-v$VERSION-$TARGET.tar.gz
export PATH="$PWD/restactd-v$VERSION-$TARGET/bin:$PWD/restact-v$VERSION-$TARGET/bin:$PATH"
restactd --version
restactd                                 # serves http://127.0.0.1:8080
```
 Data stays in memory until you set `RESTACT_DATA_DIR`, for example `RESTACT_DATA_DIR=$PWD/data restactd`. Each per-target archive unpacks to one directory named like the archive, holding `bin/`, `share/` where it has one, `README.md`, `CHANGELOG.md` and `LICENSE`. The host archives hold: 
```text
restactd-v0.1.0-x86_64-unknown-linux-gnu/
  bin/restactd
  share/restactd/runners/js_runner.wasm
  share/restactd/runners/py_runner.wasm
  share/restactd/runners/SHA256SUMS
```
 The host finds the runners by their place next to the binary: a `restactd` in `bin/` looks in the sibling `share/restactd/runners/`, so the JavaScript and Python tiers work from the unpacked directory without any setting. If you copy the binary somewhere else, copy `share/` with it and keep the two side by side, for example `/usr/local/bin/restactd` and `/usr/local/share/restactd/`. The runner variables are in [Configuration](/docs/reference/configuration/). A release build reports its version number as the first line on stdout, in place of the `dev` a source build prints. `restactd --version` prints that line without starting the server. ### Linux requirements The Linux binaries need glibc 2.28 or later. That covers Debian 10, Ubuntu 20.04, RHEL 8 and anything newer; Ubuntu 18.04 and Amazon Linux 2 are too old. On an older system the loader stops with `version 'GLIBC_2.xx' not found`; see [Troubleshooting](/docs/operate/troubleshooting/#the-system-glibc-is-too-old). ### macOS The macOS binaries are unsigned and not notarized. If you downloaded the archives with a browser, clear the quarantine flag on the unpacked directories, or Gatekeeper refuses to open the binaries: 
```sh
xattr -dr com.apple.quarantine restactd-v$VERSION-$TARGET restact-v$VERSION-$TARGET
```
 ## Run a container image Releases publish two images to `ghcr.io/stackumi/restactd`: | Image | Tags | Contents | | ----- | ---------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------- | | slim | `VERSION`, `X.Y`, `latest` | Filesystem store only. | | full | `VERSION-full`, `X.Y-full`, `full` | Adds PostgreSQL, OpenBao and OpenTelemetry. | A release candidate gets only the exact `VERSION` and `VERSION-full` tags. Run one: 
```sh
docker run --rm -p 127.0.0.1:8080:8080 -v restact-data:/data ghcr.io/stackumi/restactd:$VERSION
```
 For production, pin the image by digest rather than by tag. The digests are listed in each release’s notes, and you can read one from the registry: 
```sh
docker buildx imagetools inspect ghcr.io/stackumi/restactd:$VERSION
docker run --rm -p 127.0.0.1:8080:8080 -v restact-data:/data ghcr.io/stackumi/restactd@sha256:<digest>
```
 The attestation check also works on an image: `gh attestation verify oci://ghcr.io/stackumi/restactd:$VERSION --repo stackumi/restactd --deny-self-hosted-runners`. [Run with Docker](/docs/operate/docker/) covers volumes, host names, compose and process isolation. ## Build from source Building from source is for working on restactd itself or for a platform with no archive. restactd is built and tested on x86-64 Linux. You need `rustup` 1.28 or later, `git`, `make`, `curl`, a C toolchain and network access. The first build compiles Wasmtime and takes several minutes. 
```sh
git clone https://github.com/stackumi/restactd
cd restactd
rustup toolchain install                 # the pinned toolchain from rust-toolchain.toml
# cargo-component builds the JavaScript runner; it compiles from source
cargo install cargo-component --locked --version 0.21.1
make build-js-runner                     # the JavaScript verb tier
cargo run -p restactd                    # serves http://127.0.0.1:8080
```
 The checkout pins Rust 1.99.1 and the `wasm32-wasip2` target in `rust-toolchain.toml`, so `rustup toolchain install` fetches exactly that. The server prints its build as the first line on stdout. A build from source reports `dev` as its version, and the commit it was built from, read from git in every profile; `commit=unknown` appears only when there is no git checkout and `RESTACT_BUILD_COMMIT` is unset. Without starting it, `cargo run -p restactd -- --version` (or `restactd --version` for an installed binary) prints the same line. In a second terminal, check that the server is up: 
```sh
curl localhost:8080/healthz
```
 
```text
ok
```
 The `restact` command line is a separate binary from the same checkout: `cargo build -p restact` writes `target/debug/restact`. The [quickstart](/docs/start/quickstart/) uses it, and the [reference](/docs/reference/cli/) lists its commands. Run `cargo run -p restactd` from the repository root. The host looks for the runners and example verbs at paths relative to the working directory. ### Optional pieces Without them the server still starts. It omits a language tier whose runner is missing: discovery does not list the language, and an upload in it returns `415`. With `RESTACT_EXAMPLES=1` it also prints a `warning:` line for each example verb it cannot find. | You want | Run | Notes | | ---------------------- | ---------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | Python verbs | `make build-py-runner` | Pure Rust; fetches `monty` from crates.io, pinned to an exact version. | | The Rust example verbs | `make build-examples` | Needs `cargo-component`. Builds the demo verb behind `demo:run`, which the host seeds only with `RESTACT_EXAMPLES=1`. | | Everything | `make build-all` | Host, example verbs and both runners. | | The browser playground | `make run` | Sets `RESTACT_EXAMPLES=1` and serves the built-in site at `http://127.0.0.1:8080/playground.html`. | `./scripts/bootstrap.sh` installs the whole toolchain in one step: the pinned Rust, the `wasm32-wasip2` target, `wasm-tools` and `cargo-component` at their pinned versions, the repository’s git hooks, and the gate tools through mise (gitleaks, cargo-deny, cargo-nextest, shellcheck, node, actionlint and zizmor). It also checks that PyYAML and jsonschema are importable. It is idempotent. You need it only if you will write Rust verbs or work on the repository; the JavaScript path above needs `cargo-component` alone. If you use [mise](https://mise.jdx.dev), `mise.toml` in the repository pins the same toolchain. `make help` lists every target. ### Change the port The host binds `127.0.0.1:8080`. Set `RESTACT_BIND` to move it: 
```sh
RESTACT_BIND=127.0.0.1:9180 cargo run -p restactd
```
 Only one instance can hold a port. To stop the one on 8080, kill whatever is listening on it: 
```sh
lsof -tiTCP:8080 -sTCP:LISTEN | xargs -r kill
```
 `make kill` does the same for the configured bind address. Do not use `pkill` on the binary name: it stops every restactd on the machine. ## Build a Docker image The `Dockerfile` is a multi-stage build. The builder stage compiles the host, the example verbs and both runners. The runtime stage is the distroless `cc-debian13` base, with no shell and no package manager, running as the non-root uid 65532. It contains the binary, the built verbs and the runners. You need Docker, `git` and `make`. 
```sh
git clone https://github.com/stackumi/restactd
cd restactd
make docker-build      # builds ghcr.io/stackumi/restactd:dev with both scripting tiers
make docker-run        # serves http://127.0.0.1:8080 until Ctrl-C
```
 Things to know about the image: * It listens on `0.0.0.0:8080` inside the container, and `make docker-run` publishes that port on `127.0.0.1` only. `PORT=0.0.0.0:8080 make docker-run` publishes it on every interface, which serves an unauthenticated server to your network unless you configure authentication. * It persists to the `/data` volume. `make docker-run` mounts the named volume `restact-data` there, so data survives restarts. * It answers only to the `Host` names `localhost`, `127.0.0.1` and `[::1]` until you set `RESTACT_ALLOWED_HOSTS` to the names your clients use. * It has a health check that calls `/healthz` every 30 seconds. * `make docker-run` also turns on the built-in site and playground (`RESTACT_BUILTIN_SITE=1`) and the example content (`RESTACT_EXAMPLES=1`); the image itself seeds no example content. For a lighter image with no JavaScript or Python tier, use `make docker-build-core`. It builds faster and serves only compiled verbs. Pass any configuration variable with `docker run -e`. [Run with Docker](/docs/operate/docker/) covers volumes, compose and process isolation. ## Check the install With the server running, discovery lists what this build supports: 
```sh
restact discover
```
 Look for `text/javascript` in the `languages` line (`service.acceptedSourceLanguages` in the raw document, which `curl localhost:8080/.well-known/restact` also returns). If it is missing, the JavaScript runner was not built or was not found; for a release archive, check that `share/restactd/runners/` sits next to `bin/`; for a source build, rerun `make build-js-runner` and restart from the repository root. Caution With no authentication configured, every caller is anonymous and may deploy, replace or delete any verb. The default bind address keeps that to your machine. Read [Add authentication](/docs/guides/authentication/) and the [production checklist](/docs/operate/production/) before you expose a host. ## Next Follow the [Quickstart](/docs/start/quickstart/) to deploy a verb. Every environment variable is listed in [Configuration](/docs/reference/configuration/).

# Introduction

> restactd is the reference server for REST:ACT, a protocol that lets HTTP clients upload small sandboxed programs, called verbs, and call them at a URL. This page says what restactd is, how its model…

restactd is the reference server for REST:ACT, a protocol that lets HTTP clients upload small sandboxed programs, called verbs, and call them at a URL. This page says what restactd is, how its model fits in your head, and what ships in the repository. ## What restactd is A REST API fixes its behavior when you deploy the server. If a client needs a new calculation, a new view of the data or a new side effect, someone changes the server and releases it. restactd moves that step to run time. A client uploads a verb, a small program that runs inside a WebAssembly sandbox, and then calls it like any other resource. The verb can read and write the data of the object it is attached to, call other verbs, and nothing else unless the server grants it. restactd is not a general function-as-a-service platform, a container runtime or a database. It stores object data so verbs have something to act on, but it is meant to sit next to your data, not replace it. It also does not run arbitrary processes: a verb is a WebAssembly component, or JavaScript or Python source that a trusted runner component interprets inside the same sandbox. It is built for two kinds of reader. If you build an API and want clients (including AI agents) to add behavior without a server release, restactd is the server to try. If you implement or evaluate the protocol, restactd is the host the [conformance suite](/docs/reference/conformance/) is developed against. ## The model in 30 seconds Every request names an object path and, optionally, a verb after a colon: 
```http
GET  /v1/greeter:hello?input=Ada    # a function: safe to cache, cannot change state
POST /v1/pages/home:visit           # an action: may change state
GET  /v1/pages/home                 # no verb: the data stored on the object
```
 * Object path. `pages/home` names an object. Its data is a set of named properties, each holding a JSON value, which you read and write with `GET` and `PUT`. * Verb. `:visit` after the path names a verb attached to that object, or to a pattern such as `pages/{id}` that covers many objects. * Function or action. A function is called with `GET`, may be cached, and the host refuses any write it attempts. An action is called with `POST` and may write. * Sandbox. Each call runs under a fuel (compute) limit, a time limit and a memory limit. A verb that loops forever is stopped with `429`, not left running. * Capabilities. A verb sees only the host interfaces it was granted: logging by default, and object data, composition and similar interfaces when the deployment asks for them. * Run as the caller. By default a verb acts with the authority of whoever called it, so it cannot read or write anything the caller could not. A verb deployed with `runs_as=owner` runs with its owner’s authority instead. [The model in one page](/docs/concepts/overview/) expands each of these. ## Three ways to write a verb | Language | How it runs | Toolchain you need | | ------------------------------------------------------ | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------- | | JavaScript | Stored as source and interpreted by a shared QuickJS runner component | None beyond the server | | Python | Stored as source and interpreted by a shared runner built on the monty interpreter | None beyond the server | | Rust (or any language that emits a WASI 0.2 component) | Compiled to a WebAssembly component and uploaded as a binary | A compiler for your language | All three run under the same limits and reach the same capabilities. [Language tiers](/docs/concepts/language-tiers/) compares them, and the guides cover [JavaScript](/docs/guides/javascript/), [Python](/docs/guides/python/) and [Rust](/docs/guides/rust/). ## What is in the repository | Path | What it is | | -------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | | `host/` | The restactd server: HTTP surface, sandbox executor, object store and authentication. | | `cli/` | The `restact` client. Its `restact mcp` subcommand is the bridge that serves a host’s verbs as MCP tools for AI agents. See [Use restactd from AI agents](/docs/guides/ai-agents/). | | `sdk/rust/` | `restact-sdk`, a Rust crate for writing compiled verbs. | | `conformance/` | A black-box test suite that runs against any compliant server. See [Conformance suite](/docs/reference/conformance/). | | `examples/` | Example verbs in Rust, plus the JavaScript and Python runners. | | `wit/` | The WebAssembly interface definitions verbs compile against. See [WIT interfaces](/docs/reference/wit/). | | `plugins/` | Skills that teach coding agents to use REST:ACT. | ## Project status restactd is pre-1.0, and its HTTP surface may still change. The WebAssembly interface `rest-act:plugin@0.1.0` only grows, so verbs you compile today keep linking. The protocol itself is a draft, kept in the [stackumi/restact](https://github.com/stackumi/restact) repository along with the conceptual model and the registries. restactd is one implementation of it. The draft is written as an Internet-Draft and not yet submitted to the IETF. ## Next [Install](/docs/start/install/) restactd, then follow the [Quickstart](/docs/start/quickstart/) to deploy your first verb in five minutes.

# Quickstart

> In about five minutes you will deploy a JavaScript function and an action, call both, read the server's discovery document and watch the sandbox stop a runaway verb. You need a running restactd with…

In about five minutes you will deploy a JavaScript function and an action, call both, read the server’s discovery document and watch the sandbox stop a runaway verb. You need a running restactd with the JavaScript runner and the `restact` command line; see [Install](/docs/start/install/). `curl` appears where the raw HTTP is the point. The examples assume the host at `localhost:8080`. The default configuration has no authentication, so every call below works without credentials. Data lives in memory and is gone when you stop the server. ## 1. Start the host Start the `restactd` you installed ([Install](/docs/start/install/)): 
```sh
restactd
```
 In another terminal, put the `restact` client on your `PATH` and check that the server answers. If you built from a checkout instead, run `cargo run -p restactd` from the repository root, and `cargo build -p restact` followed by `export PATH="$PWD/target/debug:$PATH"` for the client. 
```sh
restact --version
curl localhost:8080/healthz
```
 
```text
ok
```
 `restact` talks to `http://127.0.0.1:8080` unless you point it elsewhere; see [the command line reference](/docs/reference/cli/#choosing-the-server). ## 2. Deploy a function A function is a verb you call with `GET`. Write one in JavaScript: 
```sh
cat > hello.js <<'EOF'
function invoke(input) {
  return `Hello, ${input || "world"}!`;
}
EOF
```
 Upload it: 
```sh
restact deploy hello.js --verb greeter:hello --kind function
```
 
```text
deployed greeter:hello
```
 `--verb greeter:hello` names the object `greeter` and the verb `hello`. `--kind function` makes it a function. The language comes from the `.js` extension. On the wire this is one `POST` of the file to `/v1/modules`, and you can send it with curl: 
```sh
curl -i -X POST --data-binary @hello.js \
  'localhost:8080/v1/modules?verb=greeter:hello&kind=function&lang=text/javascript'
```
 
```http
HTTP/1.1 201 Created
location: /v1/greeter:hello
…
```
 The query parameters say what you are deploying. `lang=text/javascript` selects the language; the host reads the language only from `lang`, not from `Content-Type`. The response body is a JSON object with the verb’s `verb` route, `kind`, the `sha256` of its source and its `imports`. The full descriptor is in the discovery document and at `:verb/meta`. ## 3. Call it with GET 
```sh
restact call greeter:hello --query input=Ada
```
 
```text
Hello, Ada!
```
 `input` arrives as the argument to `invoke`. Without it, the verb greets the world: 
```sh
restact call greeter:hello
```
 
```text
Hello, world!
```
 `restact call` reads the server’s discovery document and sends a `GET` for a function and a `POST` for an action. A function is called with `GET` only, so a `POST` is refused. This is the raw response: 
```sh
curl -X POST localhost:8080/v1/greeter:hello
```
 
```json
{"detail":"verb is a Function; invoke it with GET","restact_code":"method-not-allowed","restact_verb":":hello","status":405,"title":"Method Not Allowed","type":"about:blank"}
```
 Errors are RFC 9457 problem documents. `restact_code` is a stable machine-readable code. `restact` prints it with the status and exits 1 (the [exit codes](/docs/reference/cli/#exit-codes) tell failures apart): 
```sh
restact call greeter:hello --post
```
 
```text
restact: method-not-allowed (HTTP 405): verb is a Function; invoke it with GET
```
 ## 4. Deploy an action that stores data An action is called with `POST` and may write. This one is bound to every object under `notes/`. Inside it, `obj` is the object the URL names: 
```sh
cat > save.js <<'EOF'
function invoke(input) {
  const note = JSON.parse(input);
  obj.set("text", note.text);
  log("saved a note");
  return { saved: note.text.length };
}
EOF
restact deploy save.js --verb 'notes/{id}:save' --kind action
```
 `restact` sends the braces as written. (With curl you would need `-g` or `%7Bid%7D`.) Now call it twice on different objects: 
```sh
restact call notes/groceries:save --input '{"text":"buy milk"}'
restact call notes/todo:save --input '{"text":"call Sam"}'
```
 
```json
{"saved":8}
{"saved":8}
```
 The verb wrote to the object it was called on. Each object now holds its own data, which you read with a plain `GET`: 
```sh
restact get notes/groceries
```
 
```json
{"text":"buy milk"}
```
 ## 5. Read the discovery document A host describes itself at a well-known URL, so clients never have to guess what it supports: 
```sh
restact discover
```
 That prints a summary: the version, the object binding mode, the features, the accepted languages and the verbs. `restact discover --raw` prints the whole document, which is what `curl localhost:8080/.well-known/restact` returns. The document has two parts. `service` carries the accepted source languages, the list of optional `features` this host advertises, and the default limits. `verbs` lists the deployed verbs the caller may see (read, run or own) with their route, kind and capabilities. Your two verbs appear there: 
```json
{
  "route": "notes/{id}:save",
  "verb": {
    "binding": "entity",
    "kind": "action",
    "name": "save",
    "sourceMediaType": "text/javascript",
    "capabilities": { "maxComputeUnits": 200000000, "maxDurationSeconds": 2.0, "maxMemoryBytes": 33554432, … }
  }
}
```
 To read one verb’s descriptor without the rest, request its `/meta`: 
```sh
restact get greeter:hello/meta
```
 See [Discovery and optional features](/docs/concepts/discovery/) for how to read the feature list. ## 6. Watch a limit stop a verb Every call runs under a compute budget, a time limit and a memory limit. By default a call gets 200,000,000 compute units, 2 seconds and 32 MiB, as the descriptor above shows. Deploy a verb that never returns: 
```sh
echo 'function invoke(input) { while (true) {} }' > spin.js
restact deploy spin.js --verb greeter:spin --kind function
curl -i localhost:8080/v1/greeter:spin
```
 
```http
HTTP/1.1 429 Too Many Requests
content-type: application/problem+json
retry-after: 1
…


{"detail":"CPU budget exhausted","restact_code":"cpu-budget","restact_verb":":spin","status":429,"title":"Too Many Requests","type":"about:blank"}
```
 `restact call greeter:spin` shows the same refusal as `restact: cpu-budget (HTTP 429): CPU budget exhausted`. The host cut the verb off and kept serving. Budgets and how verbs share them when they call each other are covered in [Capabilities and budgets](/docs/concepts/capabilities-and-budgets/). A script that does not parse is refused at upload instead of failing at the first call: 
```sh
echo 'function invoke(i) { return ' > bad.js
restact deploy bad.js --verb greeter:bad --kind function
```
 
```text
restact: invalid-source (HTTP 422): Error: unexpected token in expression: ''
      at verb:2:1
```
 ## 7. Clean up `restact rm` removes a verb when the argument is `ROUTE:VERB`, and an object otherwise. Without a terminal it needs `--yes`: 
```sh
restact rm greeter:spin --yes
restact rm 'notes/{id}:save' --yes
restact rm notes/groceries --yes
```
 On the wire a verb’s code is its `/code` resource, and each of these is a `DELETE` that returns `204`. A call to the removed verb now returns `404`. Discovery no longer lists the route, so `--post` picks the method: 
```sh
restact call notes/todo:save --post --input '{"text":"x"}'
```
 
```text
restact: verb-not-deployed (HTTP 404): no verb deployed
```
 Stop the server with Ctrl-C. Because the data lived in memory, nothing remains. Set `RESTACT_DATA_DIR` to a directory to keep verbs and data across restarts. ## Next steps * Build a multi-user app with object data, queries, authentication and a lifecycle hook: [Tutorial: build a small app](/docs/start/tutorial/). * Understand the pieces: [The model in one page](/docs/concepts/overview/). * Write verbs in depth: [JavaScript](/docs/guides/javascript/), [Python](/docs/guides/python/), [Rust](/docs/guides/rust/). * Every route, header and status code: [HTTP API](/docs/reference/http-api/). * Turn on authentication: [Add authentication](/docs/guides/authentication/).

# Tutorial: build a small app

> You will build a link shelf: a shared collection of bookmarks that two users add to, click through and search. Along the way you store object data, write verbs that act on it, query a collection,…

You will build a link shelf: a shared collection of bookmarks that two users add to, click through and search. Along the way you store object data, write verbs that act on it, query a collection, turn on authentication so callers cannot overwrite each other, and react to new links with a lifecycle hook. Plan on about 30 minutes. You need a running restactd with the JavaScript runner (see [Install](/docs/start/install/)) and `curl`. If you have not yet deployed a verb, do the [Quickstart](/docs/start/quickstart/) first. ## 1. Start a host with authentication A shared app needs to know who is calling. Start the host with a signing secret for HS256 bearer tokens and a data directory, from the repository root: 
```bash
RESTACT_JWT_SECRET=replace-with-at-least-32-random-bytes RESTACT_DATA_DIR=./shelf-data cargo run -p restactd
```
 That secret is a placeholder. For anything beyond this walkthrough, generate one with `openssl rand -base64 32`; the host refuses to start with a secret shorter than 32 bytes. Two things change when authentication is on. Anonymous callers can read but cannot deploy verbs or write data, and the host defaults to strict object binding, which means you must create an object before you deploy a verb on it. The tutorial uses both. In a second terminal, also at the repository root, make a directory for the rest of the steps: 
```sh
mkdir link-shelf && cd link-shelf
```
 ## 2. Make two users restactd checks the signature on a token and trusts its `sub` claim as the caller’s identity. `restactd token mint` signs a token with the same secret. It reads `RESTACT_JWT_SECRET` from its own environment, so export the secret in the `link-shelf` terminal first. In a checkout, as in step 1, run it through cargo. The manifest path points cargo back at the repository root, one level above `link-shelf`: 
```sh
export RESTACT_JWT_SECRET=replace-with-at-least-32-random-bytes
ALICE=$(cargo run -q -p restactd --manifest-path ../Cargo.toml -- token mint --sub alice --ttl 1h)
BOB=$(cargo run -q -p restactd --manifest-path ../Cargo.toml -- token mint --sub bob --ttl 1h)
```
 With an installed `restactd`, the last two lines are shorter: 
```sh
ALICE=$(restactd token mint --sub alice --ttl 1h)
BOB=$(restactd token mint --sub bob --ttl 1h)
```
 The variables `ALICE` and `BOB` hold the two tokens for the rest of the tutorial. Keep the same shell open. In a new shell, `cd` to `link-shelf` and run all three lines again, because the new shell has no exported secret. ## 3. Store links as object data An object is a path with data attached. Start with the collection root `links`, then put the first link under it. Alice does both, so she owns them: 
```sh
curl -i -X PUT -H "Authorization: Bearer $ALICE" -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
  --data '{"title":"Link shelf"}' localhost:8080/v1/links


curl -i -X PUT -H "Authorization: Bearer $ALICE" -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
  --data '{"title":"The Rust Book","url":"https://doc.rust-lang.org/book/","tag":"rust"}' \
  localhost:8080/v1/links/rust-book
```
 Each returns `204 No Content`. Read the link back as Bob: 
```sh
curl -H "Authorization: Bearer $BOB" localhost:8080/v1/links/rust-book
```
 
```json
{"tag":"rust","title":"The Rust Book","url":"https://doc.rust-lang.org/book/"}
```
 Anyone can read an object’s properties by default. The first writer owns the object and is the only one who can change it. Check that Bob cannot overwrite Alice’s link, and that an anonymous caller cannot write at all: 
```sh
curl -X PUT -H "Authorization: Bearer $BOB" -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
  --data '{"title":"mine"}' localhost:8080/v1/links/rust-book


curl -i -X PUT -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
  --data '{"title":"anon"}' localhost:8080/v1/links/anon
```
 
```json
{"detail":"'bob' may not write 'links/rust-book'","restact_code":"forbidden","restact_verb":"","status":403,"title":"Forbidden","type":"about:blank"}
```
 
```http
HTTP/1.1 401 Unauthorized
www-authenticate: Bearer
…


{"detail":"authentication required to mutate on this server","restact_code":"unauthorized","restact_verb":"","status":401,"title":"Unauthorized","type":"about:blank"}
```
 A property can hold any JSON value, so the click count in the next step is stored as a number. ## 4. Count clicks with an action A verb bound to `links/{id}` acts on whichever link the URL names. This one returns the link’s URL and counts the click: 
```sh
cat > visit.js <<'EOF'
// links/{id}:visit  counts a click and returns the target URL
function invoke(input) {
  const url = obj.get("url");
  if (!url) throw new Error("this link has no url");
  const clicks = (obj.get("clicks") ?? 0) + 1;
  obj.set("clicks", clicks);
  return { url, clicks };
}
EOF
```
 Alice deploys it. Two extra parameters decide who can call it and as whom it runs: 
```sh
curl -g -X POST -H "Authorization: Bearer $ALICE" --data-binary @visit.js \
  'localhost:8080/v1/modules?verb=links/{id}:visit&kind=action&lang=text/javascript&runs_as=owner&exec=public'
```
